# Sūrah Yūnus (Chapter 10) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:1

> Alif-Lãm-Ra. These are the verses of the Book, rich in wisdom

**Expanded Commentary**

**Letters That Decline to Explain Themselves**

The chapter opens with three letters that form no Arabic word. *Alif. Lām. Rā.* They are spoken separately, not read as a name, and no gloss follows them. Twenty-nine of the Qur'an's 114 chapters begin with such disconnected letters, drawn from only fourteen of the alphabet's letters — half the alphabet, in combinations of one to five. *Alif-Lām-Rā* itself opens five chapters, including this one. The striking feature is the silence that follows. The text does not decode its own initials, and the classical exegetes record readings — an oath, a name of God, a name of the sūrah, an abbreviation of a divine attribute, an opening device to arrest attention — without settling on one. The silence is best understood as deliberate. A book that explained its own mystery would remove the one thing the letters do without assistance: they hold the listener still. Spoken aloud before a recitation, they announce that what follows is composed of nothing more exotic than the letters the hearer already owns, and they withhold the reason why those letters matter here.

**The Listener's Own Alphabet, Withheld From Explanation**

The letters belong to Arabic, and Arabic belongs to the audience being addressed. That fact does the argumentative work, and it does it without a single claim. These are the letters of Meccan poetry, of market talk, of the seven famous odes the Arabs memorised and hung in esteem. The same letters are then assembled into a discourse they cannot match — which is why the disconnected initials sit at the head of chapters whose central charge is that this recitation is not human speech. A challenge is most effective when the materials are visibly available to the challenged party, and here they are not merely available but identical. Nothing exotic has been imported; no foreign vocabulary, no impossible metre. The letters that make every Arabic sentence make this one, and the difference lies in what has been done with them. The opening of the chapter therefore states the case for inimitability in the quietest possible register: three letters, no verb, no explanation, and an implication the listener completes alone.

**“These Are the Verses of the Book”**

After the letters comes a demonstrative, and it points at something present. *Tilka āyātu l-kitāb* — these are the verses of the Book — is spoken over a recitation in progress, not over a finished object on a shelf. The word *āyāt* carries both of its senses at once: signs, and verses. The same term names a portent in the sky and a unit of revelation, which means the listener is being told that what he is hearing belongs to the same order of things as the alternation of night and day discussed later in this chapter. The Book is definite — *al-kitāb* — and most commentators take it as the Qur'an as a whole rather than this chapter alone, a reading supported by the identical openings elsewhere: “Alif-Lãm-Ra. These are the verses of the clear Book” (12:1) and “Alif-Lãm-Ra. These are the verses of the Book; the clear Quran” (15:1). Each chapter introduces itself as a part announcing the whole. The effect is to place this sūrah inside a single work with a single character.

**Ḥakīm: A Book That Is Wise and Makes Wise**

The adjective *ḥakīm* is built on the *faʿīl* pattern, and that pattern can be read actively or passively — a thing that possesses a quality, or a thing that confers it. Both readings are recorded for this word, and both hold. Passively, the Book is wise: its statements are sound, its rulings are apt, its meaning is *muḥkam*, precise and firmly joined, a word formed from the same three-letter root. That precision is the subject of 3:7, where some verses are described as precise and foundational while others are elusive. Actively, the Book makes wise: it is a source of *ḥikmah*, the capacity to place things where they belong. The two readings are not competing so much as sequential — a speech is only capable of making its hearer wise if it is itself coherent. The same description is given of the recitation's standing before God: “And indeed, it is—in the Master Record with Us—highly esteemed, rich in wisdom” (43:4), and of the chapter that follows this one: “˹This is˺ a Book whose verses are well perfected and then fully explained. ˹It is˺ from the One ˹Who is˺ All-Wise, All-Aware” (11:1). Wisdom here is a property of the speech, and by derivation of the One who sent it.

**An Opening That Fixes the Terms of the Whole Chapter**

Everything this chapter argues follows from the two claims in its first line. The verses are signs, so the chapter will spend itself on signs — the sky, the alternation of night and day, the history of destroyed peoples, the human response to hardship. The Book is wise, so the chapter will meet a demand for a different book with a refusal grounded in that wisdom. The sūrah is Meccan in the main, 109 verses long, and it takes its name from a prophet who appears in it exactly once, near the end — an ordinary feature of Qur'anic naming, where the title usually marks a striking word rather than the subject. That the opening is so short and so dense is itself a statement of method. No scene is set, no occasion is given, no narrative begins. A listener is handed three letters and one claim about a book, and the rest of the chapter is the demonstration. Read in that light, the two halves of the verse are not an ornament followed by a title. The letters establish who is speaking and from whose alphabet; the claim establishes what the speech is worth. Wisdom is the standard the chapter then applies to itself when it refuses to be edited, and the same standard it applies to the demand for a sign produced on request.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:2

> Is it astonishing to people that We have sent revelation to a man from among themselves, ˹instructing him,˺ “Warn humanity and give good news to the believers that they will have an honourable status with their Lord.”? Yet the disbelievers said, “Indeed, this ˹man˺ is clearly a magician!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wonder Is Not at the Message but at the Messenger**

The verse opens on a question about astonishment, and the astonishment is misplaced. Nothing in the sentence is strange in itself: a book has come, a warner has been sent, believers have been promised a rank. What startles the audience is the address — the messenger is a man from among themselves, someone they knew by name, whose family and poverty they could point at. It is reported that the Quraysh answered the call with a sneer rather than an argument, asking whether God could find no one to send except the orphan in the house of Abū Ṭālib. The complaint was not about content but about standing. That is why the reply here does not defend the message point by point; it questions the shape of their surprise. Astonishment is a proper response to something unprecedented, and a human being carrying a revelation is not unprecedented — it is the only form revelation has ever taken. Their wonder is therefore evidence of a wrong expectation, not of a difficulty waiting to be answered.

**A Human Messenger Was Always the Only Workable Option**

This objection returns across the Book, and every return exposes the same assumption: a heavenly message must arrive in a heavenly body. “And nothing has prevented people from believing when guidance comes to them except their protest: ‘Has Allah sent a human as a messenger?’” (17:94). The answer is given elsewhere in blunt terms — had an angel been sent, it would have had to appear as a man, and the confusion would have been worse rather than better (6:9). A messenger who cannot eat, tire, grieve, or be tempted cannot be imitated, and a messenger who cannot be imitated cannot guide conduct. Mecca mocked the very feature that made the guidance usable: “What kind of messenger is this who eats food and goes about in market-places ˹for a living˺?” (25:7). Others wanted a messenger of higher rank, “a great man from ˹one of˺ the two cities” (43:31) — Mecca or Ṭāʾif — which shows that the grievance was status, not species.

**Warn First, Then Give Good News**

The commission is stated in two halves, and the order matters. Warning comes first because the audience is hostile; glad tidings follow because terror is not the aim. The Prophet's own summary of his task is short: “I am only a man like you, ˹but˺ it has been revealed to me that your God is only One God” (18:110). A warner reminds people of an ending they are already walking toward; a bringer of news tells them that this ending has a favourable case. Neither half works alone. Warning by itself produces despair or defiance; good news by itself produces the complacency this sūrah later names as contentment with the present life. The pairing also fixes the messenger's limits. He warns; he does not sentence. He announces; he does not dispense. Both verbs point toward God and not toward himself, which is why the later demand that he edit the revelation meets a flat refusal.

**Qadam Ṣidq: A Standing Earned, Not Merely Given**

The promise to the believers is phrased as “an honourable status”, literally *qadam ṣidq* — a foot of truth. The idiom is ordinary Arabic rather than technical theology: to have a foot somewhere is to have a claim on it, a place where your weight already rests. Commentators have taken the phrase three ways — a rank of precedence with God, a reward from Him, or the Prophet's intercession on the Last Day — and these read better as degrees of one fact than as rivals. What decides the meaning is the word *ṣidq* fixed to the foot. A truthful foot is one that does not slip when tested, so the standing promised is secure rather than merely high. Set beside the intercession verse, “Who could possibly intercede with Him without His permission?” (2:255), the reward appears as a place granted by permission — and permission is precisely what makes it stable. The believers are promised a footing, and the wording insists the footing will hold.

**“Clearly a Magician” — A Charge with a Long History**

The response to the commission is a slur, and the slur is generic. “Similarly, no messenger came to those before them without being told: ‘A magician or a madman!’” (51:52). Mecca's accusation matches a template older than Mecca: “Now, the pagans are astonished that a warner has come to them from among themselves. And the disbelievers say, ‘This is a magician, a total liar!’” (38:4). The same astonishment, the same word. A charge that fits every prophet fits none of them, and its real function is to close the conversation rather than to test the claim. A variant reading of the final word gives “clear magic” in place of “clear magician”, which turns the accusation from the speaker onto the speech. Either way it is aimed at the human medium: the man is a deceiver, or his book is a trick. Both versions concede that something unprecedented has been said, and both decline to examine it.

**Three Audiences in One Sentence**

People in general are addressed first, then the disbelievers speak, and the narrowing is the point. The opening clause asks what astonishes “people” — all of them — while the closing clause reports what one group said. Between the two stand the believers, promised an honourable standing yet given no words of their own. The structure mirrors the commission it reports: warning to humanity, good news to believers, refusal from the deniers. Three audiences, three outcomes, one sentence. Such economy is characteristic of this opening, which lays out the whole case in a few verses and then spends the rest of the chapter arguing it. Nothing here is decorative. The rhetorical question is not a stylistic flourish but a diagnosis: the resistance to this message is social before it is intellectual, and a social objection is not answered by evidence alone.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:3

> Surely your Lord is Allah Who created the heavens and the earth in six Days, then established Himself on the Throne, conducting every affair. None can intercede except by His permission. That is Allah—your Lord, so worship Him ˹alone˺. Will you not then be mindful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Six Days Describe an Order, Not an Effort**

Creation is given a number, and the number carries no suggestion of strain. A measured period is not a struggle; naming six Days says that the making was staged, ordered, and deliberate rather than instantaneous. The Arabic *yawm* denotes a stretch of time as readily as a solar day, and no sun exists yet at this point in the account, so the six cannot be days measured by one. A report in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2789, from Abū Hurayra, fills the sequence with content: the soil on Saturday, the mountains on Sunday, the trees on Monday, the things entailing labour on Tuesday, light on Wednesday, the animals spread on Thursday, and Adam after ʿAṣr on Friday. It names seven acts where the verse names six Days, which is why commentators have weighed it carefully; what it shares with the verse is the shape — creation as a series of distinct acts, finished and then governed. The same pairing of six Days and the Throne appears at 7:54, 11:7, 25:59, 32:4 and 57:4, so this is a fixed formula, not a passing remark.

**Rule Stated as a Continuous Act**

After the Throne comes a present-tense verb, and the shift from past to present is the whole point. *Yudabbiru l-amr*, conducting every affair, describes management that never stops. The Throne is not a resting place after labour; it is the seat of an administration still running. The same sequence occurs where the heavens are raised without visible pillars: “It is Allah Who has raised the heavens without pillars—as you can see— then established Himself on the Throne” (13:2), which then adds that He subjects the sun and the moon, each orbiting for an appointed term, and “He conducts the whole affair” (13:2). Creation is followed by command in 7:54 as well, where the day and night overlap in rapid succession and the sun, moon and stars are subjected by His command. To say the world was made and is being run is to deny two errors at once: that God retired from the world after making it, and that the world runs itself. Between those errors sits the doctrine this verse states — a maker who is also a governor, and a governance as continuous as the orbit it directs.

**Intercession Exists Only Where Permission Reaches**

Between the description of rule and the call to worship stands one clause about intermediaries, and its placement is argumentative. “Who could possibly intercede with Him without His permission?” (2:255). The verse here is identical in meaning, and its force lies in who is limited. Permission does not restrict God; it restricts the intercessor. No one stands in a position to negotiate with the One who conducts every affair, because nothing reaches Him from outside His own order. The point is stated without qualification: “Say, ‘All intercession belongs to Allah ˹alone˺. To Him belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. Then to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned.’” (39:44). This does not close the door on intercession granted rather than seized. The Prophet ﷺ said, “My intercession is for the people who committed major sins in my Ummah” (Sunan al-Tirmidhī 2436, from Jābir b. ʿAbdullāh) — an intercession that exists because it was permitted, not because it was claimed. And the One who grants it answers to no one: “He cannot be questioned about what He does, but they will ˹all˺ be questioned” (21:23).

**“That Is Allah—Your Lord” Moves from Cosmology to Worship**

The demonstrative *dhālikum* — that is — gathers everything just said and hands it to the listener as a conclusion. Creation, the Throne, the conducting of affairs, the control of intercession: these are not four separate facts but one portrait, and the verse names it. The identical formula appears where creation is argued from the ground up: “That is Allah—your Lord! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. ˹He is˺ the Creator of all things, so worship Him ˹alone˺” (6:102). Both statements join Lordship to divinity in one breath. Lordship (*rubūbiyyah*) is what God does: He creates, sustains, disposes. Divinity (*ulūhiyyah*) is what is owed to Him: worship. Mecca accepted the first and refused the second, which is why the verse does not argue for the existence of God but for the exclusivity of His worship. Once the affair is conducted by one hand, worship directed anywhere else is not devotion but error, and the command *fa-ʿbudūhu* — so worship Him — follows as arithmetic.

**“Will You Not Then Be Mindful?”**

The closing question is not an invitation to speculate; it expects the answer yes. The particle *fa* — then, so — ties the question to everything before it, and the form “will you not…?” in Arabic rebukes a listener who already knows. Nothing further is required of the audience except to notice. Reflection here has a floor rather than a ceiling: the least degree of attention to the sky, the alternation of light, and the fact that affairs are conducted turns a person toward the One who conducts them. The Qur'an describes this exact posture in those who “remember Allah while standing, sitting, and lying on their sides, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth” (3:191), and their reflection ends in a sentence: “Our Lord! You have not created ˹all of˺ this without purpose” (3:191). That is the mindfulness this verse asks for — not mystical absorption but the ordinary act of looking at an ordered world and refusing to treat the order as an accident.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:4

> To Him is your return all together. Allah’s promise is ˹always˺ true. Indeed, He originates the creation then resurrects it so that He may justly reward those who believe and do good. But those who disbelieve will have a boiling drink and a painful punishment for their disbelief

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Return, and Nobody Makes It Alone**

The verse opens with a destination rather than an event. *Ilayhi marjiʿukum* — to Him is your return — uses the preposition of arrival, and the word *marjiʿ* names the place one goes back to, not the going. Death in this idiom is not a dissolution but an arrival, and arrival implies a receiving party. The adverb “all together” does the sharper work. Return is individual in the sense that each soul makes it, and collective in the sense that no one makes it apart from everyone else. There is no private exit from history, no version of the ending in which one person is dealt with separately from the rest. That single word also flattens every distinction the world takes seriously: rank, tribe, wealth and reputation all arrive in the same condition. The verse therefore sets up the following clauses with a fact nobody disputes — that human beings die — and then names what the fact implies. A return to someone is a return to be reckoned with, and the rest of the verse explains the reckoning.

**Origination Then Return: The Argument from the First Making**

The resurrection is not asserted here; it is inferred, and the inference runs from what has already happened. He who begins a thing can resume it, and resumption is the lesser of the two acts. The Book makes the comparison explicit: “Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘They will be revived by the One Who produced them the first time, for He has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of every created being’” (36:79). Elsewhere the reasoning is stated as a matter of degree: “And He is the One Who originates the creation then will resurrect it—which is even easier for Him” (30:27). The sceptic's question receives the same answer — “Who will bring us back ˹to life˺?” Say, “The One Who created you the first time” (17:51). Three features of the argument deserve notice. It appeals to the listener's own body rather than to testimony. It treats origination as the harder case, so the conclusion is *a fortiori*. And it places knowledge at the hinge: the One who made each being knows each being, which is why reassembly is not a problem of memory. Nothing is lost in the first making that could be missing in the second.

**Reward “With Justice” — and More Than Justice**

The purpose clause gives the reason the return exists: *li-yajziya*, so that He may reward. Recompense is not an add-on to resurrection; resurrection exists so that recompense becomes possible, and the reward is specified as just. Two other verses state the same purpose with a different emphasis — “so that He may ˹generously˺ reward those who believe and do good, out of His grace” (30:45) and “So He may reward those who believe and do good. It is they who will have forgiveness and an honourable provision” (34:4). Set beside this verse, a distinction appears that commentators have drawn from it: the believer's reward is described in terms of grace, while the disbeliever's punishment is described in terms of justice. Nobody is treated with less than fairness. Some are treated with more. That asymmetry is not arbitrariness but mercy placed on one side of the ledger, and it explains why the verse names justice for both outcomes and reserves grace for one.

**The Boiling Drink**

The punishment is described with a concrete object, and the object is a drink. *Ḥamīm* is water heated to the point where it scalds, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly: “garments of Fire will be cut out for them and boiling water will be poured over their heads” (22:19), and “˹They will be˺ in scorching heat and boiling water” (56:42). Choosing a drink is deliberate. Drink answers thirst, the need that cannot be argued with; a drink that worsens what it was supposed to relieve is the sharpest image of a desire that is met by its own opposite. The image also answers the pleasures it replaces. Those who are content with this life and drink deeply of it are given, in the end, a drink that keeps the form of satisfaction and removes the relief. Nothing in the description is arbitrary: the punishment answers the crime in kind, which is the meaning of justice in the previous clause.

**A Promise That Is True Because It Is Promised**

Between the return and its consequences the verse inserts a short sentence — “Allah's promise is ˹always˺ true” — and grammatically it is an emphatic construction, the noun thrown forward and then confirmed by an accusative of confirmation. The effect is a statement that cannot be hedged: the promise of God is a fact in the same way a completed act is a fact. This matters because everything that follows is future. Reward, punishment, the boiling drink — none of it has happened, and all of it is stated in the indicative. The certainty does not rest on evidence from the future, which nobody has; it rests on the character of the One who promised. A promise from a being who conducts every affair (10:3) is not a forecast but a decree, and the verse treats the two as identical. Belief in the resurrection is therefore not a separate article added to belief in God. It is the same belief extended to the one thing the believer has not yet seen.

**Disbelief Named as the Cause**

The final clause gives a reason: “for their disbelief”. Punishment in this verse is not presented as a reaction but as a consequence with a stated cause, and the cause is a stance rather than a single act. *Kufr* is the covering over of something known — the refusal to acknowledge a sign one has already seen — which is why the sūrah's earlier verses spend so much effort on the signs themselves. The preposition *bi-mā* ties the outcome to what “they used to do”, a continuous past, so the cause is a settled disposition and not a moment of weakness. This is the same logic that made the reward just: each outcome is matched to what preceded it. Removing the clause would leave a punishment without an account, and the verse is careful not to leave that impression. Even the harshest image here — the boiling drink — arrives attached to a reason the punished person supplied.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:5

> He is the One Who made the sun a radiant source and the moon a reflected light, with precisely ordained phases, so that you may know the number of years and calculation ˹of time˺. Allah did not create all this except for a purpose. He makes the signs clear for people of knowledge

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Different Words for Light**

The verse uses two nouns where English has one, and the distinction is exact. The sun is *ḍiyāʾ*, a radiance that comes from the thing itself; the moon is *nūr*, a light that arrives from somewhere else. Arabic keeps these apart consistently, and the Qur'an keeps them apart here and at 71:16, where the moon is placed “as a ˹reflected˺ light, and the sun as a ˹radiant˺ lamp” (71:16). A lamp burns; a reflector receives. Modern observation confirms what the vocabulary assumes — the moon emits no visible light of its own and shines by returning the sun's — but the argument of the verse does not depend on that. It depends on the fact that two bodies in the same sky have two different roles, one producing and one transmitting, and that both are described as made. The pairing also explains the second half of the verse: only a body that changes its aspect in a fixed order can be used to count, and a reflector moving around a source changes its aspect by geometry, not by caprice. The wording is precise because the use the verse intends requires precision.

**Stations Ordained So That Time Can Be Counted**

The moon is given *manāzil*, stations — fixed stopping places on a route, the word used for the halts of a caravan. The image is of a journey with appointed halts, and the Qur'an describes the last of them vividly: “As for the moon, We have ordained ˹precise˺ phases for it, until it ends up like an old, curved palm stalk” (36:39). Arab astronomers later formalised these halts as twenty-eight lunar mansions, the *manāzil al-qamar*, marking the moon's nightly position against the fixed stars. The purpose stated here is practical rather than contemplative: “so that you may know the number of years and calculation ˹of time˺”. The same purpose appears where the question was asked directly — “They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about the phases of the moon. Say, ‘They are a means for people to determine time and pilgrimage’” (2:189). A calendar is not a minor religious convenience. Fasting, pilgrimage, ʿiddah, contracts and debts all run on a reckoned month, and a community without a shared count cannot keep an obligation. The verse treats timekeeping as part of creation's design, not as a human invention layered on top of it.

**Nothing in This Was Made in Vain**

“Allah did not create all this except for a purpose” (10:5) — *bi-l-ḥaqq*, in truth — closes off a reading of the sky as decoration. Truth here carries its ordinary Qur'anic weight: the thing is real, it answers a purpose, and it was not made for play. The same claim appears in the prayer of those who reflect on the heavens and the earth: “Our Lord! You have not created ˹all of˺ this without purpose. Glory be to You!” (3:191). The negation is the operative word. A universe made without purpose would leave the human being with nothing to learn from it, since a sign only signifies if someone intended it. The verse therefore establishes the ground of interpretation before it invites interpretation: the sky is legible because it was written. This is a claim about meaning, not about physics, and it is what makes the next clause possible — signs can be clarified only where signs exist.

**Signs Clarified for a People Who Know**

The verse ends by naming an audience: “He makes the signs clear for people of knowledge” (10:5). The clarification is not withheld from others so much as wasted on those unwilling to look. Knowledge here is the condition of reading, and the Qur'an repeatedly pairs the sky with understanding — “And He has subjected for your benefit the day and the night, the sun and the moon. And the stars have been subjected by His command. Surely in this are signs for those who understand” (16:12). The link between knowledge and observation is stated as a promise elsewhere: “We will show them Our signs in the universe and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that this ˹Quran˺ is the truth” (41:53). What distinguishes the people of knowledge is not more data but a habit: they look at a regularity and ask after the One who set it. Ignorance, in this verse's terms, is not the absence of information but the refusal to draw the inference that the information invites.

**One Mechanism, Two Readings**

The same object serves two ends at once, and the verse refuses to choose between them. The moon is a clock and a sermon. As a clock it fixes the month and the year, and the ordinary person uses it without ever asking why it wanes. As a sermon it teaches finitude by demonstration: a light that is borrowed, grows, peaks, declines and vanishes, then returns — the pattern of every life that has ever been lived. The alternation itself is made the subject of another sign-verse: “Allah alternates the day and night. Surely in this is a lesson for people of insight” (24:44). Neither reading excludes the other, and this is characteristic of the Qur'anic sign: the useful and the instructive occupy the same phenomenon. A person who uses the moon for dates and a person who reads it for meaning are reading the same page, and the verse credits both to the One who set the stations.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:6

> Surely in the alternation of the day and the night, and in all that Allah has created in the heavens and the earth, there are truly signs for those mindful ˹of Him˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Reliable Sign Is the One Nobody Notices**

The verse begins with the plainest phenomenon available to a human being: night replaces day, and day replaces night. Nobody has to travel for it, pay for it, or be taught to see it. The word used is *ikhtilāf* — alternation, mutual replacement — which carries both the difference between the two and the succession of one by the other. The Qur'an returns to this sign constantly, and its recurrence in the sign-lists is a deliberate choice of evidence. “Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth; the alternation of the day and the night; the ships that sail the sea for the benefit of humanity; the rain sent down by Allah from the skies, reviving the earth after its death…” (2:164). The same pairing opens the argument elsewhere: “Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth and the alternation of the day and night there are signs for people of reason” (3:190). A sign that repeats twice daily cannot be dismissed as a coincidence, and it cannot be missed by anyone who is awake for it. That is precisely why the verse chooses it. Evidence that can be staged, bought or doubted loses its force within a generation; evidence that arrives twice a day, unannounced and unpaid for, cannot be avoided by anyone willing to look up.

**From One Sign to the Whole Inventory**

After naming the alternation, the verse widens without limit: “and in all that Allah has created in the heavens and the earth” (10:6). The move is from a single observable regularity to the totality of created things, and it changes the character of the claim. The listener is no longer being asked to weigh one argument but to accept that the category of signs has no boundary — every object is included. The longer sign-verse spells out the range: the ships, the rain, the scattered creatures, the shifting winds, the clouds drifting between the heavens and the earth (2:164). The shorter one narrows it to weather and wind: “And ˹in˺ the alternation of the day and the night, the provision sent down from the skies by Allah —reviving the earth after its death—and the shifting of the winds, are signs for people of understanding” (45:5). The logic is cumulative rather than deductive. No single item carries the proof; the sheer number of ordered things does. A person who dismisses one sign has thousands left, and the verse's generality makes that dismissal expensive.

**Signs Addressed to a Specified Reader**

The verse does not say the signs are for everyone in the same way. They are for those mindful of Him — *al-muttaqīn*, the people of *taqwā*. The word denotes a guarded posture: someone who acts as though the outcome of his conduct matters. Elsewhere the audience is described as people of reason — *ulu l-albāb* (3:190) — and elsewhere as people of understanding or insight (45:5; 24:44). The variations are not sloppy but complementary. Reason supplies the capacity; insight supplies the habit of looking; *taqwā* supplies the motive, the sense that what one concludes will have consequences for how one lives. A person with no stake in the answer can examine the sky indefinitely and draw nothing, because nothing turns on the result. The verse therefore makes a claim about the psychology of perception: seeing a sign is not a purely optical event. It requires a person who expects to be answerable, and it is that expectation which turns an observation into evidence. This is also why the sign-verses address their audience directly as often as they describe it: the reader stands inside the class being described, and the open question is not whether signs exist but whether he reads them.

**Transience Is the Lesson the Alternation Teaches**

What the succession of night and day demonstrates most directly is that nothing lasts. Each day ends; each night passes; the light that seems permanent is on a lease. The Qur'an draws exactly this inference: “Allah alternates the day and night. Surely in this is a lesson for people of insight” (24:44). The lesson is moral before it is astronomical. A life is made of days, and a day that cannot hold itself cannot hold a life. The reverent reader arrives at finitude by observation and then at God by inference, since a thing that ends must have been given its term by something that does not. The Abbasid poet Abū'l-ʿAtāhiyah (d. 213/828) compressed the whole method into a single couplet: in each thing there is a sign, proof that He is One. That line has been quoted by exegetes for a thousand years because it states the argument's shape — multiplicity of signs, singularity of the Sign-maker.

**A Sign Leaves the Will Free**

Nothing here compels belief, and the verse is honest about it. The signs are made clear, and clarity is not coercion. The promise attached to them elsewhere is patient: “We will show them Our signs in the universe and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that this ˹Quran˺ is the truth. Is it not enough that your Lord is a Witness over all things?” (41:53). The subjection of the sun, moon and stars is stated with the same restraint: “Surely in this are signs for those who understand” (16:12). A person can look at the alternation and describe it as mechanics; nothing in the sky shouts a conclusion. The verse therefore ends where the argument has to end — with a specified audience, the mindful — because the evidence is sufficient for anyone willing to be moved by it and insufficient for anyone determined not to be. The next verses turn to people in the second condition.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:7

> Indeed, those who do not expect to meet Us, being pleased and content with this worldly life, and who are heedless of Our signs

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Meeting Is the Hinge of the Whole Description**

The verse defines a class of people by a single missing expectation: they do not anticipate meeting God. *Liqāʾ* is an encounter, a face-to-face arrival, and in Qur'anic usage it names the standing of the creature before its Maker on the Day of Resurrection. The absence of that expectation is not presented as an intellectual position but as a disposition, and the rest of the verse shows what follows from it. The absence of that expectation is not presented as an intellectual position but as a disposition, and the rest of the verse shows what follows from it. Elsewhere the same expectation is made the mark of the believer: “those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and to Him they will return” (2:46), and it is made the motive for good conduct: “So whoever hopes for the meeting with their Lord, let them do good deeds” (18:110). Those who reject the idea ask for it to be replaced by spectacle: “Those who do not expect to meet Us say, ‘If only the angels were sent down to us, or we could see our Lord!’” (25:21). An expected meeting governs behaviour in the meantime; a denied meeting leaves the present as the only arena that counts. That is the whole diagnosis in the opening phrase.

**Loving the Meeting, and What the Prophet Meant by It**

The expectation described here has a positive counterpart, and the Prophet ﷺ stated it in one sentence: “Whoever loves to meet Allah, Allah too loves to meet him; and whoever hates to meet Allah, Allah too hates to meet him” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6507, from ʿUbāda b. al-Ṣāmit). ʿĀʾisha, or one of the Prophet's wives, objected that they dislike death. His answer removes the misunderstanding: the love or hatred in question is not about dying. When a believer's death approaches, he is given the good news of God's pleasure, and nothing becomes dearer to him than what lies ahead; when a disbeliever's death approaches, he is given the news of punishment, and nothing becomes more hateful to him than what lies ahead (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6507). The distinction is exact. Disliking death is human; disliking the meeting is a verdict on a life. The verse describes people in the second condition long before any punishment is mentioned.

**Contentment Is the Symptom, and the Word Is Precise**

The people described are “pleased and content with this worldly life” (10:7), and the second verb is *yaṭmaʾinnūna*, from the root of *iṭmiʾnān* — settled calm. The same root describes the highest state of the believer's heart: “Surely in the remembrance of Allah do hearts find comfort” (13:28). One word carries both meanings, and the object decides which applies. A heart at rest in God is at peace; a heart at rest in the world is settled on something that moves. The verse is not condemning pleasure or comfort as such. It is naming a resting place that cannot hold. Meccan life offered wealth, trade, status and poetry, all of it real and none of it lasting, and the danger described is not enjoying these things but becoming tranquil in them — treating a way-station as an address. That is why the same people are later described as knowing only the outward face of this life: “They ˹only˺ know the worldly affairs of this life, but are ˹totally˺ oblivious to the Hereafter” (30:7).

**Heedlessness Comes Second, Not First**

The order of the clauses is diagnostic. Contentment is stated first, and heedlessness of the signs follows as its result. These people are not described as having examined the evidence and rejected it; they are described as not looking. *Ghāfilūn ʿan āyātinā* — heedless of Our signs — names inattention rather than refutation, and the inattention has a cause: a heart already satisfied elsewhere has no appetite for questions. The sequence is psychologically exact and repeats throughout the Qur'an. Desire settles, attention narrows, and the signs that were always visible become invisible. Nobody argues a person out of this condition with evidence, because evidence is not what they declined. What they declined was the expectation that made evidence relevant, and the verse traces the whole chain back to that point. Heedlessness is the last link, not the first. The syntax makes the sequence visible: a description of contentment, then a description of inattention, both attached to the same people. Neither is presented as a decision taken at a particular moment, which is why neither is undone by a decision alone.

**A Portrait Without an Insult**

Nothing in the verse is a slur. There is no name-calling, no appeal to ancestry, no contempt for poverty or wealth. Three descriptions are given — an absent expectation, a settled satisfaction, a diverted attention — and any reader can test himself against all three. That is the verse's power and its discomfort. It is addressed to a general human condition rather than to a particular tribe in seventh-century Arabia, and the descriptions are drawn from ordinary experience: the pleasure of a good year, the comfort of an established routine, the ease of not asking what any of it means. The Book's method here is clinical. It does not need to insult the person to describe the state accurately, and the accuracy is what makes the description useful. A diagnosis that could only apply to someone else would be worthless to the one reading it. What makes the portrait uncomfortable is its neutrality. Nothing in it requires a wicked person; it requires only an ordinary one, busy and satisfied. The Qur'an's account of loss is rarely dramatic. It describes a life in which the future was never expected, the present was never questioned, and the signs were never examined — three quiet failures that harden into a single condition.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:8

> they will have the Fire as a home because of what they have committed

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence That Waited a Whole Verse to Finish**

Grammatically this verse is a continuation, and its dependence on the one before it is not a stylistic accident. The description of the heedless was left hanging — “Indeed, those who do not expect to meet Us, being pleased and content with this worldly life, and who are heedless of Our signs,” (10:7) — and the outcome arrives here, after a pause long enough to make the listener supply it himself. Arabic does this often, and the effect is to make the conclusion feel inevitable rather than announced. Anyone who heard the first verse could guess the second, and the guess is part of the argument. The delay also has a moral function. The portrait is given in full before the sentence, so the reader is asked to recognise the state before being told the consequence. Recognition first, verdict second. A punishment presented without the portrait would look arbitrary; a portrait presented without the punishment would look sentimental. The two verses do neither.

**Maʾwā: A Shelter, Not a Sentence of Exile**

The word for what the Fire becomes is *maʾwā* — a shelter, a place of resort, the lodging one turns to when there is nowhere else. The choice is deliberate and ironic. These people made this world their refuge and were tranquil in it; the verse gives them a different lodging of the same grammatical kind. The same word recurs wherever the permanence of the outcome is stressed: “But as for those who are rebellious, the Fire will be their home. Whenever they try to escape from it, they will be forced back into it” (32:20). The detail matters — escape is attempted and fails, so the shelter is a shelter in the strict sense: there is no elsewhere. Elsewhere the word carries its sting as a rebuke: “So enter the gates of Hell, to stay there forever. Indeed, what an evil home for the arrogant!” (16:29), and “Then Hell will be their home—what an evil place to rest!” (3:197). A residence is not an event. That is the difference between this punishment and a penalty. One word does the work of a paragraph: it says where these people live, and it says they do not move.

**“Because of What They Have Committed” — the Language of Earning**

The cause is given in the vocabulary of acquisition: *bi-mā kānū yaksibūn*, on account of what they used to earn. The verb *kasaba* belongs to trade and labour — what a person acquires by his own effort and holds as his gain. Its use here does three things at once. It makes the punishment earned rather than imposed, which answers the charge of injustice before it is made. It makes it continuous rather than occasional, since the imperfect with *kānū* describes a settled practice, not a lapse. And it keeps the commercial metaphor that Meccan listeners would have felt instantly: a life is a transaction, and this is the settlement. The same finality is stated without the metaphor: “They will be in Hell forever. Their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be delayed ˹from it˺” (2:162). Nothing is added to the account and nothing is discounted. The verse's brevity is itself part of the statement: the reckoning needs no elaboration because the earnings were itemised by the earners themselves.

**The Outcome Answers the Condition**

The correspondence between the description and the outcome is exact. The verse before speaks of people who were tranquil in a life that ends; this verse gives them a residence that does not end. They were heedless of signs; the lodging they receive requires no signs to be understood. They did not expect a meeting; the outcome is an arrival with no exit. Nothing in the description is exaggerated to justify the sentence, and nothing in the sentence is imported from outside it. The same rhetorical method appears in the summary formula used of destroyed peoples: “And who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or reject the truth after it has reached them? Is Hell not a ˹fitting˺ home for the disbelievers?” (29:68). The question form there — is it not a fitting home? — states the logic openly. Fit is the operative idea. The Fire is not assigned to these people from a list; it is where the condition they chose terminates.

**Why the Sentence Is So Short**

The portrait took a long verse. The verdict takes eight words. That disproportion is the verse's own commentary on the subject. Length was given to the diagnosis because the diagnosis can be missed — a person can live decades without noticing that he expects no meeting and has grown tranquil in the world. Length is not given to the outcome because the outcome needs no explanation once the condition is recognised. The Qur'an's economy here is instructive: it spends its words on the thing the reader is likely to be blind to, and refuses to dramatise the thing he already understands. There is also a mercy in the structure. The extended description is an opportunity to notice oneself in it, and the notice is available up until the moment the sentence is executed. The short verse does not argue; it closes. A second economy is at work as well. No words are spent here on the machinery of the Fire, and no witnesses, charges or defence are named. Everything the sentence needs was already stated in the verse before, in the language of what these people chose, and the brevity respects both the reader's intelligence and the plainness of the case.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:9

> Surely those who believe and do good, their Lord will guide them ˹to Paradise˺ through their faith, rivers will flow under their feet in the Gardens of Bliss

**Expanded Commentary**

**Guidance Given as a Reward, Not Only as a Starting Point**

The verse places guidance after faith rather than before it, and the preposition is the reason: their Lord guides them *bi-īmānihim*, by means of their faith. Guidance in the Qur'an is usually the beginning of the story — the thing that makes belief possible — but here it is stated as something given to those who already believe, which means it is increase rather than origin. The same dynamic is described elsewhere in explicit terms: “He is the One Who sent down serenity upon the hearts of the believers so that they may increase even more in their faith” (48:4). Faith is treated as capable of growth, and growth is supplied by God. The negative case shows what its absence costs: “How will Allah guide a people who chose to disbelieve after they had believed, acknowledged the Messenger to be true, and received clear proofs?” (3:86). The verse therefore states a principle that runs through the whole book: guidance is not a single event at the start of a life but a continuing supply, and it is conditioned on what the recipient does with it. Those who keep faith are guided further; those who abandon it are left to their choice.

**Three Readings of “By Their Faith”, and What Decides Between Them**

Commentators have taken the phrase three ways: God increases them in guidance, God grants them a light by which they are guided on the Day of Resurrection, or God guides them to Paradise as the reward of their faith and deeds. The three are not really in competition, and the attempt to choose one flattens the verse. The third is the plainest sense in context, since the sentence ends in the Gardens; but the second is supported by the description of that Day elsewhere, where the believers' light runs before them and on their right and they ask for it to be completed: “Their light will shine ahead of them and on their right. They will say, ‘Our Lord! Perfect our light for us, and forgive us’” (66:8). The first reading explains why the other two are possible at all — a faith that grew in this life is the thing that lights the next. Read together, the phrase covers the whole movement: guidance received, guidance increased, guidance completed at the door of the Garden. The disagreement among the commentators is therefore a disagreement about emphasis, and nothing in the verse requires a choice between them.

**“Under Their Feet” — Rivers and Position**

The image is precise about position. Rivers run *min taḥtihim*, from beneath them, and the preposition has been read with the gardens and with the inhabitants. Either way the picture is of a person above the water rather than beside it, and the commentators connect it with the raised couches on which the people of the Garden sit. The formula recurs throughout the Book and is one of its most repeated promises: “Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those who believe and do good that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow” (2:25), and “˹But˺ Allah will surely admit those who believe and do good into Gardens, under which rivers flow, where they will be adorned with bracelets of gold and pearls” (22:23). Water in an Arabian landscape is not ornament but survival, and its abundance marks the difference between a dwelling and a garden. Placing it beneath the inhabitants rather than in front of them makes the abundance total: it surrounds the position they occupy.

**“The Gardens of Bliss” — a Name With a Meaning**

The destination is not merely plural gardens but *jannāt al-naʿīm*, gardens of *naʿīm* — untroubled delight, the noun for a comfort so complete it becomes the thing's name. The same title appears as a straightforward statement of where the righteous will be: “Indeed, the righteous will be in Gardens and bliss” (52:17). The naming is significant because it answers the condition described in the previous verses. Those who were tranquil in this life made a temporary comfort into a dwelling; the believers are given a comfort that has the same word attached to it and does not end. The word *jannah* itself means a garden with dense cover, from the root for covering and concealment, so the name already implies shelter and shade — the two things a desert traveller wants most. Every element of the phrase is drawn from need, and the promise is that the need will be met permanently rather than intermittently. The plural matters too. Gardens, not a garden: an estate with no single centre, so that its people are never crowded into one place and movement inside it is part of the reward rather than an escape from it.

**Faith and Deeds Named Together, Never Apart**

The verse's subject is not “those who believe” but “those who believe and do good” (10:9), and the pairing is a fixed formula in the Qur'an. Neither half is treated as sufficient on its own. Faith without works is a claim; works without faith are, in this book's terms, earnings with no one to receive them. The promise attached to the pairing is consistent across the text — admission, rivers, permanence — and the pairing is what makes the promise conditional rather than automatic. “They will have Gardens under which rivers flow… They will have pure spouses, and they will be there forever” (2:25). The verse here adds the one element the others do not: the guidance that carries them there. Belief and action describe what the person did; guidance describes what God does with it. The verse holds both in a single clause, and refuses to let either side of the transaction disappear.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:10

> in which their prayer will be, “Glory be to You, O Allah!” and their greeting will be, “Peace!” and their closing prayer will be, “All praise is for Allah—Lord of all worlds!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Prayer That Has Become Pure Praise**

The word used for what the people of the Garden say is *duʿāʾ*, which ordinarily means supplication — asking. Here it cannot mean that, and commentators note the shift: in this context the word denotes magnification and praise rather than petition. The reason is structural. Supplication presupposes a lack, and the Garden is defined by the absence of lack. Nobody there is afraid, ill, poor, threatened or dying, so there is nothing left to ask for. What remains of the structure of prayer is its address — the turning toward God — with the request removed. Speech does not stop in Paradise; it changes kind. The same movement that on earth took the form of asking takes the form of glorifying, and the verse presents this as the natural end of worship rather than a new activity. A person who prayed all his life out of need continues, without needing, to do what the prayer always pointed at. The change is in the motive, not in the direction. On earth the believer turns toward God because something is missing; in the Garden he turns because nothing is, and the turning is what he was practising for.

**Subḥānaka: The Sentence That Removes Every Defect**

“Glory be to You, O Allah!” (10:10) translates *subḥānaka Allāhumma*, the formula of *tasbīḥ*. Its grammatical force is a declaration of removal: God is far from, and above, whatever is unworthy of Him — deficiency, partner, fatigue, injustice, change of mind. It is the shortest possible statement of transcendence, and it is the speech of everything that exists: “The seven heavens, the earth, and all those in them glorify Him. There is not a single thing that does not glorify His praises—but you ˹simply˺ cannot comprehend their glorification” (17:44). The people of the Garden therefore join a praise already in progress, and they do it audibly rather than inaudibly. On earth the glorification of things is beyond human comprehension; in the Garden it is the content of human speech. That continuity is the point of the verse's first clause — worship is not interrupted by death but clarified.

**Peace as the Greeting of That Abode**

The second form of speech is *salām*, and it is a greeting rather than a prayer. The word names safety, wholeness, freedom from harm, and as a greeting it means: nothing here will hurt you. It is the greeting the believers exchange and the greeting they receive: “Those who believe and do good will be admitted into Gardens, under which rivers flow—to stay there forever by the Will of their Lord—where they will be greeted with ‘Peace!’” (14:23). The direction from above is stated even more directly: “And ‘Peace!’ will be ˹their˺ greeting from the Merciful Lord” (36:58). At the gate the keepers use it as welcome and verdict together: “Peace be upon you! You have done well, so come in, to stay forever” (39:73), and on the Day of meeting it is what God gives them: “Their greeting on the Day they meet Him will be, ‘Peace!’” (33:44). The Abode itself is named for the greeting — *Dār al-Salām*, the Abode of Peace (10:25) — and the naming explains the promise in the previous verse: a place where the greeting is peace is a place where no defence is needed. The same word is used of the greeting angels bring to the dying who are upright, and of the greeting at the gate, so the believer hears it at the two thresholds of his existence and then hears it continuously inside.

**Two Directions of the Same Word**

*Salām* moves both ways, and the verse benefits from the ambiguity. The people of the Garden greet one another, and they are greeted — by angels, by the keepers of the gates, by God. “There they will never hear any idle talk—only ˹greetings of˺ peace” (19:62), and the righteous are “received with salutations and ˹greetings of˺ peace” (25:75). The absence of idle speech is the negative half of the promise; the greeting is the positive half. Social life in this world is made of talk that carries risk — flattery, argument, gossip, negotiation — and every one of those exists because people want or fear something from each other. Remove the want and the fear and what remains is the greeting. The verse is describing a community in which nothing has to be negotiated, which is a description of peace more exact than any political one.

**Closing on Praise, as the Book Itself Opens**

The final clause names the closing of their speech: “All praise is for Allah—Lord of all worlds!” The phrase is *al-ḥamdu li-llāhi rabbi l-ʿālamīn*, which every Muslim recites as the opening of the Qur'an's first chapter (1:2). The believer's speech therefore ends where the Book begins, and the circle is deliberate. *Ḥamd* is praise given to one who deserves it and has done good, distinguished from mere admiration; attaching it to “Lord of all worlds” makes the praise universal rather than personal — thanks not only for one's own portion but for the whole arrangement. Three forms of speech are named in the verse, and they move in one direction: glorification, which places God above every deficiency; salām, which settles relations between creatures; and praise, which returns the whole matter to its Owner. The sequence reads as a liturgy, and it is the same liturgy the believer performs on earth in miniature. Nothing in the verse suggests that this speech is compulsory, imposed, or repeated under instruction. It is what people say when nothing is left to ask for and nothing is left to fear — which is the Qur'an's description of the end of worship, not its abolition.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:11

> If Allah were to hasten evil for people as they wish to hasten good, they would have certainly been doomed. But We leave those who do not expect to meet Us to wander blindly in their defiance

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Prayer That Was Almost Answered**

The verse answers a request that was actually made. The Quraysh said to the Prophet ﷺ: “O Allah! If this is indeed the truth from You, then rain down stones upon us from the sky or overcome us with a painful punishment” (8:32). That is a conditional prayer for destruction, offered in sarcasm, and the verse takes it literally. If evil were delivered to people at the speed they want good delivered, the prayer would have been granted and the pray-ers destroyed on the spot. The conditional form does the work: the verse does not say God refuses to punish, but that He does not punish on demand. Human impatience runs in one direction — good now — and the divine order does not match it. What looks like indifference is a mismatch between two speeds, and the verse states which of the two is merciful. A world in which every curse took effect immediately would have no survivors left in it by the first argument. The conditional is also a statement about human speech. People call down destruction in anger, in sarcasm and in despair, and they are met with patience rather than with fire — which is the difference between a God who reacts and a God who governs.

**Slow With Harm, Quick With Good**

The comparison inside the verse is not between two punishments but between two speeds. People hasten good; God does not hasten evil in the same way. The asymmetry is the point. Requests for benefit are answered within a lifetime, often within a day, while requests for harm — including the harm people call down on themselves and each other — are held. The same asymmetry explains why the Qur'an's warnings are always future tense and its blessings often present tense. Nothing here suggests that God is unable to act quickly. The verse is describing a policy rather than a capacity, and the policy is stated elsewhere as a settled fact about how respite works: “Those who disbelieve should not think that living longer is good for them. They are only given more time to increase in sin” (3:178). Delay is real, and it is not neutral. A person given a long life has been given a long opportunity, and an opportunity is not a reward. The harm that is held back is held back for a purpose, and the purpose is that the interval be used.

**Respite That Looks Like Generosity**

The Book has a technical name for the danger inside this delay. “So leave to Me ˹O Prophet˺ those who reject this message. We will gradually draw them to destruction in ways they cannot comprehend” (68:44), followed by the sentence that names the mechanism: “I ˹only˺ delay their end for a while, but My planning is flawless” (68:45). The mechanism is described in detail elsewhere: “When they became oblivious to warnings, We showered them with everything they desired. But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise, then they instantly fell into despair!” (6:44). The gifts keep arriving while the warnings stop feeling urgent, and the recipient reads the silence as approval. This is why the Qur'an forbids treating prosperity as a verdict. The people described here are not being punished yet, and their comfort is not evidence in their favour; it is the space in which they are deciding. That single word is the entire content of a respite.

**Left to Wander Blindly in Their Defiance**

The second half names the state they are left in: wandering blindly, in their rebellion. The verb carries the sense of being made blind or left in confusion, and the object of the wandering is their own defiance — they do not leave it, they move around inside it. The same phrase describes the hypocrites: “Allah will throw their mockery back at them, leaving them to continue wandering blindly in their defiance” (2:15). The punishment is not an external event appended to their condition; the condition is the punishment. Wandering is the state of a person who has lost the road, and the verse places the loss inside him rather than in his circumstances. A person who does not expect to meet God has no fixed point to orient toward, and movement without a fixed point is what wandering means. The verse is careful not to describe them as punished by stones or by drought. It describes them as left — and being left is the outcome they asked for when they refused the meeting.

**Respite Is Not Acquittal**

The delay has a ceiling, and the Prophet ﷺ stated both the ceiling and the severity behind it: “Allah gives respite to the oppressor, but when He takes him over, He never releases him.” He then recited: “Such is the seizure of your Lord when He seizes the towns while they are wrongdoing” — the words of 11:102 (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 4686, from Abū Mūsā). The hadith supplies exactly what this verse leaves unstated. Time given is not time forgiven. The respite granted to those who do not expect the meeting is the same respite described in that report — long, deliberate, and ending in a seizure that has no release. Between the two statements sits the whole of a human life, which is why the verse is placed where it is: after the description of contentment with this world, and before the history of peoples who were given the same delay and used it the same way. The report is cited here because it fixes the endpoint of the delay, and fixes it as certain.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:12

> Whenever someone is touched by hardship, they cry out to Us, whether lying on their side, sitting, or standing. But when We relieve their hardship, they return to their old ways as if they had never cried to Us to remove any hardship! This is how the misdeeds of the transgressors have been made appealing to them

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Postures Cover the Whole Range of Suffering**

The verse lists the positions of the body — on the side, sitting, standing — and the list is not decorative. It covers every posture a sufferer occupies: the person too weak to rise, the person who can sit but not walk, the person still moving through his day while carrying a trouble. Sickness, debt and fear do not confine themselves to prayer mats, and the verse is describing prayer that happens wherever pain finds a person. The same three postures appear elsewhere in the Qur'an, but attached to a different kind of person: “˹They are˺ those who remember Allah while standing, sitting, and lying on their sides” (3:191). The parallel is exact and the difference is everything. In one verse the postures describe a life arranged around remembrance; in this one they describe the positions in which a person remembers only when he needs to. The same body, the same words, and two opposite relationships to them. The list is a small piece of realism: it describes where prayer actually happens, without approving the reason it happens there.

**A Cry That Is Sincere and Short**

The call described here is genuine. Nothing in the verse suggests the sufferer is performing. “Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘Who rescues you from the darkest times on land and at sea? He ˹alone˺ you call upon with humility, openly and secretly’” (6:63). The Qur'an returns to this pattern constantly, and its favourite setting is the ship: “When you are touched with hardship at sea, you ˹totally˺ forget all ˹the gods˺ you ˹normally˺ invoke, except Him. But when He delivers you ˹safely˺ to shore, you turn away. Humankind is ever ungrateful” (17:67). At sea there is no intermediary to appeal to and no ritual to hide behind, and the verse treats that as a revelation of what people actually believe. Sincerity under pressure is easy to produce; the difficulty is keeping it afterwards. Under pressure a person's real theology becomes visible, and the verse treats what is visible there as accurate: the One they call on is the One they believe in. The whole complaint of this verse is about the second part, and it never doubts the first.

**“As If He Had Never Called” — the Speed of Forgetting**

The simile is where the verse cuts. The person does not merely lapse; he passes over the episode as though it had not happened, and the erasure is total. The same movement is described with its consequence attached: “When one is touched with hardship, they cry out to their Lord, turning to Him ˹alone˺. But as soon as He showers them with blessings from Him, they ˹totally˺ forget the One they had cried to earlier, and set up equals to Allah” (39:8). Forgetting here is not a failure of memory but an act of interpretation. The relieved person supplies a new account of what happened — luck, skill, the doctor, the market, his own endurance — and once the account is in place the prayer becomes an embarrassment rather than a memory. That is why the verse calls the return to old ways a return: he is not discovering a new position, he is resuming one he never abandoned in his heart. The erasure is so complete that he can retell his own rescue as a story about himself.

**The Prodigal Is Someone Who Spends Well on Something Cheap**

The verse ends by naming the people it describes: the transgressors, *al-musrifūn*, from the root for excess and waste. The word's commercial sense is precise. A *musrif* is one who spends much on something small. The exegetical tradition draws out the calculation: God gave a person senses, intellect, understanding and strength, faculties capable of reaching the highest things, and whoever exhausts them on passing bodily pleasures has spent a great deal to buy very little. That is extravagance in the strict sense, not immorality in the general one. The word choice tells us what the verse finds objectionable. It is not that these people enjoy the world; it is that they pay for it with everything they have. A person who spends his whole capacity for attention on things that end has made the worst available trade, and the verse classifies him accordingly — not as wicked but as wasteful, which is a sharper word. Extravagance of this kind is invisible to the person committing it, which is why the verse has to supply the name.

**Made to Seem Fair**

The final clause uses a passive: their misdeeds have been made appealing to them. Something has done the decorating, and the Qur'an is careful about who. The pattern is set out where the same idea appears with its stages: “When they became oblivious to warnings, We showered them with everything they desired. But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise” (6:44). God's part is the provision and the respite; the adornment is what the desire does to the object once provision stops being received as a gift. The passive keeps both truths in place — the person is not merely tricked, since he chose the direction, and he is not wholly autonomous either, since the appeal grew on him. “This is how” ties it to precedent: the same sequence has run before, in other lives and other peoples, and it produces the same person every time. The passive does not cancel responsibility. It describes how the appeal grows rather than deciding who is innocent of it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:13

> We surely destroyed ˹other˺ peoples before you when they did wrong, and their messengers came to them with clear proofs but they would not believe! This is how We reward the wicked people

**Expanded Commentary**

**History Used as Case Law**

The argument turns from the individual to the record. Rather than reasoning abstractly about what happens to those who refuse, the verse cites precedents — peoples destroyed, named, dated in the memory of the audience. This is the Qur'an's ordinary method, and it is chosen for a reason. An abstract warning can be argued with; a ruin cannot. The same appeal is made in a longer form: “Have they not seen how many ˹disbelieving˺ peoples We destroyed before them? We had made them more established in the land than you. We sent down abundant rain for them and made rivers flow at their feet. Then We destroyed them for their sins and replaced them with other peoples” (6:6). The comparison inside that verse is aimed straight at Mecca: the destroyed peoples were stronger, better watered, more secure. If strength and wealth had been protection, they would have survived. The method is therefore empirical: it points at what remains rather than at what is claimed. The verse is therefore not a threat but an observation with a named sample.

**Two Conditions, Both Required**

The verse states two facts about each destroyed people, and neither is dispensable. They did wrong, and their messengers came to them with clear proofs. Wrongdoing alone does not bring destruction, and a messenger alone does not make a people guilty. The principle is stated as a rule of divine procedure: “Your Lord would never destroy a society until He had sent to its capital a messenger, reciting Our revelations to them. Nor would We ever destroy a society unless its people persisted in wrongdoing” (28:59). The pairing answers two opposite complaints at once. The first is that punishment falls on people who never heard; the verse denies it, since the messenger and the proofs come first. The second is that a people is destroyed for a single mistake; the verse denies that too, since what is described is persistent wrong. Between the proof and the destruction stands the refusal, and the refusal is what connects the two conditions. The order of the clauses matters: proof is mentioned before refusal, so that the refusal reads as a response to evidence rather than to its absence.

**“They Would Not Believe” — Refusal, Not Inability**

The Arabic construction here is not a simple past negative. It says they were not going to believe, that belief was not in their disposition — a form that denies the possibility rather than reporting the non-occurrence. The distinction matters for how the verse reads. These people did not examine the proofs and find them insufficient; they had already settled the question before the proofs arrived. The clear proofs are mentioned precisely to remove that excuse. Evidence was supplied in a form that could be examined, and the response was not analysis. The Qur'an says the same thing about the security people feel before the end arrives: “Did the people of those societies feel secure that Our punishment would not come upon them by night while they were asleep?” (7:97). The surprise was not in the punishment but in the fact that a settled refusal could survive so long without consequences. A refusal that survives evidence is not a conclusion; it is a preference, and preferences are not corrected by adding proof.

**The Same Sentence, Repeated as a Formula**

“This is how We reward the wicked people” (10:13) is a fixed formula, and it appears verbatim in the account of the wind that destroyed a whole people: “It destroyed everything by the command of its Lord, leaving nothing visible except their ruins. This is how We reward the wicked people” (46:25). Repetition of the sentence is the point. A formula is precedent; it tells the listener that the previous case governs this one. The word translated as wicked, *mujrimūn*, names people who have committed a crime rather than people who are merely mistaken, and it is the same word used in the previous verse of those whose misdeeds were made appealing to them. The connection is deliberate. The individual described in the verse before — praying under pressure, forgetting under relief — is being placed inside a class that has a documented outcome. What began as a private habit ends as a public ruin. The formula also removes any suggestion that these were exceptional peoples punished for exceptional reasons.

**Ruins Are the Evidence, Not the Decoration**

The destroyed peoples are invoked because their remains are visible, and the Qur'an insists on that visibility: “Many are the societies We have destroyed for persisting in wrongdoing, leaving them in total ruin. ˹Many are˺ also the abandoned wells and lofty palaces!” (22:45). The wells and palaces are named because they are what a traveller actually sees — infrastructure built for permanence, standing empty. Meccan caravans passed the ruins of peoples who had thought exactly what Mecca thought. The verse also fixes a time for each case: “Those ˹are the˺ societies We destroyed when they persisted in wrong, and We had set a time for their destruction” (18:59). A set time means the destruction was neither random nor immediate, and it means the delay described in the previous verse had a limit. Precedent, proof, refusal, appointed time, ruin — the verse compresses the entire procedure into one sentence and hands it to the listener as a pattern he can recognise. The listener standing in Mecca is asked to notice which item in that list he recognises. Recognition is the whole of the argument, and it asks nothing of him except honesty about what he has already seen.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:14

> Then We made you their successors in the land to see how you would act

**Expanded Commentary**

**Succession Is a Transfer of Liability**

The verse is short, and every word in it carries weight. *Khulafāʾ* — successors, those who come after and stand in the place of others — is the same word family used where the office is described as a trust: “He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth and elevated some of you in rank over others, so He may test you with what He has given you” (6:165). To be a successor is not merely to be later. It is to inherit a position with its obligations attached — the land, the means of life in it, and the accountability of the people who held it before. The same idea carries its warning explicitly elsewhere: “He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth. So whoever disbelieves will bear ˹the burden of˺ their own disbelief” (35:39). The verse here states the transfer and nothing else. The peoples destroyed in the previous verse vacated the land; the audience of this sūrah occupies it. What the verse adds is the reason for the transfer, and the reason turns the inheritance into a question.

**“To See How You Would Act” Does Not Mean God Learns**

The phrase *li-nanzura* — that We might see — has been read by commentators as an idiom, and the reading is required by everything else the Qur'an says about divine knowledge. God does not acquire information; nothing becomes known to Him that was not known. What the seeing denotes is the manifestation of the thing in existence — that what was known becomes actual, enacted, and therefore judgeable on the evidence of the person's own conduct. The same idiom appears in the verse that states the purpose of life itself: “˹He is the One˺ Who created death and life in order to test which of you is best in deeds” (67:2). A test does not inform the examiner; it produces a record for the one examined. The particle *kayfa* — how — reinforces the point. What is observed is not whether the person acts, since he must, but the manner of his acting. Two people inherit the same land and are watched in the same way; what differs is the how, and that is the whole content of the examination. The verse thus avoids both extremes: it does not make God ignorant of what will happen, and it does not make the trial a formality.

**The Question the Angels Asked, Now Being Answered**

The word for successor is the word used in the first announcement of humanity's appointment: “˹Remember˺ when your Lord said to the angels, ‘I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth.’ They asked ˹Allah˺, ‘Will You place in it someone who will spread corruption there and shed blood?’” (2:30). The angels' objection was answered at the time with a sentence rather than a demonstration: “I know what you do not know.” This verse supplies the demonstration. The appointment is followed by history, and history is where the question is settled — not by assertion but by what people actually do with the land they inherit. Read against 2:30, the destruction of the previous peoples and the succession of the next one is the same experiment running again, with a new group given the same position and the same freedom. The verse is therefore a statement about the structure of the world: the earth is handed from one people to the next, and each transfer is a trial.

**A Sentence That Turns History Into an Address**

The subject of the verse is not “them” but “you”. The destroyed peoples were the object of the previous sentence; the audience of the sūrah is the subject of this one. That shift of pronoun is the verse's rhetorical force. History is not being narrated for interest. The listener is being told that he is inside the pattern, standing on ground vacated by people who were watched in the same way. The Qur'an makes the same move when it explains why fortunes alternate: “We alternate these days ˹of victory and defeat˺ among people so that Allah may reveal the ˹true˺ believers” (3:140). Nothing about the alternation is random, and nothing about the present occupant's position is permanent.
