The verse is fifteen words long and it removes every ground of complacency a successful people might stand on — strength, wealth, ancestry and duration are all shown to be the same position held by someone else before. Nothing in the sentence is addressed to the past. It is spoken to people holding the ground now.

**What the Successors Are Being Tested On**

The verse does not specify the deeds, and the omission is deliberate: the test covers all of them. Elsewhere the scope is stated with the instrument attached: “And so We have made you ˹believers˺ an upright community so that you may be witnesses over humanity and that the Messenger may be a witness over you” (2:143). The witnesses exist because the conduct is recorded, and the recording is what makes the how observable. Succession also means that the successor is judged on what he does with what he was given rather than on what he was given. The previous peoples had rain, rivers and palaces; their successors are not graded on the inheritance but on the use. That single distinction is what separates this verse from a fatalistic reading of history. Nothing in the transfer was earned by the new occupants, and everything about their tenure will be assessed. That is the sentence this verse leaves behind: the ground is borrowed, and the borrowing has terms.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:15

> When Our clear revelations are recited to them, those who do not expect to meet Us say ˹to the Prophet˺, “Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it.” Say ˹to them˺, “It is not for me to change it on my own. I only follow what is revealed to me. I fear, if I were to disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Negotiation Opened With a Book**

The demand has two options, and both are revealing. Bring a different Qur'an, or change this one. The first treats revelation as a product that can be replaced with a better model; the second treats it as a draft open to amendment. Neither option questions the existence of revelation, which is worth noting, because the demanders are not atheists arguing against God. They are customers refusing terms. The request is addressed to the Prophet directly, as though the text were his and its contents negotiable in the way a contract is negotiable. The verse reports the request without ridicule and then reports the answer, which does not argue with the demand but denies the premise underneath it. Nothing in the reply disputes their right to dislike the recitation. What it disputes is their assumption about who holds the pen.

**What They Wanted Changed**

The demand has a specific content, and the surrounding verses make it plain: what offended the Quraysh was the Qur'an's treatment of their gods. A recitation that condemned al-Lāt, al-ʿUzzā and Manāt, that mocked the intercession attributed to them, and that placed every human being alone before a single Lord was intolerable to a city whose standing, trade and pilgrimage economy were bound up with its sanctuary. The request was therefore not for stylistic improvement but for the removal of an offence. This is why the verse connects the demanders to those “who do not expect to meet Us” (10:15) — the same people described at the beginning of the chapter. Their objection is not intellectual. A man who expects no meeting can accept almost any theology that leaves his position intact, and the Qur'an's insistence on the meeting is precisely what he cannot have edited out.

**“It Is Not for Me to Change It on My Own”**

The refusal is stated as a limit on the speaker rather than on the demand. *Mā yakūnu lī an ubaddilahu min tilqāʾi nafsī* — it is not mine to alter it of my own accord. The answer addresses the smaller request and by that same stroke the larger one, since if the messenger cannot amend a verse he plainly cannot produce a replacement book. The same limit is declared in the Prophet's own instructed words: “Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘I do not say to you that I possess Allah's treasuries or know the unseen, nor do I claim to be an angel. I only follow what is revealed to me’” (6:50). The restriction is reinforced by a statement about the source of his speech: “Nor does he speak of his own whims” (53:3). What makes the refusal powerful is that it costs him something. He is not declining an offer he finds distasteful; he is admitting, to a hostile audience, that he has no authority over the very thing they are attacking him for.

**The Refusal Is Itself Evidence**

The inability to edit is an argument, and the exegetical tradition drew it out: a man who authored a book can revise it, and a man who cannot revise it did not author it. The Qur'an makes the point from the other direction when it describes the change that does occur — and attributes it to God: “When We replace a verse with another—and Allah knows best what He reveals—they say, ‘You ˹Muḥammad˺ are just a fabricator’” (16:101). Replacement happens, and it is described as divine action with divine knowledge behind it: “If We ever abrogate a verse or cause it to be forgotten, We replace it with a better or similar one” (2:106). The distinction is between revelation revised by its Sender and revelation edited by its carrier. The Prophet's refusal in this verse places him firmly on one side of that line, and his opponents' own demand shows that they had assumed the other.

**“I Only Follow What Is Revealed to Me”**

The positive half of the answer is one clause, and it defines the office. The messenger is a conveyor, not an author, and the same sentence appears in his instructed reply on another occasion: “Say, ‘I am not the first messenger ever sent, nor do I know what will happen to me or you. I only follow what is revealed to me. And I am only sent with a clear warning’” (46:9). The limits stated there are the same: no knowledge of the unseen, no precedence, no ownership. This is not modesty as a rhetorical posture. It is a precise account of the job, and it explains the shape of the whole sūrah, in which the Prophet is repeatedly told what to say rather than left to compose a reply. A messenger who could adjust the message would be an author, and an author can be argued with, flattered, or bribed. A conveyor can only be heard or refused.

**The Fear Stated as the Reason**

The answer closes on a motive: “I fear, if I were to disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day” (10:15). The messenger places himself under the law he announces. He is not above the Day, exempt from its reckoning, or protected by his rank; he stands in the same queue as his audience, and his refusal to alter the text is grounded in his own exposure. That is the strongest possible answer to the charge of self-interest. A man editing a book for advantage would not describe the consequence of editing it as a punishment he personally fears. The clause also reverses the pressure the demanders applied. They asked him to weigh their offence against his interests; he answers by naming a weight that exceeds both. The tremendous Day is the same meeting that the demanders do not expect, and the verse ends by showing that the man they are negotiating with expects it fully.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:16

> Say, “Had Allah willed, I would not have recited it to you, nor would He have made it known to you. I had lived my whole life among you before this ˹revelation˺. Do you not understand?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Argument Built From the Audience's Own Memory**

The reply takes a different form from the one before it. Instead of a statement about authority, it is an appeal to shared history — and the history belongs to the people being addressed, not to the speaker. They lived with him. They knew his family, his trade, his temper, his habits, the forty years before the first revelation. The verse asks them to consult that knowledge, which is the one body of evidence no outsider can dispute and no argument can manufacture. This is a distinctive feature of the Qur'anic defence of its messenger: it does not offer a biography to be believed but a memory to be checked. A stranger's character is a claim; a neighbour's character is a fact. The demanders in the previous verse treated the recitation as a text they could negotiate; this verse moves the ground to something they cannot negotiate, which is what they already know about the man reciting it.

**“Had Allah Willed” — the Speaker Removed From the Sentence**

The first clause makes the recitation depend entirely on God's will, twice over: had God willed, I would not have recited it, and He would not have made it known to you. Both halves of the transaction are placed outside the messenger — the delivery and the disclosure. Nothing is left for him to own. The same position is stated in his instructed speech: “I only follow what is revealed to me” (6:50), and in the description of the revelation's origin: “And so We have sent to you ˹O Prophet˺ a revelation by Our command. You did not know of ˹this˺ Book and faith ˹before˺” (42:52). The verse adds a detail that closes the door on the idea of preparation: he did not know the Book before it came. There was no schooling, no teacher, no period of study that could account for the recitation. The will of God is not invoked here as a shield against questioning; it is invoked as the only remaining explanation once the alternatives have been excluded.

**Forty Years in Mecca, Verified by His Enemies**

The claim “I had lived my whole life among you before this” (10:16) is verifiable by the people he is addressing, and the record confirms it. He was born in Mecca, raised in the house of his uncle Abū Ṭālib, known in trade, and called *al-Amīn* — the trustworthy — before he was ever called a prophet. The strongest attestation comes from an opponent. Abū Sufyān b. Ḥarb, then Mecca's leading adversary, was questioned about him by Heraclius, who asked: “Have you ever accused him of telling lies before his claim?” Abū Sufyān answered, “No” — and later admitted that only fear of being exposed by his own companions made him tell the truth (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 7). Heraclius drew the inference himself: a man who does not lie about people will not lie about God. An enemy's unwilling testimony is stronger evidence than a follower's praise, and the verse's appeal to shared life rests on exactly that kind of evidence.

**The Same Point Put to a Christian King**

The argument travelled. When the emigrants reached Abyssinia and were asked about this new religion, Jaʿfar b. Abī Ṭālib opened his account of the messenger not with doctrine but with character: God sent to them a messenger whose truthfulness, lineage and trustworthiness they had all known. The account survives in Musnad Aḥmad 1740, in Umm Salama's narration of the migration, and its content is exactly the argument of this verse. The point of beginning with character is strategic. Before a foreign audience with no knowledge of Arabian prophecy, the credibility of the messenger is the first question, and it is answered by the testimony of the people who grew up with him. Doctrine can be debated indefinitely; a reputation established over forty years among hostile witnesses is a different kind of fact. The verse's closing question presumes that this fact is already in the room.

**“Do You Not Understand?”**

The question at the end is not a rebuke so much as a request for a single inference. Given that you know I was not a liar, and given that this recitation is not the sort of thing a man invents about God, what conclusion is left? The form of the question implies that the answer is available and has been declined. The Qur'an's method here is worth naming: it does not ask the audience to accept a mystery but to complete a deduction they are equipped to make. The two premises are theirs. The conclusion is uncomfortable, which is why the question is asked rather than stated. A stated conclusion can be rejected with a counter-claim; a question hands the audience the work and leaves them with the result whether they speak it or not.

**What the Argument Does Not Claim**

Nothing in this verse argues that a good man must be a prophet. Character alone proves nothing about revelation, and the verse does not suggest otherwise. What the forty years do is remove one hypothesis — that the recitation is a deliberate fabrication by a known liar — and once that hypothesis is gone the recitation itself has to be accounted for on its own terms. The verse therefore does the negative work and leaves the positive proof where the Qur'an always leaves it: in the speech. It is a defence of the messenger's honesty, not a demonstration of his message, and it is offered to people who had every opportunity to check it and who checked it in private while denying it in public.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:17

> Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His revelations? Indeed, the wicked will never succeed

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question With Only One Possible Answer**

The verse opens in a form that Arabic uses when no comparison is intended: *man aẓlamu mimman* — who is more wrongful than the one who… The construction does not invite a search for a worse offender; it announces that none exists. The same formula recurs wherever the Qur'an names the gravest offence, and the offences named are always of this kind: “Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His signs? Indeed, the wrongdoers will never succeed” (6:21); “Who does more wrong than the one who fabricates lies about Allah when invited to submit ˹to Him˺?” (61:7); “Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah? They will be brought before their Lord, and the witnesses will say, ‘These are the ones who lied against their Lord’” (11:18). The repetition is not stylistic. It marks a settled ranking of wrongs, in which offences against revelation stand above offences against persons, because they corrupt the ground on which every other judgement depends.

**Two Offences That Cover the Whole Range**

The verse names two crimes, and together they exhaust what a person can do with a revelation. He can invent one, or he can reject one. Fabrication is the addition of a lie to God's speech; denial is the subtraction of truth from it. Nothing else remains — everything a person does with a divine message is either an insertion or a refusal. The pairing is deliberate and it is directed at both parties in the argument the sūrah has been recording. The demanders of the previous verses asked for a different book or an edited one; the first request is a request for fabrication, and refusing the message as it stands is the second crime. Neither side of the dispute escapes the sentence, and the verse does not need to identify individuals because the categories are exhaustive. Any reader can locate himself in one of the two, which is the effect the form is designed to produce.

**Why a Lie About God Is the Worst of Wrongs**

The ranking has a reason. A lie about a person damages that person's reputation; a lie about God damages the standard by which reputations, laws and duties are judged. It borrows the highest authority in existence for a private purpose, and it makes every listener an accomplice or a victim. The Qur'an describes the fabricator's end in a scene rather than an abstraction: “Who does more wrong than the one who fabricates lies against Allah or claims, ‘I have received revelations!’—although nothing was revealed to them—or the one who says, ‘I can reveal the like of Allah's revelations!’?” (6:93), and the verse continues with the angels stretching out their hands at the moment of death, saying: “Give up your souls! Today you will be rewarded with the torment of disgrace for telling lies about Allah.” The disgrace is specified because the offence was a claim to honour. A person who spoke in God's name without authorisation is met with exposure in the presence of witnesses.

**The Wrong Returns on the One Who Does It**

The exegetical reading of this verse runs in both directions, and both are intended. If the Prophet had fabricated a lie against God — which is the charge implied in the demand that he produce a different book — he would have committed the greatest wrong, and the greatest wrong would have landed on himself. And if he is truthful, then those who deny the revelations are the ones described, and their denial is likewise a wrong against themselves. The verse therefore settles the previous exchange without adjudicating it. It states a rule and leaves the parties to apply it. That is a more effective answer than a defence would have been, because it removes the Prophet from the argument entirely: whatever is true, the rule holds, and the person who breaks it is the loser. The audience is left holding the question rather than the accusation.

**Success Means Reaching What You Were Aiming At**

*Falāḥ* is not vague wellbeing. The word denotes arriving at the good one set out for — deliverance, attainment, the opposite of loss. To say the wicked will never succeed is therefore a precise statement: they will not reach the objective, whatever it was. The fabricator sought authority and will not obtain it; the denier sought to keep his position and will lose it. The Qur'an states the outcome in a scene: “On the Day of Judgment you will see those who lied about Allah with their faces gloomy. Is Hell not a ˹fitting˺ home for the arrogant?” (39:60). The gloom is the visible form of a plan that did not arrive. Negation of *falāḥ* is also permanent in the wording — the particle used makes the failure absolute rather than temporary, which answers the obvious objection that plenty of fabricators prosper for a while. Prosperity is not attainment, and the verse is about attainment.

**Denial Is a Choice, Not a Condition**

The second crime deserves separate attention because it is the more common one. Denying God's revelations does not require inventing anything; it requires only a refusal, and a refusal feels costless at the time. The verse places it beside fabrication precisely to remove that impression. Both are described as wrong of the same magnitude, and both are described as acts rather than states — things a person does, not things that happen to him. The same description is given of those who deny and then confess too late: “They will cry, ‘They have failed us,’ and they will confess against themselves that they were indeed disbelievers” (7:37). The confession comes after the possibility of acting on it has ended. What the verse asserts is that the denial was available to be reversed at every point up to that moment, and that the failure to reverse it is the wrong being named.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:18

> They worship besides Allah others who can neither harm nor benefit them, and say, “These are our intercessors with Allah.” Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Are you informing Allah of something He does not know in the heavens or the earth? Glorified and Exalted is He above what they associate ˹with Him˺!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Idols Measured by What They Can Do**

The verse begins with a test rather than an insult. Whatever is worshipped is measured by two capacities: harm and benefit. The idols fail both. The same test is applied to them repeatedly, and always in the same terms: “Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, ‘Should we invoke, other than Allah, those ˹idols˺ which cannot benefit or harm us?’” (6:71); “Do you then worship—instead of Allah—what can neither benefit nor harm you in any way?” (21:66); “They call besides Allah what can neither harm nor benefit them. That is ˹truly˺ the farthest one can stray” (22:12). The argument is empirical rather than theological. Nobody is asked to accept a metaphysical claim; everyone is asked to notice what the object in front of him has never done. Ibrāhīm put the same question in the sharpest form: “How can you worship what you carve ˹with your own hands˺?” (37:95). A thing that cannot act cannot be appealed to, and the verse treats this as self-evident to anyone willing to look.

**The Real Dispute Was About Intermediaries, Not About God**

The second clause reveals what the argument was actually about. The Meccans did not deny God's existence or His creation; they assigned Him intermediaries. “These are our intercessors with Allah” (10:18) is a claim about access, not about divinity, and it explains why the Qur'an's polemics are so insistent on this point. The gods named in the sanctuary — “Now, have you considered ˹the idols of˺ Lât and ’Uzza, and the third one, Manât, as well?” (53:19–20) — were understood as means of approach, patrons who could put a case. That is also why the idols are described as unable to harm: the fear they inspired was not of their anger but of their displeasure being provoked by neglect. The verse dismantles the arrangement at its hinge. If the object cannot act, it cannot plead; and if it cannot plead, the worship directed at it buys nothing.

**A Question That Cannot Be Answered**

The reply takes the form of a question that has no possible answer: are you informing God of something in the heavens or the earth that He does not know? The logic is compressed and complete. Intercession of the kind described requires that the intercessor know something the judge does not — a merit, a relationship, a mitigating fact. God's knowledge of the heavens and the earth is total: “He ˹fully˺ knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them” (2:255). There is therefore nothing for an intermediary to tell Him, and a plea with no content is not a plea. The rhetorical question is not a put-down but a demonstration. It asks the audience to state what the idol would say on their behalf, and the impossibility of answering is the refutation. Nothing further needs to be argued once that silence is heard.

**Intercession Granted Is Not Intercession Claimed**

The verse does not deny that intercession exists. It denies that it can be arranged from below. Ownership is stated in a single sentence: “Say, ‘All intercession belongs to Allah ˹alone˺. To Him belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth’” (39:44), and the condition is stated with it: “Who could possibly intercede with Him without His permission?” (2:255). Even the angels operate inside that limit: “They do not intercede except for whom He approves, and they tremble in awe of Him” (21:28). Where intercession occurs, it occurs as a grant. The Prophet ﷺ said, “My intercession is for the people who committed major sins in my Ummah” (Sunan al-Tirmidhī 2436, from Jābir b. ʿAbdullāh) — an intercession conferred, not claimed, and conferred on a person whose standing with God is incomparably different from a carved stone's. The distinction the verse draws is therefore not between intercession and no intercession but between a favour received and a favour assumed.

**Transcendence Stated as the Final Word**

The verse closes with *tasbīḥ*: glorified and exalted is He above what they associate. The formula appears wherever the Qur'an has just described something unworthy of God, and it functions as a correction rather than a comment: “The command of Allah is at hand, so do not hasten it. Glorified and Exalted is He above what they associate ˹with Him in worship˺!” (16:1); “Can any of your associategods do any of this? Glorified and Exalted is He above what they associate with Him ˹in worship˺!” (30:40). The pairing in the second example is instructive — creation, provision, death and resurrection are listed, then the question is asked, then the glorification follows. Transcendence here is not a mystical abstraction but the conclusion of an argument: a Lord who does all of this is above needing assistants. The formula also protects the argument from its own force. Having dismantled the idols, the verse immediately refuses to leave the listener with a diminished picture of God.

**Why Harm and Benefit Come First**

The order of the two capacities is worth holding. Benefit is mentioned second, harm first, and the arrangement answers the way people actually relate to their objects of devotion. Fear comes before hope in practice: a person neglects what he does not fear and then courts what he hopes from. By naming harm first, the verse meets the idolater at his strongest motive and shows that it is baseless — the stone cannot punish neglect. Once fear is removed, the claim to benefit collapses on its own, since nothing that cannot prevent harm can be relied on to deliver good. The two clauses together reduce the whole arrangement to zero. What remains of Meccan religion after that calculation is a set of customs with no function, and the verse's closing glorification supplies what those customs had displaced.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:19

> Humanity was once nothing but a single community ˹of believers˺, but then they differed. Had it not been for a prior decree from your Lord, their differences would have been settled ˹at once˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Community Before There Were Parties**

The verse makes a claim about origins: humanity began as a single community. The fuller account specifies what kind of community it was and how the unity ended: “Humanity had once been one community ˹of believers before they lost faith˺. Then Allah raised prophets as deliverers of good news and as warners, and revealed to them the Scriptures in truth to judge among people regarding their disputes” (2:213). The unity was therefore a unity of belief, not of culture or language, and it did not survive contact with revelation. This is a striking feature of the Qur'anic account and one that commentators have discussed: division arrives with the books, not before them. Prophets are sent to settle disputes, and the settlement itself becomes the occasion of new division — because a ruling must be accepted, and acceptance is where human beings differ. The verse states the fact in a few words and leaves the history to the listener's own knowledge. Its interest is not in when the unity ended but in what the ending implies for the disagreements running now.

**Difference Is Built Into the Arrangement**

The verse does not present the splitting as a failure of the plan. Elsewhere the same fact is stated as something that will continue: “Had your Lord so willed, He would have certainly made humanity one single community ˹of believers˺, but they will always ˹choose to˺ differ” (11:118), and “Had Allah willed, He could have easily made you one community ˹of believers˺, but He leaves to stray whoever He wills and guides whoever He wills” (16:93). The conditional form — had He willed — is the key. Uniformity was possible; it was not chosen. A world of compelled agreement would contain no belief, only compliance, and compliance is not what the test measures. “If Allah had willed, succeeding generations would not have fought ˹among themselves˺ after receiving the clear proofs. But they differed—some believed while others disbelieved” (2:253). Difference is the visible cost of a freedom that has to exist for the test to be real. The cost is not trivial, and the verse does not pretend otherwise. Communities split, books are read against one another, and people are harmed by the divisions. What is asserted is that the alternative was not harmony but compulsion.

**The Difference Was Over the Books**

What people divided about is named precisely, and the naming is unflattering to all parties. “And no one disputed the Scriptures except the very people who received them after clear proofs had come to them—out of jealousy” (2:213). The disputes were not between the ignorant and the informed but inside the communities that held the revelation. The same diagnosis appears twice more: “They did not split ˹into sects˺ out of mutual envy until knowledge came to them” (42:14), and “But it was not until knowledge came to them that they differed out of mutual envy” (45:17). In each case the sequence is the same — knowledge arrives, then division follows, and the motive given is rivalry rather than confusion. That is a serious claim about religious history, and it locates the problem in the recipients rather than in the texts. The books were clear; the reading of them was contested, and the contest had a motive. Naming that motive is the verse's sharpest contribution. A difference caused by confusion can be cured by explanation; a difference caused by rivalry cannot, because explanation is not what the parties are after.

**The Prior Decree: Judgment Postponed, Not Cancelled**

The second sentence explains why the disagreement has not already been resolved. A word had gone before from the Lord — a decree already issued — and without it the matter would have been settled on the spot. The decree is the appointed term: judgment is fixed for a time, and until that time the dispute runs. The same conditional appears with the term named explicitly: “Had it not been for a prior decree from your Lord for an appointed term, the matter would have certainly been settled between them ˹at once˺” (42:14). And the settlement is promised with its content: “To Allah you will all return, then He will inform you ˹of the truth˺ regarding your differences” (5:48). The postponement is therefore not indifference. Every contested question in this verse's scope has a scheduled hearing, and the verse tells the listener both that the hearing exists and that it has not happened yet.

**What the Delay Protects**

An immediate settlement would end the trial. If every dispute about revelation were resolved the moment it arose, belief would become a matter of information rather than of character, and the difference between a believer and a denier would disappear — both would simply know. The delay preserves the space in which a person chooses on incomplete evidence, which is the only condition under which choice means anything. It also protects the parties from each other: a community whose differences were settled by immediate divine intervention would not need patience, forbearance, argument or self-restraint, and the Qur'an treats all of these as part of what is being tested. The verse therefore ends on a note that is neither threatening nor comforting. It states that the argument will be decided, that the decision is already scheduled, and that until then the difference stands. Every party to a religious dispute is living inside that interval. The decree is described as prior — issued before the disputes arose — so the postponement was never a reaction to the argument but part of its design.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:20

> They ask, “Why has no ˹other˺ sign been sent down to him from his Lord?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “˹The knowledge of˺ the unseen is with Allah alone. So wait! I too am waiting with you.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Demand for a Sign While the Sign Is Being Recited**

The question is asked of a man in the middle of reciting a book they cannot match. The demand is not for evidence in general but for a particular kind of evidence: something visible, spectacular, and other than the Qur'an. The same question is recorded in the same words elsewhere — “They ask, ‘Why has no ˹other˺ sign been sent down to him from his Lord?’” (6:37) — and the answer there begins by conceding God's power: “Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘Allah certainly has the power to send down a sign’—though most of them do not know” (6:37). The requesters are not confused about whether God can act. They are setting terms. The verse answers them by refusing to negotiate the terms at all, and the refusal is grounded in the messenger's position rather than in God's ability. Nothing here disputes the sincerity of a genuine seeker; the surrounding verses make clear that this is a demand from people who had already decided, and the reply is calibrated to that fact.

**The Signs They Had in Mind**

The Qur'an records what the requests looked like when spelled out, and they were material: “They challenge ˹the Prophet˺, ‘We will never believe in you until you cause a spring to gush forth from the earth for us’” (17:90), followed by demands for a garden, a house of gold, and a piece of the sky dropped on them. Similar requests appear at 13:7 — “If only a sign could be sent down to him from his Lord” — and 13:27. The verse explains why granting them would not have produced faith: “If We willed, We could send down upon them a ˹compelling˺ sign from the heavens, leaving their necks bent in ˹utter˺ submission to it” (26:4). A sign of that force would leave the necks bent — submission produced by overwhelm rather than by conviction. That is precisely what the arrangement of the test excludes. The demanders wanted a miracle that would settle the matter; the verse shows that such a miracle would settle it by destroying the conditions under which belief counts as belief.

**The Unseen Belongs to God Alone**

The answer given to the Prophet is a statement about knowledge, not about power. The unseen — *al-ghayb*, everything outside created perception, including the timing and the choice of signs — is God's alone. The same restriction is stated where the whole affair is being closed: “To Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the knowledge of what is hidden in the heavens and the earth. And to Him all matters are returned” (11:123), and where the Hour is asked about: “They ask you as if you had full knowledge of it. Say, ‘That knowledge is only with Allah, but most people do not know’” (7:187). The messenger has no key to the treasury of signs, and he cannot schedule a demonstration on request. This is the same limit declared in the verses before it — he cannot alter the recitation, and he cannot produce signs. Both limits follow from one fact: he is a messenger, and a messenger's competence extends only to what he has been given. The answer is therefore no deflection. It states where the decision about signs actually sits, and it leaves the demanders addressing the only party able to grant one.

**“So Wait!” — a Challenge, Not a Curse**

The imperative is short and its force is easy to miss. *Fantaẓirū* — so wait — is not a threat of imminent destruction and not a dismissal. It is an invitation to stay in the argument. The Prophet is instructed to place himself inside the waiting alongside them: I am waiting with you. The exegetical tradition reads the sentence as a challenge of a particular kind: wait for the sign you asked for, and I will wait with you for the judgment between us, so that it becomes clear which of us is following the truth. That reading is confirmed by the wording, which makes the waiting shared rather than imposed. Nobody is being told to leave; everybody is being told to stay and see. The verse therefore answers a demand for instant proof with an offer of time — the one thing the demanders were not asking for, and the only condition in which the answer can be discovered rather than forced.

**The Sign Is Already in the Room**

The demand for another sign treats the present one as insufficient, and the reply does not argue the point directly; it points instead at the arrangement of knowledge. Yet the surrounding verses leave no doubt about where the Qur'an places the standing evidence: “We will show them Our signs in the universe and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that this ˹Quran˺ is the truth. Is it not enough that your Lord is a Witness over all things?” (41:53). The question at the end of that verse is the answer to the question at the start of this one. A recitation that its opponents cannot imitate, delivered by a man whose forty years among them they had watched, is a sign of a kind that does not expire when the spectacle ends. The requesters wanted something they could see once; they were being given something they could examine for the rest of their lives. That is the difference between a miracle and a book, and the waiting this verse commands is the time in which the difference can be tested.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:21

> When We give people a taste of mercy after being afflicted with a hardship, they swiftly devise plots against Our revelations! Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah is swifter in devising ˹punishment˺. Surely Our messenger-angels record whatever you devise.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn Happens at Speed**

The word doing the work here is “swiftly”. The verse does not describe a slow slide back into unbelief; it describes an immediate reversal, and the speed is the offence. The same observation is made in four other places, and each one notes the haste. “But if We give them a taste of prosperity after being touched with adversity, they say, ‘My ills are gone,’ and become totally prideful and boastful” (11:10). “If We give people a taste of mercy, they become prideful ˹because˺ of it. But if they are afflicted with an evil for what their hands have done, they instantly fall into despair” (30:36). “And if We let someone taste a mercy from Us, they become prideful ˹because˺ of it. But when afflicted with evil because of what their hands have done, then one becomes totally ungrateful” (42:48). The pattern is symmetrical and unflattering: hardship produces prayer, relief produces arrogance, and neither produces reflection. The verse is naming a reflex rather than a decision, which is why it can be stated as a general rule about people rather than a report about a few.

**The Drought, the Prayer, and the Rain**

Commentators connect this verse to a specific event: a severe drought that struck Mecca, after which the Quraysh asked the Prophet ﷺ to pray for rain, and rain came in abundance. The request is recorded in the exegetical tradition as an approach made to him during the hardship, and the mercy in this verse is the rain that followed. The same mechanism is documented in a verified report from Medina. Anas b. Mālik narrated that a man entered the mosque on a Friday while the Prophet ﷺ was delivering the khutba and said: “O Allah's Messenger! The livestock are dying and the roads are cut off; so please pray to Allah for rain.” The Prophet raised his hands and said, “O Allah! Bless us with rain!” three times. A cloud appeared from behind Mount Silaʿ, spread, and rained; they did not see the sun for a week. The following Friday a man came through the same gate asking him to pray for it to stop (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 1013). The report is Medinan while the reported occasion of this verse is Makkan, and the two should not be conflated — but the shape is identical, down to the second request.

**What Plotting Against the Signs Means**

*Yamkūrūna* — they plot, scheme, contrive. Applied to the signs, the word covers more than conspiracy. It includes the counter-argument invented on the spot, the mocking interpretation, the story circulated to explain the rain without reference to the One who sent it, the private agreement to treat the whole episode as settled in one's own favour. The verse is precise about the object: not against God, who cannot be harmed, but against His signs — the evidence. That is what a person can attack, and attacking evidence is easier than answering it. The same word is used of the schemes of earlier opponents: “And ˹remember, O Prophet,˺ when the disbelievers conspired to capture, kill, or exile you. They planned, but Allah also planned. And Allah is the best of planners” (8:30). Human plotting is real and it is dangerous, and the verse never suggests otherwise. What it says is that it is not the last word.

**God Is Swifter in Devising**

The answer is given in the same vocabulary, which makes it a direct reply rather than a change of subject. They devise; God devises, and His devising is faster. The comparison is stated twice more in the same terms: “And the disbelievers made a plan ˹against Jesus˺, but Allah also planned —and Allah is the best of planners” (3:54), and “Those ˹disbelievers˺ before them ˹secretly˺ planned, but Allah has the ultimate plan. He knows what every soul commits” (13:42). God's devising is understood as His disposal of the outcome — the way events are arranged so that a scheme arrives at a result its author did not intend. It is not a counter-plot in the human sense, since it needs no concealment and no timing. The warning attached to it is about false security: “Did they feel secure against Allah's planning? None would feel secure from Allah's planning except the losers” (7:99). The person who has just been given rain and has begun to explain it away is, in the verse's terms, in exactly that condition.

**The Plotters Are Being Written Down**

The final clause removes the privacy on which plotting depends. The messengers — the angels assigned to record — write down whatever is devised. The same fact is stated in an oath: “while you are certainly observed by vigilant, noble scribes, who know whatever you do” (82:10–12), and “by the hands of angel-scribes, honourable, upright” (80:15–16). A scheme that is recorded is no longer a scheme; it is evidence. The verse therefore answers the plotter on three levels at once. Speed: God's disposal is faster. Secrecy: the recording removes it. Efficacy: the outcome belongs to the One who conducts every affair. Nothing is left of the plot except the entry in the book, and the entry is what the person will be shown.

**A Rule About People, Not Only About Mecca**

The verse is stated generally — “people”, not a named group — and its generality is what makes it useful. Every reader has been given relief after a hardship: an illness that lifted, a debt that cleared, a fear that did not arrive. The reflex the verse describes is available to all of them. What separates the grateful from the plotter is not the relief but what is done in the first hour after it. The verse places the test at precisely that moment, which is when a person is least likely to think he is being tested. That is also why the recording is mentioned: the moment feels private and unobserved, and the verse says it is neither.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:22

> He is the One Who enables you to travel through land and sea. And it so happens that you are on ships, sailing with a favourable wind, to the passengers’ delight. Suddenly, the ships are overcome by a gale wind and those on board are overwhelmed by waves from every side, and they assume they are doomed. They cry out to Allah ˹alone˺ in sincere devotion, “If You save us from this, we will certainly be grateful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Begins With a Benefit, Not a Threat**

The opening is a statement of provision: He carries you across land and sea. The verb carries the sense of being borne by something provided for the purpose — roads, mounts, hulls, sails. The sea is included in the benefaction, and the Qur'an names it as such elsewhere: “And He is the One Who has subjected the sea, so from it you may eat tender seafood and extract ornaments to wear. And you see the ships ploughing their way through it, so you may seek His bounty and give thanks ˹to Him˺” (16:14). The order matters for how the rest of the verse reads. The storm is not presented as the natural condition of the sea; it is an interruption of a system that ordinarily works. A person who begins from the benefit cannot treat the danger as the whole story, and a person who begins from the danger cannot see that he has been carried a thousand times before. The verse starts where gratitude starts.

**A Favourable Wind and Passengers Delighted**

The scene is drawn in its pleasant phase, and the pleasure is specified: a favourable wind, and delight among the passengers. Nothing is wrong. The ship is doing what a ship does, the wind is behind it, and the people aboard are enjoying the journey — which is a rare thing to say about sea travel in the ancient world. The detail serves the argument. The verse is not describing fools who ignored a warning; it is describing people in a state of ordinary satisfaction, the same satisfaction described in the previous verses. Then the turn comes, and the turn is total. By placing the delight first, the verse makes the collapse more instructive: the change is not in the passengers but in the wind, and the wind was never theirs.

**The Gale and the Waves From Every Side**

The description of the crisis is exact about direction. The waves come from every side, and the passengers conclude that they are encompassed — surrounded, closed in, with no exit. The same scene appears with a sharper image: “And as soon as they are overwhelmed by waves like mountains, they cry out to Allah ˹alone˺ in sincere devotion. But when He delivers them ˹safely˺ to shore, only some become relatively grateful” (31:32). Waves like mountains is not exaggeration for its own sake; it removes the possibility of swimming, of swimming to shore, of any human response at all. The verse's theological point depends on that totality. When every option has been taken away, what remains is not a strategy but an address — and the verse reports what the address is.

**“In Sincere Devotion” — What the Storm Exposes**

The phrase *mukhliṣīna lahu d-dīn* — devoting religion entirely to Him — is the key to the verse. In the storm, devotion is pure. No intermediary is called, no idol is named, no patron is appealed to; the religion of the person aboard is, for those minutes, exactly what monotheism claims it always is. The same observation is made at sea in another place: “If they happen to be aboard a ship ˹caught in a storm˺, they cry out to Allah ˹alone˺ in sincere devotion. But as soon as He delivers them ˹safely˺ to shore, they associate ˹others with Him once again˺” (29:65). The Qur'an treats this as evidence rather than as anecdote. Under total helplessness the association drops away on its own, without argument or instruction, which suggests it was never load-bearing. What a person believes when he can do nothing is, on the verse's account, what he believes.

**A Promise Made at the Bottom**

The words spoken in the storm are a vow with a condition: if You save us, we will be grateful. The form is honest and the intent is real at the moment of speaking. The verse records it without irony, which is what makes the following turn so damning — not that the promise was false when made, but that it was forgotten when no longer needed. Gratitude promised under pressure is a different thing from gratitude practised in comfort, and the verse is preparing the reader for that distinction. A vow is easy to make when the alternative is drowning; the test of it begins on dry land, when nothing is forcing the memory.

**Why the Sea Is the Favourite Example**

The Qur'an returns to the ship more than to any other setting for this argument, and the reason is practical. At sea there are no intermediaries to appeal to and no rituals available: no sanctuary, no priest, no offering, no ancestral custom that applies. Every passenger is in the same position regardless of rank, and the only address that makes sense is to the One who controls the water. A desert offers some illusion of self-sufficiency — a man on land can walk, dig, fight, flee. A passenger on a ship in a gale can do none of these. The example is therefore chosen as the cleanest case of a general truth: strip away everything a person can do for himself and what remains is worship, and it is worship of one God.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:23

> But as soon as He rescues them, they transgress in the land unjustly. O humanity! Your transgression is only against your own souls. ˹There is only˺ brief enjoyment in this worldly life, then to Us is your return, and then We will inform you of what you used to do

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn Happens on Shore**

The rescue is followed immediately by transgression, and the verse marks the speed of it: as soon as He saves them. The word *baghy* denotes exceeding a limit — acting beyond what is permitted, in a way that injures. The people saved from the sea do not merely forget; they act, and their acting is described as transgression in the land. The shift of setting is deliberate. The sea produced sincerity because it removed every option; the land restores the options, and the restored options are used badly. The wider statement of the same fact is stark: “Corruption has spread on land and sea as a result of what people's hands have done, so that Allah may cause them to taste ˹the consequences of˺ some of their deeds and perhaps they might return ˹to the Right Path˺” (30:41). The verse here is the individual case of that general statement — one man, saved, transgressing within the hour.

**“Without Right” — the Qualifier That Convicts**

The transgression is specified as being without right, *bi-ghayri l-ḥaqq*, and the qualifier is not redundant. It rules out the reading that would make the verse a complaint about strength or ambition as such. What is condemned is exceeding a limit that exists, and the limit is known to the one exceeding it. The passengers who vowed gratitude have a standard in their own promise; the transgression is measured against it. This is why the address shifts to all humanity in the next clause. Every person carries limits he has acknowledged — in a prayer, a vow, a moment of clarity, a conscience he has not argued away — and the transgression the verse names is the crossing of those, not of someone else's rules. The injustice is not primarily against other people, though it reaches them; it is against a boundary the person himself recognised. That is why the address that follows is addressed to humanity as a whole rather than to the passengers alone. The case in the sea is one instance of a rule that holds on land.

**The Harm Comes Back on the Doer**

“Your transgression is only against your own souls” (10:23) is a statement of consequence rather than a consolation. It tells the hearer where the injury lands. A transgression appears to gain something — wealth taken, a rival humiliated, an appetite satisfied — and the verse says the gain is not where it looks. The same principle is stated in the general register: “No soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another. Then to your Lord is your return, and He will inform you of what you used to do” (39:7). Nobody absorbs the cost on the transgressor's behalf, and nothing external carries it away. The exegetical tradition reads this clause with the next as a single proposition under one vocalisation: your transgression against yourselves is nothing but the enjoyment of this worldly life — the whole of what it buys, and no more. Read that way, the sentence states the exchange rate: a lasting injury for a temporary pleasure.

**Enjoyment, and the Word for It**

The thing gained is called *matāʿ* — an enjoyment, a brief provision for use, the word used of goods that are consumed rather than kept. The Qur'an uses it consistently to mark shortness: “Whatever ˹pleasure˺ you have been given is no more than ˹a fleeting˺ enjoyment and adornment of this worldly life. But what is with Allah is far better and more lasting. Will you not then understand?” (28:60). The comparison is drawn where the audience is asked directly: “Do you prefer the life of this world over the Hereafter? The enjoyment of this worldly life is insignificant compared to that of the Hereafter” (9:38). And the verdict is stated flatly: “Every soul will taste death. And you will only receive your full reward on the Day of Judgment… whereas the life of this world is no more than the delusion of enjoyment” (3:185). The word choice does the argument. A *matāʿ* is not a possession; it is something used up, and the person who trades his soul for it has traded a permanent thing for a consumable.

**The Return, and Being Told What One Did**

The verse ends with two futures: a return, and an informing. “Then you will be returned to the Knower of the seen and unseen, and He will inform you of what you used to do” (62:8); “To Allah you will all return, and He will inform you of what you used to do” (5:105). The informing needs explanation, since God already knows. What is promised is not new information for Him but the manifestation of the record to the person — the moment at which a life is shown to the one who lived it, without interpretation or excuse. That is why the formula appears at the end of so many passages about conduct. It closes the case. The verse's structure therefore runs from a specific scene to a universal procedure: a man is saved from drowning, transgresses on the beach, is told that the injury is his own, that the gain is temporary, and that the account will be read back to him. Every clause is a step, and the last one is the reason the earlier ones matter. The verse moves from a beach to a courtroom in four sentences, and it does so without raising its voice, because the procedure it describes does not need emphasis to be certain.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:24

> The life of this world is just like rain We send down from the sky, producing a mixture of plants which humans and animals consume. Then just as the earth looks its best, perfectly beautified, and its people think they have full control over it, there comes to it Our command by night or by day, so We mow it down as if it never flourished yesterday! This is how We make the signs clear for people who reflect

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Parable Built From the Ordinary**

The comparison is taken from farming, and every element in it is familiar to the audience: rain, mixed vegetation, grazing animals, a field at its peak. The Qur'an uses this sequence repeatedly as its standard illustration of a life cycle: “Do you not see that Allah sends down rain from the sky—channelling it through streams in the earth—then produces with it crops of various colours, then they dry up and you see them wither, and then He reduces them to chaff? Surely in this is a reminder for people of reason” (39:21). The longer version adds the human element: “This is like rain that causes plants to grow, to the delight of the planters. But later the plants dry up and you see them wither, then they are reduced to chaff” (57:20). Choosing the ordinary is the point. A parable about a rare catastrophe would allow the listener to place himself outside it; a parable about a harvest does not, because everyone has eaten and everyone has seen a field cut.

**The Peak of Beauty Is the Turning Point**

The verse does not move straight from growth to destruction. It pauses at the maximum: the earth has taken on its brilliance, it is adorned, and it looks finished. The timing is precise — “just as” — and it is the verse's sharpest observation. Danger is not greatest when things are failing; it is greatest when they are perfect, because perfection produces the assumption that the state will hold. The aesthetic language is deliberate. The earth is described as adorned, dressed, made beautiful, and the description invites the reader to feel the attraction before being told what happens to it. Nothing in the parable disparages the garden. It is genuinely beautiful, and the beauty is real. What the verse attacks is not the garden but the inference drawn from it.

**“They Think They Have Full Control Over It”**

The delusion is named in a single phrase, and it is the hinge of the parable. The people of the earth conclude that they are capable of it, that they have mastered it, that its produce is theirs by competence. The claim is not spoken aloud in the verse; it is reported as a thought, which makes it harder to disown. Nobody in the field is making an argument against God. He is simply assuming that the system is his. That assumption is what the command destroys. The same delusion is described in the shorter parable: “˹It is˺ like the plants of the earth, thriving when sustained by the rain We send down from the sky. Then they ˹soon˺ turn into chaff scattered by the wind” (18:45). The wind does the work in that version; here it is the command. Either way the agent of the ending is not the gardener, and the gardener's belief that it was is the error being corrected.

**The Command Comes by Night or by Day**

The phrase “by night or by day” is understood as meaning at any moment — the two halves of the clock covering all of it. The timing is not merely unknown but indifferent: the destruction does not wait for a convenient hour, and it does not announce itself. Commentators have taken the command here as a violent wind, a flood, or a blast of some kind, and the range is the point; the specific instrument matters less than the fact that it arrives from outside the system the gardener thought he controlled. The general statement of the same fact is shorter and applies to the gardener himself: “Every soul will taste death” (3:185). The field and the farmer share one clock. The parable's power comes from that overlap — a person who has understood the harvest can be made to understand his own life, because both are cut at a time neither of them chose.

**“As If It Never Flourished Yesterday”**

The closing image is an erasure. The field is made *ḥaṣīd* — mown, reaped, cut down to nothing — and the simile that follows is temporal: as though it had not been verdant the day before. The destruction is total and the memory of the abundance is cancelled with it. This is a claim about how loss is experienced as much as about how fields end. A person who loses a long-held prosperity often finds that the prosperity leaves no residue; the house is sold, the business closed, the season gone, and within a year the state of affairs is indistinguishable from a time when none of it existed. The verse states that this is the ordinary behaviour of the world rather than an exceptional tragedy, which is why it can be used as a parable. Nothing here counsels against planting. What is corrected is the belief that a planted field is a permanent possession.

**Addressed to People Who Reflect**

The verse ends by naming its audience: those who reflect. A parable requires work from the reader — the transfer from a field to a life is not performed by the text but by the person hearing it. That transfer is the reflection being asked for. The audience is not specialists; it is anyone willing to make the comparison, which is available to a farmer and to a merchant alike. The same formula closes other sign-passages, and it always leaves the inference with the listener rather than forcing it. The verse's method is therefore gentle in form and severe in content: an image of a beautiful garden, and a request to think about it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:25

> And Allah invites ˹all˺ to the Home of Peace and guides whoever He wills to the Straight Path

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Invitation, Two Verbs, Two Scopes**

The verse is short and its structure is exact: God calls, and God guides. The first verb has no restriction on its object — the invitation is to everyone. The second is qualified: whoever He wills. The distinction is not a contradiction but the whole content of the verse. All are called; not all arrive; and the difference between being called and arriving is not located in the call. The classical statement of the point is compact: He calls all people to the Abode of Peace, but only those who are guided enter it. The verse therefore answers a question the previous verses raise. If the world is a field that is cut down, where is anyone going? The answer is an invitation, and the invitation is open. What is particular is the guidance, and the particularity is explained in the last clause of the verse rather than left as a mystery. The two verbs also answer two different human problems. The person who has never heard the call needs the call; the person who has heard it and cannot find his way needs the guidance. Neither is left without provision, and neither is given what the other requires.

**Dār al-Salām: The Name Explains the Place**

The destination is named, and the name is theological. *Salām* is one of God's names — “He is Allah—there is no god except Him: the King, the Most Holy, the AllPerfect, the Source of Serenity” (59:23) — so an abode called the Home of Peace is an abode named after its Owner. The same title is given where the destination is described as a relationship: “They will have the Home of Peace with their Lord, Who will be their Guardian because of what they used to do” (6:127). The phrase “with their Lord” is the operative part. The contrast with the previous two verses is deliberate and complete. The world is a field that is cut at an unknown hour; this is the home of settlement — “O my people! This worldly life is only ˹a fleeting˺ enjoyment, whereas the Hereafter is truly the home of settlement” (40:39). Two abodes are being compared, and the comparison is not between pleasures of different intensity but between a place that ends and a place that does not.

**Peace Means Safety From Ending**

The exegetical tradition draws out the connection between *salām* and *salāmah* — peace and safety — and the link is what gives the name its weight. The inhabitants of Paradise are at peace because they are safe: safe from death, from loss, from decline, from the arrival of a command by night or by day. Nothing in that abode can be taken away, and that is precisely what the previous parable showed the world cannot promise. The description of the abode confirms it: “There they will never hear any idle talk—only ˹greetings of˺ peace. And there they will have their provisions morning and evening” (19:62). Every form of harm that the world distributes — bereavement, sickness, betrayal, decay — is an interruption, and the Home of Peace is defined as the place where interruption does not occur. The name is therefore a statement about permanence, not about mood. The same connection explains why the greeting of that abode is peace and why its inhabitants hear nothing idle: a place safe from ending has no need of defence, negotiation, or reassurance.

**The Straight Path Is What an Invitation Requires**

An invitation without a route is a wish. The second half of the verse supplies the road: the Straight Path. The pairing is standard in the Qur'an, where guidance is consistently described as a path rather than a state, and the messenger's role is described in the same terms: “But We have made it a light, by which We guide whoever We will of Our servants. And you are truly leading ˹all˺ to the Straight Path” (42:52). The path metaphor does specific work here. A path is walkable by ordinary people, it has a direction, and it can be missed — none of which is true of a mood or a feeling. The invitation to the Home of Peace is therefore an invitation to walk somewhere, and the guidance mentioned in the same breath is what makes the walking possible. The verse refuses to separate the destination from the route, which is why the call is universal while the arrival is not.

**“Whoever He Wills” and the Wisdom Behind It**

The qualification could be read as arbitrary, and the Qur'an closes that reading in the same breath elsewhere: “If you disbelieve, then ˹know that˺ Allah is truly not in need of you, nor does He approve of disbelief from His servants. But if you become grateful ˹through faith˺, He will appreciate that from you” (39:7). God does not approve of disbelief, so His will cannot be a preference for some people's ruin. The exegetical tradition resolves the verse accordingly: guidance is granted to those whom God knows will benefit by it, and the will is predicated on wisdom rather than on favouritism. That is the only reading consistent with an invitation addressed to all. A call that is genuinely open and a guidance that is genuinely wise leave the responsibility where the verse leaves it — with the one who hears the call and does or does not walk. The verse states both halves in a single sentence for that reason: remove either one and the picture becomes either coercion or abandonment, and the sūrah's account of human conduct requires neither.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:26

> Those who do good will have the finest reward and ˹even˺ more. Neither gloom nor disgrace will cover their faces. It is they who will be the residents of Paradise. They will be there forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Finest, and Then More Than the Finest**

The promise is stated in two parts, and the second part is what makes the first remarkable. Those who do good receive *al-ḥusnā* — the finest, the best possible thing — and *ziyādah*, an increase beyond it. A reward described as the best would already be complete; adding to it means the scale is not closed. The same phrase appears where the response to God's call is described: “Those who respond to ˹the call of˺ their Lord will have the finest reward. As for those who do not respond to Him, even if they were to possess everything in the world twice over, they would certainly offer it to ransom themselves” (13:18). The comparison inside that verse is worth holding: the finest reward is set against everything in the world doubled, and the doubled world is the losing side. The increase promised here is not a bonus tacked onto a transaction; it is the sign that the transaction is not the right model at all.

**What the Increase Is**

The exegetical tradition identifies the increase with the vision of God in the Hereafter, and the identification rests on a report. Ṣuhayb narrated that the Prophet ﷺ said, explaining this verse: when the people of Paradise have entered Paradise, a caller will call out that they have a promise from God which He wishes to fulfil. They will answer: has He not brightened our faces, admitted us to Paradise, and saved us from the Fire? Then the veil will be lifted, and nothing given to them will be dearer to them than looking at their Lord (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 181). The Qur'an's own words on that Day are two short clauses: “On that Day ˹some˺ faces will be bright, looking at their Lord” (75:22–23). The increase is therefore not an object added to a list of rewards but a change in the kind of thing being given — from what God provides to God Himself being seen. That reading explains why the increase is not itemised. Every other reward in the Qur'an's descriptions of Paradise is a thing — gardens, rivers, silk, fruit — and a list of things can be completed. The increase is kept off the list because it is not a thing, and the verse's silence about its nature is the only way to stop it being counted beside the rest. That silence also explains the verse's next move. A reward beyond description is followed at once by a description of what it does to a face, which is the only way such a reward can be spoken of at all.

**Faces That Are Not Covered**

The negative promise is as precise as the positive one. Neither gloom nor disgrace will cover their faces, and both nouns matter. Gloom is what grief looks like from outside; disgrace is what shame looks like. The faces of these people will carry neither, because they will have nothing to grieve over and nothing to be ashamed of. The Qur'an states the same fact positively: “On that Day ˹some˺ faces will be bright” (75:22), and it sets the two groups side by side elsewhere: “On that Day some faces will be bright while others gloomy” (3:106), with the bright-faced “in Allah's mercy, where they will remain forever” (3:107). Choosing the face as the site of the promise is deliberate. The face is where a person is known and where his state shows; a promise about the face is a promise about being seen without fear. The negative form is also more informative than a positive one would be. To say that gloom will not cover a face is to say that everything producing gloom has been removed — loss, regret, separation, the sight of what might have been.

**Residents, Not Visitors**

The people described are *aṣḥāb al-jannah* — the companions or residents of the Garden. The word *ṣāḥib* denotes someone who keeps company with a thing, who belongs to it; the phrase carries ownership and habit rather than admission. The clause that follows removes any doubt: they will be there forever. This is the direct answer to the parable given two verses earlier. The field was cut at an unknown hour and left as though it had never flourished; the residence here is defined by the absence of that hour. The verse's final words are the smallest possible statement of permanence, and they are placed at the end so that everything before them is read in their light. Permanence is the one feature this world cannot supply, and the verse names it last for that reason. A reward that ends is not a reward but a loan.

**The Condition Is Excellence, Not the Minimum**

The subject of the verse is those who did *iḥsān* — who acted well, beautifully, to the standard of excellence. The word is stronger than mere compliance. A person who does the minimum has discharged an obligation; a *muḥsin* has done more than the obligation required, or has done what was required with care. The reward matches the standard: the finest, and more. The verse therefore sets the condition of the promise at a level no one can claim to have reached by rote, and it pairs that condition with a reward that exceeds the scale on which deeds are counted. Both halves of the verse are doing the same work — refusing to let the relationship be reduced to an exchange. Excellence cannot be legislated in detail, which is why the standard is given in one word and its content is left to the person who has to meet it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:27

> As for those who commit evil, the reward of an evil deed is its equivalent. Humiliation will cover them—with no one to protect them from Allah—as if their faces were covered with patches of the night’s deep darkness. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**Proportion, Not Excess**

The verse opens with a rule of measure, and the rule is defensive: the reward of an evil deed is its equivalent. Nothing more. The Qur'an states the same principle with the arithmetic made explicit: “Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded tenfold. But whoever comes with a bad deed will be punished for only one. None will be wronged” (6:160), and “The reward of an evil deed is its equivalent. But whoever pardons and seeks reconciliation, then their reward is with Allah” (42:40). The asymmetry is deliberate and it runs against the punishment, not in its favour. Good is multiplied; evil is matched. The verse here states the matching side, and it does so before describing the punishment, so that the description arrives inside a declared limit. Stating the rule before the imagery is deliberate. The imagery of punishment is vivid, and a vivid image without a declared limit invites exaggeration. Nothing in what follows exceeds what was earned, and the reader is told this in the first clause rather than being asked to infer it later. The limit also answers an objection the verse anticipates. A punishment exceeding its cause would be injustice, and this sūrah has already said the return exists so that reward and punishment can be rendered justly. Proportion is what makes the outcome defensible.

**Humiliation as a Covering**

The second clause describes what will come over them: *dhilla*, abasement, the state of being brought low. The verb is one of covering, and the covering is not applied to their bodies but to their condition. Humiliation in the Qur'an is the opposite of the arrogance that produced the evil deeds, and it is described in exactly those terms: “On the Day of Judgment you will see those who lied about Allah with their faces gloomy. Is Hell not a ˹fitting˺ home for the arrogant?” (39:60). The gloom on the face is the visible form of a claim that has been withdrawn. A person who acted as though he were beyond reach is now inside a situation he cannot influence, and the covering is that situation made apparent. Nothing external is added to produce it; it is the removal of what he had assumed. Nothing is put on him; something is taken away, and what is taken away is the posture he adopted in the world.

**No Protector Between Them and Allah**

The clause in the middle is the one that answers the theology attacked earlier in this chapter. “With no one to protect them from Allah” (10:27) — no ally, no patron, no intercessor standing between them and the outcome. The same phrase describes the Day in general: “the Day you will ˹try in vain to˺ turn your backs and run away, with no one to protect you from Allah. And whoever Allah leaves to stray will be left with no guide” (40:33). The verse is closing the door the idolaters had opened when they said their gods would plead for them. Intercession exists, but it exists by permission, and permission does not extend to those who assigned partners to the One who grants it. The protection they relied on was not refused at the last moment; it never existed, and the verse states the fact at the moment when the discovery cannot be avoided. The clause also explains why the humiliation is total. A person with an ally is never fully abased, because the possibility of rescue remains; removing the protector is what makes the covering complete.

**Patches of Night on the Face**

The simile is unusually specific. Their faces are as though covered with pieces of the night, dark patches — not a uniform darkness but fragments of it, like the black sections of a moonless sky. The image does three things. It makes the state visible rather than merely internal. It makes it disfiguring, since patches on a face obscure the features by which a person is recognised. And it answers the previous verse directly: the faces of the people of the Garden are not covered by gloom or disgrace, and these faces are covered by darkness. The same organ carries both outcomes, which is why the Qur'an returns to it: “while ˹other˺ faces will be dusty, cast in gloom” (80:40–41). The face is where the inner condition is published, and this verse publishes the ugliest version of it. The simile's precision serves the same end as the parable of the field earlier in the chapter: an image exact enough to be remembered, and remembered while the choice is still open.

**Two Verses in Deliberate Symmetry**

The structure of this verse mirrors the one before it clause for clause. Those who did good: the finest and more. Those who did evil: the equivalent. No gloom on their faces; humiliation covering theirs. Residents of the Garden forever; residents of the Fire forever. The symmetry is the argument. It shows that the two outcomes are not arbitrary assignments but matched results, each answering its own cause. And the one point where the symmetry breaks is the most important feature of the pair: on the side of good there is an increase beyond what was earned, while on the side of evil there is exact proportion. The verse therefore ends as it began, on a statement of justice, and leaves the excess of mercy standing where the reader can see that it is not owed. The symmetry also makes the pair memorable, and the Qur'an's use of parallel structure is always aimed at memory as much as at argument.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:28

> ˹Consider˺ the Day We will gather them all together then say to those who associated others ˹with Allah in worship˺, “Stay in your places—you and your associate-gods.” We will separate them from each other, and their associate-gods will say, “It was not us that you worshipped

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gathering and the Order to Stay Put**

The verse opens on a scene rather than a statement, and the scene is a courtroom in which the parties are told not to move. The same Day is described in shorter form elsewhere: “˹Consider˺ the Day We will gather them all together then ask those who associated others ˹with Allah in worship˺, ‘Where are those gods you used to claim?’” (6:22). The command here — stay in your places, you and your associate-gods — is not a mercy but a fixing of positions. Nothing can be rearranged once the gathering begins; the alliances formed in the world are made to stand in their actual configuration. The commentators differ on the scope of the gathering: some take it to include both the people of the Garden and the people of the Fire, others restrict it to the disbelievers and their gods. The narrower reading fits the verse's own focus, since everything that follows concerns the relationship between worshippers and the objects of their worship. Either reading leaves the scene's function intact: it is a demonstration, staged for the benefit of those still able to act on it.

**The Partners Include More Than Stone**

A partner is anything worshipped alongside God, and the category is wider than idols. It includes human beings followed without warrant, and it includes one's own desire — a point the Qur'an makes twice in the same words: “Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ the one who has taken their own desires as their god? Will you then be a keeper over them?” (25:43), and “Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ those who have taken their own desires as their god? ˹And so˺ Allah left them to stray knowingly” (45:23). The Sufi tradition drew the practical conclusion. ʿAyn al-Quḍāt al-Hamadhānī (d. 525/1131) wrote that if a person wants self-knowledge he must abandon habit-worship, because habit-worship is idol-worship. The observation is sharp: an unexamined routine functions exactly as a god does, receiving the obedience that should be given on evidence. The verse's scene applies to that case as fully as to a carved image. The wider the category, the more readers the verse has. A person who would never bow to a statue may still be governed by an appetite, and the question here is addressed to whatever receives his obedience without examination.

**“It Was Not Us That You Worshipped”**

The denial comes from the objects themselves, and it is total. They do not claim to have been honoured; they deny the relationship altogether. The same disclaimer is stated as a future fact: “If you call upon them, they cannot hear your calls. And if they were to hear, they could not respond to you. On the Day of Judgment they will disown your worship ˹of them˺” (35:14). And the ground of the disclaimer is their emptiness: “Call upon those you claim ˹to be divine˺ besides Allah. They do not possess ˹even˺ an atom's weight either in the heavens or the earth, nor do they have any share in ˹governing˺ them. Nor is any of them a helper to Him” (34:22). The denial is not a betrayal in the ordinary sense, since there was never a bond to betray. That is the difference between this denial and a human betrayal: a person can break a bond that existed, while an object can only reveal that none did. The worshippers projected a relationship onto something that had no interior, and the Day simply reports what was always the case.

**The Separation Comes Before the Verdict**

Between the command and the speech stands an act: We will separate them. The worshippers and the worshipped are pulled apart, and the separation is the punishment's first stage. In the world the alliance gave the worshipper a sense of backing — patrons, ancestors, a tradition, a crowd. On that Day the backing is removed and the person stands alone with what he did. The verse does not describe an argument or a trial at this point; it describes an unmaking. Everything that made the false devotion feel supported — its antiquity, its community, its respectability — is detached, and what remains is the individual and the fact of his worship.
