That is why the following verse can be so short. The separation is also final. In the world the worshipper could return to his gods whenever he chose; on that Day the distance between them is fixed by command.

**Idols Given the Power to Speak**

The scene requires the objects to speak, and the Qur'an addresses the difficulty explicitly. In this world an idol cannot answer: they are described elsewhere as unable to speak, and Ibrāhīm's rebuke turns on it. On the Day, speech is given to things that had none, as part of a wider testimony: “When they reach it, their ears, eyes, and skin will testify against what they used to do. They will ask their skin ˹furiously˺, ‘Why have you testified against us?’ It will say, ‘We have been made to speak by Allah, Who causes all things to speak’” (41:20–21). The answer given by the skin is the answer to the whole scene. The God who made the tongue in the first place can make anything speak, and the testimony of a person's own body is harder to dispute than the testimony of a witness. The idols' denial in this verse is the same kind of speech — produced not by them but by the One who causes things to speak, and produced against the people who used them.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:29

> Allah is sufficient as a Witness between each of us that we were totally unaware of your worship.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Sentence That Ends an Alliance**

The verse is a single clause, and it completes the speech begun in the previous verse. The associate-gods have already denied the worship; now they add the ground of the denial and name the arbiter. The brevity is the point. A religious system that had accumulated temples, lineages, seasons and arguments is dismantled in one sentence spoken by the object of that system. Nothing is negotiated, no explanation is offered, no apology appears. The verse demonstrates what the whole arrangement was worth: a claim made by people, and a denial made by the thing claimed. Between them stands a witness, and the naming of the witness is what makes the denial final rather than merely contradictory. The verse also reverses the direction of appeal. The worshipper turned to his god in order to reach God; on that Day the god turns to God in order to be rid of the worshipper.

**“Allah Is Sufficient as a Witness Between Us”**

The formula is legal language. *Kafā bi-llāhi shahīdan baynanā wa-baynakum* — Allah suffices as a witness between us and you. A witness is someone who saw what happened and can be asked; sufficiency means no further witness is required. The same formula is used where the Prophet is instructed to stake his message on it: “Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘Sufficient is Allah as a Witness between me and you. He ˹fully˺ knows His servants’” (17:96). Applied here, the formula does something unusual: it is spoken by the false gods against their own worshippers. The party being called as witness is the One they were set beside, and His testimony is not about their divinity but about the fact of the worship. The choice of witness is exact. A witness must be independent of both parties, and the One whom the idols were set beside is the only figure in the scene who is neither accuser nor accused. His testimony cannot be dismissed as interested, which is what makes the denial unanswerable. The verse therefore turns the idolaters' own appeal inside out. They had invoked their gods as intermediaries with God; on that Day those gods invoke God as a witness against them.

**“We Were Unaware of Your Worship”**

The content of the testimony is ignorance. The objects were not present to the devotion directed at them; they did not receive it, hear it, or benefit from it. The same fact is stated with its consequence: “If you call upon them, they cannot hear your calls. And if they were to hear, they could not respond to you. On the Day of Judgment they will disown your worship ˹of them˺” (35:14). This is a more damaging statement than a rejection would be. A god who refuses a worshipper at least acknowledges him; a god who was never aware of him reveals that the worshipper was speaking to nothing for his whole life. The verse does not soften this. The religion of the idolater is shown to have been a monologue, and the monologue is what he is now answerable for. The word for unaware carries no hint of concealment. It is plain ignorance, the condition of something with no awareness to withhold. The idolaters are not told that their gods refused them; they are told that their gods were never there, and that a devotion which organised a life had no recipient.

**Satan Makes the Same Speech, With One Difference**

The parallel disclaimer is recorded, and comparing the two is instructive. Satan will say after the judgment: “Indeed, Allah has made you a true promise. I too made you a promise, but I failed you. I did not have any authority over you. I only called you, and you responded to me. So do not blame me; blame yourselves” (14:22). The idols deny awareness; Satan admits the call. Both disclaim responsibility, and both throw the case back on the one who followed. The difference matters because it locates the guilt precisely. Satan's defence is that he had no authority and used none — only an invitation, which was accepted. The idols' defence is that they were not even there. In neither case does anything remain of the excuse the follower will try to make, and in both cases the closing words are the same in substance: blame yourselves. The comparison also shows what this verse is not doing. It is not excusing the follower on the grounds that he was misled, since the misleader disclaims the power to mislead. Responsibility stays with the one who chose, and the disclaimers remove his last cover.

**Why the Verse Is So Short**

The verse is nine words in the original and it does the work of a treatise. A religious system is a set of claims about access, favour and protection; the verse removes all three at once by having the supposed providers testify that nothing reached them. What the brevity achieves rhetorically is the collapse of the argument rather than its refutation. No point is answered because no point was ever made — the worship rested on an assumption, and the assumption is now denied by its own object. The verse also protects the reader from a common mistake. It does not present the idols as deceivers who led people astray; it presents them as empty. The deception came from elsewhere, and the following verse assigns it where it belongs. Read with that verse, the account is complete: an empty object, and a person who filled it. The emptiness is not the crime. The filling is what remains when the appetite has been consulted instead of the giver, and the verse measures the difference.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:30

> Then and there every soul will face ˹the consequences of˺ what it had done. They all will be returned to Allah—their True Master. And whatever ˹gods˺ they fabricated will fail them. Questions to Pagans: 1) Who Provides?

**Expanded Commentary**

**“Then and There” — a Place, Not a Time**

The verse opens with *hunālik*, there and then, and the word points at a location: the place where the gathering of the previous verses has occurred. The effect is immediacy. Nothing intervenes between the denial of the associate-gods and the facing of one's own deeds; there is no corridor, no interval, no opportunity to compose an account or arrange a defence. The same adverb is used elsewhere in the Qur'an to mark the moment at which a claim collapses, and its force is always the same — the reader is put inside the scene at the exact point where the outcome becomes unavoidable. The verse therefore does not describe a process of judgment. It describes an arrival, and the arrival is instant. The adverb also fixes the reader's position. What follows is not a procedure observed from a distance but a scene he is being placed inside.

**Every Soul Faces What It Sent Ahead**

The verb is one of encountering: each soul will find, meet, be confronted by what it did. The deeds are not brought as evidence by a third party; they are present as the person's own doing, which is a stronger position for the judgment and a weaker one for the defence. The same confrontation is described from the individual's side: “The Day every soul will find whatever good it did ˹present˺ before it—as well as whatever evil it did” (3:30). The verse's use of “every soul” is inclusive and deliberate. The scene in the previous verses concerned the idolaters and their gods, but this clause widens to everyone gathered, since the facing of one's deeds is not a punishment reserved for association but the condition of the Day itself. The person who relied on a patron loses the patron; the person who relied on himself is left with the same record. Both meet the same thing. The formulation closes every avenue of comparison. No record is read beside another, and no one's deeds are weighed against someone else's circumstances. The phrase “every soul” closes the last escape too: there is no crowd to hide in and no representative to answer in a person's place.

**“Their True Master”**

The verse names God with a qualifier that only makes sense against the preceding scene: *mawlāhumu l-ḥaqq*, their true Master, Protector, Patron. The word *mawlā* carries exactly the relationship the idolaters had tried to build with their gods — guardianship, support, a claim on help. Adding “the true” says that every other instance of the relation was counterfeit. The same distinction is drawn where the two groups are separated: “That is because Allah is the ˹True˺ Master of the believers, whereas the disbelievers have no master” (47:11). The verse is therefore not merely returning people to God; it is telling them that the One they are returned to is the only patron the relationship ever had. What they thought they possessed in addition was not an extra support but a substitute for the real one. The verse completes the chapter's earlier polemic in this way. The idolaters were not wrong to want a patron; they were wrong about which one they had. The word “true” does the same work here that it did when God was named the true Lord: a relationship can be genuine or counterfeit, and from inside the two feel identical.

**What They Fabricated Fails Them**

The last clause uses a verb of disappearance: what they used to fabricate *ḍalla* — is lost, vanishes, goes astray from them. The objects are described as fabricated, which assigns their origin to human invention rather than to anything discovered. Their failure is not a refusal to help; it is an absence. This is the same outcome the previous two verses announced from the idols' side — they were unaware of the worship — and now it is stated from the worshippers' side: the thing they made is simply not there when it is needed. The Qur'an describes the moment in a question: “˹Consider˺ the Day We will call them and say, ‘Where are those partners of Mine ˹you˺ used to claim?’” and no answer comes. The failure is total precisely because the fabrication was total; something invented cannot appear as a fact when facts are what count. Naming the objects as fabricated serves the same purpose. A thing made up can be maintained only while nobody tests it, and the Day is the test.

**All of Them, Returned**

The verse's middle clause is universal in a way the surrounding verses are not: they will all be returned to Allah. The pronoun covers the fabricators and everyone else in the gathering. The return has been the sūrah's stated destination from early on — to Him is your return, all together — and this verse is the point at which the statement becomes a scene. Two facts are held together here without tension. Every soul faces its own deeds individually, and every soul is returned to the same Owner collectively. The individuality of the record does not make anyone separate from the return, and the universality of the return does not dissolve the record. The verse's ordering is significant: first the facing, then the return, then the loss of the fabricated gods. By the time the last clause arrives, the reader already knows that nothing except the deeds and the Owner will be present. That ordering is the verse's teaching in miniature: conduct first, ownership second, and the invented gods last, entering the account only as an absence. Fabrication is not a private matter; the failure of the fabricated thing is witnessed, and the witnessing belongs to the reckoning.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:31

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Who provides for you from heaven and earth? Who owns ˹your˺ hearing and sight? Who brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living? And who conducts every affair?” They will ˹surely˺ say, “Allah.” Say, “Will you not then fear ˹Him˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Questions the Audience Answers Correctly**

The verse is a questionnaire, and its power lies in the answers. The Prophet is instructed to ask four questions and then to report the reply: they will say, Allah. This is not a prediction but a record of what actually happened when the questions were put. The same exchange is reported four more times in the Qur'an, always with the same result. “If you ask them ˹O Prophet˺ who created the heavens and the earth and subjected the sun and the moon ˹for your benefit˺, they will certainly say, ‘Allah!’ How can they then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?” (29:61). “And if you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, they will definitely say, ‘Allah!’ Say, ‘Praise be to Allah!’ In fact, most of them do not know” (31:25). The audience is not ignorant of God. They are agreed on the largest part of the case, and the argument proceeds from the agreement rather than against it. That method is the most economical available. A refutation has to establish its own premises; this one borrows them from the people being addressed, so no premise in it can be disputed by them.

**Provision From Heaven and Earth**

The first question names two directions, and the pairing is concrete: from the sky comes rain, and from the earth comes what the rain produces. Provision is not abstract sustenance but a visible system with two halves. The same question is put in a fuller form elsewhere: “O humanity! Remember Allah's favours upon you. Is there any creator other than Allah who provides for you from the heavens and the earth? There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him” (35:3). The exegetical tradition reads the provision broadly, covering both the bodily and the spiritual, but the primary reference is the ordinary economy of food and water that every person in the audience depended on daily. Asking about it is effective because nobody in Mecca claimed that an idol sent rain. The question is designed so that the honest answer contradicts the hearer's practice. Provision is also the least deniable of the four items, since it is renewed every day and cannot be credited to anything standing in the sanctuary.

**Hearing and Sight as Something Owned by Another**

The second question shifts from the world to the body. Who has charge of your hearing and your sight? The verb is one of sovereignty, and the point is that the faculties are held rather than owned. The Qur'an states the same fact as a benefaction: “Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘He is the One Who brought you into being and gave you hearing, sight, and intellect. ˹Yet˺ you hardly give any thanks’” (67:23). The warning attached to the faculties is that they can be rendered useless: “And We gave them hearing, sight, and intellect. But neither their hearing, sight, nor intellect were of any benefit to them whatsoever, since they persisted in denying Allah's signs” (46:26). A person who will not use his hearing on the matter it was given for has not lost the faculty; he has wasted it. The question carries a second edge for that reason. It asks who owns the senses, and it implies that the owner may call for an account of how they were spent. The question therefore does double work — it establishes that the senses are given, and it prepares the rebuke for the way they are being used.

**Living From the Dead and Dead From the Living**

The third question is the resurrection argument in its shortest form, and it is drawn from agriculture. The seed is dead matter that produces a living plant; the plant dies and returns to the ground. The Qur'an states the cycle as a fact of observation: “He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And He gives life to the earth after its death. And so will you be brought forth ˹from the grave˺” (30:19), and “Indeed, Allah is the One Who causes seeds and fruit stones to sprout. He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. That is Allah! How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?” (6:95). The argument does not ask anyone to accept a miracle. It asks him to look at a field, where the transition between life and death happens twice a year in both directions, and then to say why the same agent could not perform it once more on a larger scale.

**“Will You Not Then Fear Him?”**

The particle *fa* — then, so — is the hinge of the verse. It ties the conclusion to the answers, and the conclusion is a question rather than a statement: will you not then be reverent? The expectation is that a person who has just said “Allah” to all four questions will act accordingly. The verse identifies the exact point of failure in Meccan religion. The confession was correct and the practice was not, and the gap between them is explained by the idolaters' own account of their practice: “As for those who take other lords besides Him, ˹saying,˺ ‘We worship them only so they may bring us closer to Allah’” (39:3). They were not denying God; they were adding to Him. The question the Prophet is told to ask does not argue with the addition. It asks whether a person who has just attributed provision, senses, life and government to one Being can consistently assign partners to Him. The silence that follows is the verse's real content. Nothing more needs saying to a person who has just described the whole of creation as one Being's work and then gone on worshipping others beside Him.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:32

> That is Allah—your True Lord. So what is beyond the truth except falsehood? How can you then be turned away?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demonstrative Closes the Argument**

The verse begins by pointing. *Dhālikum* — that one — gathers the four answers of the previous verse into a single identification and hands it back to the audience as their own statement. Nothing new is asserted; the audience has already supplied the content. The same formula appears where the questions have just been asked and answered: “That is Allah, your Lord, the Creator of all things. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?” (40:62), and “That is Allah—your Lord! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. ˹He is˺ the Creator of all things, so worship Him ˹alone˺” (6:102). The rhetorical method is consistent: ask, receive the answer, then name the One described. The listener is not being persuaded from outside; he is being held to what he said. The method has a further advantage. A person corrected by his own words cannot claim to have been outmanoeuvred by a cleverer opponent; the correction arrives in his vocabulary and in his order.

**“Your True Lord”**

The qualifier *al-ḥaqq* is the operative word. Lordship is being claimed by others in the audience's practice, and the verse distinguishes the real instance from the imitations by naming God as the true Lord. The distinction is drawn explicitly elsewhere: “That is because Allah ˹alone˺ is the Truth and what they invoke besides Him is falsehood, and Allah ˹alone˺ is truly the Most High, All-Great” (22:62). Truth here is not a property of statements but of a being — the One who actually is what He is claimed to be. An idol is called a lord and is not one; the mismatch between name and fact is what makes the worship false. The verse therefore does not need to attack the idols directly. It attaches the word “true” to God and lets the attachment do the work, since a true instance of a category implies that other claimants are not instances of it. The audience's own language assists the argument. They called their gods lords, and the word implies the powers the previous verse has just attributed to one Being. Attaching “true” to that Being invents no distinction; it applies one the audience already uses.

**A Question That Leaves No Third Option**

“What is beyond the truth except falsehood?” (10:32) is a statement in the form of a question, and it excludes a middle position. There is no partially true lord, no arrangement in which the truth is supplemented, no graduated scale on which an idol occupies a lower rung of the same order. The verse's logic is deliberately severe, and it is aimed at the specific compromise Mecca had adopted — God as the head of a pantheon, with lesser beings handling access. That arrangement requires a third category between truth and falsehood, and the verse refuses to grant one. The same exclusivity is stated where devotion is defined: “Indeed, sincere devotion is due ˹only˺ to Allah” (39:3). A person who wants a moderate position between monotheism and association is being told that no such position exists to occupy. The severity is not rhetorical. It reflects the structure of the claim. Lordship is not a quality that comes in degrees, so a hierarchy of lords is not a softer monotheism but a different proposition altogether, and the verse declines to treat it as a compromise.

**“How Can You Then Be Turned Away?”**

The closing question is passive, and the passivity is significant. The audience is not asked how they turned away but how they are being turned away — which implies an agent and a direction. The same question closes other passages in which creative power has just been described: “Indeed, Allah is the One Who causes seeds and fruit stones to sprout… That is Allah! How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?” (6:95), and the identical phrase at 35:3 and 40:62. The question is a rebuke aimed at reasoning rather than at information. These people have the facts; they have just answered four questions correctly. What remains unexplained is the movement away from a conclusion they themselves reached, and the verse treats that movement as the thing requiring explanation. Nobody is turned away from the truth by evidence. Something else does the turning. The question also carries bewilderment rather than anger. It is the question of someone who cannot reconstruct the road that led away from a conclusion, because no such road exists. What remains is a preference, and preferences do not answer to argument.

**The Shortest Link in the Chain**

The verse is three sentences long and it carries the whole weight of the argument that surrounds it. The questions came before it; the decree comes after. Without this verse the sequence would run from correct answers directly to condemnation, and the condemnation would look arbitrary. What the verse supplies is the identification and the exclusion — this is the true Lord, and there is nothing between the truth and falsehood — so that the following verse reads as a consequence rather than a surprise. Its brevity is functional. A long argument here would suggest that the point needed support; three short clauses state it as something already established in the mouths of the people being addressed. The verse also marks a transition the longer argument cannot mark. It is the point at which the discussion stops being about evidence and becomes a decision, stated in language plain enough that the transition cannot be missed by anyone listening carefully.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:33

> And so your Lord’s decree has been proven true against the rebellious—that they will never believe. 2) Who Creates?

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word That Has Come Due**

The verse reports the actualisation of a divine statement. *Ḥaqqat kalimatu rabbik* — the word of your Lord has come true, has been realised, has landed. The verb denotes a debt falling due or a sentence taking effect, and the image is legal rather than emotional. The same formula appears where the outcome of denial is stated as a settled matter: “And so your Lord's decree has been proven true against the disbelievers—that they will be the inmates of the Fire” (40:6). The decree is not described here as a decision made in response to the moment; it is described as something that was already in place and has now reached its term. That is consistent with the Qur'an's wider statement about divine action: “Indeed, We have created everything, perfectly preordained” (54:49). The same register appears in the accounts of destroyed peoples, where the appointed time arrives and the outcome is reported without commentary. Nothing in the verse suggests improvisation. What has happened is that a known outcome has arrived at the people to whom it applied. The legal register is worth noticing because it removes any suggestion of passion. A decree falling due is not an act of anger; it is the operation of a term set earlier, reported in the tone of a clerk rather than a judge.

**What the Decree Consists Of**

The content of the decree is given in the clause that follows: that they will not believe. The subject is specified as the rebellious — *al-fāsiqūn*, those who have departed from the limits, the same class described in the preceding verses as associating partners despite knowing better. The sequence in the passage matters for reading the decree correctly. First came the questions, which the audience answered correctly (10:31). Then came the identification and the exclusion (10:32). Then came the question about how they are being turned away. The decree is stated after all of that, which places it at the end of a process rather than before one. It is not a verdict issued before the evidence; it is the settled description of people who heard the evidence, agreed with it, and walked away from it anyway. The order of the verses makes the sequence impossible to miss: confession, identification, exclusion, then the decree. Nothing stands between the audience's own answer and the outcome except the audience's refusal. That placement distinguishes this decree from fate as the word is usually understood. It is not a sentence handed down before the conduct; it is the description of a conduct already chosen.

**“They Will Never Believe” — the Content, Not the Cause**

The phrase states an outcome and it is easy to misread it as a compulsion. Nothing in the verse says the rebellious are made unable to believe. What it says is that they will not — a statement about what will in fact happen, made by the One who knows what will happen. The distinction is the same one that runs through the Qur'an's treatment of divine knowledge and human choice: the knowledge does not produce the act; it records it in advance. The verse's own context demonstrates the point. The people it describes have just spoken the correct answers aloud. They are not incapable of belief; they are unwilling. A decree about an unwilling person is a prediction that will be confirmed, not a lock applied to a door. The distinction is not a technicality. Were the decree a lock, the questions of the previous verse would be pointless, since a man unable to believe gains nothing from being asked who provides for him. The questions presuppose that the answer matters and could have gone the other way.

**Why the Decree Falls on This Group**

The reason is embedded in the word chosen for them. The rebellious are those who exceeded a limit they knew. That knowledge is what the preceding verses established — they attribute provision, hearing, sight, life and the conducting of affairs to Allah, and then assign partners to Him. The rebellion is therefore not ignorance but inconsistency, and inconsistency maintained against one's own confession is what the Qur'an calls *fisq*. The same outcome is described elsewhere with the cause attached: “And so your Lord's decree has been proven true against the disbelievers—that they will be the inmates of the Fire” (40:6). The decree is not distributed at random across humanity. It is applied to a defined condition, and the condition is visible in the verses immediately before it. That specificity makes the verse useful rather than merely grim. A decree attached to a condition can be avoided by avoiding the condition, and the condition is one any reader can check: saying the right thing and doing the opposite.

**A Statement, Not a Sentence Pronounced in Anger**

The tone of the verse is flat, and the flatness is deliberate. There is no threat, no description of punishment, no rhetorical question. A fact is reported in the same register in which one reports that a term has arrived. The restraint is what makes the verse severe: it treats the outcome as unremarkable, already known, and beyond negotiation. The reader is left with the only question that remains useful — whether the condition described in the previous verses applies to him. The verse does not ask it, and it does not need to. The decree has been proven true against a named class, and the class is defined by conduct rather than by identity. Nothing here invites despair, and nothing offers comfort either. A fact is stated about a category, and the reader is left to determine whether he is standing inside it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:34

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Can any of your associate-gods originate creation and then resurrect it?” Say, “˹Only˺ Allah originates creation and then resurrects it. How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?” 3) Who Guides?

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Answered by Silence**

The question is put to the associate-gods through their worshippers, and no answer comes. The verse does not report a reply as the previous verse did; it moves directly to the Prophet's own statement, which supplies the answer the idols cannot give. The silence is the argument. A being asked whether it can originate creation and then bring it back has only two possible responses, and neither is available to a carved object: it cannot claim the power, and it cannot deny the question, because it cannot speak. The same incapacity is stated where the idols are addressed directly: “If you call upon them, they cannot hear your calls. And if they were to hear, they could not respond to you” (35:14). The verse therefore places the associate-gods in a position where their absence of function is demonstrated by the form of the question itself. The Prophet's own answer, supplied immediately, is also a courtesy. It stops the silence being read as a trick, and it states the position openly so that the audience can disagree with a claim rather than with a pause.

**Origination and Return as One Act**

The two verbs are joined, and the joining is the substance of the argument. Originating creation is the first act; bringing it back is the second, and the Qur'an treats the second as the easier of the two. The claim is stated in the sūrah's own earlier words: “Indeed, He originates the creation then resurrects it so that He may justly reward those who believe and do good” (10:4). The same pairing appears with the inference drawn: “He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And He gives life to the earth after its death. And so will you be brought forth ˹from the grave˺” (30:19). By asking about both acts in one question, the verse closes the escape route of a lesser divinity. A being that could make something but not restore it would be a partial cause; the question allows no partial claim, because the two acts are attributed to the same agent in the same breath. The pairing also answers the objection that resurrection is a different kind of act from creation. It is not; it is the same act performed on the same material, and both belong to whoever performed the first.

**“How Can You Then Be Deluded?”**

The closing question is a fixed formula, and it appears wherever creative power has just been described. “Indeed, Allah is the One Who causes seeds and fruit stones to sprout. He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. That is Allah! How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺?” (6:95). The same phrase closes 35:3 and 40:62. Its recurrence is not repetition for emphasis; it marks a consistent exegetical move. Once the creative act is established, the question of how anyone could be diverted becomes the only question left. The diversion is not explained by evidence, since the evidence has been conceded. The formula therefore names the phenomenon the whole passage is investigating — a correct belief accompanied by an inconsistent practice — and leaves it standing as something that requires explanation from the person doing it. The formula's recurrence across the Qur'an shows a settled move rather than a local argument. Wherever creative power is established the question follows, because the inconsistency it names is the same in every case.

**Why the Physical Argument Comes First**

This verse concerns bodies; the one that follows concerns guidance. The order is deliberate and it follows the order of a person's own existence. He is created before he is guided, and a claim about who made him is more immediately testable than a claim about who directs him. The Qur'an consistently argues from the visible act to the invisible relation, and it does so because the visible act cannot be disputed by the person who is looking at it. Nobody in the audience claimed that an idol produced him or that an idol would raise him. The argument is therefore built on ground the audience already occupies, and the following verse can move to guidance because the question of origination has been settled by their own silence. The order also mirrors the reader's own case: he exists before he believes, and the argument that reaches him most directly is the one about his existence.

**The Question Exposes a Category Error**

The phrase “your associate-gods” is used ironically, and the irony is the point of the question. The audience's theology treated these beings as lesser powers within a hierarchy — partners with a share, intermediaries with influence. The question asks whether they perform the defining act of divinity, and the answer reveals that they do not occupy a lower rung of the same order. They are not in the order at all. A partner who cannot create is not a smaller creator; he is not a creator. The verse therefore dismantles the pantheon not by attacking its members but by applying a test that the category itself cannot survive. What remains after the test is a single agent and a number of objects, and the objects are where the audience's own answer has already placed them. The verse's economy is its strength. It does not argue against the pantheon; it applies one test the pantheon cannot pass, and a test the audience has already answered elsewhere.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:35

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Can any of your associate-gods guide to the truth?” Say, “˹Only˺ Allah guides to the truth.” Who then is more worthy to be followed: the One Who guides to the truth or those who cannot find the way unless guided? What is the matter with you? How do you judge

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Bodies to Souls**

The previous verse asked about origination and resurrection; this one asks about guidance. The shift is from what a person is made of to where he is going, and the sūrah makes the two questions consecutive for a reason. A being that cannot produce a body plainly cannot direct a soul, but the second question is not simply the first repeated. Guidance is the activity that religion exists for. Provision, senses, life and death are shared with animals; guidance is what distinguishes a responsible creature. By moving the argument here, the verse addresses the one function the idolaters might have tried to preserve for their gods — not creation, which they never claimed for them, but influence over direction, favour and access. Attacking that last claim is the harder task, because influence cannot be tested as directly as the making of a body. The verse meets the difficulty by changing the test rather than the subject.

**Guidance to the Truth Belongs to God Alone**

The answer is given before the audience can respond, and it is exclusive: Allah guides to the truth. The Qur'an states the same exclusivity in the Prophet's instructed speech: “Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘Allah's guidance is the ˹only˺ true guidance. And we are commanded to submit to the Lord of all worlds’” (6:71), and it describes the mechanism: “But We have made it a light, by which We guide whoever We will of Our servants. And you are truly leading ˹all˺ to the Straight Path” (42:52). The object of the guidance is specified as the truth — *al-ḥaqq* — which is the same word used of God in the previous verse. Guidance to the truth is therefore not guidance to a set of preferences; it is direction toward what is actually the case, and only the One who is the truth can supply it. The wording also excludes a common misunderstanding. Guidance to the truth is not persuasion, encouragement, or the supply of motives; it is direction toward what is actually the case, and that requires knowledge of the case.

**The Comparison That Settles the Question**

The verse puts the two candidates side by side and asks which deserves to be followed. On one side stands the One who guides to the truth. On the other stands one who cannot guide unless he is himself guided. The description of the second candidate is devastating in its plainness. The object of worship is not merely less powerful; it is dependent. It needs direction in order to have any, and something that requires guidance cannot be the source of it. The comparison does not require the audience to accept a theology. It requires him to notice that a follower who cannot find his own way is not a guide, and that following him is not a lesser form of the same activity but a different activity entirely — following nobody. The comparison is drawn in ordinary terms for that reason. Nobody needs theology to see that a lost man cannot give directions, and the verse is content to leave the matter at that level of plainness.

**“What Is the Matter With You? How Do You Judge?”**

The two closing questions are addressed to the reasoning rather than to the belief. *Famā lakum kayfa taḥkumūn* — what is wrong with you, how do you judge? The rebuke is not that they lack information; the preceding verses established that they have it. It is that their judgment does not follow from what they know. The same rebuke appears where the same inconsistency is described: “And if you ask them who created the heavens and the earth, they will definitely say, ‘Allah!’ Say, ‘Praise be to Allah!’ In fact, most of them do not know” (31:25). The verse treats the failure as a failure of inference. A person who says “Allah” to four questions about creation and then directs his worship elsewhere has made a judgment, and the judgment does not match the answers. Asking how he judges is asking him to show his working, and no working exists. The double question is also a kindness. It lets the hearer notice the gap himself instead of being told about it, and noticing it is the first step toward closing it.

**A Test for Everything That Is Followed**

The criterion the verse supplies applies beyond stone idols. Anything followed is being treated as a guide, and the question is whether it can guide or whether it needs guidance itself. The Qur'an names the most common substitute: “Have you seen ˹O Prophet˺ the one who has taken their own desires as their god? Will you then be a keeper over them?” (25:43). A desire is the clearest case of something that cannot guide unless guided — it moves, it changes, it requires direction from something else, and it is followed anyway. Customs, inherited opinions and the opinions of respected people fall under the same test. The verse's final questions are therefore addressed to every reader who follows anything, and the test is single and simple: does this thing know the way, or is it looking for it too? The verse leaves the answer with the reader, and it does not need to supply it. Anything that shifts with mood, fashion or appetite is looking for the way, and every reader knows which of the things he follows behaves that way.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:36

> Most of them follow nothing but ˹inherited˺ assumptions. ˹And˺ surely assumptions can in no way replace the truth. Allah is indeed All-Knowing of what they do

**Expanded Commentary**

**Assumption Named as the Thing They Follow**

The verse identifies what the audience is actually following, and the word is *ẓann* — assumption, conjecture, a belief held without knowledge. The verse is not accusing them of stupidity; it is describing an epistemic state. They are not following a demonstrated position or a refuted one. They are following something they have never examined. The same diagnosis is applied to the naming of the gods: “These ˹idols˺ are mere names that you and your forefathers have made up—a practice Allah has never authorized. They follow nothing but ˹inherited˺ assumptions and whatever ˹their˺ souls desire, although ˹true˺ guidance has already come to them from their Lord” (53:23). The pairing of assumption and desire in that verse is exact, and it explains the mechanism. An unexamined belief is held because it is wanted, and it is wanted because it is comfortable, and it stays unexamined for the same reason. The verse names the state rather than arguing with it, which is the right approach. A person holding an unexamined belief cannot be refuted, because there is no position to refute — only a habit. What can be done is to point at the habit and name it. Naming it is not a small thing either. A habit that has never been looked at cannot be answered, and the first act of examination is to see that there is something there to examine.

**“Most of Them” — a Qualifier That Leaves a Door**

The verse says most, not all, and the restriction is significant. The audience is not written off as a block. Some of them are people whose assumptions will be replaced by conviction, and the verse four places later says so explicitly: some will believe in it, others will not. The qualifier also protects the argument from an easy escape. If every hearer were following mere conjecture, the person who disagrees could dismiss the whole description as applying to somebody else. By saying most, the verse puts each reader in the position of asking which group he is in — and that question cannot be answered by assumption, since assumption is precisely what is under examination. The restriction is also historically accurate. This sūrah's audience included men who later became its most reliable transmitters, and men who fought it to the end. The division did not follow the lines the audience would have predicted — not clan, wealth or age.

**Assumption Cannot Outweigh Truth**

The middle clause states a rule: assumption avails nothing against the truth. The formulation is deliberately absolute. It does not say assumption is weak or that it usually loses; it says it contributes nothing to the contest. The reason is that the two are not comparable quantities. Truth is a matter of what is the case; assumption is a matter of what is supposed. Confidence, numbers, antiquity and social weight can make an assumption durable, and none of them makes it true. The verse therefore removes the three defences available to an inherited belief — that everyone holds it, that it has always been held, and that it feels secure — by pointing out that none of the three is evidence. What is left of the belief after that observation is exactly what the verse says it was: a supposition. The clause also explains why the Qur'an argues at all. If assumptions were weightless, no argument would be needed; if they carried weight, no argument would suffice. They are neither. They are durable, and durability is what argument addresses.

**The Inheritance Mechanism**

The assumption described here is not privately generated; it is received. The word used in the parallel verse makes the transmission explicit — the names are ones “you and your forefathers have made up” (53:23). Inheritance is the most efficient way for a belief to avoid examination, because it arrives before the person is able to examine anything and it comes bundled with loyalty to the people he loves. Rejecting it feels like rejecting them. That is why the verse is severe about it. The assumption is not innocent just because it was inherited; the person holding it is capable of asking where it came from, and the asking is the whole of what is required to break the chain. The Qur'an's repeated references to forefathers are not an attack on ancestors but on the substitution of lineage for evidence. The mechanism deserves its precise name, because it is still operative. A belief received in childhood arrives attached to affection, and examining it feels like a betrayal of the people who gave it. The verse treats that difficulty as real and asks the question anyway.

**Knowledge as the Closing Word**

The verse ends with a statement about God's knowledge of what they do, and the statement functions as a warning. The exegetical tradition reads it that way: a threat addressed to people living on conjecture, telling them that their rejection and their conduct are fully known. The placement matters. After a verse that denies any value to assumption, the last word belongs to the One who knows everything — which is the sharpest possible contrast. Their position rests on what they do not know; His on what He does. The verse therefore closes the argumentative section of the passage on a note of exposure rather than of conclusion. Nothing has been hidden from the account, and the assumption that made the account bearable is the thing being taken away. The verse's three clauses form a complete diagnosis: what they follow, what following it is worth, and who is watching. Nothing in that diagnosis depends on the reader having accepted the rest of the chapter.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:37

> It is not ˹possible˺ for this Quran to have been produced by anyone other than Allah. In fact, it is a confirmation of what came before, and an explanation of the Scripture. It is, without a doubt, from the Lord of all worlds

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Negative That States a Fact About the Book**

The verse opens with an impossibility. *Mā kāna hādhā l-qurʾānu an yuftarā min dūni llāh* — this Qur'an could not have been fabricated apart from God. The construction is not a prohibition but a denial of possibility, and it is stated before any evidence is offered in the verse itself. The evidence has been accumulating since earlier in the chapter: the messenger cannot edit the recitation (10:15), he lived forty years among his audience before it and never read or wrote (10:16), and the demand that he produce a different book met a refusal grounded in his own lack of authority. This verse draws the conclusion those facts support. The negative is also a claim about the book rather than about the man. What makes fabrication impossible is not the Prophet's honesty alone but the nature of what he brought — a text whose character no human being in that society could have produced. The negative is also a challenge in disguise. To say that fabrication is impossible is to invite the attempt, and the following verse issues the invitation openly.

**A Confirmation of What Came Before**

The positive half of the verse begins with continuity. The Qur'an confirms the scriptures that preceded it, and the claim is made repeatedly: “Believe in My revelations which confirm your Scriptures. Do not be the first to deny them” (2:41); “He has revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book in truth, confirming what came before it, as He revealed the Torah and the Gospel” (3:3). The confirmation is not a quotation but a corroboration — the same core teaching about one God, prophecy, judgment and accountability, arriving again in a new language to a new people. The claim is also a testable one in its context. Mecca was not ignorant of the earlier revelations; it traded with Byzantium and Abyssinia and hosted Jews and Christians in the peninsula. A book that contradicted the substance of those scriptures would have been identified quickly, and the verse invites the comparison rather than avoiding it. Confirmation is also a claim about this book's contents. A text that disagreed with the Torah and the Gospel on monotheism, prophecy and judgment would not have survived contact with the communities holding them, and such communities were present in the peninsula.

**An Explanation of the Scripture**

The second description is *tafṣīl al-kitāb* — a detailed exposition of the Book. The phrase is read by most commentators as referring to the Qur'an's own contents: the laws, beliefs and rulings set out in detail, as against a general revelation that requires elaboration. The Qur'an claims the same function where its own status is asserted: “This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺” (2:2). Detail is what makes a revelation usable. A general affirmation of monotheism can be agreed with by almost anyone; a text that specifies how to pray, what to eat, how to divide an inheritance and what to do when a contract is disputed is a different kind of thing, and it is exposed to checking in a way that generalities are not. Detail is also what turns a revelation into a law. A general call to worship one God produces agreement; a specified code produces a community. The claim to explain the Book is a claim to be lived by rather than admired. The verse therefore claims two things that pull in opposite directions — continuity with the past and specificity in the present — and holds both.

**“From the Lord of All Worlds”**

The final clause names the source, and the name chosen is deliberate. *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* — Lord of all worlds — is the phrase that opens the Qur'an's first chapter (1:2), and it denotes a lordship not confined to a tribe, a land or a sanctuary. Mecca's religious economy rested on a local claim: this house, these gods, this city's standing among the Arabs. A book that arrives in the name of the Lord of all worlds is a direct challenge to that arrangement, and the phrase does the challenging without argument. It also explains the scope of the confirmation mentioned earlier. A revelation from the Lord of all worlds would be expected to corroborate what the same Lord sent elsewhere, and the verse states that it does. The phrase also closes a circle inside this sūrah. The chapter opened by calling its verses the verses of a Book rich in wisdom; the argument closes by naming the source of that wisdom.

**The Cumulative Case**

Read in sequence, the verses from 15 to 38 form one argument with stages. The messenger cannot alter the text; he cannot therefore author it. He lived among his audience for a lifetime before it, known for truthfulness and unable to read; he cannot therefore have prepared it. The audience says he fabricated it; the verse denies the possibility and offers the test — produce a chapter like it. Nothing in this verse asks the reader to accept a claim on authority. Each stage relies on a fact the audience already grants, and the conclusion is the only one left standing when the alternatives have been excluded. That is why the verse can be so direct. It asks not for assent to a mystery but for acceptance of an inference the reader has already half made, and the half already made is where the argument does its work.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:38

> Or do they claim, “He made it up!”? Tell them ˹O Prophet˺, “Produce one sûrah like it then, and seek help from whoever you can—other than Allah—if what you say is true!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge in Its Plainest Form**

The verse takes the accusation seriously and answers it with a test. If the Qur'an was invented by a man, then men can invent something like it, and the test is set at the smallest unit available: one chapter. The same challenge appears in four places with varying scope. “And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true” (2:23). The demand is escalated elsewhere to ten chapters: “Or do they say, ‘He has fabricated this ˹Quran˺!’? Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘Produce ten fabricated sûrahs like it and seek help from whoever you can —other than Allah—if what you say is true!’” (11:13). It is escalated again to the whole of creation: “If ˹all˺ humans and jinn were to come together to produce the equivalent of this Quran, they could not produce its equal, no matter how they supported each other” (17:88). And it is reduced to its barest form: “Let them then produce something like it, if what they say is true!” (52:34). The variation in scope also removes an objection about difficulty. A challenge tailored to one audience could be dismissed as calibrated; one that ranges from a single chapter to all humanity and the jinn cannot be.

**One Chapter Is the Minimum, and It Is Small**

The unit chosen is significant. A chapter is not a book; the shortest in the Qur'an runs to a few lines. The challenge does not ask for a comparable work, a comparable corpus, or a comparable lifetime of output. It asks for one chapter — which means the claim of fabrication can be tested by anyone who can compose Arabic prose at all. That is a demanding audience in seventh-century Arabia, where poetry was the recognised measure of a man and where compositions were memorised, compared and ranked publicly. The challenge was addressed to people whose professional competence was language, in their own idiom, using their own unit of measurement. No concession was made for difficulty. The unit also has a practical advantage: a chapter is a complete composition with its own structure and closure, so the test concerns a whole discourse rather than a line of eloquence.

**“Seek Help From Whoever You Can”**

The concession of assistance removes the last excuse. The challengers are not required to work alone; they may gather poets, scribes, orators, and — as the parallel verse makes explicit — the whole of humankind and the jinn (17:88). The phrase “other than Allah” is the only exclusion, and it is pointed: the help may come from anywhere except the One whose speech is being imitated. The historical record is that the challenge was not met. The Quraysh had every motive to meet it — their sanctuary, their trade, their ancestors' religion and their social standing were all under attack by this book — and they had the linguistic resources of the most verbally accomplished society in the region. What they produced instead were counter-accusations: magic, poetry, madness, stories of the ancients. Each of those is an alternative explanation, and each concedes the point the challenge was designed to establish. The absence of an attempt is itself evidence. A society that ranked its poets publicly did not lack candidates willing to try, and the motive to succeed was as strong as any motive has ever been. A single successful imitation would have ended the matter, and every interest in Mecca was served by producing one.

**“If What You Say Is True”**

The condition is attached to the challenge, and it ties the test to the specific claim. The accusation was fabrication — that a man made this up. A fabrication has an author, and an author has a capacity. The verse asks the accusers to demonstrate that the capacity exists in human beings by exercising it. The condition is therefore not a rhetorical flourish; it is the logical structure of the answer. If the claim is true, the test can be passed. If the test cannot be passed, the claim is unsupported. Nothing in the verse requires the accuser to accept anything beforehand, and nothing in it allows him to escape by changing the subject — which is what the historical responses did. The condition protects the challenger as well. He is not asked to prove a negative but to perform an act, and the act is within his power if the claim is true.

**Why the Test Remains Open**

The challenge is addressed to a specific audience in a specific century, and it has never been formally closed. The Arabic of the Qur'an remains available, the shortest chapter remains a few lines long, and the resources of the language are greater now than they were in Mecca. The verse's form — an imperative with a condition — does not expire. That is what distinguishes this argument from the miracles attributed to earlier prophets, which were witnessed once by people present and are now matters of report. This one can be attempted by anyone who doubts, at any time, and the attempt is the test. The verse's brevity is therefore not economy of words but economy of requirements: one chapter, any help you like, and the truth of your own claim as the stake. That openness is unusual among religious claims. Most rest on testimony that cannot be re-examined; this one rests on a test anyone who doubts can repeat.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:39

> In fact, they ˹hastily˺ rejected the Book without comprehending it and before the fulfilment of its warnings. Similarly, those before them were in denial. See then what was the end of the wrongdoers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Rejection That Precedes Understanding**

The verse describes a sequence, and the sequence is the diagnosis. The rejection came first; comprehension never arrived. The word *bal* at the opening — in fact, rather — signals a correction of what the audience would say about itself. They would claim to have examined the Qur'an and found it wanting. The verse says they did not examine it. The same observation is attributed to ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib in a line the exegetes quote here: people are the enemies of what they do not know. The remark is not an insult but a description of a mechanism. Hostility arrives before knowledge, and once hostility is in place the work of knowing feels like a concession to an opponent. The verse does not soften the description, and it does not need to; the haste is visible in the sequence itself. The mechanism has a second stage. Once hostility is declared publicly, changing one's mind costs standing, and the cost rises with every argument won against the book. Refusal becomes an investment, and investments get defended. The verse is describing a cost rather than a conviction, and the cost is paid in the currency of reputation, which is why it is heaviest for the people with most to lose.

**“Before the Fulfilment of Its Warnings”**

The second half of the clause names a specific difficulty. The Qur'an contains statements about the future — promises, threats, the Hour, the return, the outcomes of belief and denial — and at the moment of rejection none of them had happened. The audience was asked to accept a book whose predictions were still pending. The phrase *taʾwīl* here carries that sense: the eventual working-out of what the text said. The verse identifies this as a real obstacle and then notes that it does not justify the rejection, since the alternative was to wait. A person who rejects a claim about the future before it can be tested has not tested it; he has declined to. The verse treats the pending predictions honestly. It does not claim they have been fulfilled, and it does not ask the reader to pretend otherwise. It asks him to notice that a refusal issued before the test is not a result of the test. The same posture is described where the sceptics demand the Hour be brought forward, and the Qur'an's answer throughout is that the timing is not the hearer's to set.

**“Similarly, Those Before Them Were in Denial”**

The verse places its audience inside a pattern, and the pattern is what makes the warning concrete. Denial is not new; it has a documented history with documented outcomes. The same injunction to look at that history appears repeatedly: “Similar situations came to pass before you, so travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers” (3:137); “So We seized him and his soldiers, casting them into the sea. See then what was the end of the wrongdoers!” (28:40); “Have the deniers not travelled through the land to see what was the end of those ˹destroyed˺ before them?” (12:109). The comparison is not a threat of identical punishment. It is a statement that the same choice has been made before by people who were stronger and better provided for, and that the outcome is a matter of record rather than of speculation. The comparison also removes the comfort of uniqueness. Every generation assumes its situation is unprecedented; the verse supplies the list of precedents and leaves the reader to find his place in it.

**“See Then What Was the End of the Wrongdoers”**

The closing imperative is one of the Qur'an's most repeated commands, and its repetition is a method. The Qur'an does not ask its audience to accept a theory of history; it asks them to travel and look at the ruins. The injunction is applied to deniers, to the guilty, to the corrupt, to the schemers and to those who mocked the warners, and it appears in more than a dozen places. The method is empirical in a literal sense: the evidence is physical, visible to a caravan, and independent of anyone's testimony. A Meccan trader passing the remains of a destroyed settlement was looking at the argument. The verse's final sentence is therefore not a flourish but a direction — go and see — and it assumes the reader can. The assumption is not trivial. It means the evidence is public and durable, surviving long enough for later generations to inspect it, which is why the command can be issued to people who were not alive when the events happened.

**The Haste Is the Point**

What the verse returns to is speed. The rejection was hasty, and the haste is not incidental to the failure; it is the failure. A book that requires reading was refused without reading. Predictions that required waiting were rejected without waiting. A pattern that required looking at was described without looking. Every element of the diagnosis concerns time that was not spent. The verse therefore does not argue with the audience's conclusion so much as with their method, and the method is one any reader can check in himself. The question it leaves is not whether the Qur'an is true but whether the person denying it has done anything that would qualify as finding out. That question is addressed to the reader directly, and it can be answered honestly in a moment. The verse's severity is proportionate to the ease of the answer.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:40

> Some of them will ˹eventually˺ believe in it; others will not. And your Lord knows best the corruptors

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Division Inside One Audience**

The verse reports a split, and the split runs through a single group rather than between two groups. Some of them will believe in it, others will not — the same audience, hearing the same recitation, arriving at opposite outcomes. The commentators read the first class as those among the Quraysh who rejected the message at the time and later accepted it, and the second as those who never would. History bore that out. Men who had argued against the Prophet ﷺ for two decades — including Abū Sufyān b. Ḥarb, whose testimony about him before Heraclius is recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 7 — entered Islam at the conquest of Mecca, while others who had heard the same verses from the beginning died without accepting them. The verse's prediction was therefore testable within the lifetime of its audience, and it was tested.

**“In It” — the Book or the Messenger**

The pronoun can refer to the Qur'an or to the Prophet, and both readings have been recorded: some will believe in it, or some will believe in him. The difference is small and the substance is the same, since belief in the messenger is belief in what he brought. What matters is that the object of belief is fixed and unchanged. The recitation does not alter between the two groups; nobody is promised a version better suited to him. Both readings of the pronoun agree on that point: the object of faith is fixed, nobody is invited to believe a modified claim, and nobody is offered a lower standard than his neighbour. The same words produce conviction in one person and refusal in another, which locates the variable in the hearer rather than in the text. The verse is therefore a statement about the audience that doubles as an answer to the charge of the previous verses — that the book was rejected without being comprehended. Comprehension was available, and some of them took it.

**God Knows Best the Corruptors**

The closing clause names a knowledge that exceeds the audience's own. The Prophet is being told that his Lord knows best who the corruptors are — those who spread disorder, who actively work against the message rather than merely declining it. The statement answers a practical difficulty. From the outside, the two groups look identical: both are currently rejecting, both argue, both remain in Mecca. The distinction between a person who is struggling toward belief and one who is organising resistance is invisible to everyone except the One who sees the interior. The verse places that knowledge where it belongs and relieves the messenger of the task of judging it. He is not required to sort his audience; he is required to address it. The relief is practical as well as theological. A preacher who tries to judge interiors will despair of the wrong people and waste effort on the right ones. The verse assigns the sorting to the One who can see it, and the address to the one who can deliver it. The word for corruptors is specific too: it does not cover everyone who declines, but those who actively spread disorder.

**The Argument Closes Without a Verdict on Individuals**

The verses from 31 to 39 form the chapter's argumentative core — the questions about provision, senses, life and guidance, the identification of the true Lord, the challenge to produce a chapter. This verse closes that section, and it does so without naming anyone as lost. The decree mentioned earlier applied to a class defined by conduct; this verse states that the class has members and that their membership is known. The effect is to leave the argument standing and the individuals open. Nobody in the audience is told that his case is finished, and nobody is told that it is settled in his favour. The verse ends the debate at exactly the point where further argument would be useless, because what remains is not a question of proof. That is the right place to stop. An argument carried past the point of decision ceases to be an argument and becomes pressure, and this sūrah's method throughout has been to state the case and leave the response free.

**What the Split Teaches the Reader**

The most useful feature of the verse is that it makes the outcome a matter of the hearer's own doing. Two people with the same evidence, the same upbringing, the same social pressure and the same text arrive in different places. The verse does not explain the difference by reference to the book, to the messenger, or to the quality of the argument — all of which were common to both. It leaves the difference where the reader can examine it: in the disposition each person brought to the hearing. That is an uncomfortable conclusion, because it removes every external excuse. The recitation is not obscure; the messenger is not unknown; the challenge was not hidden. Some believed it, and their belief is the evidence that believing was possible. The verse is therefore the chapter's quietest and sharpest statement about responsibility. It blames neither the book, nor the messenger, nor the audience's circumstances. It records a split, states that the split was known in advance, and leaves each hearer to work out which side of it he is on. The verse also disposes of a common explanation for unbelief — that the message was unclear, badly delivered, or aimed at the wrong people. On this account none of that was true, and the same recitation produced both outcomes in the same city.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:41

> If they deny you, then say, “My deeds are mine and your deeds are yours. You are free of what I do and I am free of what you do!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Separation, Not a Curse**

The verse instructs the Prophet ﷺ in what to say if his audience rejects him, and the words contain no threat. There is no invocation of punishment, no prediction of ruin, no appeal to vengeance. What there is, is a declaration of separate responsibility: my deeds belong to me, yours belong to you. The same instruction appears where the Prophet is told how to answer disobedience among his own family: “But if they disobey you, say, ‘I am certainly free of what you do’” (26:216), and it becomes the closing line of the shortest sūrah in the Qur'an: “You have your way, and I have my Way” (109:6). The formula is not a withdrawal of concern. It is a statement about the structure of accountability, made at the point where argument has run out, and it is stated without bitterness — which is itself instructive about how a rejected messenger is to speak.

**The Formula of Separate Accounts**

The wording has a settled form across the Qur'an, and the form is legal. “Say, ‘Would you dispute with us about Allah, while He is our Lord and your Lord? We are accountable for our deeds and you for yours’” (2:139). The same clause appears in the Prophet's instructed address to the People of the Scripture: “Allah is our Lord and your Lord. We will be accountable for our deeds and you for yours. There is no ˹need for˺ contention between us. Allah will gather us together ˹for judgment˺” (42:15). Three features recur in every instance. The Lordship is shared, so the dispute is not about which God exists. The accounts are separate, so nobody carries another's case. And the gathering is common, so the separation is temporary rather than final. The verse is therefore not a statement of permanent rupture; it is a statement about who answers for what, made in advance of the hearing.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

It does not say the Prophet stops. The separation of accounts is not the end of the mission, and the verses around this one make that plain. The duty is described elsewhere in the narrowest terms: “The Messenger's duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. And Allah ˹fully˺ knows what you reveal and what you conceal” (5:99), and “Whether We show you ˹O Prophet˺ some of what We threaten them with, or cause you to die ˹before that˺, your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. Judgment is for Us” (13:40). The declaration in this verse is about responsibility, not about effort. A person who says “your deeds are yours” is not saying “your case does not matter”; he is saying that the case will not be carried by him on the Day, and that no amount of urgency on his part changes that. The distinction matters, because a messenger who believed he carried his audience's account would either coerce them or despair.

**Peace as the Response to Hostility**

The Qur'an supplies the manner in which this separation is to be expressed, and it is specific. Describing those who hear slanderous talk, it says: “they turn away from it, saying, ‘We are accountable for our deeds and you for yours. Peace ˹is our only response˺ to you! We want nothing to do with those who act ignorantly’” (28:55). The formula of separate accounts is accompanied by a greeting of peace and a refusal to engage with ignorance on its own terms. That combination is the verse's practical content for anyone who has argued a serious matter to exhaustion. The account is separated, the greeting is kept civil, and the argument is dropped — not because it was lost but because it has been made, and repeating it serves nothing. The verse is a rule of conduct for the defeated argument, which is the situation most people find hardest to handle well.

**The Argument Closes and the Waiting Begins**

This verse sits at the end of the chapter's long argumentative section. The questions about provision and creation have been asked, the challenge to produce a chapter has been issued, the split in the audience has been named. What remains is not more argument. The verse supplies the sentence that ends a discussion without ending a relationship: the accounts are separate, and the gathering will settle them. Nothing in it forecloses the possibility that the person being addressed will change; the following verses continue to describe those who listen and those who look, which is language addressed to people still in range of the message. The declaration is a boundary, not a door shut.

**Separation as a Form of Respect**

There is a further point in the wording that is easy to pass over. To say “your deeds are yours” is to treat the other person as a responsible agent. It refuses two things at once: the claim to carry his case, and the claim to dismiss him as incapable of having one. The verse does not describe the audience as fools, children, or objects to be managed. It states that they hold an account and will answer for it, which is a serious view of them. Coercion would be a lower estimate, since it assumes that a person's position can be set from outside; indifference would be lower still, since it assumes the position does not matter. Separation of accounts sits between the two, and it is the only posture that keeps both truths in place — that the disagreement is grave and that the other party is free. The verse's calm is therefore not coldness. It is the tone of someone who takes his opponents seriously enough to let them be wrong on their own account.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:42

> Some of them listen to what you say, but can you make the deaf hear even though they do not understand

**Expanded Commentary**

**Listening Without Hearing**

The verse describes a real situation: there are people present while the Qur'an is being recited, and they are listening. Nothing in the description suggests they are absent or pretending. The problem is not attendance but comprehension, and the Qur'an describes the mechanism elsewhere in physiological language: “There are some of them who ˹pretend to˺ listen to your recitation ˹of the Quran˺, but We have cast veils over their hearts—leaving them unable to comprehend it—and deafness in their ears. Even if they were to see every sign, they still would not believe in them” (6:25). The veil is over the heart, and the deafness is in the ear, and the two are linked: the ear receives and the heart does not process. That is a precise account of what happens when a person hears a sentence and nothing arrives. The words are audible; their meaning does not reach the place where meaning would change anything.

**A Question About the Prophet's Limits**

The second half is a question addressed to the Prophet, and its expected answer is no. “Can you make the deaf hear, or guide the blind or those clearly astray?” (43:40). The same question recurs with the metaphor extended: “You certainly cannot make the dead hear ˹the truth˺. Nor can you make the deaf hear the call when they turn their backs and walk away” (27:80; 30:52). The rhetorical function is to fix a boundary. The Prophet's competence extends to delivery; it does not extend to producing the response. The verse is not doubting his ability as a speaker — the challenge to produce a chapter like this one rests on exactly that ability — but it distinguishes clearly between addressing a person and changing him. The first is his task and he performs it. The second is not his, and no exertion on his part will supply it.

**Deafness as a Condition of the Will**

The metaphor is not about the body. Nobody in the audience had a hearing impairment, and the verse's force depends on that. The deafness described is a refusal to process, and the Qur'an locates it precisely: it is the hearts in the chests that go blind, not the eyes (22:46). The same verse names the cure for the condition and it is not information — “Have they not travelled throughout the land so their hearts may reason, and their ears may listen?” (22:46). Travel is prescribed because it disrupts habit, and habit is what the deafness consists of. The verse therefore identifies an obstacle that evidence cannot remove. A person who will not process a statement is not a person who lacks the statement, and adding statements to his supply does not address the problem. What addresses it is a change in the disposition, and the verse leaves that where it belongs.

**What the Messenger Can and Cannot Do**

The boundary drawn here is stated elsewhere as a definition of the office. “The Messenger's duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺” (5:99), and the same limitation appears where the outcome is placed entirely outside his control: “Whether We show you ˹O Prophet˺ some of what We threaten them with, or cause you to die ˹before that˺, your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. Judgment is for Us” (13:40). The pairing of duty and limitation is what makes the office bearable. A messenger told to deliver and nothing more can perform his task completely in a room where nobody is convinced. A messenger told to produce belief could never succeed, and his failure would be permanent. The verse's question is therefore not a criticism of the Prophet's method; it is the reason his method is delivery rather than persuasion by any means available.

**A Verse That Relieves the Messenger**

Read in context, this verse comes immediately after the declaration of separate accounts, and it does the same work from a different direction. The accounts are separate, and the deafness of the audience is not a debit in the messenger's ledger. The Qur'an returns to this point repeatedly because the burden of other people's belief is the heaviest thing a sincere preacher carries. Every earnest person who has tried to convince someone knows the specific pain of watching a true statement fail to land, and the temptation to conclude that the statement was badly made. The verse removes that inference. The failure is located in the hearer's disposition, the delivery is located in the speaker's duty, and the two are not measured by the same standard. What the verse leaves the Prophet with is his task, intact and completable.

**What the Verse Leaves the Listener**

The deafness described here is a disposition, and dispositions can change. The verse does not say these people are incapable of hearing; it says they are not hearing, and it asks whether the Prophet can make them. The distinction leaves something with the hearer. A person who cannot hear a recitation has no responsibility for missing it; a person who can hear it and is not processing it has the whole responsibility. The verse therefore does the opposite of excusing its audience. It locates the failure precisely where the audience can reach it, and it does so by describing the failure rather than by accusing anyone of it.
