The reader is left with a question that can be checked in a moment: when the words are read, does anything arrive? If nothing does, the verse has named the reason, and the reason is not in the words. The words remain available, and the hearing remains possible, which is why the verse is addressed to the reader rather than closed.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:43

> And some of them look at you, but can you guide the blind even though they cannot see

**Expanded Commentary**

**Looking Without Seeing**

The verse names the second channel of guidance and reports that it is also blocked. Some of them look at the Prophet — they see him, they have seen him for years, and looking is not the difficulty. The Qur'an states where the difficulty is: “Have they not travelled throughout the land so their hearts may reason, and their ears may listen? Indeed, it is not the eyes that are blind, but it is the hearts in the chests that grow blind” (22:46). The organ is functioning and the perception is not occurring. The verse's question — can you guide the blind? — is therefore not about physical sight. It is about the kind of blindness that consists in looking at evidence and not registering it as evidence, which is a disposition rather than a defect. That distinction is what makes the condition morally significant and what makes it possible to describe it as a failure rather than a limitation.

**The Pair: the Heard Word and the Seen Sign**

This verse and the one before it form a matched pair, and the pairing covers the two ways a revelation reaches a person. The message is heard, and the messenger is seen. Guidance arrives through speech and through the visible evidence of the speaker's life and conduct, and the Qur'an treats both as channels: “Nor are the dead and the living equal. Indeed, Allah ˹alone˺ makes whoever He wills hear” (35:22). The two verses together close both channels, and the closing is symmetrical — the deaf do not hear the recitation, the blind do not see the sign. That completeness is the point. It is not that one avenue was blocked and another remained open; the audience had every avenue available and used none of them. The verse's argument is therefore not that the evidence was insufficient but that the reception was absent, and the two verses establish this for both modes of reception.

**Guidance Cannot Be Transferred**

The question implies an answer about the nature of guidance: it is not something one person can hand to another. The Qur'an states the limit and its exception in the same sentence: “Nor can you lead the blind out of their misguidance. You can make none hear ˹the truth˺ except those who believe in Our revelations, ˹fully˺ submitting ˹to Allah˺” (27:81; 30:53). The exception is not a restriction imposed from outside; it is a description of where the address lands. A person already disposed to submit receives what is said; a person not so disposed does not, and no increase in the clarity of the speech changes that. Guidance is therefore a transaction requiring something from the recipient. The verse's rhetorical question makes the same point in negative form: a guide cannot lead someone who will not take a step, and the blindness described is the refusal to take one.

**The Sign They Watched for Twenty Years**

The object of their looking deserves attention. They are looking at a man they have known since childhood, whose honesty they had verified in commerce, and who now recites something they cannot match. The verse is therefore describing people in possession of an unusual piece of evidence: a long acquaintance with the character of the man making the claim. That evidence is not available to later generations in the same form, and the audience of this verse had it in full. Their blindness is consequently harder to explain than that of a distant sceptic, and the verse's question carries that difficulty. It is not asking whether a stranger could be convinced by a distant report. It is asking what could be said to a neighbour who has watched the speaker's whole life and still declines to draw the inference. The answer the verse gives is that nothing further can be said, and the reason is in the hearer.

**The Second Person Is Deliberate**

The question is addressed to the Prophet in the second person: can you guide the blind? The form is significant. A third-person statement — the blind cannot be guided — would be a fact about the audience. A second-person question makes it a fact about the limits of the one asking, and it does so gently. The Prophet is not told that he has failed; he is asked whether a task he was never given could have been performed by anyone. The same form is used where the limits of prophetic power are stated most plainly: “Say, ‘I have no power to benefit or protect myself, except by the Will of Allah’” (7:188). What the verse accomplishes is a transfer of the burden. The guidance of another person was never in his hands, and the verse says so in the shape of a question he can answer.

**Seeing Is a Discipline, Not an Accident**

The Qur'an does not treat sight as something that simply happens to a person with working eyes. It commands it. “Have they not travelled throughout the land so their hearts may reason, and their ears may listen?” (22:46). Travel is prescribed because it breaks the frame in which a person's assumptions were formed, and the breaking is what allows perception. The same command appears where the fate of earlier deniers is invoked: travel through the land and see what happened to them. Looking, in this vocabulary, is an act with a method — go, observe, compare, and let the heart reason about what the eyes have brought back. The people described in this verse are not failing to perform an involuntary function. They are declining a discipline, and the discipline is available to anyone willing to undertake the discomfort it requires. That is why the blindness is described as a condition of the heart rather than of the eye, and why it can be reversed by an act of attention.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:44

> Indeed, Allah does not wrong people in the least, but it is people who wrong themselves

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Principle Stated in a Single Line**

The verse is one sentence, and it settles a question that the surrounding verses raise. After two verses describing people who cannot be made to hear or see, the natural inference would be that their condition was imposed. The verse forecloses it. God does not wrong people at all; they wrong themselves. The same statement is made with a measure attached: “Indeed, Allah never wrongs ˹anyone˺—even by an atom's weight. And if it is a good deed, He will multiply it many times over and will give a great reward out of His grace” (4:40). The atom's weight is the smallest unit available in the language, and its use is a way of saying that the absence of injustice is not approximate. Nothing is deducted unfairly, nothing is added unfairly, and the asymmetry in that verse runs in the believer's favour: good is multiplied, evil is matched.

**A Formula Repeated Until It Becomes Doctrine**

The statement is not local to this verse. It appears as a refrain, and its repetition marks it as a settled principle rather than a passing claim. On the Day the wrongdoers will be told: “This is ˹the reward˺ for what your hands have done. And Allah is never unjust to ˹His˺ creation” (3:182; 8:51; 22:10). The same declaration closes a statement about the fixedness of the divine word: “My Word cannot be changed, nor am I unjust to ˹My˺ creation” (50:29), and it accompanies the rule that each deed returns on its doer: “Whoever does good, it is to their own benefit. And whoever does evil, it is to their own loss. Your Lord is never unjust to ˹His˺ creation” (41:46). The formula's placement is consistent: it appears wherever an outcome might look harsh, and it functions as a guarantee that the outcome has been measured. The verse here is the principle in its barest form, without the scene attached.

**Self-Wrong as the Real Category**

The second half of the verse names the actual offence, and the naming shifts the whole discussion. The wrong is not against God, who cannot be diminished by it; it is against the self. The Qur'an explains how this is possible in the verse about the trust: “Indeed, We offered the trust to the heavens and the earth and the mountains, but they ˹all˺ declined to bear it, being fearful of it. But humanity assumed it, ˹for˺ they are truly wrongful ˹to themselves˺ and ignorant ˹of the consequences˺” (33:72). The trust is responsibility, and the description of humanity in that verse is exactly the pair of qualities that produce self-wrong: boldness in accepting and ignorance of the cost. A person who takes on a responsibility without calculating what it requires will fail it, and the failure lands on him. The verse is therefore not making a theological abstraction. It is describing the ordinary way human beings damage themselves — by undertaking what they do not fully understand.

**No Coercion, So the Choice Is Real**

The principle stated here is what makes moral language meaningful. If God wronged people, their conduct would be produced rather than chosen, and reward and punishment would both be arbitrary. The verse's denial of divine injustice is therefore a denial of coercion, and the two are inseparable. The exegetical tradition states the point directly: because God is not unjust to people, He does not compel them to wrong actions, and when they wrong themselves they do so by their own choice. That is the necessary condition for everything else the sūrah has said — for the test described when the previous peoples were made successors, for the delay granted to those who plot, for the return and the informing. A test administered to a coerced subject is not a test. The verse supplies the premise that the whole structure requires, and it supplies it in eleven words.

**The Answer to the Chapter's Own Warnings**

Placed where it is, the verse performs a specific function. The sūrah has just described an audience that will not hear or see, and has warned repeatedly about the outcome of that condition. A reader could easily conclude that the outcome was arranged. The verse blocks that conclusion in both directions. God did not make them deaf and blind as a punishment; their deafness and blindness are what they did to themselves. And God will not punish them beyond what they earned; the measure is exact. The verse is therefore the sūrah's answer to the hardest question its own warnings provoke — whether the warnings are fair. The answer is that the injustice in the situation is entirely on one side, and it is not God's.

**The Principle Applied to the Reader**

Self-wrong is not an abstraction, and the verse's usefulness depends on its being diagnosable. A person wrongs himself whenever he spends a durable thing on a perishable one — attention on what will be forgotten, health on what will be regretted, a life on a position he will not be able to defend. The vocabulary the sūrah uses makes this concrete: transgression against one's own soul, enjoyment that is used up, a field cut down after its owners believed they controlled it. Each image names the same transaction from a different angle. The verse supplies the standard by which the transaction can be audited. Nothing is required from God's side for the audit to work, and nothing is concealed from it. The reader has the accounts, and the verse tells him who will read them and when. The practical question it leaves is not about God's justice but about the direction of one's own spending.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:45

> On the Day He will gather them, it will be as if they had not stayed ˹in the world˺ except for an hour of a day, ˹as though they were only˺ getting to know one another. Lost indeed will be those who denied the meeting with Allah, and were not ˹rightly˺ guided

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Lifetime Compressed Into an Hour**

The verse states a fact about how time will be experienced on the Day, and the compression is extreme. A whole life — its ambitions, its accumulation, its arguments — will feel as though it lasted an hour of a day. The same statement is made in several places with slightly different measures. “On the Day they see it, it will be as if they had stayed ˹in the world˺ no more than one evening or its morning” (79:46). “And on the Day the Hour will arrive, the wicked will swear that they did not stay ˹in this world˺ more than an hour. In this way they were always deluded ˹in the world˺” (30:55). The variation in the measures is instructive: an hour, an evening, a morning, ten days, a day. None of them is offered as a calculation; all of them describe the collapse of a duration into something negligible. The verse is not saying the life was short. It is saying it will be experienced as short, and the experience is the fact being reported.

**Getting to Know One Another**

The clause about acquaintance describes a scene of recognition. The gathered people turn to each other and are briefly aware of one another, the way travellers who have shared a road become aware of their companions when the journey ends. The same exchange appears in the questioning on the Day: “They will reply, ‘We remained ˹only˺ a day or part of a day. But ask those who kept count’” (23:113), and the answer given to them: “You only remained for a little while, if only you knew” (23:114). The reference to those who kept count is a small stroke of realism — in the confusion of the gathering, the people appeal to whoever might have kept better track. The verse's mention of acquaintance is therefore not decorative. It describes the social texture of the moment: strangers and enemies and neighbours, all reduced to the same condition and briefly aware of each other in it.

**Why the Compression Happens**

The collapse of duration is not a trick of perception but a consequence of comparison. Time is measured against what it is set beside, and a life set beside what follows it loses its scale. The verse's own earlier parables made the point from the other side: a garden at its peak, cut down as though it had never flourished yesterday. What is being described here is the same fact experienced from inside. The years that felt substantial while they were running are, at the end, an hour — not because they were empty but because everything they produced has been overtaken by something that does not end. The verse therefore teaches a proportion rather than a duration. It is addressed to people currently inside the hour, and its usefulness depends entirely on their being able to feel the comparison before the moment arrives.

**“Lost Indeed”**

The word for what happens to them is *khusr* — loss, the term of commerce. The verse is not describing suffering in the abstract; it is describing a transaction that ended in deficit. The same word is used where the outcome of denial is stated: those who denied the meeting are described as lost, and the loss is theirs rather than God's. The commercial language is consistent throughout this sūrah — deeds are earnings, transgression is a bad trade, the enjoyment of this world is a *matāʿ*, a consumable good. Within that vocabulary, loss has a precise meaning: the capital was spent and the return did not come. The verse's exclamation — lost indeed — carries the force of a verdict announced at the close of an account, and the account is the one the person kept himself.

**Denied the Meeting, and Were Not Guided**

The final clause gives two facts, and their order matters. They denied the meeting with God, and they were not guided. The meeting is the same expectation whose absence was diagnosed at the start of the chapter: those who do not expect to meet Us are content with this world and heedless of the signs. The denial of the meeting is therefore not a new fact but the original one, now shown at its terminus. The second clause — they were not rightly guided — is not presented as the cause of the first. The guidance was offered; the sūrah has spent forty verses offering it, in questions, parables and challenges. What was refused was not available afterwards, and the verse states the result without softening it. The exclamation at the end is the only emotional note in a passage that has otherwise been flat and procedural, and it is spent on the fact of the loss.

**The Hour, Seen From Inside**

The verse's compression has one use, and it is available now rather than later. A person inside the hour cannot feel its shortness; shortness is only visible from the far end, and by then nothing can be done with the observation. What the verse offers is the far end brought forward — a way of experiencing the scale of a life before the life is over. That is the whole function of eschatological language in the Qur'an: not to frighten but to resize. A reader who takes the verse seriously does not become morbid; he becomes proportionate, and proportion is what makes ordinary decisions reasonable. The hour is not a threat hanging over the reader. It is the condition he is already in, described from a vantage point he has not yet reached, and offered so that he can make his choices with the ending in view rather than against it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:46

> Whether We show you ˹O Prophet˺ some of what We threaten them with, or cause you to die ˹before that˺, to Us is their return and Allah is a Witness over what they do

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Possibilities, One Outcome**

The verse sets out two alternatives and shows that they converge. Either the Prophet lives to see part of what his audience has been threatened with, or he dies before it. In either case the return is to God, and the witnessing is His. The same sentence appears elsewhere with the messenger's duty attached: “So be patient ˹O Prophet˺. Surely Allah's promise is true. Whether We show you some of what We threaten them with, or cause you to die ˹before that˺, to Us they will ˹all˺ be returned” (40:77), and with the limit of the office attached: “Whether We show you ˹O Prophet˺ some of what We threaten them with, or cause you to die ˹before that˺, your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺. Judgment is for Us” (13:40). The three versions share a structure: an unresolved alternative about timing, and a fixed statement about the destination. The verse's purpose is to remove the timing from the list of things that need settling.

**Some of the Threats Were Fulfilled in His Lifetime**

The first alternative was not hypothetical. The Prophet ﷺ lived to see a portion of what the sūrah's warnings described. The exegetical tradition points to the Battle of Badr, fought in the second year after the migration, where a number of the men who had led the opposition in Mecca were killed and the balance of power in the peninsula changed. Later he entered Mecca itself, and the sanctuary that had been the centre of the opposition came under the authority of the message it had rejected. The verse therefore speaks from experience as much as from expectation. Some of the threatened outcome was visible, and the remainder was not; the verse treats both situations as equally complete, because neither the seeing nor the not-seeing changes where the matter ends.

**“To Us Is Their Return” — the Fixed Term**

The clause in the middle is the sūrah's recurring statement, and it appears here in its shortest form. The return has been named since the fourth verse — to Him is your return, all together — and the verse repeats it at the point where a messenger might reasonably want to know whether he will see the result. The answer is that the result does not depend on his seeing it. A person who measures the truth of a warning by whether he witnesses its fulfilment has made his own lifespan the criterion, and the verse withdraws that criterion. The return is not an event scheduled inside anyone's lifetime; it is the destination of everyone mentioned, including the one being addressed. What the Prophet sees or does not see is a detail of his own term, not a condition of theirs.

**“Allah Is a Witness Over What They Do”**

The verse closes on witnessing, and the word is the one used when the Prophet is instructed to stake his message on it: “Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, ‘Who is the best witness?’ Say, ‘Allah is! He is a Witness between me and you. And this Quran has been revealed to me so that, with it, I may warn you and whoever it reaches’” (6:19). The witnessing described here is not observation from a distance. It is the presence of a party to the transaction, the One who can testify between the messenger and his audience. For a man whose message is being called a fabrication, the fact that the Sender is a witness is the whole of his defence. The verse supplies it in the same breath as the uncertainty about timing, which is a deliberate pairing: the timing may be unknown, and the witnessing is not in doubt.

**A Verse That Settles a Messenger's Burden**

The pastoral function of the verse is easy to miss because its language is so flat. A preacher who has warned a people for years carries a specific question: will any of it come to anything while I am here? The verse answers without reassurance and without discouragement. It does not promise that he will see the outcome, and it does not suggest that his work is wasted if he does not. It states that the outcome exists independently of his observation of it, and that the account is being kept by someone other than him. That is the only answer that can sustain a long and largely unrewarded effort. The verse is addressed to the Prophet, and through him to anyone who has spent a life delivering a message whose confirmation lies beyond it.

**The Witness Is Not a Passive Observer**

Witnessing in this verse is not surveillance from a distance. The One who witnesses is the One who will testify, and the testimony is the ground of the judgment. The recording of what is witnessed is described elsewhere in the Qur'an with the same seriousness: “while you are certainly observed by vigilant, noble scribes, who know whatever you do” (82:10–12). The pairing of the witnessing and the return in this verse is therefore deliberate. The return is the hearing, and the witnessing is the evidence presented at it. For the audience being warned, this is the fact that makes the delay survivable and dangerous at the same time: nothing is being lost in the interval. Every act performed while the term runs is being retained, and the retention is what makes the eventual judgment an account rather than an impression. The verse's calm tone is thus not indifference. It is the tone of a case already documented.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:47

> And for every community there is a messenger. After their messenger has come, judgment is passed on them in all fairness, and they are not wronged

**Expanded Commentary**

**Revelation Is Not a Local Event**

The verse makes a claim about the whole of human history: every community has had a messenger. The claim is stated elsewhere with its content included: “We surely sent a messenger to every community, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods.’ But some of them were guided by Allah, while others were destined to stray. So travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers!” (16:36), and again as a general rule: “We have surely sent you with the truth as a deliverer of good news and a warner. There is no community that has not had a warner” (35:24). The scope is universal, and it is one of the Qur'an's major themes. No people is described as abandoned, unaddressed, or outside the range of the message. The verse therefore sets the sūrah's local argument — Mecca, its idols, its demands for signs — inside a history far larger than Mecca, and it makes the Meccan case an instance rather than an exception.

**Judgment Follows the Messenger**

The order of the clauses is a rule of procedure. The messenger comes first, and the judgment comes after: “And We would never punish ˹a people˺ until We have sent a messenger ˹to warn them˺” (17:15). The verse removes the most serious objection to divine justice — that people are held to a standard they never received. Nobody is judged on a message that did not reach him, and the arrival of the messenger is what makes the judgment possible. This is also why the verse insists on fairness as a consequence rather than as an aspiration. Once the messenger has come, the judgment is rendered *bi-l-qisṭ*, in all fairness, and the reason it can be fair is that the condition for fairness has been met. The pairing of the messenger and the fairness is therefore not two ideas but one argument: the proof was supplied, so the sentence can be just.

**One Message, Different Laws**

The universality of the messenger does not mean uniformity of the law. Each messenger comes to a particular people in a particular time, and the Qur'an states the consequence plainly: “To each of you We have ordained a code of law and a way of life. If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community, but His Will is to test you with what He has given ˹each of˺ you” (5:48). The reason for the particularity is also given: “We have not sent a messenger except in the language of his people to clarify ˹the message˺ for them” (14:4). A messenger who addressed humanity in general would address nobody in particular. What is constant across the communities is the core — worship God, avoid false gods — and what varies is the specific code suited to the people receiving it. The verse therefore supports both the unity of revelation and the diversity of religions, and it does so without making either claim at the expense of the other.

**“In All Fairness, and They Are Not Wronged”**

The closing clause repeats the sūrah's guarantee. Fairness in judgment has been asserted at the return, in the reward of the doers of good, and in the proportion of the punishment for evil. Here it is attached to the communities as wholes, and the addition — they are not wronged — is the negative form of the same guarantee. The verse is careful to state both: the judgment is fair, and no injury is done. The second clause matters because fairness could in principle be administered harshly; the denial of wrong removes that possibility. It is the same denial the previous verse made in general terms — God does not wrong people at all, they wrong themselves — now applied to the historical process by which peoples are judged. Nothing in the process takes from a community more than it earned, and nothing is charged to it that it was not told.

**What the Verse Means for the Audience Hearing It**

For the Meccans the verse has an uncomfortable edge. The universality of the messenger is a comfort to anyone who has never heard one, and it is a problem for anyone who has. Their messenger has come. The condition that makes judgment fair has been satisfied in their case, and they know it, because the verse's own audience is the community being described. The verse therefore converts a general doctrine into a specific situation. The fairness it promises is not an abstraction about distant peoples; it is the standard by which the people hearing the verse will themselves be judged, and the messenger whose arrival makes that standard applicable is the one they have been demanding signs from.

**The Arrival Ends the Excuse**

There is one further consequence of the verse's order, and it applies to every community without exception. Before the messenger comes, a people cannot be faulted for what they did not know; after he comes, ignorance is no longer available as a defence. The verse therefore marks a change of status rather than a change of information. Something has happened in the world when a messenger arrives, and the change is not in the message but in the position of those who receive it. This is why the Qur'an pairs the universality of the messengers with the command to travel and look: “So travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers!” (16:36). The ruins are evidence that the procedure has been carried out before, on communities that had the same structure as this one — a messenger, a response, a term, and an outcome. The verse states the procedure; the ruins are its record.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:48

> They ask ˹the believers˺, “When will this threat come to pass if what you say is true?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Appears Five Times**

The wording of this question is so fixed that the Qur'an records it in nearly identical form in five separate chapters: “They ask ˹the believers˺, ‘When will this threat come to pass if what you say are true?’” (21:38; 27:71; 34:29; 36:48; 67:25). The repetition is not a copying of phrasing but a record of a demand that kept being made in the same words, in different settings, to different audiences. Its recurrence tells us something about its function. A genuine question changes as the person asking it learns more; this one does not change, because it is not being asked in order to learn. The stability of the wording is the evidence of its purpose. It is a formula of challenge, repeated because it works socially — it puts the believers on the spot without requiring the asker to examine anything.

**The Form of the Question Assumes Its Answer**

The construction is conditional in a way that rigs the outcome: when will this happen, if what you say is true? The condition invites the listener to notice that it has not happened, and the implied inference is that it will not. The question is therefore not open. It has a preferred answer built into it, and the preferred answer is dismissal. The Qur'an identifies the same posture elsewhere: those who challenge the Prophet to hasten the punishment, when the punishment would certainly have come to them at once had a time not already been set (29:53). The demand is made from a position of assumed safety, and the safety is what makes the demand possible. A person who genuinely believed the threat was real would not ask for its schedule; he would ask what to do about it. The verse records the question without answering it, and the absence of an answer is the answer.

**Why the Prophet Could Not Give a Date**

The question is addressed to believers rather than to the Prophet, and the reason appears in the verse that follows: he has no power over benefit or harm except by God's will. The knowledge of the Hour is not his. The Qur'an states the limit explicitly and instructs him to say so: “They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ regarding the Hour, ‘When will it be?’ Say, ‘That knowledge is only with my Lord. He alone will reveal it when the time comes. It is too tremendous for the heavens and the earth and will only take you by surprise.’ They ask you as if you had full knowledge of it. Say, ‘That knowledge is only with Allah, but most people do not know’” (7:187). The final clause diagnoses the question precisely: they ask as though he knew. The expectation behind the demand is that a prophet is a fortune-teller, and the verse refuses the category. A messenger carries a message; the schedule is not part of it.

**What the Demand Reveals**

The request for a date is revealing about the demanders' theory of belief. They are treating the threat as a prediction to be verified on a timetable, which means they have decided in advance what would count as confirmation and what would not. A date met would be accepted; a date missed would be fatal; and no date is available, so the demand cannot be resolved. That structure guarantees perpetual dismissal without requiring any engagement with the content of the warning. The verse exposes the mechanism by refusing to supply the date. It also places the question in its proper context: a warning is not a forecast. A warning concerns what the hearer should do now, and its force does not depend on knowing when the consequence arrives — which is why the demand for a schedule misses the nature of what is being said.

**The Question Is Put to the Community**

The verse specifies the addressees: they ask the believers. The Prophet is not the target here; the community is. That detail is significant for anyone reading it later. The people who carry a warning are the ones asked to defend it, and they are asked by people who do not want an answer. The believers' position is described in the verse that follows, and it is a position of acknowledged limitation rather than of superior knowledge: they cannot produce the punishment, cannot advance it, and do not know its time. What they can do is deliver what they were given and decline the demand for a date. The verse is therefore instruction for the community as much as report about Mecca, and it tells the community how to handle the most common challenge it will face.

**How the Question Is Actually Answered**

The reply is supplied in the verses that follow, and it is not a date. The believers are told to state their own position: no power to bring benefit or harm except by God's will, and a fixed term for every community that cannot be moved in either direction. That is the whole answer, and it is sufficient. The demand assumed that a warning without a schedule is a warning without content; the answer shows that the content lies in the term rather than in the timing. A person who knows that his community has an appointed hour, and that the hour cannot be advanced or delayed by anyone, has everything he needs in order to act. The date would add nothing to that, and its absence removes nothing. The believers' reply is therefore not a confession of ignorance dressed up as piety. It is a correction of the question, and the correction is the point at which the challenge loses its force.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:49

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I have no power to benefit or protect myself, except by the Will of Allah.” For each community there is an appointed term. When their time arrives, they cannot delay it for a moment, nor could they advance it

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Begins With the Messenger's Own Poverty**

The reply to the demand for a date opens with a statement about the speaker, and the statement is one of complete dependence: I have no power to benefit or protect myself, except by the will of Allah. The Prophet is instructed to place himself inside the same condition as everyone else. He cannot bring benefit to himself, cannot ward off harm from himself, and therefore cannot schedule either for anyone else. The same statement is given at greater length elsewhere: “Say, ‘I have no power to benefit or protect myself, except by the Will of Allah. If I had known the unknown, I would have benefited myself enormously, and no harm would have ever touched me. I am only a warner and deliverer of good news for those who believe’” (7:188). The hypothetical in that verse is the proof: if he possessed knowledge of the unseen he would have used it on himself first. The absence of such use is the evidence of its absence.

**An Appointed Term for Every Community**

The second sentence supplies the framework the question ignored. Every community has an *ajal* — a term, a fixed span. The same statement appears where the destruction of past peoples is described: “For each community there is an appointed term. When their time arrives, they can neither delay it for a moment, nor could they advance it” (7:34). The term is understood both as the natural span of a people and as the point at which a people may be punished before that span ends, and both readings serve the same purpose: the timing is fixed and it is not human property. The wider statement of the principle makes the mercy in it visible: “If Allah were to punish people ˹immediately˺ for their wrongdoing, He would not have left a single living being on earth. But He delays them for an appointed term” (16:61). The delay is not negligence; it is the term running.

**The Term Cannot Be Moved in Either Direction**

The symmetry of the verse is exact: they cannot delay it by an hour, and they cannot advance it. Both directions are closed, and the closure is stated in the same measure — a single hour. The individual case is stated with the same precision: “But Allah never delays a soul when its appointed time comes” (63:11). The double closure is what defeats the demand completely. The people asking for the threat to come sooner are requesting the one thing that no party to the arrangement can supply — not the Prophet, not themselves, not anyone. A term that could be advanced by request would not be a term. The verse therefore answers the question not by refusing it but by showing that it asks for an impossibility, and it does so in language that applies equally to the person asking and to the person being asked.

**Why the Demand to Hasten Is Absurd**

The people making this demand are asking for their own punishment to arrive early, and the verse treats this as a symptom rather than as a challenge. The same request is recorded elsewhere with its context: they challenge the Prophet to hasten the punishment, and had a time not already been set, it would have come to them at once (29:53). The demand is made because the demanders are certain nothing will happen, and the certainty is what makes the demand feel clever. The verse removes the cleverness by removing the possibility. Nothing is being withheld out of reluctance; the timing is not available to be moved. What remains of the demand is a statement about the demanders' confidence, and the verse leaves that statement standing without comment, because the term will supply the comment.

**Removing the Prophet From the Argument**

The verse's structure achieves something specific. The demand was addressed to the Prophet as though he controlled the schedule. The answer begins by denying the control and ends by locating it in a fixed order that applies to everyone. In between, the Prophet disappears from the transaction. He is not the one withholding, not the one deciding, and not the one who will be blamed for the delay. The audience's frustration is thereby redirected from the messenger to the arrangement itself, where it belongs. The same relocation appears in the verses around it: the return is to God, the witnessing is God's, the judgment is rendered by God. The Prophet is a delivery point in a system he does not operate, and the verse states his position in the plainest possible terms so that no further demand can be addressed to him.

**The Term Applies to the Individual Too**

The verse speaks of communities, and the same structure holds for single lives. “But Allah never delays a soul when its appointed time comes. And Allah is AllAware of what you do” (63:11). The pairing in that verse is instructive: the fixed term is stated immediately beside the statement that everything is known, which is exactly the pairing in this verse — a term that cannot be moved, and a witness over what is done within it. For the individual, the consequence is the same as for the people. The hour is set, it will not be moved by request, and the interval before it is the only space in which anything can be done. The demand to hasten the punishment is therefore a demand made inside the only period available for avoiding it, which is what makes the demand so costly. The verse's second sentence is not merely a statement about history; it describes the frame every reader is standing in.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:50

> Tell them ˹O Prophet˺, “Imagine if His torment were to overcome you by night or day—do the wicked realize what they are ˹really˺ asking Him to hasten

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Hypothetical Put Back to the People Who Asked for It**

The verse answers the demand by granting it in imagination. Imagine, it says, that the punishment you have been requesting arrives — by night or by day. The instruction is not to predict or to threaten but to run the scenario the demanders have been proposing, and then to ask them a question about it. The move is dialectically precise. The demand was made in confidence because its fulfilment seemed impossible; the verse makes it possible in thought, where confidence is harder to maintain. Nothing in the hypothetical requires the audience to accept anything they have rejected. It asks only that they consider the thing they have been asking for as though it were real, which is the minimum condition of making a request seriously.

**By Night or By Day**

The phrase is the same one used in the parable of the garden earlier in this chapter, where the command came upon the field by night or by day and left it as though it had never flourished. The two halves of the clock cover all of it, and their function is to remove any hope of preparation. A punishment that arrives at a known hour can be met; one that arrives at any hour cannot. The same idea appears in the question put elsewhere: “Ask, ‘Imagine if Allah's punishment were to overwhelm you with or without warning—who would be destroyed other than the wrongdoers?’” (6:47). The addition of “with or without warning” in that verse makes explicit what this verse implies by naming both halves of the day. There is no time at which the arrival can be avoided by being ready, which is what makes the demand for it so strange.

**“What Are They Asking to Hasten?”**

The closing question is the verse's real content. It does not ask whether the punishment is coming; it asks whether the people demanding it know what it is. The implication is that they do not. A request made in mockery is made without content — the asker has never pictured the thing he is requesting, because picturing it would end the mockery. The Qur'an states the same fact elsewhere with the outcome attached: “They challenge you ˹O Prophet˺ to hasten the punishment. Had it not been for a time already set, the punishment would have certainly come to them ˹at once˺. But it will definitely take them by surprise when they least expect it” (29:53). The surprise is the point. The punishment will arrive in the condition the demanders have been assuming it never would, and the arrival will be as unexpected as it was requested.

**The Wicked Named Inside the Question**

The verse shifts person in its final words. It speaks to them, and then refers to them in the third: do the wicked realise what they are asking to hasten? The shift is a judgement delivered inside a question. The word *mujrimūn* — the same word used earlier of those whose deeds are rewarded in exact proportion — is applied to the people being addressed, and it is applied while they are still able to dispute it. That is the mercy in the construction. A verdict announced in the third person to the people it concerns gives them the chance to notice that they are being described, and to object. The verse does not press the label; it leaves it in the sentence where the hearers can find it or ignore it. The demand for haste is thereby shown to be not a challenge to the messenger but evidence about the demanders.

**Ignorance of One's Own Request**

The verse identifies a common failure: people make demands without examining what the demands mean. The demanders are not liars in the ordinary sense; many of them probably believed nothing would happen, and their words were performance rather than petition. The verse treats the performance as a real request and asks whether the performers have considered its content. The question is applicable far beyond Mecca. Every person who has said, in anger or in jest, that he would rather have the truth settled now than later, has made the same request — and the verse asks the only question that matters about it: do you know what you are asking for? The answer, in almost every case, is no. The verse's refusal to grant the request is therefore not a withholding but a protection.

**The Restraint Is the Mercy in the Verse**

The verse declines to satisfy the demand, and the refusal deserves notice. God could bring the punishment at the moment it is requested; the Qur'an says as much elsewhere — had a time not already been set, it would have come at once (29:53). What stands between the demand and its fulfilment is the term, and the term is described in the same place as a delay granted rather than a delay imposed. The verse therefore contains a mercy that its subject conceals: the people asking to be destroyed are being kept alive, and the keeping is what allows the question to be asked at all. A person who is being punished cannot ask for punishment. The demand itself is evidence that the delay is running, and the verse's decision to answer with a question rather than with an event leaves that delay intact. The restraint is not reluctance; it is the only thing making the demanders' position reversible. Read that way, the verse is gentler than its subject suggests.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:51

> Will you believe in it only after it has overtaken you? Now? But you always wanted to hasten it!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Belief That Arrives Too Late to Be Belief**

The verse poses a question whose answer is obvious, and the obviousness is the point. Will you believe only when the punishment has already reached you? The scenario is not hypothetical in the way the previous verse's was; it describes a real possibility with a known outcome. Belief at that point would not be belief in the ordinary sense, because belief requires the option of not believing. Once the threatened thing is present, assent is not a choice but a description of what one sees. The Qur'an states the rule in the language of repentance: “However, repentance is not accepted from those who knowingly persist in sin until they start dying, and then cry, ‘Now I repent!’ nor those who die as disbelievers” (4:18). The timing is what invalidates the statement, not the words. The verse therefore identifies a distinction that is easy to lose: the content of a confession and the condition in which it is made are not the same thing.

**Pharaoh as the Documented Case**

The scenario has a precedent in this very chapter, and it is described in detail. As Pharaoh drowned: “I believe that there is no god except that in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am ˹now˺ one of those who submit” (10:90). The words are correct, and the answer he receives is not a denial of their correctness: “Now ˹you believe˺? But you always disobeyed and were one of the corruptors” (10:91). The rebuke is entirely about timing. The same word — now — appears in both this verse and Pharaoh's answer, and the echo is deliberate. The case is then closed with a statement about its use: “Today We will preserve your corpse so that you may become an example for those who come after you” (10:92). The precedent is preserved precisely so that later people can see what a correct confession is worth when it arrives after the option has closed.

**“Now?” — the Shortest Rebuke in the Passage**

The single word *ālʾān* — now? — does more work than any sentence in the surrounding verses. It is not an answer but a repetition of the believer's own timing, thrown back at him. The effect is to make the person hear his own position from outside. Nothing needs to be added to the word, because the situation it names is complete: the demand was made when there was time, and the belief is offered when there is none. The Qur'an uses the same device where Pharaoh's confession is answered, and in both cases the monosyllable carries the whole verdict. A long rebuke would argue with the person; this one simply holds up the moment he chose to speak in.

**What the Demand to Hasten Actually Proved**

The verse's final clause turns the demand back on itself. You wanted to hasten it — and that wanting was evidence. The same demand is recorded in several places, always with the same tone: “Do they ˹really˺ seek to hasten Our torment?” (26:204); “Do they ˹really˺ wish to hasten Our punishment?” (37:176). The reply given elsewhere distinguishes the two groups precisely: “Those who disbelieve in it ˹ask to˺ hasten it ˹mockingly˺. But the believers are fearful of it, knowing that it is the truth” (42:18). The demand to hasten is thus a marker. A person who believes a threat is real does not ask for it sooner; he asks how to avoid it. The verse treats the demand as a confession made in the language of challenge, and the outcome is stated in the same vocabulary: “˹They will be told,˺ ‘Taste your torment! This is what you sought to hasten’” (51:14).

**The Assumption the Verse Attacks**

Underlying the demand is an assumption worth naming: that belief is a conclusion one can adopt at any time, and that adopting it late is as good as adopting it early. The verse denies this. Belief has a context, and the context includes the freedom not to believe. A statement made under compulsion — by danger, by pain, by the sight of the thing threatened — does not carry the same weight as the same statement made when nothing forced it. This is not a technicality about the afterlife. It is a claim about what belief is: an act performed while alternatives remain open. The verse therefore does not say that late belief is insincere; it says it is not belief in the sense that matters, because the condition that made belief meaningful has ended. The same principle explains why the Qur'an records Pharaoh's confession at length rather than passing over it: the case is the clearest available demonstration that the words are not what is being evaluated.

**Why This Verse Follows the Hypothetical**

The structure of the passage is deliberate. The previous verse asked the demanders to imagine the punishment arriving by night or day; this verse asks what they would do in that imagined situation, and supplies the answer. The sequence moves from possibility to consequence. Nothing is asserted that the audience has not already accepted — they have been requesting the event, so they cannot object to its description. What the verses add is the part they had not considered: their own behaviour inside it. The rhetorical method is the same one used throughout the chapter. Take the audience's own position, follow it to its end, and let them look at where it stops. The verse adds one further element to that method: it makes the audience the subject of the final scene rather than its audience. They are no longer watching a hypothetical punishment arrive at someone else. They are the ones inside it, speaking, and the words they speak are the ones they have been mocking.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:52

> Then the wrongdoers will be told, “Taste the torment of eternity! Are you not rewarded except for what you used to commit?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**“Taste” — the Language of Direct Experience**

The sentence begins with a verb of the mouth, and the choice is exact. *Dhūqū* — taste. The demand that produced this moment was a demand to see: when will this threat come to pass? The answer converts seeing into tasting, which is a different relation to a thing entirely. Seeing keeps a distance; tasting does not. The same verb is used in the parallel sentence: “˹They will be told,˺ ‘Taste your torment! This is what you sought to hasten’” (51:14), and again where the reason is stated: “So taste ˹the punishment˺ for neglecting the meeting of this Day of yours. We ˹too˺ will certainly neglect you. And taste the torment of eternity for what you used to do!” (32:14). The verb is not metaphorical decoration. It names the transition from a threat discussed at a distance to an experience undergone, and the transition is what the whole passage has been describing.

**The Torment of Eternity**

The phrase *ʿadhāb al-khuld* — the torment of eternity, or of long duration — is the object of the tasting. The word *khuld* carries permanence, and the Qur'an uses it consistently of an abode without end. The exegetical tradition records a minority reading: the jurist and theologian Ibn Qayyim al-Jawziyyah (d. 751/1350) argued that the root *kh-l-d* can denote a long but ultimately finite span, and on that basis took this punishment and the one at 32:14 as having a limit. The position is a real one in the tradition and it should be recorded, but the dominant reading takes the word with the verses that describe the abode as permanent, and the Qur'an's own phrasing elsewhere points that way — the residents of the Fire are described as remaining forever. What both readings agree on is that the punishment is measured, that it corresponds to the deeds, and that its duration is set by God rather than by the sufferer.

**“Are You Not Rewarded Except for What You Committed?”**

The second sentence is a question, and it is the defence of the sentence rather than an addition to it. The same formula appears wherever the outcome of evil is stated: “And whoever comes with an evil deed will be hurled face-first into the Fire. Are you rewarded except for what you used to do?” (27:90); and in the exchange between the followers and their leaders: “They will ˹all˺ hide ˹their˺ remorse when they see the torment. And We will put shackles around the necks of the disbelievers. Will they be rewarded except for what they used to do?” (34:33). The question is rhetorical because its answer is not in dispute. Nobody in the scene claims to have been given more than he earned. The formula's function is to state, at the moment of the punishment, that the punishment is a settlement rather than an infliction — and to say it in the hearing of the ones being punished.

**The Vocabulary of Earnings**

The word used for what they committed is from the root of earning, the same commercial vocabulary that runs through this chapter. Deeds are acquisitions; a life is a transaction; the outcome is a settlement of accounts. The Qur'an keeps the metaphor consistent, and the consistency is what makes its moral language coherent. The same image appears in the question asked about the disbelievers: “˹The believers will be asked,˺ ‘Have the disbelievers ˹not˺ been paid back for what they used to do?’” (83:36). Payment is the right word because it implies a prior debt, and the debt was incurred by the person paying it. The verse therefore does not present the punishment as something done to the wrongdoers from outside. It presents it as the arrival of what they accumulated, which is a more severe description and a fairer one.

**Who Speaks the Sentence**

The verse says the wrongdoers “will be told”, and the tradition records two possibilities: the speaker is God, or the angels who guard the Fire, as at 32:14 where the same words are addressed to those who neglected the meeting of that Day. The ambiguity is not a defect. In either case the content is identical, and the content is what the verse is concerned with. What matters is that the sentence is spoken to the ones receiving it, in their hearing, and in the form of a question they cannot dispute. A punishment announced silently would leave room for the belief that an injustice had occurred; a punishment announced with its reason attached removes that possibility. The verse is careful about this, and the care is theological rather than dramatic.

**The Question Expects No Answer**

The final sentence ends on a question, and no reply is recorded. That silence is the verse's last word. The wrongdoers are not asked to justify themselves, to explain, or to appeal; they are asked whether the settlement matches the account, and the question is not put in order to hear a defence. It is put so that the fact of proportion is established in their presence. The verse therefore closes the sequence that began with the demand to hasten the punishment. The demand was made in mockery; the hypothetical followed; the arrival was described; and the settlement is now stated in a form that leaves nothing to argue with. What remains of the demanders' position is the acknowledgement that they were paid what they earned. The verse's finality comes from that acknowledgement rather than from the punishment itself. Suffering can be protested; a settlement that matches the account cannot.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:53

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺, “Is this true?” Say, “Yes, by my Lord! Most certainly it is true! And you will have no escape.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Direct Question and an Unhedged Answer**

The question is short and the answer is shorter. Is this true? Yes. Nothing in the reply qualifies the affirmation, offers a range of interpretations, or refers the matter to a future clarification. The Qur'an's answers are often complex, and where a question is loaded the reply usually dismantles the loading first — as it did with the demand for a date. Here the question is not loaded, or at least it is answered as though it were sincere, and the answer is given in the plainest form available in Arabic: *balā*, yes indeed, the particle used to affirm against a doubt. The verse therefore treats the question as a genuine request for information, and it meets it with an unqualified statement. That is unusual enough to be significant. The Prophet is not being asked to interpret or to persuade; he is being asked whether the warning is real, and he says that it is.

**The Oath**

The affirmation is reinforced with an oath by God: *balā wa-rabbī*, yes, by my Lord. The Prophet is instructed to swear, and the thing sworn by is his Lord rather than anything created. Oaths in the Qur'an are rare in the Prophet's own speech and they are reserved for matters of the highest consequence, and this is one of them. The oath does something that a bare assertion cannot: it places the speaker's standing behind the statement. A man known for forty years as trustworthy, swearing by his Lord that the warning is real, has put everything he has into a single sentence. The doubled affirmation that follows — most certainly it is true — reinforces it further, and the reinforcement is needed because the thing being affirmed is precisely what the audience has been treating as improbable.

**“You Will Have No Escape”**

The third clause adds what the affirmation implies: the matter cannot be frustrated. The word *muʿjizūn* means those who render something incapable of reaching its object — those who escape, outstrip, or defeat it. The denial is absolute: you will not escape. The same recognition is recorded elsewhere in the strongest terms, in the speech of the jinn who heard the recitation: “˹Now,˺ we truly know that we cannot frustrate Allah on earth, nor can we escape from Him ˹into heaven˺” (72:12). The two directions are named there and both are closed, which is the sense of the phrase here. The clause completes the answer by removing the alternative to acceptance. A warning that could be avoided by flight would not require belief; this one cannot, and the verse says so in the same breath as the affirmation.

**A Different Question From the One Before**

The audience has been asking questions throughout this passage, and this one differs from the rest. The earlier demand was about timing — when will this threat come to pass? — and it was mockery in the form of a question. This question is about reality: is it true? The shift matters. A person asking when something will happen has already assumed it will not; a person asking whether it is true has not settled the matter. The verse takes the question at its face value, which is a courtesy, and it answers in a way that leaves no room for the earlier evasion. The audience is offered the chance to treat the warning as a claim about the world rather than as a schedule, and the answer is given for that question rather than for the one they had been asking.

**The Shortest Answer in the Passage**

The reply is three clauses long, and its brevity is a feature of its content. Certainty does not require elaboration. Where the Qur'an answers a loaded question it produces a paragraph; where it answers a plain one it produces a sentence. The structure of the answer is also worth noting: affirmation, oath, reinforcement, consequence. Nothing is added that does not bear on the truth of the warning, and nothing is withheld that the questioner needs. The verse is therefore a model of how a certain statement should be made — without hedging, without decoration, and with the consequence stated rather than implied. It stands in deliberate contrast to the long argumentative sequences around it, and the contrast is the point: after all the questions about creation, provision and guidance, the answer to the simplest question is the shortest. The verse therefore also models something for the community that will inherit the task. When asked whether the warning is real, the reply is not an argument. It is an affirmation, sworn, with the consequence attached — and that is enough.

**What the Verse Assumes About the Asker**

By answering directly, the verse treats the questioner as someone capable of receiving a true statement. That is not a small thing. The surrounding verses have described people who listen without hearing and look without seeing, and it would have been easy to dismiss the question as another evasion. The verse does not. It supplies the information requested, in the strongest form available, and leaves the response with the asker. The final clause is not a threat added to an answer; it is part of the answer, since a warning that can be escaped is a different kind of warning from one that cannot. The verse gives the audience exactly what it asked for and nothing that would soften it. There is a lesson in that for anyone carrying the same message. The response to hostility is not always argument; sometimes it is the plain statement of what is known, delivered without qualification and left with the hearer.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:54

> If every wrongdoer were to possess everything in the world, they would surely ransom themselves with it. They will hide ˹their˺ remorse when they see the torment. And they will be judged in all fairness, and none will be wronged

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ransom That Cannot Be Paid**

The verse opens with a conditional that is answered in the negative by every parallel passage. If the wrongdoer possessed all that is on the earth, he would offer it to ransom himself — and the Qur'an states repeatedly that it would not be accepted. “Indeed, if each of those who disbelieve then die as disbelievers were to offer a ransom of enough gold to fill the whole world, it would never be accepted from them” (3:91). “As for the disbelievers, even if they were to possess everything in the world twice over ˹and offer it all˺ to ransom themselves from the punishment of the Day of Judgment, it would never be accepted from them” (5:36). The escalation is deliberate: the whole world, then the whole world doubled. The verse here states the willingness and leaves the refusal to the passages that complete it, which is why it can be so brief. What it establishes is the value the wrongdoer assigns to his own safety at that moment: everything.

**The Wish Extends to Everything a Person Has**

The ransom is not limited to property. The Qur'an describes the wish in its most extreme form: “˹although˺ they will be made to see each other. The wicked will wish to ransom themselves from the punishment of that Day by their children” (70:11), and the passage continues with spouses, brothers, clan and everyone on earth. The sequence moves from wealth to kin to the whole of humanity, and the logic is that of a person who has run out of categories. When everything is available to be given, nothing is being held back, and the verse's point is that the offering has no purchasing power at all. A ransom presupposes a party willing to accept it, and the verse's whole context is that the settlement has already been made. The wrongdoer's willingness is therefore not a mitigating fact; it is a measure of how completely he misjudged the situation beforehand.

**Remorse Hidden at the Sight of the Torment**

The second clause describes a specific reaction: they will conceal their remorse when they see the punishment. The concealment is social. Remorse in private is one thing; remorse displayed before the people one misled, or before those one despised, is another. The same detail appears in the exchange between the followers and their leaders: “The lowly will say to the arrogant, ‘No! It was your plotting by day and night’… They will ˹all˺ hide ˹their˺ remorse when they see the torment” (34:33). The hiding is temporary, and the Qur'an records what follows it — an open confession: “They will cry, ‘Our Lord! Our ill-fate took hold of us, so we became a misguided people’” (23:106). The sequence is psychologically exact. Shame arrives first, while the pride that caused the situation is still intact; the confession comes later, when nothing is left to protect.

**Judged in Fairness, and None Wronged**

The final clause returns to the refrain. The judgment is rendered in justice, and nobody is wronged. The pairing is the sūrah's standard guarantee and it appears here at the moment of greatest severity, which is where it is most needed. “Whoever does good, it is to their own benefit. And whoever does evil, it is to their own loss. Your Lord is never unjust to ˹His˺ creation” (41:46); “My Word cannot be changed, nor am I unjust to ˹My˺ creation” (50:29). The placement is the argument. A description of the ransom and the remorse could easily leave the impression of a scene of pure suffering; the clause restores the frame and states that everything occurring in it has been measured. The verse therefore contains both the severity and its justification, and it does not allow either to be read without the other.

**Three Movements in One Verse**

The verse moves in three stages, and the order is significant. First the wish — everything offered for safety. Then the shame — remorse concealed. Then the judgment — rendered fairly. The stages describe the collapse of a position rather than a sequence of events. What the wrongdoer relied on in the world was wealth and standing; the first stage shows wealth worthless, and the second shows standing gone, since shame exists only where standing was claimed. The third stage removes the last defence, which is the claim of injustice. By the end of the verse nothing remains of the position that was held in the world, and the removal is complete rather than partial. That completeness is what makes the verse's final clause credible: the judgment is fair because there is nothing left to dispute.

**What the Verse Says About Value**

Read as a statement about worth, the verse is severe and precise. It reports what a person will give for his safety when the question is finally concrete, and the answer is everything he has. That is a judgement on what he gave for it earlier — which was, on the sūrah's own account, very little. The disproportion between the two prices is the verse's teaching. Nothing here counsels against owning things; the condemnation throughout the chapter is aimed at the belief that ownership is control. The verse shows the moment at which the belief is tested, and the test is failed not because the wealth is insufficient but because it was never the kind of thing that could answer this question. The verse is therefore a warning delivered in the form of a price list, and the prices are stated on both sides.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:55

> Surely to Allah belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth. Surely Allah’s promise is ˹always˺ true, but most of them do not know

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ownership Stated Before the Promise**

The verse makes two assertions, and the first grounds the second. To God belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth. God's promise is true. The connection between them is not decorative; it is the reason the promise carries weight. A promise is credible in proportion to the promiser's control over what he has promised, and the verse establishes the control before relying on it. Nothing in the heavens or the earth lies outside the ownership being described, so nothing can prevent the fulfilment of what has been promised. The same reasoning appears where a believer answers a sceptical son: “Pity you. Have faith! Surely Allah's promise is true” (46:17). The affirmation there is made in the face of ridicule, and it rests on the same ground — the promiser is not a party who can be prevented.

**The Promise Is a Fact, Not a Forecast**

The phrase *waʿda llāhi ḥaqqan* uses an emphatic construction: the promise of God, true. The same construction appeared early in this chapter, where the return was stated, and its force is identical — a divine promise is not a prediction about the future but a settled fact stated in the future tense. The distinction matters for the reader's relation to it. A forecast can be revised by events; a decree cannot. The verse therefore asks the audience to give the promise the same epistemic status they give to what has already happened, and it does so without argument, simply by placing the two assertions side by side. The first is a statement about the present arrangement of the world, which nobody in the audience disputed; the second is placed beside it as an item of the same kind.

**The Promise Includes the Punishment**

The context fixes the reference. The verses immediately before have described the ransom, the concealed remorse and the judgment, so the promise in view is the threatened punishment as much as the promised reward. That is worth stating plainly, because the word “promise” in ordinary use suggests something desirable. The Qur'an uses it in both directions, and here it covers the outcome the audience has been mocking. The effect is to remove the last comfort available to them. If the promise were only of reward, a sceptic could accept the reward and doubt the punishment; the verse treats both as equally covered by the same word and the same certainty. The ownership of the heavens and the earth is what makes both halves unavoidable. It is also what makes the promise personal rather than general: the One who owns the arrangement is not waiting on any part of it.

**“Most of Them Do Not Know”**

The closing clause is a diagnosis rather than a reproach, and the word for not knowing is neutral. It does not say they are stupid or malicious; it says they do not know. The exegetical tradition identifies two causes and they are compatible: ignorance, in the sense of never having examined the matter, and heedlessness, in the sense of having examined it and then stopped attending to it. Both are described in this chapter — the people who follow assumption rather than knowledge, and those who were heedless of the signs. The clause is therefore a summary of the chapter's account of its audience. Nothing prevented them from knowing. The signs were given, the messenger came, the questions were asked and answered correctly by the audience itself. What was missing was the act of knowing, and the verse records its absence in three words. It is the shortest statement in the chapter about its audience, and one of the most severe, because it attributes their position to nothing more dramatic than a failure to know.

**Two Certainties and One Ignorance**

The structure of the verse is worth holding onto. Two emphatic statements about God — His ownership and the truth of His promise — and one statement about people: most of them do not know. The disproportion is the verse's whole content. The facts do not depend on being known, and their certainty is not increased by recognition or reduced by denial. The ignorance is on one side only, and it is the side that suffers from it. That arrangement is the sūrah's basic posture toward its audience throughout. The chapter does not argue that the truth is uncertain and needs defending; it argues that the truth is fixed and that the audience is elsewhere. The verse states this in the smallest possible space.

**The Ground of the Whole Chapter**

This verse gathers the argument that has run since the opening. The letters and the wise Book; the creation in six Days and the conducting of every affair; the return and the just reward; the sun and moon made for reckoning; the signs for those who are mindful; the parable of the garden cut down; the Home of Peace; the questions about provision, senses, life and guidance; the challenge to produce a chapter. Every one of those is an instance of the two claims made here. What is in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, and what He has promised will occur. The ignorance named at the end is the only thing standing between the audience and the conclusion, and the verse identifies it without dramatizing it. That restraint is characteristic of the chapter. The sūrah does not need to insult its audience in order to refute them; it needs only to state what is owned, what has been promised, and what has not been known.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:56

> He ˹is the One Who˺ gives life and causes death, and to Him you will ˹all˺ be returned

**Expanded Commentary**

**Both Ends of a Life in One Hand**

The verse states two acts and one destination. God gives life; God causes death; to Him all return. The pairing is not two separate claims but one claim about a single domain, and the Qur'an states it in exactly this form wherever authority is at issue. “O humanity! I am Allah's Messenger to you all. To Him ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. He gives life and causes death” (7:158). “Indeed, to Allah ˹alone˺ belongs the kingdom of the heavens and the earth. He gives life and causes death. And besides Allah you have no guardian or helper” (9:116). In both cases the statement is used to close a question about who has charge. The verse here does the same work at the end of a passage that has been asking who provides, who guides, who originates and who will judge.

**The Argument With the King**

The pairing has a documented use in argument, and the Qur'an records it. “˹Remember˺ when Abraham said, ‘My Lord is the One Who has power to give life and cause death.’ He argued, ‘I too have the power to give life and cause death.’ Abraham challenged ˹him˺, ‘Allah causes the sun to rise from the east. So make it rise from the west.’ And so the disbeliever was dumbstruck” (2:258). The exchange is instructive because the king's claim is not obviously false — rulers do execute and pardon. What Abraham does is shift the claim to a domain the king cannot enter. The verse here makes the same distinction without the dialogue: giving life and causing death belong to the One who does both as a matter of course, and the return belongs to Him as well. No rival can claim the third item, and the third item is what makes the first two decisive.

**The Return**

The final clause is the verse's distinctive addition. Life and death are visible facts; the return is not, and it is the clause that carries the weight. It also changes the meaning of death. If death were simply an end, the verse would need only its first half; because it is a return, death is a transition between two states, and the second state is where the account is settled. The Qur'an draws the practical consequence of this in the context of loss: “Allah makes such thinking a cause of agony in their hearts. It is Allah who gives life and causes death. And Allah is All-Seeing of what you do” (3:156). The statement is placed there to relieve a specific grief, and it works because the giving and the taking are not random events but acts of a single Agent to whom everyone returns. The clause turns bereavement from an absurdity into a transfer, and it is the only part of the statement that could do that.

**Why the Two Acts Belong Together**

Life and death are named as one operation because the Qur'an treats them as a single arrangement with a purpose. “˹He is the One˺ Who created death and life in order to test which of you is best in deeds. And He is the Almighty, All-Forgiving” (67:2). Death is listed first in that verse, which is unusual and significant — it is the frame within which life has its meaning. The verse here reverses the order to match the sequence a person experiences: given life, then taken in death, then returned. Read with 67:2, the arrangement is a test with a stated object, and the return is the point at which the result is declared. Nothing in the sequence is arbitrary, and that is what the verse asserts in three short clauses.

**A Further Reading in the Tradition**

The exegetical tradition extends the verse beyond physical life and death, and the extension is worth recording. The commentator Rūzbihān al-Baqlī read the two verbs as describing God's dealings with two kinds of spiritual people: He enlivens the hearts of those who know Him by their knowing and witnessing Him, and He slays the egos of those who approach Him through detachment by the lights of their awe and vigilance. On that reading the return is also differentiated — the knowers return to the witnessing of His Beauty and Majesty, and the ascetics to His Bounty and Blessing. The reading is an extension rather than the plain sense, but it preserves the verse's structure: a giving, a taking, and a return that differs according to what a person was occupied with.

**The Verse as a Summary of the Chapter**

This verse does for the chapter what 10:55 does for the argument that preceded it. The sūrah has established that God created and conducts, provides and withholds, guides and leaves astray, judges and returns. The verse compresses all of it into the two acts no creature can perform and the destination no creature can avoid. It also supplies the ground on which the earlier promises rest: a return is credible only if the One who receives it is the One who gave the life in the first place. The same logic appears where resurrection is argued from the visible world: “See then the impact of Allah's mercy: how He gives life to the earth after its death! Surely That ˹same Creator˺ can raise the dead” (30:50). The verse states the principle; the earth supplies the demonstration.

**Why the Clause Follows the Judgment Verses**

The placement of the return at this point in the chapter is deliberate. The verses immediately before have described a ransom that cannot be paid, remorse concealed, and a judgment rendered in full fairness. Those scenes require a destination, and the verse supplies it. A settlement presupposes a place where the account is closed, and the clause names the One to whom the closing belongs. Read in sequence, the passage moves from the demand to hasten the punishment, to the tasting of it, to the affirmation that the warning is true, to the worthless ransom, to the truth of the promise, and finally to the return that makes all of it coherent. The verse is the hinge. Without it the preceding descriptions are a series of images; with it they are stages in a single process with a stated end.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:57

> O humanity! Indeed, there has come to you a warning from your Lord, a cure for what is in the hearts, a guide, and a mercy for the believers

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Names for One Thing**
