The verse describes the revelation with four terms, and each does distinct work. *Mawʿiẓa* — an exhortation or admonition, a warning that moves the hearer rather than merely informing him. *Shifāʾ* — a cure, the language of medicine applied to the interior. *Hudā* — guidance, direction toward a destination. *Raḥma* — mercy, the quality of what is given rather than its content. The four are not synonyms piled up for effect. A warning addresses the will, a cure addresses a condition, guidance addresses a direction, and mercy addresses the giver. The verse therefore describes the revelation from four angles at once, and the completeness of the description is the point: nothing a person needs from a revelation is left unnamed. The announcement that it “has come to you” is stated as an accomplished fact rather than a promise.

**A Cure for What Is in the Hearts**

The medical term is the verse's most distinctive claim. The object of the cure is specified: what is in the breasts. The exegetical tradition identifies the diseases — ignorance, doubt, hypocrisy and hatred — and the list is diagnostic rather than rhetorical. Each of those is a condition of the interior that produces outward behaviour, and each is treated elsewhere in the Qur'an as an illness rather than as a mere error. The same description is given where the effect is stated with its limit: “We send down the Quran as a healing and mercy for the believers, but it only increases the wrongdoers in loss” (17:82). And again, in the context of a challenge to the audience's capacity to receive it: “Say, ˹O Prophet˺, ‘It is a guide and a healing to the believers. As for those who disbelieve, there is deafness in their ears and blindness to it ˹in their hearts˺’” (41:44). The cure is real and its efficacy depends on the condition of the patient.

The description was not only theological. A group of the Prophet's companions once passed a tribe that refused them hospitality; when that tribe's chief was bitten by a snake — or stung by a scorpion — and nothing else worked, one of the companions recited Sūrat al-Fātiḥa over him and gathered his saliva, and the man was cured. They were given a flock of sheep in payment but would not take it until they had asked the Prophet, who smiled and said, “How do you know that Sūrat al-Fātiḥa is a ruqya? Take it, and assign a share for me” (Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 5736, on the authority of Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī). The report is preserved because it confirms the practice, and it belongs beside this verse: the recitation was treated as medicine by the people who had received it as revelation.

**Why the Address Is to Humanity**

The verse opens with *yā ayyuhā l-nās* — O humanity. The Qur'an's addresses fall into two broad classes: those to the believers and those to people at large. This is the second kind, and the choice matters. The revelation is being described not as a possession of a community but as something that has arrived in the world and is available to whoever is addressed. The sūrah has spent fifty-six verses arguing with a specific audience about specific claims; the address here steps back from that argument and speaks to everyone. The effect is to place the revelation outside the dispute. Whatever the Makkan opponents conclude, the thing has come, and it has come to humanity rather than to a faction. The chapter's argument was local and specific; the thing it argues for is not.

**The Order of the Four Terms**

The sequence is not arbitrary, and the first position is the most instructive. The warning comes before the cure. A medicine given to someone who does not believe he is ill will be refused, and the Qur'an's method throughout is to establish the illness before offering the treatment. The whole preceding argument — the questions about provision, senses, life and guidance, the parables of wealth and the garden, the descriptions of the return — has been doing that work. The warning is what makes the cure intelligible. Guidance follows the cure because direction is useless to someone who cannot see, and mercy is named last because it describes the whole transaction from the giver's side. The verse is therefore a compressed account of the method the sūrah has been using.

**The Last Two Are Specified for the Believers**

Guidance and mercy are qualified: for the believers. The qualification is not a restriction on the revelation's availability — it has come to humanity — but a statement about where it takes effect. Guidance requires someone willing to be guided, and mercy is received by those who accept what is given. The Qur'an makes the same distinction sharply elsewhere: “We have revealed to you the Book as an explanation of all things, a guide, a mercy, and good news for those who ˹fully˺ submit” (16:89). The Book is described in the same fourfold way, and the recipients are named in the same way. The verse therefore holds two things together without contradiction: the offer is universal and the benefit is conditional, and the condition is on the receiving side rather than the giving side. That asymmetry is stated without apology and without explanation, because it is a fact about guidance rather than a policy: direction cannot be delivered to someone who refuses to be directed.

**Its Place in the Chapter**

The verse arrives at a turning point. Everything before it has been argument, warning and description; everything after it is response — rejoicing, gratitude, and the conduct of the community in the world. The verse supplies the transition by naming what has been given. Having established that the audience cannot provide for itself, guide itself, or judge itself, the chapter states what has been supplied for each of those needs. The verse is thus a summary of the sūrah's positive claim, and it is stated in the plainest possible terms: something has come, it addresses the interior, it gives direction, and it is mercy. The next verse tells the audience what to do with that fact.

**Which Term Answers Which Need**

The four descriptions are not interchangeable, and the distinction is practical. A person who knows what is right but cannot bring himself to do it needs the exhortation — the warning that moves. A person who is confused, doubtful, or divided inside needs the cure. A person who does not know which way to go needs the guidance. And a person who has failed and fears that the failure is final needs the mercy. The verse names all four because the audience it addresses contains all four conditions, and the same reader moves between them over a lifetime. The claim being made is therefore not that the revelation is useful in a general sense but that it is equipped for each of the states a person actually occupies. That specificity is what distinguishes the description from an ordinary commendation of a book.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:58

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “In Allah’s grace and mercy let them rejoice. That is far better than whatever ˹wealth˺ they amass.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Instruction to Rejoice**

The verse commands a feeling, and that is worth pausing on. *Falyafraḥū* — let them rejoice. The imperative form is used, which means rejoicing is being treated as something a person can be directed to do rather than as a mood that arrives unbidden. The Qur'an does not often command emotions, and where it does, the command is attached to an object: rejoice in this, and not in that. The verse therefore assumes two things. First, that joy is directed — it always has an object, and the object can be chosen. Second, that people are in fact rejoicing, and usually at the wrong things. The command is not a call to manufacture an emotion from nothing; it is a correction of the target. Read this way, the verse is practical rather than devotional: it tells a person where to place the rejoicing he already has.

**What the Grace and the Mercy Are**

The objects of rejoicing are named as God's *faḍl* and His *raḥma*, and the exegetical tradition identifies them concretely: the bounty is the Qur'an, and the mercy is Islam — or, in another reading, the Prophet himself, who was sent as a mercy to the worlds. “We have sent you ˹O Prophet˺ only as a mercy for the whole world” (21:107). Both identifications are supported by the immediate context, since the preceding verse has just described the revelation as guidance and mercy. The point of naming them is to make the objects specific. Rejoicing is being directed at something that has been given, something that arrived in history, something that can be described and examined. The verse is not commending a general feeling of blessedness; it is pointing at the thing described in the previous verse and saying that this is what deserves the response.

**Better Than What They Amass**

The comparison is explicit and it is the verse's centre. The grace and mercy are better than *mā yajmaʿūn* — what they gather or amass. The verb is the same one used of collecting wealth, and the chapter has already described the collecting in detail: the ornaments gathered, the wealth hoarded, the garden tended, the enjoyment used up. The verse sets the two against each other and declares a result. The comparison is not between a spiritual good and a material evil; it is between two things that both have value, one of which is better. That is what makes the statement useful. A person who has amassed something has not necessarily done wrong, and the verse does not say so. It says that what has been given to him is better than what he has gathered, and it leaves him to act on the comparison.

**The Chapter's Other Rejoicings**

The command gains its force from what has come before it. This chapter has described rejoicing that went wrong. The owner of the garden was described in terms of his enjoyment; the people of the world were described as content with the life of this world and at rest in it; the parables of water and rain ended with the owners rejoicing in the crop. In each case the rejoicing was attached to something perishable, and in each case the verse supplied the ending. This verse reverses the pattern. The object of rejoicing here cannot be cut down, cannot be withheld, and cannot be lost through a bad season, because it is not a thing in the world. The command is therefore not a prohibition of other joys but a statement about their durability relative to this one.

**Joy as a Marker of Belief**

There is a further implication in the placement of the command. Rejoicing at the revelation is a description of what believers actually do, and the verse states it as an instruction because it is also a test. A person who has been given what the previous verse describes and does not rejoice has not received it in the way it was given. The Qur'an treats the response to revelation as diagnostic elsewhere, and the response is not merely intellectual assent but a change in what a person values. The verse therefore functions as a question addressed to the reader: what are you rejoicing in? The answer is available at any moment, and it is more informative than any statement of belief, because rejoicing is not easily performed. A person can recite a creed without believing it; he cannot rejoice at something he does not value. The verse therefore offers the reader a test that cannot be passed by saying the right words.

**Why the Verse Follows the Description**

The sequence of the two verses is exact. The first describes what has come; the second orders the response. Description without the command would leave the revelation as information; the command without the description would leave the rejoicing without an object. Together they make a complete statement: here is what has been given, and here is what it deserves. The verse closes the unit that began with the questions about provision and guidance. The audience was asked who provides and who guides; the answer has now been given in the form of a thing that has arrived, and the response to it has been specified. What follows turns to the people who made their own provisions lawful and unlawful, and the contrast between the two units is deliberate.

**Rejoicing at What Cannot Be Amassed**

There is a structural point in the verse's comparison. The verb used for what people gather is the verb of accumulation, and accumulation has a defining feature: the accumulated thing is held, defended, and at risk. The grace and mercy described in the previous verse cannot be accumulated. They are received or they are not, and receiving them does not remove them from anyone else. The verse therefore contrasts two modes of relation to a good rather than two goods. Amassing is competitive and anxious; receiving is neither. A person who has understood that difference has understood most of what the chapter has been saying about wealth, and the command to rejoice is the practical form of that understanding.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:59

> Ask ˹the pagans, O Prophet˺, “Have you seen that which Allah has sent down for you as a provision, of which you have made some lawful and some unlawful?” Say, “Has Allah given you authorization, or are you fabricating lies against Allah?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Provision Came Down Whole**

The verse begins with what God did, and the wording is precise. He sent down provision — *anzala* — and the exegetical tradition notes that the verb here carries the sense of created and supplied, as in the statement about cattle: “And He produced for you four pairs of cattle” (39:6). The provision is described as having come from God in a complete state. Nothing about it was partial, conditional, or awaiting human completion. The verse's first move is therefore to establish the origin of the thing being discussed, and it does so before raising the charge. The audience is not being accused of inventing the provision; they are being accused of altering its terms after receiving it. That distinction is what gives the question its force.

**Then It Was Divided by Human Decision**

The second clause states what the audience did with it: you made some of it lawful and some unlawful. The practice is documented elsewhere in the Qur'an in concrete detail. “They say, ‘These cattle and crops are reserved—none may eat them except those we permit,’ so they claim. Some other cattle are exempted from labour and others are not slaughtered in Allah's Name—falsely attributing lies to Him. He will repay them for their lies” (6:138). And the named categories are given: “Allah has never ordained the ˹so-called˺ baḥîrah, sâ'ibah, waṣîlah, and ḥâm camels. But the disbelievers just fabricate lies about Allah, and most of them lack understanding” (5:103). The verses describe a system of religious restrictions built around categories God had not established — animals declared sacred, others declared forbidden, others released from work — all attributed to divine command.

**“Have You Seen?”**

The question that opens the verse, *araʾaytum*, is not a request for visual information. It means: have you considered, have you examined this? The same formula introduced the questions earlier in the chapter about provision, hearing, sight, life and guidance, and in each case it invited the audience to follow a line of reasoning to its end. Here the reasoning is shorter and sharper. The provision came from God; you divided it; on what basis? The question does not need to be answered because the answer does not exist, and that is what the audience is being asked to notice. The formula is used because it puts the audience in the position of examining their own practice rather than being told about it, which is the chapter's consistent method.

**Authorization or Fabrication**

The second command supplies the alternatives, and there are only two. Has God given you permission, or are you fabricating lies against Him? The disjunction is exhaustive. Either the restriction has a divine source, in which case it can be produced, or it does not, in which case it is a lie attributed to God. There is no third option — no category of human religious legislation that stands on its own authority and yet binds conscience. The Qur'an states the prohibition in general terms elsewhere: “Do not falsely declare with your tongues, ‘This is lawful, and that is unlawful,’ ˹only˺ fabricating lies against Allah. Indeed, those who fabricate lies against Allah will never succeed” (16:116). The verse here makes the same point in the form of a question with no available answer.

**A Statement About Who May Legislate**

Read as a principle, the verse is one of the clearest in the Qur'an on the question of religious authority. Making something lawful or unlawful is an act of legislation, and the verse treats it as belonging exclusively to the One who sent the provision down. The reasoning is not merely jurisdictional. A restriction attributed to God carries a weight that no human decision can support, and to attach that weight to a human preference is to misuse both. The chapter has spent fifty-eight verses establishing that God provides, guides, gives life and judges; the verse draws the consequence for the one domain the audience had claimed for itself. If everything else belongs to Him, the terms of His provision belong to Him as well. The verse is therefore a limit on religious authority stated in the form of a question, and the limit is not negotiable, since the alternatives given admit no middle case.

**The Fourth Question in a Series**

The verse belongs to a sequence, and its position gives it additional weight. The chapter asked who provides from the heavens and the earth, who owns hearing and sight, who brings the living from the dead, who guides to the truth. Each question was answered correctly by the audience. This verse returns to the first of those subjects — provision — and turns the answer into a charge. The audience has already agreed that God provides. The verse asks what follows from that agreement when the provision is divided by human decision. The method is the chapter's standard one: take what the audience has conceded, and ask it to hold the concession consistently. The chapter has been doing this since the opening verses, and the charge here is the point at which the method produces an accusation rather than a conclusion.

**The Charge in Its Historical Setting**

The practices the verse attacks were concrete and socially entrenched. Certain camels were declared sacred and released from labour; others were reserved so that only specified people could eat them; some animals were slaughtered without God's name being invoked; crops and livestock were divided into categories of permitted and forbidden by tribal decision. Each restriction was presented as religious, and each carried the weight of divine command. The verse's charge is therefore not about food preferences or local custom. It is about the attribution — the act of attaching God's authority to a human arrangement. That is why the alternatives given are authorization or fabrication, with nothing between them, and why the same charge is repeated wherever the practice is described.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:60

> What do those who fabricate lies against Allah expect on Judgment Day? Surely Allah is ever Bountiful to humanity, but most of them are ungrateful

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About Expectation**

The verse opens with a question that has no answer, and the absence of an answer is the point. What do those who fabricate lies against God reckon will happen on the Day of Resurrection? The word *ẓann* covers reckoning, supposing and expecting, and the question asks what expectation the fabricators are operating under. Nothing in the preceding verse suggested they had one. They had made things lawful and unlawful in God's name and had not appeared to consider the consequences. The question therefore exposes a gap rather than requesting information. It asks the audience to state the plan, and the inability to state one is the answer. The same method runs through the chapter: a question that cannot be answered reveals the position it was hiding.

**The Bounty and the Ingratitude in One Sentence**

The second sentence is the verse's balance. God is possessed of bounty toward humanity; most of them are ungrateful. The two clauses are placed side by side without a connecting argument, and the juxtaposition does the work. The formula is a refrain in the Qur'an: “Surely your Lord is ever Bountiful to humanity, but most of them are ungrateful” (27:73); “Surely Allah is ever Bountiful to humanity, but most people are ungrateful” (40:61). The repetition indicates a settled observation rather than a rhetorical flourish. The bounty is stated as a fact about God's dealings, and the ingratitude as a fact about human response, and the verse does not attempt to reconcile them. The disproportion is the content of the statement.

**What the Bounty Consists In**

The tradition specifies the bounty, and the specification matters because it determines what ingratitude means here. It is not merely material provision. The enumeration includes intelligence, the sending of messengers, and the revelation of books — the capacities and the guidance together. The chapter has described each of these: the creation of hearing, sight and intellect; the messengers sent to every community; the Book sent down as an exhortation, a cure, a guidance and a mercy. The verse therefore gathers the sūrah's own account of what has been given. Against that list, the fabrication described in the previous verse is not a small offence. It is the misuse of the intelligence that was given, directed against the messengers who were sent, in order to alter the terms of a book that had been revealed.

**Ingratitude as a Failure to Use**

The definition of ingratitude supplied by the exegetical tradition is active rather than emotional. The ungrateful are those who do not employ their intellects to ponder God's signs, who do not follow the call of His messengers, and who do not listen to and benefit from His revealed books. Each item names a use that was not made. The Qur'an describes the scale of what is being declined: “And He has granted you all that you asked Him for. If you tried to count Allah's blessings, you would never be able to number them. Indeed humankind is truly unfair, ˹totally˺ ungrateful” (14:34). The verse therefore treats ingratitude as a failure of employment rather than a failure of feeling. A person can be grateful in sentiment and ungrateful in practice, and it is the practice that the verse measures.

**“Most of Them”**

The qualifier appears throughout this chapter and it should be read as a finding rather than as a rhetorical habit. Most of them follow assumption; most of them do not give thanks; most of them do not know; most of them are unaware of the meeting. The word is a statement about the observed distribution. Its presence does two things at once: it keeps the statement accurate, and it leaves the minority open. The verse does not say that ingratitude is necessary or that gratitude is impossible. It reports the proportion and leaves the reader on whichever side of it he finds himself. In a chapter that has been addressing a specific audience with specific charges, the qualifier is the one door left open, and it is left open without comment. The reader is not told which side he is on; the verse leaves him to find out.

**The End of a Unit**

This verse closes a section that began with the questions about creation and provision. The unit has moved through a fixed sequence: what God has made, what the audience has done with it, what will follow, and what has been given in the meantime. The final verse states the summary judgement — bounty on one side, ingratitude on the other — and does so without introducing anything new. The turn that follows is toward the Prophet and the community: what he is to do, what they are to expect, and how the two groups will be distinguished. The verse therefore stands as a conclusion rather than a transition, and its closing word is the one that will govern everything after it. Ingratitude is not presented as the end of the matter; it is presented as the condition the remainder of the chapter addresses.

**The Two Words That Frame What Follows**

Bounty and ingratitude are the terms on which the rest of the chapter will run. The Prophet is about to be told what to say and how to stand; the two groups are about to be distinguished; the people of Nineveh are about to be held up as the one community that believed in time. Each of those passages is a working out of the pair stated here. What God has given is described as bounty, which means it was not owed and cannot be claimed; what people do with it is described as gratitude or its absence, which means the response is theirs. The verse therefore sets up the remainder of the sūrah in a single sentence, and it does so by naming both sides of the relation rather than only one.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:61

> There is no activity you may be engaged in ˹O Prophet˺ or portion of the Quran you may be reciting, nor any deed you ˹all˺ may be doing except that We are a Witness over you while doing it. Not ˹even˺ an atom’s weight is hidden from your Lord on earth or in heaven; nor anything smaller or larger than that, but is ˹written˺ in a perfect Record

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Things Named, One Witness Over All of Them**

The verse lists three items and then puts all of them under a single gaze. What you are occupied with. What you recite of the Qur'an. What you all are doing. The three are not a random selection. The first covers the Prophet's ordinary activity — the tasks, errands and dealings of a life. The second covers the one act that defines his role: the recitation itself. The third widens the frame from the singular to the plural, taking in the community and everyone in it. The progression moves from a man's day, to a man's vocation, to a world's conduct. Nothing in the sequence is exempted, and nothing is added by exception. The verse then states the consequence in one clause: We are a Witness over you while you are engaged in it. The word for witness is the same one used elsewhere of God's testimony to His own unity — “Allah ˹Himself˺ is a Witness that there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him” (3:18) — and it carries the same weight of formal attestation rather than casual notice.

**The Witness Is Present During the Act**

The temporal clause is the verse's most precise feature. Not after the deed, not when it is reported, not at the reckoning: *while you are engaged in it*. The witnessing is contemporaneous with the action, which changes what the verse is claiming. A record made afterward can be incomplete; a witness present during the act cannot be. The Qur'an makes the same point about the recording itself: “not a word does a person utter without having a ˹vigilant˺ observer ready ˹to write it down˺” (50:18), and again in the description of those who are observed by vigilant, noble scribes who know whatever you do (82:10–12). The verse therefore removes the possibility of an interval in which something could escape. There is no private moment, and the verse says so without dramatizing it — simply by placing the witnessing inside the doing.

**The Atom's Weight**

The second sentence turns from activity to measurement. Not an atom's weight — *mithqāl dharra*, the weight of a small ant or of a speck of dust — escapes your Lord, on earth or in the heavens. The phrase is the Qur'an's standard unit of the negligible, and it appears in the sūrah of the earthquake where the reckoning is described: “So whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it. And whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it” (99:7–8). The pairing there is exhaustive, and the unit is the smallest thing the language had. The verse here adds the two locations — earth and heaven — which closes the spatial dimension as well as the quantitative one. Nothing is too small to be noticed, and there is no place where the noticing does not reach.

**Smaller and Larger, in Both Directions**

The clause that follows is easy to read quickly and easy to lose. Nor anything smaller than that, nor anything larger. The escalation runs in both directions, and the second direction matters more. It would have been enough to say that nothing smaller than an atom is hidden; the verse adds that nothing larger is hidden either. That addition is not redundant. It forecloses the assumption that magnitude brings obscurity — that a great matter is too complex, too spread out, or too politically entangled to be seen whole. Small things are hidden by size; large things are hidden by scale. The verse denies both forms of hiding in a single phrase, and it does so in the order that moves from the obvious case to the less obvious one.

**The Perfect Record**

The verse ends with a book. Everything described is in a *kitāb mubīn* — a clear, manifest, plain Record. The exegetical tradition explains the term as a book God has written in which He has fixed the forms of all knowable things, and the Qur'an uses the same phrase of revelation itself: “There certainly has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book” (5:15). The double use is deliberate. The record of deeds and the record of guidance are described with the same words, which makes the two inseparable: what is written down will be read against what was sent. The verse therefore does not present the recording as a hostile act of surveillance. It presents it as part of an order in which things are known, written, and eventually made plain — and in which the standard of judgment has already been delivered.

**Why the Verse Begins With the Prophet**

The address starts with the Prophet and only then widens. That order is significant and it has two effects at once. The first is consoling: a man who has been told repeatedly that he cannot guide, hasten or bring the punishment is now told that everything he is doing is seen. The second is sobering, and it applies to him first. The witnessing is not a privilege reserved for the messenger's supporters; it is stated over him before it is stated over anyone else. The verse therefore establishes the standard in the person who carries it. A reader who notes the order will notice that the community's deeds are included in the third item, but that the first two belong to the Prophet alone — his tasks and his recitation. Both are under the same witness, and the recitation is not exempt because of its source.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:62

> There will certainly be no fear for the close servants of Allah, nor will they grieve

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Negations Facing Two Directions**

The verse removes two things, and they are not the same kind of thing. Fear faces forward; it is the anticipation of something not yet arrived. Grief faces backward; it is the presence of something already lost. Between the two they cover the whole of a person's relation to time — what is coming and what has gone — and the verse declares both absent. The emphatic opening, *alā*, functions as an announcement: take notice. What follows is then stated absolutely, with no qualification about degree, duration or circumstance. The structure is unusual for a promise. Most descriptions of reward describe something positive to be gained; this one describes two burdens to be removed, and the removal is the reward. A person who has neither fear nor grief has nothing left to suffer, and the verse treats that as sufficient.

**Who the Awliyāʾ Are**

The term translated as close servants is *awliyāʾ*, the plural of *walī*, and it is one of the richest words in the Qur'an's vocabulary of relation. Its root carries nearness, protection, allegiance and succession, and the same word is used of God's relation to believers and of believers' relations to one another. The Qur'an regulates those relations carefully: “Believers should not take disbelievers as guardians instead of the believers—and whoever does so will have nothing to hope for from Allah— unless it is a precaution against their tyranny” (3:28). Here the word is used of God's own friends, and the tradition reads it at two levels. In the general sense it denotes the reverent among the believers; in the specific sense it denotes a particular group, closer to what later religious language would call saints. The verse's own next line supplies the definition, so the category is not left to be filled by anyone's preference.

**What Is Reported About Them**

The tradition preserves a description of this group in the Prophet's words: when they are seen, God is remembered. The description is remarkable because it is entirely about effect rather than status. Nothing is claimed about their rank, their knowledge or their miracles; the only test given is what happens to an observer in their presence. That is consistent with the definition that follows in the next verse, which is behavioural rather than honorific. The same concern with nearness to God is developed at length in a ḥadīth qudsī recorded in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 6502, on the authority of Abū Hurayra: God says, “Whoever shows hostility to a pious worshipper of Mine, I declare war against him,” and then describes how a servant draws near through the obligatory acts and continues to draw near through supererogatory ones until God loves him. The report grounds the verse's category in practice rather than in title.

**Why There Is No Fear**

Fear in the Qur'an is almost always rational, and the verse does not say that these people are brave. It says they have nothing to fear, which is a statement about their situation rather than their temperament. Fear arises where an account is open and the outcome uncertain. The verse's premise, stated two verses earlier in this chapter and throughout the Book, is that the account has already been made and that the witnesses have been present throughout it. A person who has attended to his obligations, avoided what was forbidden, and kept God's command in view has no unresolved matter ahead of him. The absence of fear is therefore not a psychological achievement. It is the natural condition of someone whose case has been settled in his favour, and the verse states it as a fact about the Hereafter rather than as a state to be cultivated.

**Why There Is No Grief**

Grief is the harder of the two to remove, because it concerns what is already gone. A person can be reassured about the future; he cannot be reassured about the past. The verse nevertheless removes grief, and the reason lies in what these people lost. What is grieved is normally the loss of something valued — wealth, position, people, time. The category described here is defined by having valued something that cannot be lost, and by having spent on it rather than hoarded it. Nothing given for God's sake is gone in the sense that generates grief, because the transaction is recorded and the return guaranteed. The verse therefore does not describe people who never suffered loss; it describes people whose losses were made in a direction where loss does not occur.

**What the Verse Does in the Chapter**

The verse arrives immediately after the statement about the witness over every act. That placement is deliberate, and it answers a question the previous verse raises. If nothing escapes notice — not an atom's weight, not anything smaller or larger — then the prospect is severe for anyone who has neglected his account. The verse supplies the other half of the same fact. The witnessing that makes the negligent person's case certain also makes the attentive person's safety certain, and it is the same witnessing in both cases. The chapter therefore does not present the record as a threat in isolation. It presents it as the ground of two outcomes, and it names the second outcome first, in a single sentence, with an announcement to make sure it is not missed. The severity of the record and the safety of the attentive are the same fact seen from two sides, and the verse chooses the side that the reader needs to hear before the other.

**The Two Negations Are Stated Absolutely**

Neither negation is qualified, and the absence of qualification matters. The verse does not say that fear will be reduced, or that grief will be brief, or that both will be outweighed. It says they will not come and will not occur. That is a stronger claim than most descriptions of reward make, and it is why the verse opens with an announcement rather than simply stating the fact. The tradition reads the negation as complete rather than comparative: these people are not merely less afraid than others. The claim is therefore about a change of condition rather than a change of degree, and the change is explained by the two verses that follow — a defined category, and a guarantee that the promise to it cannot be altered.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:63

> ˹They are˺ those who are faithful and are mindful ˹of Him˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Category Defined in Two Words**

The previous verse announced that God's close servants have no fear and no grief, and it did not say who they are. This verse supplies the definition, and the definition is two words: those who believe and are mindful. *Āmanū* — they believed, they were faithful. *Yattaqūn* — they are mindful, they guard themselves, they keep God's command in view. Nothing else is added. No lineage, no office, no spiritual state, no title, no lineage of teachers, no miracle, no knowledge beyond what belief and mindfulness require. The verse therefore closes the category against every claim that might be made on grounds other than these two. A reader looking for a description of the saints will find here the plainest possible one, and its plainness is the point: the category is public, and anyone can check whether he meets it.

**Why the Definition Is So Short**

The brevity deserves attention because the verse could have said much more. The surrounding passage has described the witness over every act, the record of every atom, the good news in both worlds. In the middle of all that, the definition of God's friends is given in six words of Arabic. The shortness is not a defect of the description; it is a claim about the thing described. Whatever nearness to God consists in, it is not complicated, not esoteric, and not hidden behind a technical vocabulary. The two terms are the same two terms that open the Qur'an's account of the guided — belief in what is unseen, and mindfulness of God. The verse therefore places its category at the centre of ordinary religious life rather than at its margins, and it does so by refusing to elaborate.

**Belief and Mindfulness Together**

The two terms are joined, and the conjunction is meaningful. Belief without mindfulness is a claim that does not reach conduct; mindfulness without belief is discipline without an object. The Qur'an pairs them constantly because each needs the other, and the pairing appears wherever a state is being defined rather than merely praised. Belief supplies the content — what is held to be true about God, His messengers and the return. Mindfulness supplies the posture — the sustained attention that keeps the content operative. Neither is a mood. The first is a set of commitments, and the second is a habit of conduct. The verse's definition is therefore a description of a life rather than of an experience, and it is stated in the present tense for the second term, which indicates something ongoing rather than something achieved once.

**The Present Tense of the Second Word**

The verb forms are worth noticing. They believed — a completed act, stated in the past. They are mindful — a continuing state, stated in the imperfect. The asymmetry is exact. Belief is entered; mindfulness is maintained. A person becomes a believer by an act that cannot be repeated, and remains mindful only by an attention that must be renewed. The verse therefore describes the category as consisting of people who have done one thing and are still doing another. That is a realistic description, and it excludes both the person who claims a past belief he no longer practices and the person who practices attentively without having committed to anything. Neither half alone meets the definition, and the verse gives no indication that either can be substituted for the other. The description is therefore continuous rather than terminal — there is no point at which a person finishes being mindful and simply is.

**What the Definition Excludes**

Read as a boundary, the verse is severe in what it leaves out. The exegetical tradition speaks of a special group within the general one, and the description given of them is that when they are seen, God is remembered. Even that description is an effect rather than a qualification. Nothing in the verse grants the category on the basis of a spiritual rank conferred by others, a lineage of initiation, or a private experience that cannot be examined. The two conditions are the ones the whole Book requires of everyone. What distinguishes the group is therefore not an extra requirement but the degree to which the ordinary requirements are met, and degree is invisible to anyone but God. The verse keeps the definition public precisely so that no one can claim the category on grounds the community cannot assess.

**Why the Definition Comes Between Two Promises**

The verse sits between the promise of freedom from fear and grief and the promise of good news in both worlds. Its position makes it function as a condition rather than as a description. The reader encounters the first promise, is told who receives it, and is then told what else is given. The order prevents the promises from being taken as general reassurance. They belong to a category, the category is defined, and the definition is available to anyone willing to meet it. The chapter has spent sixty-two verses describing the argument, the warnings and the two outcomes; this verse states the criterion in the smallest space the chapter uses, and leaves the application to the reader. No intermediary is required, and no one is entitled to decide on his behalf whether he has met it.

**The Definition Uses the Chapter's Own Vocabulary**

Both terms in the definition have already been at work in this chapter. Belief is what the audience has been accused of lacking, and it is what the messengers demanded from their people. Mindfulness — *taqwā* — is the quality the chapter's parables turn on: the garden cut down belongs to people who were not mindful, and the Home of Peace is prepared for those who are. The definition therefore introduces nothing new. It names the two qualities the chapter has been describing from its opening verses, and it identifies the people who possess them as God's friends. That continuity is what makes the definition credible. A category invented at the point of reward would be suspect; a category built from the chapter's own terms is simply the naming of what has already been described.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:64

> For them is good news in this worldly life and the Hereafter. There is no change in the promise of Allah. That is ˹truly˺ the ultimate triumph

**Expanded Commentary**

**Good News in Both Worlds**

The verse extends the promise beyond the Hereafter, and that extension is the verse's distinctive claim. *Bushrā* — good news, glad tidings — is given to them in the life of this world and in the life to come. Most of the Qur'an's promises are deferred, and deferral is what makes faith difficult. This verse states that part of the return is immediate. The believer is not only promised something later; he is given something now, and the something now is of the same kind as the something later. The wording does not specify a quantity or a form, which leaves the claim general enough to cover whatever a believer actually experiences. The verse therefore answers a common objection to religious commitment — that it costs the present for the sake of the future — by denying the premise. The present is not only paid from; it is paid to.

**What the Worldly Good News Is**

The tradition identifies the immediate portion concretely, and the identification is interior rather than material. It is the sweetness and the joy a person experiences in contemplating his Lord — the settled satisfaction that comes from a life ordered toward God rather than toward acquisition. That description is consistent with everything the chapter has said about wealth. The good news is not a larger share of the world's provision; it is a different relation to it. The verse does not promise prosperity, and it would be a misreading to take it that way, since the surrounding passages have repeatedly described the prosperous denier as being in a worse position than the poor believer. What is promised is something the world cannot supply and cannot confiscate, which is why it is described as good news rather than as gain.

**No Change in the Words of God**

The second sentence is the guarantee, and it is stated as a fact about God rather than as a reassurance to the reader. There is no changing the words of God. The same formula appears elsewhere with the same force: “The Word of your Lord has been perfected in truth and justice. None can change His Words. And He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing” (6:115). And in the context of recitation and reliance: “Recite what has been revealed to you from the Book of your Lord. None can change His Words, nor can you find any refuge besides Him” (18:27). The three passages make the same point from different angles: the words are complete, they cannot be altered, and there is nowhere else to go. Applied here, the formula means that the promise to the friends of God is not contingent on anything they might subsequently do or fail to do, and not subject to revision by anyone.

**The Ultimate Triumph**

The final clause names the result with the Qur'an's own superlative. *Dhālika huwa l-fawzu l-ʿaẓīm* — that is the great, the ultimate triumph. The phrase is a technical term in the Qur'an for final success, and it is used where the outcome is described in full: escape from the Fire and entry into the Garden. Its use here is significant because the preceding verses described the reward in negative terms — no fear, no grief — and this clause converts the negation into a positive verdict. The verse therefore completes the description. The removal of fear and grief, the good news in both worlds, and the certainty of the promise together amount to what the Book calls the greatest success. Nothing further is added, because the term is already maximal. The verse does not need to describe the Garden, since the word it uses is the one the Book reserves for that description.

**Why the Guarantee Is Stated in the Middle**

The structure of the verse places the guarantee between the promise and the verdict, and the placement does work. A promise without a guarantee invites the question of whether it will be kept; a guarantee without a promise has nothing to attach to. The verse supplies the promise, immediately answers the doubt, and then draws the conclusion. The reader is not left to supply the middle step. That matters because the chapter has spent much of its length on the opposite case — people who were warned, promised, given time, and then overtaken. The guarantee here is the same guarantee that made those warnings certain, and the verse's insistence on it is an insistence that the two halves of God's speech carry equal weight.

**The Triumph Compared With the Chapter's Other Victories**

The chapter has described several kinds of success, and the comparison clarifies this one. There is the success of the man whose garden flourished and was then cut down. There is the success of those who gathered wealth and rejoiced in it. There is the success of a community given respite and increase. Each of those was a real success in its own terms and each of them ended. The triumph named here differs in kind rather than in degree: it belongs to people whose good news begins before the end arrives and continues after it. The verse therefore supplies the chapter's final answer to the question it has been asking since the opening verses — what a person should count as gain — and it answers without argument, in three short sentences.

**The Verse's Answer to Impatience**

There is one further thing the verse does, and it concerns the difficulty of waiting. A promise deferred indefinitely is hard to hold, and the chapter has described people who could not wait long enough to take the warning seriously. The verse answers that difficulty by locating part of the return inside the present. The good news is not entirely future, so the waiting is not empty. That is not a consolation designed to make deferral bearable; it is a claim about what belief does to a life while the life is still being lived. The verse therefore removes the sharpest objection to commitment — that it costs the only time a person certainly has — by denying that the cost is total.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:65

> Do not let their words grieve you ˹O Prophet˺. Surely all honour and power belongs to Allah. He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command Addressed to a Feeling**

The verse opens with a prohibition, and the thing prohibited is grief. *Lā yaḥzunka qawluhum* — let not their speech grieve you. The command is addressed to the Prophet, and it concerns not what people do but what they say. The distinction is significant. Harm can be guarded against; speech cannot be prevented, and its effect arrives without permission. The verse therefore addresses the one part of the situation the Prophet cannot control, and it addresses it directly rather than through an argument about why the words do not matter. That is why the instruction is a command rather than a consolation. Consolation would leave the grief in place and argue with it; the command treats the grief as something that can be declined, and it supplies the reason immediately afterward.

**The Answer Is a Fact, Not a Comfort**

What follows the prohibition is not reassurance but a statement of fact: all honour and power belong to God. The word *ʿizza* covers might, honour, invincibility and the standing that comes from being unbeaten. The verse locates all of it in one place, and the location is the whole answer. The speech that grieves the Prophet is speech that claims standing for its speakers — mockery, accusation, challenge, the assertion that he is a sorcerer or a fabricator. Each of those claims borrows a share of honour for the one making it. The verse denies the borrowing. Nothing of that kind exists outside God, so nothing said by a creature can raise or lower anyone's real position. The instruction not to grieve is therefore grounded in metaphysics rather than in psychology, which is what makes it possible to obey.

**Two Names Close the Verse**

The verse ends with the Hearing and the Knowing, and the pairing is exactly suited to the situation. Their speech is heard — nothing said in mockery escapes notice. Their intentions are known — what is concealed behind the words is not hidden either. The two names cover the two things the Prophet might doubt: whether the words reach God at all, and whether God knows what they mean. Both are answered in two words. The same pairing appears wherever a statement is made about God's attention to what people say and intend, and its placement at the end of a verse about hostile speech is not incidental. The verse therefore does not ask the Prophet to pretend the words are harmless. It tells him that they are heard and understood, which is the only answer that removes the need for grief.

**The Same Instruction Given Elsewhere**

The command is not unique to this verse, and the parallel is close. “So do not let their words grieve you ˹O Prophet˺. Indeed, We ˹fully˺ know what they conceal and what they reveal” (36:76). The two passages give the same instruction and support it with the same kind of reason: God knows what is hidden and what is open. The repetition indicates a sustained feature of the Prophet's situation rather than a single incident. He was spoken against continuously, and the Qur'an returns to the subject because the grief was a real and recurring cost of the task. The verse here adds what the other does not: the statement about honour. That addition gives the instruction its ground, and it is why this version of the command is the fuller of the two.

**Why Honour Cannot Be Held by Anyone Else**

The reasoning behind the claim is stated elsewhere in the Book, and it is severe. “And do not invoke any other god with Allah. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. Everything is bound to perish except He Himself. All authority belongs to Him” (28:88). If everything perishes except God, then no lasting honour can belong to anything else, and the honour that creatures hold is temporary by definition. The Qur'an draws the practical conclusion in a verse addressed to those who seek it: “Whoever seeks honour and power, then ˹let them know that˺ all honour and power belongs to Allah. To Him ˹alone˺ good words ascend, and righteous deeds are raised up by Him. As for those who plot evil, they will suffer a severe punishment” (35:10). The verse therefore redirects the search rather than condemning it. Honour is a real thing and it can be sought, but only where it exists.

**What the Verse Asks of the Messenger**

The demand is practical, and it is harder than it looks. The Prophet is asked to continue delivering a message to people who respond to it with contempt, and to do so without carrying the contempt into the work. Grief over hostile speech is not weakness; it is the natural response of someone who takes his task seriously. The verse does not tell him that the words are untrue or that they are trivial. It tells him that they change nothing about where honour lies, and that they are heard by the One who holds it. That is enough to remove the grief without removing the seriousness. The same instruction is what allows a messenger to keep speaking to an audience that has stopped listening, which is the position the chapter has been describing throughout. It is also what allows anyone else to keep doing the same work in a smaller setting.

**Where Honour Is Actually Sought**

The verse's claim has a practical consequence that is easy to miss. If all honour belongs to God, then the search for it is not forbidden — it is misdirected, and the misdirection is correctable. The Qur'an states the correction plainly: good words ascend to Him and righteous deeds are raised up by Him (35:10). Honour is therefore obtained by what goes up rather than by what is claimed down here. A person who understands that stops competing for standing with people and starts attending to the two things the verse names. The instruction to the Prophet is thus not a call to indifference. It is a redirection of the same desire that produced the hostile speech in the first place.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:66

> Certainly to Allah ˹alone˺ belong all those in the heavens and all those on the earth. And what do those who associate others with Allah really follow? They follow nothing but assumptions and do nothing but lie

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ownership Stated Before the Question**

The verse makes a claim and then asks a question that the claim already answers. To God belongs everyone in the heavens and everyone on the earth. What, then, do those who call on partners beside Him actually follow? The order is not decorative. The audience is being asked to consider the status of the beings they invoke, and the verse settles that status first: whatever those beings are, they belong to God. An angel is owned; a prophet is owned; a jinn is owned; a righteous person who has died is owned. The verse therefore removes the only basis on which association could make sense. If the invoked party is himself a possession, he cannot be a partner. The question that follows is then genuinely rhetorical — not because the answer is unknown, but because it has already been given in the first clause.

**The Question Is About Following, Not About Believing**

The verb the verse uses is significant. It does not ask what they worship or what they believe; it asks what they follow — *yattabiʿūna*. The choice shifts the enquiry from opinion to conduct. Association is not presented here as a mistaken idea but as a practice, something a person does with his life, day after day. That framing matters because it makes the charge harder to evade. A person can defend a belief by explaining how he came to hold it; he cannot easily explain what he is following when the thing followed has no existence independent of the following. The verse therefore asks about the object of the practice rather than the content of the creed, and the answer it supplies is that there is no object at all. What remains is the practice itself, sustained by nothing outside the one performing it.

**Following Assumption**

The answer is given in two halves, and the first names the substitute for an object. They follow nothing but assumption — *ẓann*. The word covers conjecture, supposition and the acceptance of something without evidence, and the Qur'an treats it as the characteristic epistemic state of those who reject revelation. The same pairing of assumption and falsehood appears in a warning addressed to the Prophet: “˹O Prophet!˺ If you were to obey most of those on earth, they would lead you away from Allah's Way. They follow nothing but assumptions and do nothing but lie” (6:116). The formulation is identical, which shows that the verse here is not making a local accusation but applying a general description. Assumption is not a mild word in this vocabulary. It denotes a claim held without support, and holding it is the actual offence.

**And Doing Nothing but Lie**

The second half is stronger. They do nothing but lie — the verb is from a root meaning to conjecture, to invent and to utter what has no basis. The verse therefore moves from what they rely on to what they produce. Assumption is the internal state; lying is its outward form, and in matters of religion the outward form is a statement attributed to God. That is why the charge escalates. A person who is merely mistaken holds a private error; a person who invents religious content has made a public claim on God's behalf. The chapter has already treated this exact offence where it described the division of provision into lawful and unlawful, and the verse here generalizes it. Association is not presented as a different religion but as fabricated speech, which places it in the same category as every other invented restriction.

**Why the Verse Follows the Statement About Honour**

The placement gives the verse additional force. The preceding verse told the Prophet that all honour and power belong to God, and it told him not to grieve over the speech of those who deny. This verse turns to that same speech and asks what stands behind it. The answer is that nothing does. The people whose words caused grief are following assumption and producing lies, which means their words carry no weight in either direction — they cannot lower the Prophet's standing and they cannot raise their own. The verse therefore completes the consolation by examining the source of the offence rather than merely discounting it. Once the source is identified, the grief loses its object, which is what the previous verse asked for. The two verses together form a single instruction: do not grieve, and here is why the words do not deserve grief.

**What Following Actually Requires**

Read as a criterion, the verse supplies a test that can be applied to any claim. To follow something, there must be something to follow: a known object, a known source, and a known reason. The verse denies all three for the association it describes, and it does so by naming what has taken their place — conjecture in the position of knowledge, invention in the position of report. The test is available to anyone. A religious practice can be asked where it came from, who authorized it, and what evidence supports it, and a practice that cannot answer those questions is, on the verse's own account, not a following but a fabrication. The three questions are not difficult to ask, which is what makes the refusal to ask them significant. The chapter has been applying this test since its opening questions about creation and provision, and this verse states it in the shortest form the chapter uses anywhere.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:67

> He is the One Who has made the night for you to rest in and the day bright. Surely in this are signs for people who listen

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Ordinary Things Made Into Evidence**

The verse takes the most familiar pair in human experience — night and day — and treats them as proof. Nothing is invoked that requires travel, study or special access. Everyone addressed has slept and everyone has worked by daylight, and the verse asks them to consider what that arrangement implies about its source. The method is deliberate. The chapter has argued from provision, from the senses, from life and death, and from the heavens; here it argues from the daily rhythm that nobody notices. A sign that requires expertise can be disputed by those without it. A sign that everyone experiences cannot, and the Qur'an uses that fact by choosing the most universal phenomenon available. It is also the cheapest to verify: no one has to take anyone's word for it, because everyone has already lived the evidence. The verse therefore makes its case to the widest possible audience, including those who have never heard an argument before.

**Rest and Seeing**

The two halves of the day are assigned two functions, and the functions are complementary. The night is made for rest — *sakan*, the stilling after exertion, the settling of a body and a household. The day is made for seeing — *mubṣir*, bright, giving sight. The first addresses recovery; the second addresses work. Together they describe a complete economy of a life: effort by day, restoration by night, repeated without interruption. The same description appears where the bounty is stated: “It is Allah Who has made the night for you to rest in and the day bright. Surely Allah is ever Bountiful to humanity, but most people are ungrateful” (40:61). And where the alternation is listed among the signs: “And one of His signs is your sleep by night and by day ˹for rest˺ as well as your seeking His bounty ˹in both˺. Surely in this are signs for people who listen” (30:23).

**The Verse Is Built From Two Existing Passages**

There is a formal feature worth recording. The first half of this verse is identical to part of 40:61, and the second half to part of 30:23. The verse is therefore assembled from wordings that already exist elsewhere in the Book. That is not a defect of composition; it is a feature of how the Qur'an builds its arguments. A formulation established in one place is reused in another, carrying its meaning with it. A listener who has heard the sūrah of the Forgiving Believer will recognize the phrase about night and rest; one who has heard the sūrah of the Romans will recognize the phrase about signs for those who listen. The repetition is what makes the recitation cumulative, and it is why the Qur'an can state a complex claim in a single line. A listener who has heard neither still receives the whole argument, because the wording carries its own explanation.

**“A People Who Listen”**

The closing phrase names the audience that benefits, and the verb is hearing rather than seeing. *Li-qawmin yasmaʿūn* — for a people who listen. The chapter has used similar phrases elsewhere, sometimes with reflection and sometimes with knowledge, and the variation is meaningful. The sign in this verse is not something observed from a distance; it is something lived. A person does not watch his own sleep as evidence; he undergoes it. Hearing is therefore the appropriate faculty, and the tradition glosses the phrase as those who listen by reflecting on God's signs. Listening here is not the passive reception of sound. It is the act of attending to something already present, which is why the Qur'an can call the alternation of night and day a sign rather than a mere fact.

**Why the Familiar Is Used**

The choice of the obvious over the spectacular is a consistent feature of the Qur'an's argumentation, and it has a purpose. A miracle can be dismissed as an anomaly; the daily return of daylight cannot. The verse therefore builds its case on the one phenomenon that admits no exception and no alternative explanation from experience. Every human society has organized itself around the alternation, and none has been able to alter it. That is the force of the phrase *jaʿala* — He made. The alternation is not presented as a fact that happens to be useful but as something arranged for a purpose, and the purpose is stated in the verse itself: rest and sight. An arrangement with a stated purpose implies an arranger, and that is the inference the verse leaves the listener to draw. The inference is not forced, which is why the verse names the kind of person who draws it.

**What Listening Requires**

The verse ends by identifying a quality rather than a proof, and that is instructive. Nothing prevents a person from experiencing night and day without drawing any conclusion, and most people do exactly that. The sign is available to everyone; the benefit is not. Listening requires the interruption of habit — a moment in which the familiar is looked at as though for the first time. The chapter has been asking for that interruption since its opening, and it has been describing the failure to make it as heedlessness. The failure is not intellectual. It is a refusal to stop long enough for the familiar to become visible again. The verse therefore does not add a new argument. It points at an existing arrangement and asks for attention, which is the smallest possible demand and the one most often refused.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:68

> They say, “Allah has offspring.” Glory be to Him! He is the Self-Sufficient. To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. You have no proof of this! Do you say about Allah what you do not know

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim, Then Four Responses**

The verse reports a statement and answers it four times over. They say: God has taken a child. Then: glory be to Him. Then: He is the Self-Sufficient. Then: to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth. Then: you have no proof. Then a final question about speaking without knowledge. The accumulation is unusual, and it indicates the gravity the Qur'an attaches to this particular claim. Other false statements in the chapter are corrected once; this one is met with a denial, a name, an ownership claim, an epistemic charge and a question. Each response attacks a different part of the assertion — its content, its implication, its consequence, its evidence and its manner. The verse therefore does not simply reject the claim; it dismantles it from five directions in a single breath.

**“Glory Be to Him”**

The first response is the shortest and the most absolute. *Subḥānah* — glory be to Him, far is He above what they say. The phrase is the Qur'an's formula of exclusion, used wherever something is asserted of God that cannot be. It does not argue; it removes. The effect is to place the claim outside the range of what can be said rather than inside the range of what can be disputed. The same formula introduces the correction elsewhere: “They say, ‘Allah has offspring.’ Glory be to Him! In fact, to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and the earth—all are subject to His Will” (2:116). The verse here follows that pattern exactly, and then goes further by naming the attribute that makes the claim impossible.

**The Self-Sufficient**

The name given is *al-ghanī* — the One who is without need, self-sufficient, requiring nothing from anything. The choice is precise, because offspring exists for a reason: for company, for help, for continuation, for the extension of a line. Every one of those reasons presupposes a need. A being who lacks nothing cannot have offspring in the sense the claim intends, since offspring answers a want that does not exist in Him. The name therefore does not merely deny the claim; it explains why the claim is incoherent. The same reasoning appears where the assertion is repeated with its own refutation attached: “O People of the Book! Do not go to extremes regarding your faith… Allah is only One God. Glory be to Him! He is far above having a son!” (4:171). The name therefore does the work that an argument would otherwise have to do, and it does it in one word.

**Ownership as the Second Refutation**

The verse then states ownership: to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. The clause is not a change of subject. It is the second step of the refutation, and it works by removing the category in which offspring would have to exist. Everything that could be a child is a possession. An angel, a prophet, a righteous being — all of them fall inside the ownership being described, and a possession cannot be a partner or a son. The Qur'an makes the same point where the claim is stated most bluntly: “They say, ‘The Most Compassionate has offspring.’ You have certainly made an outrageous claim” (19:88–89). The outrageousness lies in placing a creature inside the category of the Creator, and the ownership clause is what makes that placement impossible. Everything invoked is already accounted for, and accounted for as belonging.

**“You Have No Proof”**

The fourth response changes register from theology to evidence. *Mā lakum min sulṭān* — you have no authority, no warrant, no proof. The word *sulṭān* is the Qur'an's technical term for demonstrable authorization, and it is invoked repeatedly in the context of association: those who take protectors beside God have no proof (3:151); those who dispute about God after He has been responded to have no proof before Him (42:16). The charge is therefore not that the claim is unpleasant but that it is unsupported. The verse does not ask the audience to produce a better argument; it states that they cannot produce any, which is a different and stronger assertion. A claim about God made without authorization is, on the verse's own terms, not a religious position but a fabrication. That is why the next verse states the consequence in the form of a general rule.

**Speaking About God Without Knowledge**

The final question is the verse's most general statement, and it applies beyond the claim about offspring. Do you say about God what you do not know? The question identifies the underlying offence, which is not the particular content but the manner of asserting it. Religious speech about God requires knowledge, and knowledge requires a source. The chapter has been building toward this point: the division of provision into lawful and unlawful was attacked on the same ground, and the general prohibition is stated elsewhere — “Do not falsely declare with your tongues, ‘This is lawful, and that is unlawful,’ ˹only˺ fabricating lies against Allah” (16:116). The verse therefore converts a theological dispute into a rule about speech, and the rule is one the reader can apply to his own statements. Silence about God is never presented as a loss; speech about Him without knowledge always is. The verse is therefore a limit on religious language as much as a warning about one particular claim, and the limit binds the believer no less than the denier.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:69

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Indeed, those who fabricate lies against Allah will never succeed.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Rule Stated Without Qualification**

The verse is one sentence, and it is universal in scope. Those who fabricate lies against God will not prosper. No time limit is given, no exception is made, no category of fabricator is excluded. The statement covers the person who invents a claim about God's nature, the person who attributes a prohibition He did not give, and the person who speaks in His name without authorization. The verse therefore generalizes the charge made in the previous verse and turns it into a principle. That is why it is so short. A rule with conditions needs elaboration; a rule without any does not, and the absence of qualification is what gives the sentence its weight. It is delivered as a command to speak, which means it is intended for public proclamation rather than for private reflection.

**Fabrication Is Not Error**

The word used is from the root *f-r-y*, which means to cut, to devise and to invent. It denotes something produced rather than something mistaken. The distinction is important and the Qur'an maintains it carefully. A person who holds a false belief through ignorance is described as unaware; a person who invents religious content is described as fabricating. The first is an epistemic failure, the second an act. The chapter has already used the term where it described the invention of lawful and unlawful categories, and the parallel formulation appears in a general prohibition: “Indeed, those who fabricate lies against Allah will never succeed” (16:116). The identity of the wording across the two chapters shows that this is a fixed charge with a fixed consequence, not a contextual rebuke. The same sentence therefore stands wherever the act occurs.

**“They Will Not Succeed”**

The consequence is stated in the Qur'an's own term for success. *Falāḥ* covers prosperity, deliverance, attainment and the reaching of what one sought. Its negation is therefore comprehensive. A fabricator may gain followers, wealth, position and even temporary religious authority, and the verse does not deny any of that. It denies *falāḥ*, which in the Qur'an's usage always extends beyond this life to the final outcome. The claim is that whatever a fabricator achieves, he does not arrive at the thing he was actually seeking, because the thing he was seeking is God's acceptance and the outcome he obtains is the opposite. The verse therefore makes a statement about the end rather than the middle, and it is the end that determines whether anyone succeeded.

The sequence gives the rule its edge. The previous verse reported the assertion that God has taken a child, refuted it, and then asked whether its speakers were saying about God what they do not know. This verse supplies the answer to that question in the form of a verdict. They are fabricating, and fabricators do not succeed. The placement converts a theological refutation into a practical warning, and it does so for the benefit of the listeners rather than the Prophet. The audience is being told where their claim stands and what follows from it. The verse is therefore not a comment on the previous one but its conclusion, stated in the shortest possible form.

**The Rule Includes the Listeners**

There is nothing in the verse that confines it to the people who made the claim about offspring. The formula is general — those who fabricate lies against God — and it applies to anyone who does what they did. That includes the audience hearing it, and it includes later communities. The Qur'an uses the same charge against the religious innovations of previous communities, connecting invented practice with invented attribution in a single category. The verse therefore functions as a standing rule rather than as a historical accusation. A reader who encounters it is not reading about someone else's offence; he is reading a description of an act he is capable of committing, and of the outcome that follows it.

Taken together with the verses around it, this sentence supplies the criterion the chapter has been applying throughout. God sent down provision; people divided it and attributed the division to Him. God is without need; people ascribed offspring to Him. In both cases the offence is the same — speech about God without authorization — and in both cases the verdict is the same. The chapter therefore does not treat these as separate errors requiring separate refutations. It treats them as instances of one act, and it states the consequence of that act once, plainly, in a verse that can be memorized. The brevity is what makes the standard portable.

**The Rule Applied to the Community That Inherits It**

There is a further use for the verse, and it falls on those who accept it rather than on those it condemns. A community that has been told what fabrication is now carries a responsibility about its own speech concerning God. Every statement made in His name — every ruling attributed to Him, every prohibition presented as His, every promise offered on His behalf — falls inside the category the verse describes. The rule is therefore a discipline as much as a warning, and it applies most strictly to those with the most authority to speak. The chapter's earlier charge about inventing lawful and unlawful categories makes the same point from the other direction: the danger is not confined to those outside the community. It belongs to anyone who speaks about God, which is why the verse is given as a public proclamation rather than as a private judgement. A private judgement can be revised without anyone noticing; a public proclamation stays on the record and binds the one who makes it as much as those who hear it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:70

> ˹It is only˺ a brief enjoyment in this world, then to Us is their return, then We will make them taste the severe punishment for their disbelief

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Little Enjoyment, and the Word That Limits It**

The verse opens with a qualifier that does most of the work. What the fabricators have is *matāʿ* — a brief enjoyment, a temporary provision, the use of a thing for a while. The word is the Qur'an's standard term for what the world offers, and its force lies in what it excludes: possession, permanence and ownership. Enjoyment is what a person gets from a thing he does not keep. The verse therefore concedes the fact without granting the interpretation. Yes, there is enjoyment. It is limited, and the limitation is stated in the same breath as the concession. The chapter has used the same vocabulary repeatedly — the enjoyment of the worldly life, the wealth used up, the garden cut down after its owners rejoiced — and this verse states the general rule behind all of those images in three words.

**Three Stages Marked by “Then”**

The verse moves in three steps, and each step is introduced by *thumma* — then. Enjoyment in this world. Then the return. Then the tasting of severe punishment. The particle is not neutral; it marks sequence with interval, and it is the same particle used where the stages of a life are described. The effect is to lay out a timeline with only three entries, and to make the first of them the shortest. Nothing intervenes between the enjoyment and the return except the passage of the term, and nothing intervenes between the return and the punishment except the judgment.
