The verse therefore compresses a whole life into a single sentence and leaves the reader with the proportions: one brief stage, then two permanent ones.

**The Return, Stated Again**

The middle clause is the hinge. *Ilaynā marjiʿuhum* — to Us is their return. The same formulation appeared earlier in this chapter where life and death were described, and it carries the same meaning: the destination is a person, not a place. The verse does not say that they will die; it says they will return, which presupposes that they came from somewhere and belong to someone. The wording also removes any suggestion of an intermediate destination. There is no other party to whom the return could be made, no alternative jurisdiction and no escape route. The chapter has been establishing this since its opening verses, and the clause states it in the fewest words available. Everything the fabricators gained is left behind at this point, and nothing of it can be carried into what follows.

**Tasting**

The verb is the same one used where the punishment was described earlier in the chapter, and the repetition is deliberate. To taste is to experience directly, without intermediary and without distance. The demand that produced the earlier passage was a demand to see; the answer was that they would taste. Here the same verb is applied to the fabricators, and the object is specified as severe punishment. The word for severe — *alīm* — denotes pain that reaches and remains, and the Qur'an uses it consistently of the punishment of those who denied. The verse therefore does not describe a symbolic consequence. It describes an experience, and it uses the vocabulary of the senses to say so, which is the strongest form of description available. Nothing is left to inference.

The final clause states why. *Bi-mā kānū yakfurūn* — for what they used to disbelieve, or because of their disbelief. The attachment of the reason is a consistent feature of the chapter's descriptions of punishment, and it appears in the same form where the wrongdoers are told that they are rewarded only for what they committed. The verse therefore does not present the punishment as arbitrary or as disproportionate. It presents it as the settlement of a specific account, and it names the entry in that account. Disbelief here is not merely the absence of belief; it is the act described in the previous verses — inventing speech about God and attributing it to Him. The reason clause closes the case by showing that the punishment matches the offence.

**What the Verse Closes**

This verse ends a unit that began with the claim about God's offspring. The sequence has been complete: the claim was reported, refuted on five grounds, given a verdict in the form of a general rule, and now given an outcome. Nothing further is said about the fabricators, because nothing further is needed. The verse's brevity matches the unit's conclusion — three stages, one reason, no elaboration. What follows turns away from the argument entirely and toward the stories of the earlier messengers, beginning with Noah. The shift is deliberate: the chapter has finished stating the case and now begins supplying its precedents.

The compression of a life into three stages is not a rhetorical device but an argument about proportion. The verse does not say the enjoyment is worthless; it says it is brief, and brevity is what makes the proportion visible. A person evaluating his choices needs to know how long each option lasts, and the verse supplies the durations: one short stage, then two that do not end. Nothing in the description requires the reader to despise the world. What it requires is that he weigh it correctly, and correct weighing depends on knowing the length of each term. The verse therefore performs a calculation rather than issuing a prohibition, and it leaves the whole of the conclusion with the reader to draw. The calculation can be performed at any moment and asks for nothing except an honest estimate of how long each of the two terms lasts.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:71

> Relate to them ˹O Prophet˺ the story of Noah when he said to his people, “O my People! If my presence and my reminders to you of Allah’s signs are unbearable to you, then ˹know that˺ I have put my trust in Allah. So devise a plot along with your associate-gods—and you do not have to be secretive about your plot—then carry it out against me without delay

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Relate**

The verse begins with an instruction rather than a statement: relate to them. *Utl* — recite, recount, tell. The chapter turns at this point from argument to narrative, and the turn is commanded rather than incidental. Stories in the Qur'an are not illustration; they are evidence of a different kind. An argument can be disputed, but a recorded outcome cannot, and the outcome is what the story supplies. The command is addressed to the Prophet and directed at the same audience that has been arguing with him, which means the story is not a digression for the believer's benefit. It is the next move in the dispute. The audience is being shown a case identical in structure to their own: a messenger, a people who found him unbearable, and an ending.

**Noah States the Problem Plainly**

Noah's speech opens by naming the difficulty rather than by answering it. If my presence is unbearable to you, and my reminding you of God's signs. The two items are joined, and the joining is exact. What the people cannot bear is not the message alone but the messenger's continuing presence among them — a man who will not leave, who will not stop, and whose existence is a daily reminder of what they have refused. The exegetical tradition glosses the first phrase as "if my being among you causes you distress" and the second as "my warning and admonishment to you". Noah therefore identifies the real source of the conflict with unusual clarity. It is not a disagreement about doctrine. It is the intolerable fact of a witness who remains.

**Trust Placed Before Any Response**

The answer Noah gives is not a defence, a compromise or a withdrawal. It is a statement of reliance: I have put my trust in God. The word is *tawakkalt*, and it denotes the consignment of an affair to another after having done what one can. The exegetical gloss is direct — I care what you will try to do to me not at all, because I have committed my affair to God. The placement of the statement matters. It comes before the challenge, not after it, which means the challenge is issued from a position of settled reliance rather than from defiance. A man who has not already placed his affair elsewhere could not speak as Noah speaks in the next clause. The trust is the ground of the courage, and the verse gives them in that order.

**The Challenge to Plot in the Open**

What follows is the most striking sentence in the passage. Devise your plan, you and your partners — and let there be no ambiguity about it. The demand for openness is the point. Conspiracy depends on concealment; Noah removes concealment from the equation and asks them to act in full view. The exegetical tradition reads the clause as an instruction not to be obscure, equivocal or hesitant — do not hide what you intend and do not act in a doubtful manner. The effect is to expose the weakness of the position. People who plot secretly are people who fear the consequences of being seen. By inviting the plot into the open, Noah demonstrates that his own case does not depend on their restraint. The challenge is therefore a test of their sincerity as much as of their power.

**“Grant Me No Respite”**

The final clause completes the challenge: carry it out against me and give me no delay. *Lā tunẓirūn* — do not respite me, do not hold back. The phrase is a direct inversion of what the audience has been demanding throughout this chapter. They asked God to hasten the punishment if the warning were true. Noah asks his enemies to hasten their own action if their position is true. The parallel is exact, and it is the verse's sharpest device. Both parties are being asked to act on their claim, and the one making the demand is the one with less to lose if it is granted. Noah's demand costs him nothing if his trust is well placed, and it would cost them everything if their gods had any power at all.

**Noah in the Chapter's Sequence**

Noah is the first of the messengers narrated here, and the choice is significant. He is described elsewhere as having remained among his people for a thousand years less fifty (29:14), which makes his case the longest recorded instance of the situation the chapter is describing. The same story is told in several places — in the Heights, in Hūd, in the Believers, in the Poets, in those Ranged in Ranks, in the Moon, and in the sūrah that bears his name. The repetition is not redundancy. Each account selects different parts of the same events, and this one selects the moment of confrontation, because that is what the chapter needs. The Prophet has been told repeatedly that he cannot compel belief; he is now shown a messenger who said so to his people's faces and then invited them to do their worst.

**Why the Story Arrives Here**

The verses immediately before have described the fabricators, their brief enjoyment and their return. The story that follows is the demonstration of what that description means in practice. A people who found their messenger unbearable, plotted against him, and were destroyed — the sequence is the one the chapter has been predicting in general terms. The audience hearing the story is in the position of Noah's people, and the verse's command to relate it is a command to make that clear. Nothing is added to the accusation; the precedent does the work.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:72

> And if you turn away, ˹remember˺ I have never demanded a reward from you ˹for delivering the message˺. My reward is only from Allah. And I have been commanded to be one of those who submit ˹to Allah˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Conditional That Concedes Everything**

The verse opens with a concession. If you turn away — the possibility is stated without protest. Noah does not argue against the turning, does not warn that it will fail, and does not ask for reconsideration. He accepts it as a live option and then makes a statement that only makes sense if the option is taken. The structure is unusual for a prophetic speech. Most appeals argue for acceptance; this one assumes refusal and asks what follows from it. What follows, on Noah's account, is not a change in his position but a clarification of it. The verse therefore removes any suggestion that the message was an offer with terms attached. There were never any terms.

**No Reward Ever Requested**

The central claim is stated absolutely: I have not asked you for any reward. The negation covers the past as well as the present, and it covers every form of payment. This is one of the recognized marks of prophecy in the Qur'an, and it is stated as a criterion rather than as a boast. The sūrah of Yā-Sīn puts it in the form of an instruction to the audience: “Follow those who ask no reward of you, and are ˹rightly˺ guided” (36:21). The two conditions are joined there as they are joined here — no payment, and right guidance — and the joining makes the absence of payment evidential. A person seeking gain from a message will shape it to please those who pay. A person seeking nothing can say what is true regardless of its reception. The claim therefore supports the message rather than merely describing the messenger.

**Why the Claim Matters to the Audience**

There is a further function to the statement, and it concerns suspicion. A people who reject a messenger commonly explain him by interest: he wants money, position, followers, or supremacy. The charge appears in this very chapter's account of Moses, where the accusation is that he has come so that supremacy may belong to him and his brother. Noah forecloses the charge before it can be made. He has asked for nothing, taken nothing, and accumulated nothing across a period of nine and a half centuries. The audience cannot explain his persistence by interest, and the verse leaves them without an alternative explanation except the one he offers. The claim is therefore defensive in the strict sense: it removes a defence available to the people who reject him.

The second clause follows from the first and completes it. If no reward has been requested from the people, then the reward must lie elsewhere, and Noah states where. The exclusivity is the point — *illā ʿalā llāh*, only upon God. The statement has two consequences. The first is independence: a man whose payment comes from God cannot be bought, pressured or silenced by those who refuse to pay. The second is certainty: the reward does not depend on the audience's response, which means the mission can fail in their terms and still succeed in his. The verse therefore explains how a messenger can spend centuries without converts and continue. His account is not with them.

**“I Have Been Commanded to Be Among Those Who Submit”**

The speech closes with a statement about himself, and the wording is careful. I have been commanded to be among those who submit — *an akūna mina l-muslimīn*. He does not say he is the first, the greatest or the only one. He says he has been commanded to belong to a group, and the group is defined by submission. The phrase places the messenger inside the community of the obedient rather than above it, which is a significant self-description for a prophet addressing a people who regard him as a rival. Submission is also the answer to the charge of seeking supremacy. A man who has been ordered to be one of the submitting cannot simultaneously be seeking to rule. The closing therefore answers the accusation implicit in the audience's rejection without naming it.

The two verses together form a complete address, and its structure is worth noting. It opens with trust placed in God and closes with submission to God. Between them sit the challenge to plot openly and the statement that no reward has been sought. The arrangement puts God at both ends and the audience in the middle, which is exactly the position the message assigns them. Nothing in the speech depends on their response: not the trust, not the challenge, not the reward, not the submission. The verse therefore leaves Noah with nothing to negotiate, and it leaves his people with nothing to explain his conduct except the truth of what he says.

**What the Speech Leaves the People With**

The address closes without a threat, a curse or a prediction of punishment, and the absence is meaningful. Noah has stated his reliance, issued his challenge, denied any interest in payment, and declared his submission. Nothing further is demanded from the audience, and nothing is offered to them in exchange for belief. The verse therefore leaves the people in the position the chapter describes throughout: free, informed and unpressured. That is not indifference. It is the only posture consistent with the claim being made. A messenger whose reward comes from God has no stake in the outcome that would justify coercion, and the speech ends by demonstrating that fact rather than asserting it. The people are left with a decision that is entirely theirs, and with a man whose conduct over centuries has already testified to the sincerity of what he says.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:73

> But they still rejected him, so We saved him and those with him in the Ark and made them successors, and drowned those who rejected Our signs. See then what was the end of those who had been warned

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Words That End a Millennium**

The verse opens with the shortest possible report: they denied him. *Fa-kadhdhabūhu*. After nine and a half centuries of warning, after the speech recorded in the previous verses, after the challenge to plot openly — the outcome is given in one verb. The compression is not a stylistic choice. It is a judgement on the length of the exchange. A thousand years of argument produce a single word of result, and the verse states that word without comment. The effect is to make the reader feel the disproportion between the effort expended and the response received, which is the disproportion the Prophet was living with. The verse does not moralize about it. It reports it and moves immediately to what God did.

**Salvation and Succession**

The response has two parts, and the first is positive. God saved Noah and those with him in the Ark, and He made them successors — *khulafāʾ*. The word denotes those who come after others and take their place, and the Qur'an uses it in several related senses. It is the word used of the authority placed on earth: “I am going to place a successive ˹human˺ authority on earth” (2:30). It is used of the general human condition: “He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth and elevated some of you in rank over others, so He may test you with what He has given you” (6:165). And it is used of Noah's people specifically, in the address to the ʿĀd: “Remember that He made you successors after the people of Noah and increased you greatly in stature” (7:69). The verse here makes the same point in miniature — the saved do not merely survive; they inherit.

**The Drowning in the Same Sentence**

The second part of the response is stated without pause: and We drowned those who denied Our signs. The two clauses are joined by a single conjunction, and the joining is the verse's structure. Salvation and destruction are not two separate events but one event seen from two sides, and the verse refuses to separate them. The same construction appears in the parallel account: “But they rejected him, so We saved him and those with him in the Ark, and drowned those who rejected Our signs. They were certainly a blind people” (7:64). The wording is nearly identical, which shows that this is a fixed formulation rather than an improvised summary. The pairing is what makes the story usable as a precedent: the outcome is not partial, and it is not delayed beyond the term.

The verse ends with a command to look. *Fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu l-mundharīn* — see, then, what the end of those who were warned was. The word for those warned — *mundharīn* — is the operative term. It does not say the end of the wicked, the stubborn or the powerful. It says the end of those who had been warned, which identifies the relevant fact about them. They were not destroyed for ignorance; they were destroyed after notification. That is the point the verse wants the reader to take, and it is the point that makes the story applicable to the audience hearing it. They too have been warned. The command to look is therefore not an invitation to gloat but a demand that the parallel be recognized.

**Why the Story Is Compressed**

The whole account occupies three verses: the speech, the speech's conclusion, and the outcome. Other chapters give the same story far more room, describing the building of the Ark, the mockery, the boiling of the oven, the loading of the pairs. This chapter gives none of that. The compression is deliberate, and it follows from what the chapter needs. The argument here is about the pattern — messenger, refusal, warning, outcome — and the details would obscure it. By stripping the story to its structure, the verse makes it transferable. A reader who has never heard the account of Noah can follow this one, because everything included is essential to the pattern being demonstrated.

The final command makes the audience explicit. They are the ones who must look, and what they are looking at is not ancient history but a description of their own position. The chapter has spent seventy-two verses making a case; this verse supplies the first precedent for its conclusion. The audience is being told that the sequence they are inside has been completed before, on a people who were warned by a man they found unbearable, and that the completion took the form described. The verse therefore does not ask them to feel anything about Noah's people. It asks them to observe the end, which is the only part of the story that bears on them.

**The Ark as the Only Distinction**

One detail in the verse carries more weight than its brevity suggests. Those who were saved were saved in the Ark, and nothing else distinguished them. They were not stronger, better armed, higher in status or more numerous than those who drowned. The single difference was that they were aboard when the water came. The verse therefore reduces salvation to a matter of position rather than merit in the worldly sense, and the position was available to anyone who accepted the warning. That is the harshest and clearest feature of the account. A thousand years of notice preceded the event, the means of safety was built in public view, and the mockery directed at its builder is recorded elsewhere in the Qur'an. The verse's compression removes every distraction from that sequence and leaves only the choice and the outcome.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:74

> Then after him We sent ˹other˺ messengers to their ˹own˺ people and they came to them with clear proofs. But they would not believe in what they had rejected before. This is how We seal the hearts of the transgressors

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Messengers Between Noah and Moses**

The verse moves from a single case to a sequence. After Noah, messengers were sent to their own peoples. The tradition identifies them: the pre-Islamic Arabian prophets Hūd, Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb, together with the biblical figures Abraham and Lot, among others. The phrase "to their people" is repeated deliberately — each messenger was sent to his own community, in its own language, addressing its own practices. The verse therefore establishes the pattern the chapter asserted earlier: for every community there has been a messenger. What is new here is the temporal ordering. The messengers are not a simultaneous plurality but a succession, each following the last, which makes the history a series of comparable cases rather than a single unusual event.

The verse states that they came with clear proofs — *bayyināt*, the evident things, the demonstrable signs. The word is the Qur'an's standard term for the evidence accompanying a messenger, and it is used consistently across the accounts. The verse then states the result: they would not believe. The combination is the verse's central fact, and it is presented without explanation at first. Evidence was given and belief did not follow. The verse does not say the proofs were insufficient, ambiguous or poorly delivered. It says they were clear and that they were rejected, and it then supplies the reason in the clauses that follow. The pairing is what makes the verse a case study rather than a complaint.

**“What They Had Rejected Before”**

The phrase is the verse's most difficult and most important element, and the tradition explains it. Those who rejected their messengers were already habituated to denial; they had closed themselves to the possibility of accepting guidance before it arrived. The rejection was therefore not a response to the proofs but a continuation of a prior disposition. The wording — they would not believe in what they had denied beforehand — indicates that the denial preceded the encounter. This is a claim about how refusal works over time. A single act of rejection is a decision; a settled habit of rejection is a condition, and the condition determines what a person can subsequently perceive. The verse therefore locates the failure before the evidence rather than in it.

**The Sealing of Hearts**

The final clause names the mechanism: thus do We seal the hearts of the transgressors. The sealing is a Qur'anic term with a precise meaning. It is described in the sūrah's opening: “Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment” (2:7). The image is of a closure that prevents anything from entering, and the Qur'an uses it of people whose repeated refusal has produced an inability. The verse here states it as a divine act — We seal — which raises the question of whether the sealing is arbitrary. The answer is supplied by the preceding clause. The sealing follows the habituated denial; it is what that denial becomes. The transgression is named as the condition of the sealed, not as an incidental description.

**The Formula of a Law**

The opening of the final clause — *kadhālika*, thus, in this way — signals a general rule rather than a local event. The verse is not describing what happened to one group; it is stating how the mechanism operates. The same formula is used throughout the Qur'an to introduce a pattern that applies beyond the case in hand. The verse therefore converts a historical sequence into a principle: repeated refusal produces incapacity, and incapacity produces further refusal. The principle is testable in ordinary experience, which is what makes it persuasive. A person who has refused something often enough stops being able to consider it, and the verse describes that process in the language of the heart rather than of the mind.

Two misreadings need to be excluded. The sealing is not a punishment inflicted on the innocent; the verse names the transgressors, and the transgression precedes the sealing. Nor is it irreversible in the sense that it excuses — the Qur'an continues to address sealed hearts with commands and warnings, which would be pointless if the address could not be heard. The sealing describes a real difficulty, not an impossibility. The verse therefore holds two things together: the condition is severe, and responsibility remains. That is the same combination the chapter has maintained throughout — the audience is described as unable to hear, and is simultaneously addressed as though hearing were available.

**The Sequence as Evidence of Patience**

There is a second thing the verse establishes, and it concerns God rather than the peoples. After Noah, more messengers were sent. The sending continued after a millennium of refusal, which means the pattern is not one of a single test followed by a verdict. It is a sustained offer, repeated community by community across generations, each time with clear proofs and each time with the same result. The verse therefore supplies the strongest available evidence against the idea that the punishment follows quickly on the rejection. Between the sending and the outcome there is a messenger, a lifetime of warning, and an opportunity to reverse. What the verse compresses into one sentence covers centuries, and the compression is what makes the patience visible — a long succession of chances, each one genuine, each one offered in full, and each one refused. Nothing in the sequence suggests haste on either side, and the unhurried repetition of the sending is itself part of the argument the chapter is making.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:75

> Then after these ˹messengers˺ We sent Moses and Aaron to Pharaoh and his chiefs with Our signs. But they behaved arrogantly and were a wicked people

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Messengers Sent Together**

The verse names Moses and Aaron, and the pairing is distinctive. Most of the messengers in the preceding verse are sent singly, each to his own people. Here two are sent to one people, and the Qur'an explains the reason elsewhere: Aaron was given as a support at Moses' own request. The arrangement is significant for what it says about the mission rather than about the men. The task was addressed to a regime, not to a tribe, and it required two voices. The verse therefore treats the pairing as part of the sending rather than as an incidental detail, and it names both men in the same clause so that neither is subordinated to the other in the account.

**To Pharaoh and His Chiefs**

The recipients are named in two groups, and the two groups represent the two levels of power. Pharaoh is the ruler; the chiefs — *al-malaʾ* — are the dignitaries around him, the people of highest social standing in Egyptian society. The verse addresses the mission to both, which means the message was delivered at the top of the structure rather than at its base. That is unusual. Most of the messengers narrated in the Qur'an are sent to their own tribes and speak to their neighbours. Moses and Aaron are sent into a court. The naming of the chiefs alongside Pharaoh also distributes the responsibility: the rejection that follows belongs to the ruling group as a whole, not to one man's obstinacy alone.

The signs are specified as God's own — *bi-āyātinā* — and the possessive matters. They are not Moses' achievements or demonstrations of his skill. The Qur'an enumerates them elsewhere: “We surely gave Moses nine clear signs” (17:101), and the tradition identifies the hand and the staff as the two that appear first. The signs are described as clear proofs in the parallel accounts, and their clarity is what makes the rejection culpable. A messenger who arrives without evidence can be dismissed; one who arrives with demonstrable signs cannot. The verse therefore establishes the evidential basis before describing the response, and the order is the same one used in the previous verse.

**“They Behaved Arrogantly”**

The response is given in one verb: *istakbarū* — they behaved arrogantly, they considered themselves too great. The word is the Qur'an's standard term for the specific failure of those who reject a messenger, and it is used of Pharaoh's court repeatedly: “So We plagued them with floods, locusts, lice, frogs, and blood—all as clear signs, but they persisted in arrogance and were a wicked people” (7:133). The arrogance is not pride in the ordinary sense. It is a refusal grounded in self-assessment — the conviction that accepting the message would be beneath the accepter, and that the accepter's standing would be reduced by the act of receiving it from another. The Qur'an names the consequence of that state directly: “Surely those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell, fully humbled” (40:60). The verse therefore identifies the obstacle as internal rather than intellectual.

**“A Wicked People”**

The final clause is a verdict, and its placement is significant. It does not say that they became wicked after the signs; it says they were a wicked people, which describes a standing condition. The arrogance is the expression of the condition rather than its cause. The verse therefore supplies the explanation that the previous verse gave in general terms — the habituated denial that precedes the evidence. Pharaoh's court is presented as a case of the same pattern: a people already settled in transgression, addressed with clear proofs, responding with self-regard. The verdict is stated in three words and is not elaborated, because the account that follows will supply the elaboration.

The verse closes the general summary and opens the detailed case. Noah was narrated in three verses; the messengers between him and Moses are summarized in one. Moses receives the remainder of the section, and the allocation is deliberate. His story is the fullest in the Qur'an, appearing in more chapters than any other narrative, because it contains every element the chapter is arguing about: a messenger rejected by a regime, a demand for signs, an accusation of magic, a contest in public, and an outcome in water. The verse therefore moves from pattern to instance, and it selects the instance that most closely resembles the Prophet's own situation — a man confronting established power with nothing but a message and its evidence.

**What the Court's Position Reveals About Power**

The verse names two parties and the asymmetry between them is total. On one side stand two men with signs they did not produce. On the other stands the most powerful regime of its time, with its ruler, its dignitaries, its treasury and its specialists. Nothing in the verse suggests that the imbalance matters to the outcome, and the reason is the one the chapter has been stating since its opening: the signs belong to God, and the regime belongs to Him as well. The court's power is real and it is formidable, and it is also entirely derivative. The verse therefore sets up the confrontation in a way that makes the result predictable to anyone who has followed the chapter's argument. Power does not decide the question; the origin of the signs does. That is the proposition the following verses will test in public, in front of the magicians, the court and the crowd. The crowd will watch both parties act, and what it sees will settle the question more completely than anything either side could say.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:76

> When the truth came to them from Us, they said, “This is certainly pure magic!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Source Is Stated Before the Accusation**

The verse opens by fixing where the thing came from. When the truth came to them from Us. The preposition matters, and it is doing argumentative work before the accusation is even reported. The audience is told where the thing came from at the moment they are told that it arrived. The signs are described as God's in the previous verse; here the truth itself is described as coming from Him. The verse therefore establishes the origin of what was rejected in the same breath as the rejection, which makes the accusation that follows a statement about the accusers rather than about the thing accused. Nothing in the verse questions the character of the signs. The question it raises is about the people who named them.

The words reported are specific: this is clear magic — *siḥrun mubīn*. The accusation is not that Moses is confused, mistaken or ambitious. It is that what he produced belongs to a known category of skilled deception, and that the category is recognizable to the audience. The charge is therefore technical rather than dismissive. It says: we know what this is, we have seen it before, and it is a craft. That is a stronger accusation than ignorance would produce, and it is why the Qur'an takes it seriously enough to answer it at length in the verses that follow. A regime that can name the phenomenon is a regime that believes it has explained it.

**A Charge Made Against Every Messenger**

The accusation is not peculiar to Pharaoh's court, and the Qur'an records its recurrence across the messengers. Speaking of Jesus: “How I prevented the Children of Israel from harming you when they came to them with clear proofs and the disbelievers among them said, ‘This is nothing but pure magic’” (5:110). And of the Prophet himself: “Yet when the Prophet came to them with clear proofs, they said, ‘This is pure magic’” (61:6). The identical wording in both passages shows that the charge is a standard response rather than a considered finding. It is what is said when a sign cannot be denied and its source cannot be accepted. The verse therefore places Pharaoh's court inside a pattern, and the pattern is what makes the story useful to the audience hearing it.

**Why Magic Is the Chosen Explanation**

The choice of accusation is instructive. Magic in that society was a recognized profession with practitioners, fees and public performances, and Pharaoh's court had access to the best of them. Naming the signs as magic placed them inside a familiar frame in which the court held the expertise. The effect was to convert an unprecedented event into a competition, and a competition can be arranged, judged and won. The accusation therefore served a political purpose as well as an explanatory one. It allowed the regime to respond without conceding anything, and it set up the contest that the following verses describe. The charge was not a conclusion; it was a strategy, and it worked until the day it was tested in front of the people whose opinion mattered.

The verse is one sentence, and the compression is deliberate. The whole exchange — the arrival of the truth and the naming of it — occupies a single line. The Qur'an's method in narrative is to give full length to what needs examination and minimal length to what does not. The accusation does not need examination because it is transparent; its motive is visible in the verse that follows. What the narrative expands is the response, and the response occupies the next several verses. The verse therefore functions as a hinge: it states the charge and moves immediately to the answer, without pausing to consider whether the charge has any merit. Nothing is conceded to it, and nothing is spent on it.

**What the Verse Establishes for What Follows**

Two things are settled here and neither is revisited. First, the signs are true and they came from God; the verse states this as fact rather than as Moses' claim. Second, the accusation is a response to the signs rather than an assessment of them. With both points established, the narrative can proceed to the contest without needing to re-argue the nature of the evidence. The verse therefore does the foundational work of the section in a single sentence, and it does it by stating the origin before the reaction.

The construction also fixes the burden of proof. Once the origin is given as God's, the accusers owe an explanation of how a sign from that source could be a trick, and no explanation of that kind is available to them. Everything that follows is the working out of that impossibility.

**The Word “Truth” Before the Word “Magic”**

The verse places two nouns in a single sentence, and the order is the argument. The truth came; they said it was magic. The reader is given the correct description before the incorrect one, which means the accusation arrives already refuted. That is a deliberate construction. Had the verse reported the accusation first and the origin afterward, the reader would have had to hold the charge in mind while evaluating it. As written, there is nothing to evaluate. The verse establishes what the thing is, then records what it was called, and the gap between the two is the entire content of the account. The reader is never invited to weigh the accusation, because the weighing has already been done by the order of the words.

An event that cannot be placed is dangerous to a regime, because it leaves the audience without a frame. Naming the signs as magic supplied a frame immediately, and the frame came with experts, precedents and a known set of outcomes. The accusation was therefore a service to the court's stability before it was a claim about Moses. The verse records the naming without endorsing it, and it records it in the exact words used, so that the reader can see what was said and compare it with what was there. The comparison is the verse's whole method, and it requires no commentary because the two terms are irreconcilable.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:77

> Moses responded, “Is this what you say about the truth when it has come to you? Is this magic? Magicians will never succeed.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question Instead of a Denial**

Moses does not answer the accusation with a counter-accusation or with a defence of his own character. He answers it with a question that turns the charge back on the speakers: is this what you say about the truth when it has come to you? The question is not rhetorical in the empty sense; it identifies an inconsistency. The truth has arrived, and the response to it is a label. By putting the two side by side in a single sentence, Moses makes the disproportion visible. Nothing needs to be added to the question, because the juxtaposition does the argument. The verse therefore shows a messenger handling hostile speech in the most economical way available — by asking the speakers to look at their own words.

Then comes the shorter question: is this magic? *A-siḥrun hādhā*. The form expects a negative answer, and the expectation is the point. Moses is not asking for a demonstration; he is asking whether the label fits. The question is addressed to people who know what magic looks like, since their court employed magicians professionally. It therefore appeals to their own expertise rather than disputing it. A craftsman can recognize the work of his craft, and Moses asks these men to apply that recognition honestly. The verse thus converts the accusation into a test that the accusers are qualified to perform and cannot pass.

**“Magicians Will Not Succeed”**

The final clause is a general principle rather than a personal prediction. *Lā yufliḥu s-sāḥirūn* — the magicians do not prosper. The reasoning is supplied by the tradition: their craft is founded on deception and falsehood, and what is founded on deception cannot reach a lasting end. The same statement appears where Moses is instructed before the contest: “Cast what is in your right hand, and it will swallow up what they have made, for what they have made is no more than a magic trick. And magicians can never succeed wherever they go” (20:69). The clause therefore does two things at once. It predicts the outcome of the coming contest, and it states a rule about the craft itself — that its methods exclude the possibility of real success.

**The Principle Applies to the Accusers**

There is an edge in the final clause that is easy to miss. If magicians do not prosper, and if the accusation is true, then Moses' mission will fail and there is nothing to fear. But if the accusation is false, the accusers are the ones engaged in deception, and the principle applies to them. The verse therefore does not merely defend Moses; it places the speakers inside the category they invoked. A person who names the truth as magic in order to escape it is practicing deception, and deception is what the clause says cannot succeed. The argument is closed: either reading leads to the same conclusion about the accusers.

**Why Moses Argues at All**

The verse raises a question about method. Moses has already been given clear signs, and the previous verse has established that the signs came from God. Why argue with an accusation rather than simply produce another sign? The answer lies in the audience. The contest will be witnessed by the court and by the magicians themselves, and the argument here is what makes the contest intelligible to them. A sign produced without a preceding argument is a spectacle; a sign produced after the accusation has been examined is evidence. The verse therefore supplies the reasoning that allows the coming event to be read correctly, and it supplies it in three short sentences.

The structure of the reply is worth holding onto. It does not deny, does not attack and does not elaborate. It asks two questions and states one principle. The questions force the speakers to examine their own words, and the principle supplies the criterion by which the examination will be judged. Nothing in the reply depends on the audience's goodwill, and nothing in it concedes the accusation. The verse therefore presents a way of answering hostile characterization that is available to anyone in a similar position: put the label next to the thing it was applied to, and ask whether it fits.

**The Principle of Non-Success Is Broader Than Magic**

The final clause has a reach beyond the contest. Prosperity — *falāḥ* — is the Qur'an's term for reaching what one sought, and the verse denies it to the practitioners of deception as a class. The reasoning applies to any enterprise built on making the false appear true, whether the medium is a rope and a staff or a word attributed to God. That is why the chapter can move from the magicians to the fabricators without a change of vocabulary. Both are described as people who will not prosper, and in both cases the reason is the same: the foundation cannot bear the weight placed on it.

The reply contains no demonstration. Moses argues, and then the narrative moves to the contest rather than to an instant proof. The delay has a purpose. A sign produced in the middle of an argument would be received as part of the argument, judged by the same audience on the same terms. A sign produced after the accusation has been examined, in a public setting arranged by the accusers, cannot be received that way. The verse therefore shows Moses building the conditions in which the evidence will be undeniable, and it does so with words rather than with miracles. Words prepare the ground; the sign then falls on ground already prepared, and the two together produce a conviction neither could produce alone.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:78

> They argued, “Have you come to turn us away from the faith of our forefathers so that the two of you may become supreme in the land? We will never believe in you!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Argument From Ancestral Practice**

The reply opens with the charge that appears most often in the Qur'an's accounts of rejected messengers. Have you come to turn us away from what we found our fathers upon? The objection is not that the message is false, incoherent or unsupported. It is that it disrupts an inheritance. The same response is recorded of the Makkan opponents in almost identical terms: “When it is said to them, ‘Come to Allah's revelations and to the Messenger,’ they reply, ‘What we found our forefathers practicing is good enough for us.’ ˹Would they still do so,˺ even if their forefathers had absolutely no knowledge or guidance?” (5:104). The question appended there is the answer the verse here leaves implicit. Ancestral practice is not evidence of correctness; it is evidence of age.

**The Second Half of the Charge**

The accusation continues: so that supremacy in the land may belong to you two. This is the motive the court assigns, and it is the same motive Noah denied in the verses above. The pattern is consistent across the accounts — a messenger is explained by ambition rather than by conviction, because ambition is a motive the court understands and conviction is not. The charge is also politically effective. It converts a religious claim into a power struggle, and a power struggle can be answered with power. The verse therefore records the moment at which the court reframes the dispute in terms it can handle, and the reframing is what allows it to avoid addressing the signs.

**Two Motives Joined in One Question**

The two charges are joined by a single particle, and the joining is significant. Turning people from their ancestors' practice is presented as the means; gaining supremacy is presented as the end. The court therefore offers a complete explanation of the mission that requires nothing supernatural and nothing sincere. That is the function of the accusation. If the messengers want power, then their signs are instruments, their message is a tool, and their persistence is explicable without reference to truth. The verse records the explanation without commenting on it, because the events that follow will test it. A man seeking supremacy would negotiate; the account shows no negotiation.

The final clause is short and final: we will not believe in you two. The dual form is used, addressing both Moses and Aaron together, which confirms that the mission was received as a joint one. The refusal is stated in the strongest available form — not a hesitation, not a request for time, not a condition. It is a settled decision, and it is announced rather than argued. The verse therefore shows the point at which the court stops reasoning. Everything before this clause was an argument, however weak; this clause is not an argument at all. It is a declaration, and it marks the transition from dispute to confrontation that the following verses describe.

**Why the Verse Records the Accusation in Full**

The Qur'an could have summarized the court's response in a phrase. Instead it reports the full sentence, including both the ancestral objection and the charge of ambition. The reason is that both charges are still in use. The appeal to inherited practice and the explanation of religious speech by ambition are the two most common responses to a messenger in any period, and the verse preserves them in their original wording so that they can be recognized. The account is therefore not merely historical. It supplies the reader with the two standard evasions, stated by people who had every reason to use them, so that later audiences can identify them when they appear again.

The chapter has been describing a single pattern since its opening verses: a message given, a response evading it, and an outcome following. This verse supplies the clearest instance of the evasion. The court does not deny the signs; it explains them away by attributing a motive. The chapter has treated the same move where it described the demand for the Hour's date and the division of provision into lawful and unlawful. In each case the response avoids the claim rather than answering it. The verse therefore completes the diagnostic picture, and the events that follow supply the result.

**The Qur'an's Answer to the Ancestral Objection**

The objection is not left standing anywhere in the Book, and the answer is always the same shape. The practice of the forefathers is examined for knowledge rather than for age. The question appended in the parallel passage is blunt: would they still follow, even if their forefathers had absolutely no knowledge or guidance? (5:104). The same question appears where the response is described as following what Satan invites them to (31:21). The argument is therefore not that tradition is worthless but that it is not evidence. A practice inherited from people who did not know is inherited ignorance, and the verse here records the objection in its purest form so that the answer can be applied to it directly.

The second charge has a structural weakness worth naming. An accusation about motive cannot be disproved, because any denial can be read as further evidence of concealment. That is precisely why it is useful to those who make it. The court does not need to demonstrate that Moses seeks supremacy; it needs only to assert it, and the assertion cannot be answered by anything Moses does. The verse records the charge without rebutting it for that reason, and it leaves the rebuttal to the events. A man who wanted the land would not have stood in it with nothing but a staff, and the account that follows shows what he actually did with the opportunity he was accused of seeking.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:79

> Pharaoh demanded, “Bring me every skilled magician.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Regime's Response to a Sign**

The verse records Pharaoh's answer to everything that has preceded it, and the answer is not an argument. He does not produce evidence, examine the signs, or address the charge that magicians do not prosper. He summons professionals. The response is administrative rather than intellectual: a problem has appeared, and the state's specialists will handle it. That is the verse's central observation. When a regime cannot answer a claim on its merits, it responds with resources, and the resources are chosen to match the category it has assigned to the claim. Having named the signs as magic, the court now acts on that naming.

The demand is exhaustive: every skilled magician — *kulla sāḥirin ʿalīm*. The two descriptors are joined, and both matter. *Sāḥir* identifies the profession; *ʿalīm* identifies mastery within it, the one who knows the craft deeply. The verse therefore describes a mobilization rather than a request. The same demand appears in the parallel accounts, with the same emphasis on completeness: the chiefs are told “to bring you every clever magician” (7:112), and elsewhere they propose dispatching mobilizers to all the cities “to bring you every skilled magician” (26:36–37). The scale of the mobilization is itself evidence of how seriously the court took the signs, and it contradicts the dismissal implied by the accusation of magic.

**What the Summoning Reveals**

There is an irony in the verse that the narrative leaves unspoken. A regime that genuinely believed it faced a trickster would not need every master of the craft in the country. The exhaustive summoning shows that the court understood the signs to be something beyond its specialists' ordinary work, and that it intended to answer them with the full weight of its expertise. The verse therefore records a contradiction between the stated position and the action taken. The accusation was contemptuous; the response was maximal. The Qur'an reports both without comment, and the contrast does the work. A contemptuous explanation and an emergency mobilization do not belong together, and the verse places them side by side without drawing attention to the fact.

By summoning the magicians, Pharaoh determines the setting, the timing and the judges of the coming encounter. The contest will be public, it will be held at a place of his choosing, and it will be assessed by an audience loyal to him. The arrangement is designed to produce a predictable result, and the verse shows the confidence behind it. Nothing in the demand suggests fear. Pharaoh is not defending himself; he is staging a demonstration. The verse therefore sets up the following verses as a reversal — the event arranged to confirm the regime's account becomes the event that destroys it. Every advantage the court built into the arrangement works against it once the demonstration begins.

**Brevity at the Point of Confrontation**

The verse is five words in Arabic, and the brevity is characteristic. The Qur'an's narratives compress what does not require analysis and expand what does. Pharaoh's demand requires no analysis; its meaning is transparent and its motive is visible. What follows — the arrival of the magicians, Moses' instruction to them, the casting, the swallowing, the prostration — receives the space. The verse therefore functions as a stage direction, and it is given exactly the length a stage direction needs.

The chapter has moved from argument to narrative, and the narrative has now reached the point of confrontation. Moses has stated his case, answered the accusation of magic, and been refused absolutely. The regime has responded with a mobilization. Everything that remains is action. The verse marks the end of speech in this section and the beginning of the event, and it does so by recording the shortest possible transition: a command, issued in confidence, that will produce the opposite of what it intended.

**The Two Descriptors and What They Imply**

The phrase *sāḥirin ʿalīm* deserves closer attention, because the second word does real work. A *sāḥir* is anyone who practices the craft; an *ʿalīm* is one who knows it thoroughly, a master rather than a performer. Pharaoh does not ask for magicians in general. He asks for every practitioner who has reached the top of the profession. The demand therefore excludes the possibility that a lesser craftsman will fail and leave the question open. Whatever the best in the country can do will be done, in public, against the signs. That is a high standard to set, and setting it is a statement of confidence. It is also, retrospectively, the condition that makes the outcome decisive. A demonstration that defeats the mediocre proves little; one that defeats the masters settles the matter.

**The Court's Confidence and Its Basis**

Nothing in the verse suggests doubt. Pharaoh's command is issued in the imperative, without qualification, without contingency and without any indication that he considers the possibility of losing. The confidence is not irrational. He holds the state, the specialists, the venue and the audience, and he has already named the phenomenon in a way that places it inside his experts' competence. On his own premises the outcome is assured. The verse therefore records the moment at which a regime's self-understanding is most complete and most mistaken, and it does so without a word of commentary. The reader who has followed the chapter knows what the premises are worth, because the chapter has spent its length establishing where real power lies.

There is a practical dimension to the demand that the narrative leaves for the reader to supply. Summoning every master magician in the country requires messengers, travel, time and payment, and the parallel accounts record the magicians asking what they will receive if they prevail (7:113). The mobilization is therefore an expensive act of state, undertaken to answer a single man. The disproportion is itself informative. The court is spending real resources to maintain a characterization, and the characterization is what the following verses will remove. The verse thus records the cost of a false explanation before the explanation collapses, which is the pattern the chapter describes wherever wealth and truth are set against each other.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:80

> When the magicians came, Moses said to them, “Cast whatever you wish to cast!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Arrival Is Reported in Three Words**

The verse opens with the arrival: when the magicians came. Nothing is said about their number, their preparations or their confidence, although the previous verse has established that every master of the craft in the country was summoned. The compression is deliberate. The narrative has already supplied the scale; it does not need to repeat it. What matters is that the mobilization succeeded and that the confrontation is now unavoidable. The verse therefore moves directly to the exchange that follows, and it does so without any description of the scene, which leaves the reader's attention entirely on the words.

The order of speaking is significant. The magicians have arrived at Pharaoh's summons, on Pharaoh's terms, in a contest designed to produce Pharaoh's conclusion — and the first words are Moses'. He does not wait to be challenged, does not respond to a provocation, and does not ask for his turn. He opens the encounter. The parallel accounts record a negotiation about who casts first, and in each of them Moses ends up yielding the first move deliberately: “They said, ‘O Moses! Either you cast, or let us be the first to cast.’ Moses responded, ‘No, you go first’” (20:65–66). The verse here compresses that exchange into a single instruction, and the instruction has the same effect.

**“Whatever You Wish to Cast”**

The wording is unusually open. Moses does not specify what they should produce, does not set conditions, and does not limit the demonstration. *Mā kuntum ṣāniʿīn* — what you were going to make, whatever your craft can do. The openness is the point. A man who feared the outcome would impose restrictions; a man who knows the result will differ from what the court expects has no reason to. The verse therefore records a confidence that is not stated as a claim but demonstrated in the form of the invitation. The same instruction appears verbatim in the parallel account: “Moses said to them, ‘Cast whatever you wish to cast’” (26:43), which shows that this is a fixed formulation rather than an improvised line.

Allowing the magicians to go first looks like a concession, and in a spectacle it would be. In an evidential contest it is the opposite. Whoever performs first sets the standard, and whoever answers afterward must exceed it. By yielding, Moses ensures that the full extent of the craft is displayed before anything of his own is shown. The effect on the audience is decisive: they see the maximum the magicians can produce, and then they see what happens to it. The verse therefore records a tactical choice with an evidential purpose, and the purpose is confirmed by the outcome described in the following verses, where the craft's product is swallowed rather than merely matched.

**The Magicians Are Addressed Directly**

There is a further feature in the verse that is easy to overlook. Moses speaks to the magicians, not to Pharaoh or to the crowd. The instruction is addressed to the craftsmen themselves, which places them in the position of participants rather than instruments. That matters for what follows. These are the people who will fall prostrating within a few verses, and the account of their conversion begins here — with a man who speaks to them as agents capable of choice. The verse therefore does something the court did not: it treats the magicians as people who can see and decide, rather than as tools of a demonstration.

Everything in the section up to this point has been speech: the mission, the accusation, the argument, the refusal, the summons. This verse is the last line before the event. The narrative has established the origin of the signs, the nature of the accusation, the motive of the court and the confidence of the messenger. What remains is the demonstration itself, and the verse places it in motion with a single permissive sentence. The reader is left at the moment immediately before the casting, with the outcome already indicated by the way the invitation was framed.

**The Instruction Is Not a Challenge to the Crowd**

It matters that the sentence is addressed to the magicians rather than delivered to the audience. A spectacle is staged for spectators, and Pharaoh's arrangement was designed as one. Moses' instruction redirects the encounter to the practitioners themselves, which changes its character. They are the ones who will judge what they see, because they are the only people present qualified to judge it. The parallel account records what their craft actually produced — ropes and staffs made to appear as though they were slithering (20:66) — and it is precisely those men who will recognize, within moments, that what they are watching is not of the same kind. The verse therefore places the verdict in the hands of the only competent judges, and it does so by speaking to them directly.

**Why This Verse Belongs Where It Does**

The chapter could have skipped from the summons to the outcome. Instead it pauses on the instruction, and the pause is the point. Between the regime's command and the demonstration's result stands a single sentence in which the messenger, outnumbered and out-resourced, sets the terms of what is about to happen. That sentence is the hinge of the whole account. It shows that the contest was not decided by whoever summoned it, and it establishes the confidence that the following verses will confirm. The verse is therefore short because it records one act — an invitation — but it is placed where it is because that act determines everything after it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:81

> When they did, Moses said, “What you have produced is mere magic, Allah will surely make it useless, for Allah certainly does not set right the work of the corruptors

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict Arrives Before the Outcome**

The magicians cast, and the first thing that happens is not a miracle but a sentence. Moses names what they have produced before anything has failed. That order is the verse's central feature. A verdict delivered after the event would be a commentary on a visible result; a verdict delivered at the moment of casting is a statement of knowledge. The verse therefore presents Moses as someone who already knows what the craft can and cannot do, and who says so while it is still working. The audience watching has no way to confirm the claim at the moment it is made, which is precisely why the claim carries weight when the confirmation follows.

The wording concedes something, and the concession is deliberate. *Mā jiʾtum bihi* — what you have brought, what you have produced. Moses does not say that nothing happened. He acknowledges a production, an artifact, a thing made by skill. The concession removes the easiest evasion available to the spectators: the suggestion that the magician denies the obvious. Nothing is denied. What is asserted is the category the production belongs to, and the category is magic. The verse therefore argues by classification rather than by denial, which is a stronger form of argument because it grants the evidence and disputes only its meaning.

**Nullification Promised, Not Performed by Moses**

The second clause assigns the action to God. *Sayfaʿaluhu llāh* — Allah will bring it to nothing, will nullify it. Moses does not claim to undo the craft himself, and he does not describe a contest of power between two practitioners. The nullification is divine, and the verb used elsewhere for the same event is explicit about the mechanism: “Cast what is in your right hand, and it will swallow up what they have made, for what they have made is no more than a magic trick. And magicians can never succeed wherever they go” (20:69). The verse therefore keeps the roles distinct: the magicians produce, Moses names, and God nullifies. Nothing in the sequence makes Moses a competitor.

**Corruptors' Work Is Not Set Right**

The final clause is a general principle rather than a comment on the event. Allah does not set right the work of those who spread corruption. The verb *yuṣliḥ* means to put in order, to make sound, to allow something to stand and endure. The principle is therefore not that corruptors fail immediately but that their work is never made sound. It may succeed for a time, attract followers, and produce visible effects; it will not be established. The verse places the magicians' craft inside that rule, and the rule is broad enough to cover every enterprise built on deception, including the religious fabrications the chapter attacked earlier.

The tradition explains the timing. Moses told them to cast not because he affirmed their craft but so that the onlookers would see its falsity — the falsity would become apparent when the work was brought to nothing. The instruction and the verdict are therefore two halves of one method. Had Moses spoken after the nullification, the audience would have seen an event and been told what it meant. Because he spoke before it, the audience heard a claim and then watched the claim verified. The verse is constructed to produce that sequence, and the sequence is what converts a spectacle into evidence.

**A Rule About Deception**

Taken on its own, the verse states something that applies far beyond the contest. Deception has a ceiling. It can produce effects, and those effects can impress; it cannot produce something that endures. The verse gives the reason: the One who orders things does not order deception. That is not a moral observation but a metaphysical one, and it explains why the chapter can move between magicians and fabricators without changing its vocabulary. Both are described as people whose work will not prosper, and in both cases the ground is the same. What is built on making the false appear true has no foundation capable of bearing weight.

The setting was arranged by the court, and the court's purpose was to display mastery. The verse redirects the display. The audience came to watch magicians perform, and they leave having watched a claim about magic made and confirmed. The difference is the verse's achievement. Nothing about the arrangement was changed — the venue, the judges and the performers are all Pharaoh's — but what the event demonstrated was not what it was designed to demonstrate. The verse therefore records the moment at which a regime's staged proof becomes the evidence against it, and it records it in a single sentence spoken before anything happened.

**Why the Nullification Matters More Than the Production**

The verse gives most of its weight to the second half of Moses' statement, and the distribution is instructive. Naming the craft as magic settles a classification; promising its nullification settles a question about the future. The classification could in principle be disputed — a craftsman might insist that his work is genuine. The nullification cannot be disputed once it occurs, because it happens in public and is visible to everyone present. The verse therefore rests its case on the event rather than on the label, and the label is offered only so that the event will be understood when it comes. That is why the general principle is attached to the promise rather than to the naming: what will not be set right is the work itself, not the description of it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:82

> And Allah establishes the truth by His Words—even to the dismay of the wicked.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Truth Established by Words**

The verse states the mechanism by which the truth prevails, and the mechanism is speech. *Yuḥiqqu llāhu l-ḥaqqa bi-kalimātihi* — Allah establishes the truth by His words. Not by superior force, not by a larger army, and not by the magicians being outperformed. The words in view are the promises made to Moses, and the tradition identifies them with the assurance given elsewhere: “We will certainly strengthen your arm through your brother, and grant you both authority, so they will not be able to harm you. ˹Go˺ with Our signs. You and your followers will prevail” (28:35). The verse therefore describes how a promise operates in history: what God has said becomes what happens, and the becoming is the establishment. The truth was already true before the contest; what the contest did was make it apparent.

**“Even Though the Criminals Dislike It”**

The final clause names a reaction and dismisses it as irrelevant. *Wa-law kariha l-mujrimūn* — though the criminals hate it. The dislike is real and it is stated without minimization. What the clause denies is any causal connection between the dislike and the outcome. The truth is established regardless of how it is received, and the verse treats the reception as a fact about the receivers rather than about the truth. That distinction is the chapter's recurring concern. A message does not become false because it is resented, and it does not become true because it is popular. The verse states the principle in six words, at the moment when the resentment is at its maximum.

There is a formal feature here worth recording. The closing quotation mark appears at the end of this verse, not the previous one, which means Moses' speech runs across both. The two verses are a single utterance divided by the verse numbering. Read together, the speech has three movements: the naming of the craft, the promise of its nullification, and the statement of how the truth is established. The third movement is the widest, and it is placed last. Moses therefore ends by moving from the particular event to the general rule, which is characteristic of prophetic speech in the Qur'an — the immediate case is used to state something that outlasts it.

**Why Words Rather Than Power**

The choice of *kalimāt* as the instrument is significant. A reader might expect the verse to say that God establishes the truth by His power, and the substitution changes the meaning. Power compels; words inform. A truth established by power would be accepted under pressure and abandoned when the pressure lifted. A truth established by words is accepted because it has been seen to be true, and the seeing is what makes the acceptance durable. The verse therefore describes the method God uses throughout the Qur'an: the sign is given, the word is spoken, and the establishment happens in the recognition of the people watching rather than in their subjugation.

The verse's final clause forecloses a common move. When a claim is resisted by people with standing, the resistance is often taken as evidence against it. Pharaoh's court had every form of standing available in that society — political authority, religious office and professional expertise — and all of it was deployed against the signs. The verse records the deployment and then states that it changes nothing. The criminals' dislike is reported as a fact about them, not as a consideration bearing on the truth. That is a severe claim, and it is one the chapter has been building toward since its opening questions about who creates and who guides.

**The Principle Applied to the Chapter's Own Case**

The verse is spoken inside a story about Moses, and it applies to the audience hearing the story. The Prophet has been told that the truth has come from his Lord, and the audience has responded with the same resentment Pharaoh's court showed. The verse supplies the answer in advance. The truth is established by God's words, and the establishment does not depend on the reception. A reader who takes the verse seriously will not measure the message by its opponents' hostility, because the verse has already accounted for that hostility and assigned it no weight. The closing quotation mark ends Moses' speech and leaves the principle standing on its own.

There is a distinction in the verse between the truth being true and the truth being established, and the distinction does real work. The truth of the message did not begin at the contest; it was true when Moses arrived, and it was true while the court was calling it magic. What the contest produced was not truth but manifestation — the truth becoming apparent to people who had refused to see it. The verse assigns that manifestation to God's words, which means the appearance of the truth is itself an act of God rather than a result of the argument. The reader is therefore told that the clarity of the message is not a human achievement, and that its obscurity in any given moment is not evidence against it. What has been said will become apparent, and the timing belongs to the One who said it.

**The Verse and the Chapter's Own Audience**

The principle is spoken to a court that no longer exists, and it is preserved for an audience that does. The chapter has been addressing people who resent the message, and the verse supplies the answer to resentment in general. It does not ask the resenter to reconsider; it states that his resentment has no bearing on the outcome. That is a hard thing to hear and a hard thing to accept, and the verse offers no softening. The truth will be established, the establishing will be visible, and the people who disliked it will see it established anyway. The closing words of Moses' speech are therefore the chapter's plainest statement about the relation between reception and reality, and they are placed at the end of the contest because the contest is where the relation was tested.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:83

> But no one believed in Moses except a few youths of his people, while fearing that Pharaoh and their own chiefs might persecute them. And certainly Pharaoh was a tyrant in the land, and he was truly a transgressor

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Outcome of the Contest Was Not Conversion**

The verse opens with a fact that the preceding narrative does not prepare the reader for. The magicians prostrated; the crowd saw what happened; and almost nobody believed. The verse states the result without softening it: none believed in Moses except a few. After the clearest demonstration recorded in the story, the number of believers is given as a remnant. The verse therefore refuses to let the miracle do the work that miracles are assumed to do. Evidence was produced, and evidence did not produce belief. That is the chapter's central claim about the relation between signs and faith, and this verse supplies its hardest instance.

The phrase translated as a few youths is *dhurriyya*, which denotes offspring or progeny, and its reference is disputed. The pronoun can attach to Moses or to Pharaoh. On the first reading, the believers were a small number of young Israelite men. On the second, they included people attached to Pharaoh's own court, among them his wife, whose belief is recorded elsewhere: “And Allah has set forth an example for those who believe: the wife of Pharaoh. She pleaded, ‘My Lord! Build me a house with You in Paradise, save me from Pharaoh and his evil deeds, and save me from the wrongdoing people’” (66:11). The second reading is less likely on the grammar but stronger on the evidence, since it accounts for believers inside the regime. Both readings agree on the number: few.

**Belief Recorded Together With Fear**

The verse does not present the belief as fearless. It states it alongside the fear — they believed while fearing that Pharaoh and their chiefs would persecute them. The conjunction is important. Fear is not treated as disqualifying, and the belief is not discounted because it was accompanied by dread. The verse therefore describes a realistic condition rather than an ideal one. A small group inside a terror state believed, and they were afraid the whole time. Nothing in the wording suggests that the fear made their belief less valid; it made it more costly, which is a different thing entirely.

The objects of the fear are named in pairs: Pharaoh and their own chiefs. The second group is the significant addition. Persecution did not come only from the throne but from the notables of the believers' own community — the people whose standing depended on the regime's favour. That is the harder pressure to resist, because it arrives through kin, neighbours and patrons rather than through soldiers. The verse identifies it explicitly, which means the account is not simply about a tyrant and his victims. It is about a social structure in which belief cost a person his position among his own people as well as his safety from the state.

**“A Tyrant in the Land”**

The description of Pharaoh is given in two terms, and the first concerns height. *ʿĀlin fī l-arḍ* — exalted, elevated in the land. The elevation is not metaphorical. It describes a man who raised himself above the people he ruled, and the Qur'an records the point it reached: “But he denied and disobeyed. Then he turned his back, striving. Then he gathered ˹his people˺ and called out, saying, ‘I am your lord, the most high!’” (79:21–24). The verse here states the same fact in three words. The elevation is what made the fear rational, and the verse supplies it so that the believers' fear is understood as a response to a real situation rather than as weakness.

**“Among the Transgressors”**

The second term is *musrif* — one who exceeds, who goes beyond the limit, who is extravagant in the wrong direction. The word is used elsewhere in this chapter of the person who transgresses against himself, and it carries the sense of excess rather than of a single offence. Pharaoh is therefore described not as a man who committed a crime but as one whose whole conduct was over the line. The pairing of the two descriptions — exalted and excessive — is precise. The elevation produced the excess, and the excess reached the point of a claim to lordship. The verse states the character of the regime in a single clause, and it does so after naming the fear, so that the fear is measured against the thing feared.

The verse's lesson is uncomfortable and specific. A demonstrated miracle, witnessed by a court and a crowd, produced a handful of believers who remained afraid. Nothing in the account suggests that more evidence would have changed the number. The verse therefore separates the sufficiency of the evidence from the sufficiency of the response. The signs were enough; the people were not available. That distinction governs the rest of the chapter's account of the story, and it is the reason the narrative moves next to instruction and prayer rather than to further demonstration.

**The Number Itself Is the Argument**
