The verse could have omitted the count. It would have been possible to say that the people did not believe and move on to the crossing. The count is given because it is the point. A few believers after a public miracle is a fact about the audience rather than about the evidence, and the verse records it so that no reader can conclude that clearer signs would have produced a larger response. The chapter has spent most of its length on the sufficiency of what has been given — the creation, the provision, the senses, the alternation of night and day. This verse supplies the empirical result: sufficiency did not produce belief, and the shortfall was almost total. The number therefore does the work of an argument, and it is stated in three words.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:84

> Moses said, “O my people! If you do believe in Allah and submit ˹to His Will˺, then put your trust in Him.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Conditions and One Command**

The verse is a single sentence with a precise logical shape. If you believe in God — and if you are submitting — then put your trust in Him. The two conditions are joined, and the command follows both. The structure is not redundant. Belief and submission are distinct: the first is a conviction about God, the second is a posture toward His will. A person can hold the conviction without the posture, and the verse requires both before issuing the command. The command itself is the third element, and it is the only thing the verse asks for. Nothing is demanded about the outcome, the timetable or the means of deliverance.

The verse treats trust as something that follows from belief rather than as a separate virtue. That ordering is significant. Trust — *tawakkul* — is the consignment of an affair to the One believed in, and it presupposes the belief. A person who does not believe that God governs cannot entrust anything to Him. The verse therefore presents trust as the practical form of belief under pressure. It is what belief looks like when the situation is dangerous, and the situation here is as dangerous as the story gets: a small group inside a terror state, facing a ruler who has claimed divinity.

**The Command Is Addressed to the Few**

The audience of this speech is the remnant named in the previous verse, and that fact changes its meaning. Moses is not addressing a nation; he is addressing a handful of frightened people. The command to trust is therefore not a general exhortation but a specific instruction to a specific group in a specific danger. The verse does not soften the danger or promise an outcome. It tells them what to do with the fear described a verse earlier, and what they are to do with it is hand it over. That is a harder instruction than courage, because courage can be performed in front of others while trust cannot be performed at all — it either exists or it does not.

If trust followed automatically from belief, the verse would not need to command it. The command indicates that it does not. A person can believe that God is sovereign and still be governed by fear, because fear operates on a different register from conviction. The verse addresses that gap directly. It does not argue the believers out of their fear or deny that the danger is real; it directs them to an act that the fear does not prevent. The command is therefore practical rather than rhetorical, and it is given in a form that can be obeyed immediately, without waiting for the fear to pass.

**No Outcome Is Promised**

There is something notable in what the verse does not contain. Moses does not say that they will be saved, that the sea will open, or that Pharaoh will fall. He says: put your trust in God. The absence of a promised outcome is deliberate, and it is what makes the instruction a test of trust rather than a calculation. A person who trusts because rescue has been guaranteed is not trusting; he is waiting. The verse therefore asks for the disposition without supplying the reassurance that would make it easy, and it does so at the point in the story where the reassurance is least available. The believers are told what to do, not what will happen, and the difference between those two is the difference between trust and calculation.

**Its Place in the Story's Movement**

The verse sits between the fear and the prayer. The previous verse described the believers' fear of persecution; the verses that follow record their prayer and the instruction about houses of worship. This verse is the hinge between the two. Fear has been named, and the response to it is not action but reliance. Only after the reliance is stated does the narrative move to petition and to practical arrangement. The sequence is therefore: fear named, trust commanded, prayer offered, provision made. The verse supplies the second step, and nothing in the following steps would be coherent without it.

The command is not confined to its historical setting. Its form — if you believe and submit, then trust — is addressed to anyone in the two conditions it names, and the conditions are the ones the chapter has been describing throughout. The verse therefore functions as a summary of the response the whole sūrah asks for. The chapter has argued about creation, provision, guidance and judgment; this verse states what a person who accepts the argument is to do next. The answer is not a further argument but a transfer of the affair, and it is given in the shortest form the chapter uses.

**Trust as a Practice With a Method**

The verse commands trust without describing it, and the tradition supplies the method. One commentator glosses the instruction in these terms: bring the hand of submission out from the sleeve of contentment, withdraw from turning to others, and know that people possess nothing. The gloss identifies three movements — acceptance, withdrawal from other sources, and a settled understanding that no creature holds anything independently. Read that way, trust is not a feeling but a set of recognitions with consequences for conduct. The person who has made them stops addressing his needs to people who cannot meet them. The verse therefore asks for something concrete, and the concreteness of the request is exactly why it can be commanded rather than merely admired.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:85

> They replied, “In Allah we trust. Our Lord! Do not subject us to the persecution of the oppressive people

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reply Matches the Command**

The verse records an immediate response, and the response is the exact thing that was asked for. Moses said: put your trust in God. They said: in God we trust. Nothing is added, nothing is negotiated, and no condition is attached. The correspondence between the command and the reply is complete, and the verse gives it in the shortest possible exchange. That economy is meaningful. The previous verse raised a question about whether trust follows automatically from belief; this verse shows a group producing it on demand, in one sentence, in the middle of a danger that the verse itself goes on to describe.

**The Name Placed First**

The word order carries the emphasis. *ʿAlā llāhi tawakkalnā* — upon God we have relied. The object precedes the verb, and in Arabic that construction restricts and singles out. The sense is not merely "we have relied on God" but "on God, and on no one else, we have relied." The fronting excludes alternatives without naming them. For a group facing a regime, the exclusion is the substance of the statement. They have patrons, chiefs and kin whose protection might have been sought, and the word order rules all of them out in two words. The verse therefore records not only an act of trust but a refusal of every other available option.

The same sentence turns from statement to petition without a break. In God we trust; our Lord, do not make us a trial for the wrongdoing people. The transition is immediate, which tells the reader something about how the verse understands trust. Trust is not a substitute for asking. The group has just declared its reliance and in the same breath asks for protection, and nothing in the verse suggests tension between the two. Trust and petition belong together: the first establishes to whom the affair has been committed, and the second states what is being asked of Him.

**“Do Not Make Us a Trial”**

The word used is *fitna*, which covers trial, temptation, testing and the state of being made an occasion of error for others. The request is unusual, and its direction is worth noting. They are not asking primarily to be spared; they are asking not to be made into a temptation for the oppressors. The concern is that their suffering will be used as evidence against the message — that the oppressors will say the believers were punished because their cause was false. The same concern appears in the prayer recorded elsewhere: “Our Lord! Do not make us a target for the wrongdoing people” (60:5). The request therefore concerns the meaning of their fate rather than only its pain.

There is a further layer in the petition that is easy to miss. The believers are asking that their own persecution not become an occasion of misguidance for the people persecuting them. That is a concern for the spiritual state of their enemies, voiced at the moment of greatest danger. The verse therefore shows a group whose fear has not narrowed its attention into self-interest. They are afraid, and they are also worried about what their fear will teach other people. The petition is not noble in the sentimental sense; it is precise about a real consequence, and it identifies that consequence as the thing most worth praying against.

**The Sentence Ends Unfinished**

The verse breaks mid-prayer, with a comma rather than a full stop, and the closing quotation mark appears only at the end of the next verse. The division is a feature of the verse structure rather than of the sense. The petition runs across two verses as a single utterance, which means the reader encounters it as an incomplete request and must carry it forward. The effect is to hold the prayer open. Nothing is resolved at the end of this verse; the request is still being made, and the second half of it arrives in the following line. The verse therefore ends in the middle of an act of asking, which is the appropriate place for a passage about people who have no control over what happens next.

**What the Petition Does Not Ask For**

The content of the request is as informative for what it omits as for what it contains. The believers do not ask for Pharaoh's destruction, for victory, for wealth, or for a sign. They ask two things, both of them negative: not to be made a trial, and to be delivered. Nothing in the prayer seeks advantage over the oppressors. That restraint is significant given the situation — a persecuted group with every reason to want its persecutors destroyed. The verse records a petition that stays inside the bounds of what the group actually needs, and it does not extend into retribution. The following verses show what happens when a prayer for retribution is made; this prayer is not that, and the difference between the two is one of the chapter's careful distinctions.

Read across the two verses, the believers' speech has three movements and no more: a declaration of reliance, a request about meaning, and a request about safety. Each movement is short, and none is elaborated. The economy is characteristic of the group as the chapter describes it — few in number, afraid, and without leverage. What they say is exactly proportionate to what they hold. There is no rhetoric, no appeal to their own merit, and no attempt to justify themselves. The verse therefore presents a model of speech under pressure: state where your reliance lies, name the outcome you fear most, and ask for mercy. The three steps require nothing the believers do not already have.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:86

> and deliver us by Your mercy from the disbelieving people.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer Completes Itself in One Clause**

The petition begun in the previous verse is finished here, and the completion is brief. Deliver us by Your mercy from the disbelieving people. Two requests were made across the two verses: not to be made a trial for the wrongdoers, and to be delivered from them. The second is the more obvious of the two, and it is stated in the plainest available terms. The verse therefore ends the prayer with the thing the believers actually want, after having first named the thing they most feared. The order is instructive — the concern about meaning precedes the concern about safety — and the completion returns to safety without retracting the first request.

The single qualifier in the petition is decisive. *Bi-raḥmatika* — by Your mercy. The believers do not ask to be delivered because they deserve it, because they are numerous, or because their case is strong. They ask on the ground of mercy, which is a ground that requires nothing from them. The choice is exact for a group that has just been described as a frightened remnant. They have no leverage and they claim none. The verse therefore records a petition with no basis except the character of the One addressed, and that is the only basis available to anyone in their position. Mercy is invoked precisely because merit cannot be.

**From the Disbelieving People**

The object of the deliverance is specified, and the specification is moral rather than political. They do not ask to be delivered from Pharaoh's soldiers, from the state, or from a particular decree. They name the people by their defining feature: those who disbelieve. The framing matters because it places the conflict where the believers understand it. The threat is not primarily physical; it comes from a group defined by its rejection, and the danger is that rejection will act on them. The verse therefore describes the situation in the terms the whole chapter has been using, and it asks for deliverance from the thing the chapter has identified as the real danger.

Read together, the two verses contain a single petition with two parts, and both parts are addressed to God rather than to any human agent. Nothing in the prayer asks for an intermediary, an alliance or a patron. The believers have just declared that they rely on God alone, and the petition is consistent with the declaration. That consistency is the verse's quiet argument. A group that relies on God and then petitions God is coherent; a group that relies on God and then seeks protection from the regime's notables would not be. The verse therefore shows the remnant holding its position under the pressure described two verses earlier, and holding it in the form of a prayer.

**Why the Prayer Is Recorded at This Length**

The Qur'an records many prayers briefly and some at length. This one is given two full verses, including an unusual request that most people in danger would not think to make. The length is justified by the content. The prayer supplies a model for a situation that recurs: a small group inside a hostile power, afraid, and unable to change its circumstances. What such a group is shown doing is naming its reliance, asking that its suffering not mislead others, and asking for mercy on the only ground available. Nothing in the prayer requires power, numbers or leverage, which is why it can be used by anyone in the same position.

The closing quotation mark falls here, and with it the remnant's voice in the narrative. Everything after this point is instruction and action: houses appointed, prayer established, a prayer of Moses against the regime, an answer from God, the crossing, the drowning. The believers are not heard again in this account. The verse therefore closes their part with a petition, and the petition is the last thing they are recorded doing before deliverance arrives. That placement is significant. The narrative moves from asking to receiving without any intervening act on their part, which is consistent with the trust they declared in the verse before.

**Mercy Invoked Where Merit Cannot Be**

The choice to ground the petition in mercy deserves a further word, because it is the only ground the group has. A person with leverage asks on the basis of a claim: I have served, I have suffered, I am owed. The believers have none of those available in a form that would oblige anyone. Their belief is recent, their numbers are small, and their fear has been stated openly in the previous verse. What remains is the character of the One addressed, and the petition names it. That is not a weakness in the prayer but its strength. A request grounded in the petitioner's merit fails when the merit runs out; a request grounded in mercy does not depend on the petitioner at all.

**The Prayer's Place in the Chapter's Argument**

The chapter has argued at length that God provides, guides, gives life and judges, and that nothing escapes His notice. This prayer is the practical form of that argument. A group that believes the argument addresses its needs to the One it describes, and asks in the terms the argument makes available. The verse therefore shows the argument being lived rather than stated, and it does so in a single clause. Nothing in the petition requires the reader to accept a new premise; it requires only that the premises already established be taken seriously enough to act on. That is the chapter's method throughout, and this verse is its clearest instance in the narrative section.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:87

> We revealed to Moses and his brother, “Appoint houses for your people in Egypt. Turn these houses into places of worship, establish prayer, and give good news to the believers!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Houses Designated in Egypt**

The verse records an instruction with a specific practical content. Appoint houses for your people in Egypt — choose dwellings, settle them, fix a place. The command is not spiritual in the first instance; it is about location. A group under threat needs a place, and the verse begins by providing one. The instruction is addressed to a community that has no temple, no public sanctuary and no right of assembly, and it responds to that condition with a directive about buildings. The verse therefore treats the material arrangement of a religious community as a matter of revelation rather than of expediency.

The second clause converts the dwellings. *Wa-jʿalū buyūtakum qibla* — make your houses places of worship, or, on another reading, orient them toward prayer. The tradition offers two explanations, and they are not mutually exclusive. The first: Pharaoh had destroyed the Israelites' places of worship, so God instructed Moses and Aaron to select dwellings that would function as mosques, allowing the community to re-establish its devotional duties. The second: out of fear of persecution, the Israelites were permitted to establish prayer spaces inside their own homes. Both readings describe the same situation from different angles — public worship had become impossible, and private worship was made sufficient. The verse supplies the legal ground for praying at home when the mosque is not available.

**Prayer Established Under Persecution**

The third clause is the shortest and the most important: establish the prayer. *Wa-aqīmū ṣ-ṣalāh*. The verb is the one used throughout the Qur'an for the establishment of prayer as a regular, structured act rather than an occasional feeling. Its placement here is significant. The community has been given houses and told to make them places of worship; the purpose of both is now named. Nothing about the danger reduces the requirement, and nothing about the loss of the temples excuses it. The verse therefore establishes that the obligation attaches to the people rather than to the building, which is why the building can be replaced by a house. That principle has consequences well beyond this story: a community deprived of its mosque has not been deprived of its prayer.

**Good News at the Worst Moment**

The final clause is the most striking. Give good news to the believers. The command is issued at the point of maximum danger — after the failure of the contest to produce belief, after the description of Pharaoh's tyranny, after the prayer for deliverance. The instruction to bring glad tidings is therefore not a response to good circumstances. It is a command given in bad ones, and its object is the same small group described earlier. The verse treats the announcement of good news as a duty of leadership under pressure, and it places that duty beside the practical arrangements rather than after them. The community is to be housed, given prayer, and told what is coming, and the three are given in that order because a promise addressed to people with no place and no practice would have nothing to hold.

**Addressed to Both Brothers**

The revelation is given to Moses and his brother together, and the joint address continues the pattern established when they were sent. Aaron is not a subordinate recipient here; the instruction is revealed to both, and the commands that follow are in the plural. The verse therefore maintains the pairing throughout the account, which matters because the mission was addressed to a regime and required two voices. The practical arrangements that follow — houses, prayer, glad tidings — are the work of both, and the verse records them as such.

This verse marks the transition from confrontation to consolidation. Everything before it has been public: the mission, the signs, the accusation, the contest, the refusal. Everything after it is communal: a settlement, a devotional life, a prayer against the regime, and then the exit. The verse is the hinge because it establishes the community as a community. A group that has houses, a place of prayer and a promise is no longer only a crowd of frightened individuals. The verse therefore does the work of formation, and it does it with four commands in a single sentence.

**Why Practical Instruction Appears Here**

A reader might expect the narrative to move directly from the prayer for deliverance to the crossing. Instead it inserts a verse about buildings and prayer. The insertion is the point. Deliverance is not the only thing a community needs; it needs a life to be delivered into. The verse supplies that life in advance of the rescue, which means the group is being formed while it is still under threat. The arrangement is therefore not a delay in the narrative but a statement about what sustains a people between the promise and its fulfilment: a place, a practice, and someone telling them what is coming.

The four commands in the verse are not a list but a structure, and the order matters. Houses first: a community needs a location before it can have anything else. Places of worship second: the location is then given a purpose. Prayer third: the purpose is filled with an act. Glad tidings fourth: the act is sustained by a promise. Remove any one of the four and the arrangement fails. A community with houses and no prayer is a settlement; one with prayer and no promise is a group enduring without direction. The verse therefore supplies a complete account of what a religious community requires under persecution, and it supplies it in the order in which the elements must be built. That is why the verse can be so short and still be sufficient.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:88

> Moses prayed, “Our Lord! You have granted Pharaoh and his chiefs luxuries and riches in this worldly life, ˹which they abused˺ to lead people astray from Your Way! Our Lord, destroy their riches and harden their hearts so that they will not believe until they see the painful punishment.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer Opens With an Observation**

Moses does not begin with a request. He begins by stating a fact: You have given Pharaoh and his chiefs adornment and wealth in the life of this world. The two nouns are distinct — *zīna*, ornament and display, and *amwāl*, wealth and property — and together they cover both the appearance and the substance of the regime's power. The observation is addressed to God as an acknowledgment rather than a complaint. Nothing in the wording questions the giving; the giving is stated as God's act. What the prayer goes on to dispute is the use to which the gift has been put, and the distinction between the two is what makes the request that follows intelligible.

The purpose clause is the prayer's pivot: so that they may lead people astray from Your way. The wealth is not condemned for existing but for its function. The regime's display has become an instrument of misguidance — the visible prosperity of the court is used as evidence that its position is correct, and the evidence works. That is precisely the mechanism the chapter described earlier under the heading of gradual drawing-on: gifts keep arriving, warnings stop feeling urgent, and prosperity is read as approval. The verse therefore names a specific social effect of wealth and locates it inside a prayer.

**An Alternative Reading of the Purpose Clause**

The tradition records a second reading of the Arabic particle, and it changes the sense substantially. On that reading the clause means "lest they lead astray" — God gave the wealth so that they would not mislead. Al-Rāzī notes the possibility. The first reading is stronger, for two reasons. It fits the context, since the regime demonstrably did lead people astray. And it fits the prayer's logic, which asks for the wealth to be removed because of its use. The second reading would make Moses' observation a statement about a purpose that was not achieved, and it would weaken the connection between the observation and the request. The dominant reading is therefore the one the verse's own structure supports.

The first request uses a verb of obliteration. *Iṭmis ʿalā amwālihim* — efface their wealth, wipe it out, blot it away. The word denotes the removal of a thing's form so that it no longer appears as what it was. The request is therefore not for redistribution or reduction but for erasure. Its object is the very thing identified in the observation: the adornment and property that gave the regime its persuasive power. The prayer is internally consistent — the instrument of misguidance is the thing asked to be removed — and it asks for removal rather than for the punishment of the people holding it.

**“Harden Their Hearts”**

The second request is the hardest in the passage. *Wa-shdud ʿalā qulūbihim* — tighten their hearts, harden them. The Qur'an speaks of God hardening hearts elsewhere, and the mechanism is consistent: repeated refusal produces incapacity. The request is difficult because it appears to ask for something that forecloses belief, and the verse supplies its own limit in the clause that follows. Read with that limit, the prayer is not a request that God make innocent people unable to believe. It is a statement that the course the regime has chosen has an end, and a request that the end come rather than that the misguidance continue. The alternative — further wealth, further display, further leading astray — is what the prayer asks to be stopped.

**“Until They See the Painful Punishment”**

Moses states the terminus himself, and the statement is the key to the whole prayer. They will not believe until they see the painful punishment. That is a description of a settled condition, not a wish. The chapter has already established the principle in general terms: those against whom the word of the Lord has come due will not believe, even if every sign comes to them, until they see the painful punishment. The prayer therefore aligns itself with a stated pattern rather than requesting an exception to it. What Moses asks for is that the pattern be allowed to run rather than that the means of misguidance be extended.

There is a severe implication in the prayer that should be stated plainly. Moses has spent his mission on the guidance of this regime, and the prayer concedes that it will not occur. The concession is not despair; it is an assessment based on what has been witnessed — the contest, the accusation of magic, the absolute refusal, and the near-total absence of belief. The verse therefore shows a messenger arriving at a conclusion about his audience and acting on it. The prayer is the form that conclusion takes, and its severity is measured by the fact that it asks for the removal of wealth rather than for the destruction of the people.

**What the Prayer Asks For and What It Does Not**

The two requests are limited in a way worth stating. Neither asks for the death of Pharaoh, the death of his chiefs, or the destruction of the population. The objects are wealth and hearts — the instrument of misguidance and the disposition that uses it. The prayer is therefore directed at the mechanism rather than at the people, and it leaves open what the answer will do with them. That openness matters, because the verses that follow record the outcome in a form the prayer did not specify: a pursuit, a drowning, and a preserved body. Moses asked for the misguidance to stop; the answer determined how. The verse therefore shows a messenger stating a need without prescribing the means of meeting it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:89

> Allah responded ˹to Moses and Aaron˺, “Your prayer is answered! So be steadfast and do not follow the way of those who do not know.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer Is Immediate and Explicit**

The verse reports a response in the clearest terms available. *Qad ujībat daʿwatukumā* — your prayer has been answered. The particle *qad* with the past tense indicates a completed fact: the answer has already been given, not merely promised. Nothing is deferred, no condition is attached, and no part of the prayer is refused. The verse therefore settles the petition of the previous verse in a single clause, and it settles it before describing what follows. The reader is told that the request succeeded before being told what to do with the success.

The verb and the pronoun are both dual, and the dual is significant. Your prayer — the prayer of the two of you. The verse confirms what the previous verse's content implied: Aaron joined in the supplication. The tradition notes this explicitly, since the prayer is recorded in Moses' words but answered to both. The detail matters because it maintains the pairing that runs through the whole account. Two were sent, two were given the instruction about houses, two prayed, and two are now addressed. The mission is consistently a joint one, and the verse preserves that fact in its grammar rather than in a separate statement.

**“So Be Steadfast”**

The answer is followed immediately by a command, and the command is not what a reader might expect. It is not "wait for the punishment" or "prepare to leave." It is *fa-staqimā* — stand firm, be upright, keep to the straight course. The command concerns conduct rather than outcome. The two men have just been told that their prayer succeeded, and the instruction that follows is about how they are to behave in the interval. The verse therefore separates the answer from the arrival. The prayer has been granted; the fulfilment has not yet appeared; and the requirement in between is steadiness.

The second command is a prohibition, and it is broader than the first. Do not follow the path of those who have no knowledge. The warning is not about a specific action but about a manner of proceeding — the way of people who act without knowing. The tradition connects the phrase to impatience and to the abandonment of a course once undertaken. The prohibition therefore addresses a real risk in the situation. A man whose prayer has been answered and whose fulfilment is delayed may be tempted to act on his own, to accelerate, or to abandon the position he has held. The verse forecloses that, and it does so by naming the alternative as the way of the ignorant.

**Why Commands Follow a Grant**

The structure of the verse — answer, then two commands — is worth holding onto. A grant without instruction could be received as permission to relax; instruction without a grant would be a demand without support. The verse supplies both, in that order, and the combination defines the interval. The two men know the outcome is settled, and they are told that what remains is their conduct within it. That is a realistic account of how promises operate in a life. The fulfilment is not in the recipient's hands; the manner of waiting is. The verse assigns each party its part and leaves nothing ambiguous.

The phrase has a specific edge in this context. The regime's ignorance has been the subject of the whole account — the accusation of magic, the charge of ambition, the refusal stated absolutely. The prohibition therefore tells Moses and Aaron not to adopt the methods of the people they are confronting, even in pursuit of a goal God has already granted. The means are not made flexible by the certainty of the end. That is a demanding standard, and it is stated at the moment when the temptation to shortcut would be strongest. The verse therefore closes the exchange between the prayer and its fulfilment with a rule about conduct that applies well beyond the story.

**The Interval Between Grant and Fulfilment**

The verse describes a period that most accounts of answered prayer skip. The prayer has been answered and nothing has happened yet. The sea has not opened, the regime has not fallen, and the wealth has not been removed. What exists is the answer, and the answer is not visible. The verse fills that gap with two commands, and both concern the conduct of the men waiting rather than the arrival of the event. That is a realistic description of how promises work in a life: the assurance arrives first, the fulfilment later, and the interval in between is where the recipient's part lies. The verse assigns that part explicitly — steadiness and a refusal to act like those who do not know.

**What the Verse Leaves Unstated**

There is no description of the coming punishment, no indication of its timing, and no account of how the wealth will be removed. The verse answers the prayer and then goes quiet on the details. The silence is consistent with the chapter's method throughout: the fixed term is stated, the date is not. The two men are given the assurance and denied the schedule, which means their steadiness cannot be measured against a known deadline. A man who knows when relief arrives can endure until then; a man who does not must endure indefinitely. The verse asks for the second kind of endurance, and it asks for it in a single clause after a single clause of reassurance. That combination is what makes the command bearable: the assurance removes the doubt, and the command supplies something to do with the waiting.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:90

> We brought the Children of Israel across the sea. Then Pharaoh and his soldiers pursued them unjustly and oppressively. But as Pharaoh was drowning, he cried out, “I believe that there is no god except that in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am ˹now˺ one of those who submit.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crossing in One Clause**

The deliverance that the whole account has been moving toward is given in a single phrase: We brought the Children of Israel across the sea. No detail is supplied — no divided water, no dry path, no description of the passage. The chapter that gave four commands about houses and prayer compresses the miracle into four words. The compression is deliberate. The crossing is not the point of this account; the confession that follows is. By refusing to elaborate the miracle, the verse prevents the reader from resting on it, and it moves immediately to the event the chapter actually needs: a statement of belief made at the moment it can no longer count.

**The Pursuit Described With Two Adverbs**

The second sentence characterizes the pursuit before reporting its end. Pharaoh and his soldiers pursued them *baghyan wa-ʿadwan* — in injustice and in aggression, or in envy and enmity. The two terms cover both the groundless nature of the act and the hostility behind it. The characterization is placed before the drowning so that the reader understands what is being ended. The pursuit is not a military operation with a rationale; it is described as excess and aggression, which is consistent with the description of Pharaoh two verses earlier as exalted in the land and among the transgressors. The verse therefore connects the pursuit to the character already established.

The timing is the verse's entire subject. The confession is not made when the sea opens, not when the pursuit begins, and not when the water closes. It is made when drowning has overtaken him. The verse states the moment precisely — *adrakahu l-gharaq*, the drowning reached him — and then gives the words. The placement is what gives the confession its meaning. A statement of belief made before the danger would have been a choice; made inside it, it is a report of what is now unavoidable. The verse therefore records the words in full, because the full wording is what makes the timing visible.

**The Content of the Confession Is Correct**

There is nothing false in what Pharaoh says. He affirms that there is no god except the One in whom the Children of Israel believe, and he declares himself among those who submit. Both statements are true and both are the statements the messengers demanded. The verse therefore cannot be read as a case of a man saying the wrong thing. The failure is not in the content. It is located entirely in the moment of speaking, and the verse makes that isolation unmistakable by giving the confession in direct speech, exactly as it would be given if it were being accepted.

The Qur'an could have summarized: Pharaoh believed when he was drowning. Instead it quotes him. The reason is that the words need to be seen to be correct, because their correctness is what makes the case instructive. A reader who is told only that Pharaoh believed late might conclude that the content was deficient or that a better formulation would have worked. The quotation removes that possibility. What is preserved here is a perfect confession arriving at the only moment when a confession has no value, and the preservation is what allows the lesson to be drawn in the following verses.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Central Case**

This is the case the chapter referred to earlier when it asked whether the audience would believe only after the punishment overtook them. The hypothetical posed there has now been narrated, and the narration supplies the detail the hypothetical could not: the exact words, the exact moment, and the exact futility. The verse therefore closes the circle that the middle of the chapter opened. The demanders of a hastened punishment are shown what a person says when the punishment arrives, and what he says is true, and it does not help him. Nothing further needs to be argued, which is why the verse ends with the quotation and leaves the response to the verses that follow.

The verse states a principle that the chapter has been building toward and that it will state explicitly shortly afterward. Belief requires a condition: the possibility of not believing. Once that possibility is gone, the statement remains true but it ceases to be belief in the sense that carries weight. The verse demonstrates this rather than asserting it, which is the stronger method. The reader is shown a man in the water saying the right words, and is left to notice that nothing about his situation has changed except that he can no longer choose otherwise. The demonstration is therefore complete without any commentary, and the verse that follows can answer the confession with a single word.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse reports the confession and stops. It does not say the confession was rejected, that it was worthless, or that Pharaoh was condemned for making it. Those statements come in the verses that follow, and their separation from this one is deliberate. The confession stands alone in the text, unaccompanied by any verdict, so that the reader encounters it exactly as it was spoken — complete, correct and useless. Had the verse appended the rejection, the words would have been read as flawed. Left alone, they are read as sufficient in content and void in timing, which is the distinction the chapter needs to make. The verse therefore achieves its effect by refusing to comment.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:91

> ˹He was told,˺ “Now ˹you believe˺? But you always disobeyed and were one of the corruptors

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question in Place of a Verdict**

The response to the confession is not a statement. It is a question: now? The verse does not say the belief is rejected, does not declare it void, and does not pronounce a sentence. It asks a single word, and the question contains the verdict without stating it. The form is deliberate. A verdict can be argued with; a question that points at the timing cannot. The reader is left to supply the answer, and the answer is obvious enough that supplying it is the same as accepting it. The verse therefore achieves in one word what a paragraph of condemnation would achieve less well.

*Ālʾān* — now? The word isolates the only fact that matters. Nothing in the question concerns the content of the confession, the sincerity of the speaker, or the quality of his words. All of those are left untouched. What is put in question is the moment. The same word appeared earlier in this chapter, in the hypothetical about believing after the punishment has overtaken, and the echo is exact. The verse therefore answers its own earlier question with a real case, and the answer is a repetition of the word that made the question meaningful. Timing was always the issue, and the verse reduces the entire exchange to it.

**“But You Always Disobeyed”**

The second clause supplies the record. *Wa-qad ʿaṣayta qablu* — you disobeyed before. The particle *qad* with the past tense marks a completed fact, and *qablu* fixes it in the past: before this moment, before the water, before the pursuit. The clause is therefore not an accusation of a new offence. It is a reference to a history that the confession does not address. The verse places the past and the present side by side and lets the contrast do the work. A life of disobedience is not undone by a sentence spoken when the alternative has been removed, and the verse states the life rather than arguing the point.

The final clause assigns a category. *Wa-kunta mina l-mufsidīn* — you were among those who spread corruption. The same term appeared in Moses' statement at the contest, where the principle was given: God does not set right the work of those who spread corruption. The verse now applies the term to the man whose regime the contest was held under. The classification is significant because it is not about a single act of disobedience but about a pattern of conduct that affected others. Pharaoh is described as one of a group whose work was corruption, and the earlier principle explains what happens to such work. The verse therefore connects the confession to the principle stated ten verses before.

**The Question Expects No Answer**

A question addressed to a drowning man is not an invitation to reply. The rhetorical form is used because it is the only form available at that moment — there is no time for an exchange, and no possibility of one. The question is therefore directed elsewhere. Its real audience is everyone who hears the account, and its function is to fix the meaning of the confession before anyone can reinterpret it. By asking "now?", the verse forecloses the reading that Pharaoh's belief was accepted and merely came too late to save his life. The question establishes that the lateness is not an accident of the situation but the reason the confession does not count.

There is something strange in a speech delivered to someone who cannot hear it, and the strangeness is informative. The verse is not addressed to Pharaoh for his benefit. He is past benefit. The address is a record, and it is preserved for the people who will hear it recited. The chapter has been building toward this case since its middle verses, where the audience was asked whether they would believe only when the punishment overtook them. The verse supplies the documented instance, complete with the words spoken and the answer given. The address to a dying man is therefore a way of speaking to the living, and it is the most economical way available.

**The Verse Completes the Case Begun Earlier in the Chapter**

The hypothetical posed in the middle of this chapter has now become a narrative. The question there was: will you believe when it comes to pass, now, when you used to ask for it to be hastened? The verse here is the answer enacted. A man asked for haste, received it, believed at the moment of receiving, and was told that the moment was the problem. The circle is closed, and the closure is complete. Nothing in the account is left to inference: the confession is quoted, the timing is stated, and the response is a single word. The chapter therefore does not need to draw a conclusion from its central case, because the case has been narrated in a form that draws its own.

**What the Verse Says About Repentance**

The Qur'an states the rule explicitly elsewhere, and the rule is what this verse demonstrates. “However, repentance is not accepted from those who knowingly persist in sin until they start dying, and then cry, ‘Now I repent!’ nor those who die as disbelievers” (4:18). The wording is almost identical to the word the verse uses, and the correspondence shows that the verse is not describing an exception but applying a general principle. Repentance has a window, and the window closes when the choice closes. The verse therefore does something the rule alone cannot do: it shows a man inside the closed window saying the right words, and it records what those words are worth.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:92

> Today We will preserve your corpse so that you may become an example for those who come after you. And surely most people are heedless of Our examples!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Rescue That Is Not a Rescue**

The verse opens with the verb of salvation. *Fa-l-yawma nunajjīka bi-badanka* — today We will save you in your body. The word is the same one used of the deliverance of the Children of Israel, and its application to Pharaoh is the verse's central device. He is saved, and the saving is limited to his corpse. Nothing else of him is preserved — not his life, not his kingdom, not his claim. The verse therefore uses the language of rescue to describe the most complete defeat in the account, and the contrast between the word and the thing is what gives the sentence its force.

The tradition explains the mechanism. The bodies of Pharaoh's soldiers sank; his did not. His body was preserved on a raised surface so that it would be visible, and its visibility is the stated purpose. The preservation is therefore not an honour and not a mercy. It is the making of an exhibit. A body lost at sea is forgotten within a generation; a body displayed becomes a fact that can be pointed at. The verse specifies the medium of the sign with unusual precision, and the precision indicates that the physical fact matters — that the sign is intended to be seen rather than merely reported.

**A Sign for Those Who Come After**

The purpose clause names the audience: so that you may be a sign to those after you. The sign is not for the people who witnessed the drowning. It is for later generations, which means its function is documentary. A man who claimed to be the lord most high is preserved as a corpse, and the preservation is the refutation. Nothing needs to be argued against the claim; the body argues it. The verse therefore makes the refutation permanent and independent of any narration. A reader of any later period can be shown the thing rather than told about it, which is the strongest form the account could take.

The closing observation is the verse's most severe statement, and it is not addressed to Pharaoh. He is dead. The statement concerns the people for whom the sign was made. Signs are given, and most people do not attend to them. The verse therefore supplies the sign and immediately doubts its reception, and the doubt is the chapter's recurring finding — most of them follow assumption, most of them do not give thanks, most of them do not know. The clause is placed at the end of the account so that the reader understands his own position. He has been given the sign; the verse tells him that most people in his situation will not use it.

**The Irony of the Whole Verse**

Everything in the verse inverts what a reader would expect. Salvation is given to a corpse. Preservation is a punishment. A sign is made for people who will not see it. The inversions are not decorative; they are the verse's argument about power. The man who claimed lordship is now an object, and the object serves a purpose he would have despised. The verse therefore completes the account of Pharaoh by showing what remains of him: a body, a purpose, and an audience largely indifferent to both. Nothing in the inversion is stated as a moral. It is simply reported, and the reporting is the moral.

There is a further point in the choice of a body rather than a story. Stories can be disputed, embellished or denied. A body cannot. The verse therefore selects the one form of evidence that admits no interpretation, and it makes that evidence permanent. The claim Pharaoh made — I am your lord most high — is answered by the thing that remains of him, which is matter arranged in the shape of a man. The verse does not need to argue against the claim, and it does not. It states the preservation, states the purpose, and observes that most people will not attend. The argument is finished by the object rather than by the words.

**The Verse Closes the Narrative**

This verse ends the account of Pharaoh. Everything after it turns to the settlement of the Children of Israel, to the Prophet's own position, and to the general principles the chapter draws from the story. The closure is therefore placed at the point of maximum finality — a body preserved for later inspection — and the chapter moves on. The verse's last words are about heedlessness, which is the condition the remainder of the chapter addresses. The narrative is complete, and what remains is its application to the people hearing it.

There is a specific target in the verse, and it is the claim Pharaoh made. He announced himself as the lord most high, and the announcement was received by a society organized around it. The verse answers the announcement with the thing that remains of the man who made it. A lord is not preserved as an exhibit; a lord is not a sign for later generations to inspect. The preservation therefore performs the refutation in the medium the claim was made in — the visible, the public and the lasting. Nothing in the verse argues with the claim directly, because the argument has already been conducted by the crossing, the pursuit and the drowning. What the verse adds is the monument, and the monument is the corpse of the man who said he was a god.

**Why the Sign Is Permanent**

The verse specifies that the sign is for those who come after, which means it is not tied to the memory of witnesses. Memories fade and accounts compete; a preserved body does neither. The choice of a permanent physical sign indicates that the account is meant to survive the generation that heard it, and that its credibility is not meant to depend on the reliability of narrators. The verse therefore builds the durability of the story into the event itself. What later readers receive is not only a report but a report with an object attached to it, and the object is the one thing in the account that cannot be reinterpreted.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:93

> Indeed, We settled the Children of Israel in a blessed land, and granted them good, lawful provisions. They did not differ until knowledge came to them. Surely your Lord will judge between them on the Day of Judgment regarding their differences

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Settled Dwelling in a Blessed Land**

The verse begins with the fulfilment. The people who crossed the sea are settled, and the settlement is described with two qualifiers: it is a sure dwelling, and the land is blessed. The tradition identifies the location as a comely dwelling in Palestine or Syria. The word for settled conveys the placing of a people in a dwelling that suits them, and the blessing attached to the land indicates that the provision is built into the place rather than added to it. The verse therefore describes a completed act of favour: a people delivered from a terror state and given a land capable of sustaining them.

The second clause names what was given. *Razaqnāhum mina ṭ-ṭayyibāt* — We provided them with good things. The term covers the pure, the wholesome and the lawful, and the tradition glosses it as the good and pure foods made lawful for them. The provision is therefore not merely abundant but permitted, which connects this verse to the chapter's earlier charge about people who divided provision into lawful and unlawful on their own authority. Here the division is made by God and given as a gift. The verse therefore presents the correct relation to provision: received, defined by the One who gave it, and used within the terms He set.

**“They Did Not Differ Until Knowledge Came”**

The third clause is the verse's central claim, and it inverts the usual assumption. Difference is normally treated as a symptom of ignorance — people disagree because they do not know. The verse states the opposite. There was no disagreement before the knowledge arrived; the disagreement followed it. The tradition specifies that the differences were over matters of religion and that they arose only after knowledge of the Torah came. The claim is therefore not a complaint about learning but an observation about its effects. Knowledge does not produce agreement automatically, and in some cases it produces the opposite, because it supplies the material over which people can dispute.

**Why Knowledge Produces Difference**

The mechanism is worth stating plainly. Before the Torah there was less to disagree about; after it there was a text, a law, and a body of determinations that could be interpreted in more than one way. Difference arises where there is something determinate to differ over. The verse therefore describes a real feature of religious life rather than a failure of the community. The same observation appears elsewhere: “Mankind was one community. Then Allah raised up the prophets as bearers of good news and as warners. And He revealed with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people regarding their differences. Yet none differed about it except those who were given it—after clear proofs had come to them—out of mutual jealousy” (2:213). The verse here makes the same point in fewer words.

**The Judgment Deferred to the Day**

The final clause states what will happen with the differences. Your Lord will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning what they differed in. The deferral is significant. The verse does not resolve the disputes, does not name which party was right, and does not declare a winner. It states that the judgment belongs to God and that it will occur. That is both a limit and a comfort. It is a limit because no human party can settle the matter finally; it is a comfort because the matter will be settled. The verse therefore removes the dispute from human jurisdiction without dismissing it as unimportant.

The verse is about the Children of Israel, and it is preserved for a community that will face the same situation. A people given a book, settled in a land, and provided with good things will also differ, and the differences will also arise after knowledge rather than before. The verse therefore supplies a precedent that is diagnostic rather than merely historical. It tells the reader what to expect and where the judgment lies. Nothing in the verse suggests that difference is a sign of failure; it suggests that difference is a consequence of having received something determinate, and that its resolution is not in human hands.

**The Verse Closes the Story and Opens the Application**

The narrative of Moses ends here. The crossing, the drowning, the preserved body — all are complete, and the verse adds the settlement as the final element. What follows turns directly to the Prophet: if you are in doubt about what We have sent down to you, ask those who read the Scripture before you. The transition is exact. The story has been told, the people in it have been given their land and their book, and the Prophet is now addressed about his own relation to what he has received. The verse therefore functions as both a conclusion and a hinge, and it is placed where the account's lessons become directly applicable.

There is a temptation to read the third clause as a criticism of the community — they were given a book and they quarrelled over it. The verse does not support that reading. It states a sequence, not a fault. The provision came, the knowledge came, and difference followed, and nothing in the wording suggests that the difference voided the provision. The same community is described as settled in a blessed land and given good things, and those descriptions stand. The verse therefore separates the gift from the reception of it, and it leaves the reception to the judgment named at the end. That separation is what allows the verse to be useful to a later community that will also differ.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:94

> If you ˹O Prophet˺ are in doubt about ˹these stories˺ that We have revealed to you, then ask those who read the Scripture before you. The truth has certainly come to you from your Lord, so do not be one of those who doubt

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Conditional Addressed to the Prophet**

The verse opens with a conditional whose condition is not expected to be met. If you are in doubt about what We have sent down to you. The tradition is explicit that the Prophet was not in fact in doubt, and it offers two readings of the address: the intended audience is the disbelievers, or it is people generally. Both readings preserve the literal form while locating the instruction elsewhere. The verse therefore uses the second person to reach a wider audience, a device the Qur'an employs at several points. The effect is that the instruction is stated in its strongest form — addressed to the messenger himself — so that it cannot be treated as a concession for the weak.

The instruction names a specific group and a specific action. Ask those who recite the Book before you — the Jews and Christians, who recite the Torah and the Gospel. The verse therefore treats the earlier scriptures as a witness to the account just narrated. The stories of Moses, Pharaoh and the crossing are not new material; they exist in prior revelation, and the people who hold that revelation can confirm it. The same point is made elsewhere: “Those to whom We gave the Scripture know it as they know their own sons” (2:146). The verse therefore does not ask for belief on the basis of the Prophet's word alone. It points to an independent corpus that can be consulted.

**Why an External Witness Is Offered**

There is a methodological point in the instruction that deserves notice. A messenger who asks his audience to consult another tradition is not a messenger who fears comparison. The verse invites verification against sources the audience already possesses, and it does so without qualification or caveat. The invitation is therefore an argument in itself. A fabrication could not survive consultation with the Torah; a genuine account can. The verse therefore places the claim in the position most favourable to its falsification, which is the strongest available evidence of its truth. The reader is not asked to trust; he is told where to check.

The middle clause is an assertion, and it does not depend on the verification just proposed. The truth has come to you from your Lord. The verse therefore holds two things together: an invitation to check, and a statement that the truth is already established. The relation between them is not one of proof and conclusion. The checking is offered for the benefit of the hearer, not for the establishment of the fact. The verse therefore does not make its truth contingent on the consultation; it makes the consultation available to anyone who needs it. That is a confident structure, and it is the structure of a claim that does not require confirmation.

**“Do Not Be One of Those Who Doubt”**

The final clause is a prohibition, and it is the strongest form the verse uses. Doubt is not presented as a neutral state or as a stage on the way to belief. It is something a person is told not to be among. The wording — do not be among the doubters — places doubt as a category of people rather than a mood, and the prohibition is against joining that category. The verse therefore treats doubt after the arrival of the truth as a position rather than a condition, which is why it can be forbidden. A person who has been given the account, the witness and the source has no ground left on which to stand and doubt.

**Why the Verse Comes After the Story**

The placement is exact. The narrative of Moses has just concluded, with the settlement, the provision and the deferred judgment. The verse then addresses the Prophet about the account he has just been given. The sequence supplies the reason for the instruction: the story is long, its details are specific, and its people still exist. The verse therefore meets the natural question a hearer might raise — how do we know this account is accurate? — by pointing to the community that holds the earlier version. The instruction is not a general reassurance; it is a response to a specific situation created by the narrative that precedes it.

Read beyond its immediate setting, the verse establishes a method for verifying religious claims. A revelation that agrees with what came before can be checked against it. A revelation that contradicts everything prior cannot. The verse therefore supplies a test that the audience can apply, and it applies it first to the messenger himself, which removes any suggestion that the test is only for other people. The chapter has been arguing from creation, provision and history; this verse adds the argument from prior revelation, and it adds it in the form of an invitation rather than an assertion.

**Why the Prophet Is Named in the Instruction**

The instruction could have been addressed to the audience directly: ask the People of the Book whether this account is in their scriptures. Instead it is addressed to the Prophet, and the indirection changes its force. A messenger told to verify his own revelation is a messenger whose claim does not depend on being believed. The audience hearing the instruction is therefore shown that the standard applies first to the one delivering it. Nothing about the verse suggests the verification is needed; what it suggests is that the verification is available, and that its availability is not a concession. The reader is left with a claim that has offered its own test, which is the strongest position a claim can occupy.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:95

> and do not be one of those who deny Allah’s signs or you will be one of the losers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Prohibition**

The verse continues the sentence begun in the previous one, and it adds a second prohibition to the first. Do not be among the doubters; and do not be among those who deny the signs of God. The two are joined as a single instruction, and the joining indicates that they are distinct rather than synonymous. Doubt concerns the state of the mind; denial concerns an act performed on something already known. The verse therefore forbids both, and it forbids them in the order in which they arise. A person who does not resolve doubt may arrive at denial, and the verse closes both doors in a single breath.

The distinction is the verse's central content. To doubt is to lack certainty; to deny the signs is to reject what has been seen. The Qur'an reserves its stronger language for the second, and the reason is that denial presupposes recognition. A person cannot deny a sign he has not perceived; the act of denial contains an admission. The verse therefore identifies the more serious condition and forbids it separately, which means the prohibition is not a repetition of the first but an addition to it. The reader is told that arriving at certainty is not sufficient; what is done with the certainty matters more.

**“Or You Will Be One of the Losers”**

The consequence is stated in the chapter's recurring commercial vocabulary. *Fa-takūna mina l-khāsirīn* — you will be among the losers. The word denotes one who has lost in a transaction, whose capital has gone and whose account is in deficit. The chapter has used the same imagery throughout: deeds as earnings, lives as transactions, the settlement at the return. The verse therefore states the consequence in the terms the chapter has established, and it states it as a certainty rather than a risk. The particle *fa-* with the subjunctive makes the loss a direct result of the denial, not a possible outcome of it.

The verse does not specify the loss, and the omission is deliberate. The chapter has already specified it elsewhere — the enjoyment of this world is brief, the return is fixed, and the punishment follows the disbelief. Nothing further needs to be added. The reader who has followed the chapter knows what losing means in this vocabulary: not a reduction of pleasure but the expenditure of a life on something that will not carry it. The verse therefore relies on the argument already made rather than restating it, which is why it can be so short. The consequence is not threatened here; it is referred back to a description the chapter has already given in full.

**The Address to the Prophet as Address to Everyone**

The prohibition is formally addressed to the Prophet, and the tradition notes that the intended audience is wider. The device is the same one used in the previous verse, and its effect is the same: the instruction is stated at its most demanding so that no one can claim it was meant for someone else. A prohibition addressed to the messenger cannot be treated by his community as a concession for the weak. The verse therefore reaches every reader through the form of its address, and it does so without breaking the narrative frame. What is said to the Prophet about doubt and denial is said to everyone who receives the account.

**The Verse Completes a Single Sentence**

There is a formal feature worth recording. The verse begins with "and", and it completes the sentence begun in the previous verse. The two are one utterance divided by the verse numbering, and the division is not arbitrary. The first half offers verification and asserts the truth; the second half forbids the two responses that would reject it. Read together, the sentence moves from evidence to obligation: here is where you can check, here is what has come to you, and here is what you are not to do with it. The verse therefore supplies the practical conclusion of the argument, and it supplies it in the form of a prohibition rather than a recommendation.

The chapter has spent ninety-four verses persuading. It has argued from creation, provision, the senses, the alternation of night and day, the history of the messengers and the fate of those who rejected them. This verse stops arguing and forbids. The shift is significant. There comes a point in any argument where further evidence does not change the outcome, because the obstacle is no longer informational. The verse identifies that point and responds to it appropriately. The reader who has received the account, the witness and the source is not lacking information; he is being told what to do with what he has, and the telling is a command rather than an invitation.

**The Two Prohibitions as One Progression**

Read as a sequence, the two prohibitions describe a slide. Doubt comes first, and it is survivable; a person who doubts can still ask, check and arrive. Denial comes second, and it is not a stage on the way to something else; it is a settled position taken against what has been seen. The verse forbids both, but it forbids the second with a consequence attached, which indicates where the danger lies. The progression is therefore not symmetrical. The first prohibition protects a person from a state he can leave; the second protects him from a state he cannot. The verse supplies both warnings in one sentence because the distance between them is shorter than it appears, and because a person who has been warned about both is less likely to mistake the first for a permanent condition.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:96

> Indeed, those against whom Allah’s decree ˹of torment˺ is justified will not believe—

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word That Has Come Due**

The verse names a decree and describes its status. *Ḥaqqat ʿalayhim kalimatu rabbika* — the word of your Lord has come due upon them, has been confirmed against them. The verb *ḥaqqa* denotes something that has become fixed, established and unavoidable; it is the same verb used where the chapter earlier described the word coming due upon those who wronged. The "word" here is God's stated determination, and the verse treats it as a fact rather than as a threat. It has already occurred in the sense that matters: the determination is settled, and what remains is its appearance in time.

The negation is absolute. They will not believe — *lā yuʾminūn*. The verse does not say they are unlikely to believe, that they will find belief difficult, or that belief will be delayed. It denies the act. The form is a statement about a specific group rather than about people in general, and the group is defined by the decree that has come due upon them. The verse therefore describes a completed determination rather than a prediction about character. Nothing in the wording suggests that the group was selected arbitrarily; the verses that follow and the earlier accounts supply the history that preceded the determination.

**The Verse Ends Unfinished**

The verse closes with what functions as a dash, and the sentence runs into the next. The division is a feature of the verse structure rather than of the sense, and it produces a specific effect. The reader encounters a statement about people who will not believe and is held there, without the qualification that follows. The suspension forces attention on the negation itself before the limit is given. The next verse supplies the limit — even if every sign comes, until they see the punishment — and the two together form a single proposition. The verse therefore ends in the middle of a claim, and the incompleteness is what makes the reader wait for the condition.

The translation renders the verb as justified, and the sense deserves precision. The decree is not justified in the sense of being defended; it is confirmed, made real, established as a fact. The wording implies that something preceded the confirmation — a history of refusal that made the determination what it is. The verse does not narrate that history, but it refers to it through the verb. A decree that has "come due" is one whose conditions have been met, and the conditions are supplied by the accounts the chapter has already given: the peoples of Noah, the messengers after him, and the court of Pharaoh. The verse therefore summarizes a pattern rather than introducing a new doctrine.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Theological Statement**

Everything before this verse has been narrative or argument. This verse states the conclusion in doctrinal form, and it does so in the shortest space the chapter uses. There is a category of people for whom the determination has been fixed, and the fixed determination concerns belief. The statement is severe, and its severity is not softened by the verse that follows — it is explained by it. The reader is given the fact first and the mechanism second, which is the order the chapter has used throughout: the observation is stated plainly, and the reasoning is supplied afterward.

The two preceding verses forbade doubt and denial, addressed to the Prophet and through him to everyone. This verse follows with a statement about people who will not believe at all. The sequence is deliberate. The prohibition establishes that belief is required and that its rejection is a loss; the verse then establishes that the rejection can become fixed. The two together describe the full range: a person is commanded to believe, and a person can reach a state in which belief no longer occurs. Nothing in the verse suggests that the second state is where anyone begins. The accounts that precede it all show people arriving there by a long route of refusal.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

Two things are absent and their absence matters. The verse does not name anyone. No individual and no community is identified as belonging to the group, which means no reader can locate himself in it by name. Nor does the verse say that the determination is independent of conduct; the verb "has come due" implies conditions met rather than a decision made without reference to anything. The verse therefore states a fact about a category while leaving the membership of the category undisclosed. That is the only form in which the statement could be useful: it warns without providing a list.

The chapter has described the same phenomenon earlier, in different language. After the messengers who came with clear proofs, it states: thus do We seal the hearts of the transgressors. The sealing and the decree coming due are two descriptions of one condition, and both are given as divine acts following human conduct. The verse here states it more starkly, and the starkness is what requires the qualification in the next verse. Read together, the passages describe a process rather than a selection: refusal repeated until it becomes incapacity, and incapacity confirmed as a fact. Nothing in the verse suggests that the process begins with the confirmation. It ends with it.

**Why the Statement Is So Short**

The verse is one clause long, and the brevity is characteristic of the chapter's hardest statements. The determination that the friends of God have no fear is given in one clause. The determination that the fabricators will not prosper is given in one clause. This verse follows the same pattern, and the pattern is deliberate. A statement that severe does not benefit from elaboration; elaboration would give the impression that it is negotiable. The verse therefore states the fact in the fewest words available and leaves the mechanism to the verse that follows, which is the chapter's standard method for its most difficult claims.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:97

> even if every sign were to come to them—until they see the painful punishment

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Sign, and Still No Belief**

The verse supplies the condition that the previous verse left open, and the condition is exhaustive. Even if every sign came to them. The wording admits no remainder — not one sign is excluded, not one kind of evidence is held back. The verse therefore forecloses the most common objection to the preceding statement: that the people in question were not given enough. They are described as receiving everything available, and the result is unchanged. The chapter has been building toward this claim since its opening verses, where the sufficiency of the signs in creation and revelation was established. This verse extends the sufficiency to its maximum and states the outcome anyway.

The second clause gives the terminus. Until they see the painful punishment. The word *yaraw* means they see, and the seeing is direct rather than reported. The verse therefore identifies a specific moment at which the negation ends — not a change of heart, not a gradual persuasion, but the arrival of the thing threatened. The limit is what makes the statement coherent. A refusal that could never end under any circumstances would be a claim about incapacity; a refusal that ends at a specified point is a claim about disposition. The verse states the point, and the point is the sight of the punishment.

**Belief Born of Despair**

The tradition offers two readings of what happens at that moment. On the first, belief arises out of despair as the person faces impending doom — the case of Pharaoh, described in this very chapter. On the second, the reference is to belief at the time of punishment in the Hereafter, when everyone will "believe" in the sense of seeing what was described. Both readings agree on the essential point: the belief that arrives at that moment is not the belief the messengers asked for. It is a recognition compelled by the thing recognized, and it carries none of the weight that free belief carries. The verse therefore describes a real event and denies its value in the same statement.

**The Verse Completes the Case of Pharaoh**

The account narrated earlier in this chapter is the demonstration of the principle stated here. A man pursued a people out of aggression, was overtaken by drowning, and believed — correctly and completely — at the moment of overtaking. The verse generalizes that case into a rule, and the rule explains why the confession was answered with a question rather than an acceptance. The chapter therefore does not leave the reader with an unexplained narrative. It supplies the principle that accounts for the narrative, and it supplies it immediately after the prohibitions on doubt and denial, so that the two are read together.

The proposition spans two verses, and the division is not accidental. The first verse states the fact — they will not believe. The second states the extent and the limit — even with every sign, until they see the punishment. Separating them allows each element to stand alone. A reader who encounters only the first verse has the fact; a reader who encounters both has the mechanism. The division also places the exhaustive clause at the beginning of the second verse, where it receives maximum emphasis. The construction is therefore deliberate, and it produces a statement that can be quoted in whole or in part without losing its sense.

**The Verse's Answer to a Demand for More Evidence**

The chapter records a recurring demand: produce another sign, tell us when the Hour is, show us something we cannot dismiss. This verse answers the demand by taking it to its limit. Suppose every sign were produced. Suppose nothing were held back. The verse states the result in advance, and the result is that nothing would change for the people it describes. The answer is therefore not a refusal to provide evidence but a statement about the reception of evidence.
