The obstacle is not the quantity of what is given; it is the condition of the one receiving it, and no increase in the first alters the second.

The verse describes a category and leaves the reader outside it, which is where the warning lies. No one is named, and no reader can know that he belongs to the group. What he can know is the route by which people arrive there: repeated refusal, each instance making the next easier, until the disposition is fixed. The chapter has described that route in detail — the sealed hearts of the transgressors, the people who follow assumption, the ones who were heedless. The verse therefore supplies the end of a process the reader can observe in himself, and it supplies it without naming anyone, so that the observation remains available.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Statement About Evidence**

The whole chapter has been an argument from evidence: the creation, the provision, the senses, the alternation of night and day, the history of the messengers. This verse states the limit of that argument. Evidence does not compel belief, and the verse says so by taking the evidence to its maximum — every sign — and reporting the result. The statement is therefore not a discouragement but a clarification. The chapter is not offering signs in the hope that more of them will eventually work. It is offering them because they are true, and it tells the reader in advance that their sufficiency is not the same as their persuasiveness. That distinction is the verse's contribution, and it is why the chapter can be so calm about refusal. Calm is possible only where the outcome is already settled, and the chapter has known the outcome since the verses describing the fixed term.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:98

> If only there had been a society which believed ˹before seeing the torment˺ and, therefore, benefited from its belief, like the people of Jonah. When they believed, We lifted from them the torment of disgrace in this world and allowed them enjoyment for a while

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Wish Expressed About History**

The verse opens with a particle of unfulfilled wish. *Fa-lawlā kānat qarya āmanat* — if only there had been a town that believed. The construction expresses something desired and absent, and its use here is striking. The verse is not stating a rule but lamenting an absence: across the whole recorded history of the warned communities, one exception exists. The framing therefore makes the exception visible. A reader who encounters the verse understands immediately that what follows is unusual, and the unusualness is the point of the verse rather than an incidental detail.

The people of Jonah are named, and they are the only community in the Qur'an described as believing before the punishment arrived and being spared for it. Their story is given elsewhere in brief: Jonah was sent to a hundred thousand or more, he left them in anger, they were warned, and they believed. The account in those Ranged in Ranks records the outcome: “So they believed, and We let them enjoy ˹life˺ for a while” (37:148). The verse here states the same sequence with the additional detail that the punishment was lifted from them in this world, which is the distinctive feature of the case. No other warned community is described as receiving that reversal.

**Their Belief Benefited Them**

The clause *fa-nafaʿahā īmānuhā* — its belief benefited it — is the verse's central statement, and its significance lies in the contrast with what precedes. The previous verses described people who will not believe until they see the punishment, and whose belief at that point is worthless. Here a community believed before the sight, and its belief counted. The distinction between the two cases is entirely one of timing, and the verse makes the timing explicit by attaching the benefit to the belief rather than to the community. Belief has an effect, and the effect depends on when it occurs.

The verse specifies what was removed: the punishment of disgrace in the life of this world. The wording is precise. The punishment was already determined and already approaching; the tradition records that its signs had become visible to the people. Their belief intervened, and the intervention took effect in this life rather than being deferred to the next. That is what makes the case unique. Other communities received forgiveness or reward in the Hereafter; this one had a sentence suspended. The verse therefore establishes that the determination described in the previous verses is not in every instance irreversible before the term arrives.

**“Enjoyment for a While”**

The final clause uses the chapter's own vocabulary. *Wa-mattaʿnāhum ilā ḥīn* — We gave them enjoyment for a time. The same word described what the fabricators have: a brief enjoyment in this world, then the return. The shared vocabulary is significant. The people of Jonah were not given permanence; they were given time. The verse therefore does not present their case as an escape from the general order but as a reprieve inside it. They believed, the punishment was lifted, and they lived out their term like everyone else. The benefit of their belief was real and it was temporal, and the verse states both facts in a single clause.

The placement is exact. The two preceding verses described a determination that appears final: people who will not believe even with every sign, until the punishment arrives. This verse immediately supplies the exception, and the exception prevents the reader from drawing the wrong conclusion. The determination is not a mechanism that operates without reference to human response; it is the outcome of a settled refusal, and where the refusal is reversed in time, the outcome changes. The verse therefore balances the severity of what precedes it with a documented case of reversal, and it places that case in the life of this world where the reader can see it.

Every other community narrated in this chapter ends in destruction. Noah's people drowned; the messengers after him were rejected and their peoples sealed; Pharaoh was preserved as a corpse. The people of Jonah are the single case in which a warned community turned and lived. The verse therefore carries more weight than its length suggests. It is the chapter's evidence that the pattern it describes is not a closed system, and it supplies that evidence in the form of a wish — if only there had been a town that believed — which implies both that the case is rare and that it is possible.

**The Order of the Verse's Clauses**

The verse is built in four steps, and the order carries the argument. First the wish: if only a town had believed. Second the exception: except the people of Jonah. Third the mechanism: they believed, and their belief benefited them. Fourth the outcome: the punishment was lifted and enjoyment was given for a time. Each step depends on the one before it, and the final step is the one that matters for the reader. A belief that benefited its holders, in this world, by removing a punishment already approaching — that is the claim the verse makes, and it makes it about a real community rather than a hypothetical one. The four steps together convert the severity of the preceding verses from a rule into a tendency with a documented exception, and the documentation of that exception is what makes it usable by a later community rather than merely theoretical in the hands of a commentator.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:99

> Had your Lord so willed ˹O Prophet˺, all ˹people˺ on earth would have certainly believed, every single one of them! Would you then force people to become believers

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Counterfactual Stated as Certain**

The verse opens with a conditional about God's will, and the consequence is given as certain. *Wa-law shāʾa rabbuka la-āmana man fī l-arḍi kulluhum jamīʿan* — if your Lord had willed, all who are on earth would have believed, all of them together. The construction leaves no room for partial fulfilment. The verse therefore establishes something about divine power before it says anything about human choice: universal belief is entirely possible, and nothing prevents it except that it has not been willed. The statement is not a limitation on God but a description of what He has not done.

The final word is emphatic, and it deserves attention. *Jamīʿan* — all together, in one body, simultaneously. The verse does not describe a gradual spread of belief across generations or a majority arriving at the truth over time. It describes unanimity. The emphatic addition therefore excludes a weaker reading in which most people believe and a few resist. What is said to be possible is complete agreement, and what is said to be the case is that it has not occurred. The gap between the two is the verse's subject, and the gap is not explained by any deficiency in the message or in the messengers.

**The Question That Follows**

The second sentence is a question addressed to the Prophet. *A-fa-anta tukrihu n-nāsa ḥattā yakūnū muʾminīn* — are you then going to compel people until they become believers? The question expects the answer no, and the expectation is the point. The verse moves from what God could do to what the Prophet cannot do, and the contrast is deliberate. God could produce universal belief by an act of will; the messenger cannot produce belief in a single person by an act of force. The question therefore establishes a limit on the mission that is absolute, and it establishes it in the form of an address to the one carrying it.

The verse has been read throughout the tradition as one of the Qur'an's clearest statements against compelled belief, and the reasoning is structural rather than merely moral. If belief could be produced by force, the question would be pointless. The question assumes that compulsion does not produce belief — it produces compliance, which is a different thing. The verse therefore excludes coercion not on the grounds that it is unjust but on the grounds that it does not work. A person compelled to say the words has not believed, and the account of Pharaoh's confession, narrated earlier in this same chapter, is the demonstration.

**Free Choice as the Condition of Belief**

The tradition draws the theological consequence directly. Compulsion was available and was not used; what was willed instead is that people arrive at belief on their own. The Qur'an states the same principle in the form of an offer: “So let whoever wills take a way to his Lord” (73:19), and again in the sūrah of Man, where the identical wording is repeated. The verse therefore grounds the mission in the freedom of the one addressed. A messenger is sent to people who can refuse, and the possibility of refusal is not a defect in the arrangement but the condition that makes acceptance meaningful.

There is a second function in the verse, and it concerns the one addressed. The chapter has described a messenger whose people refuse him, whose mission produces a handful of believers, and whose warnings are answered with mockery. The verse tells him that the outcome was never in his control, and that its absence is not a failure on his part. God could have produced unanimity and did not; the Prophet cannot produce it and is not asked to. The verse therefore removes the burden of the result from the messenger entirely and leaves him with the delivery, which is the only part of the task that was ever his to perform.

The two preceding verses described a determination that appears fixed and an exception that is rare. This verse supplies the frame that makes both intelligible. Belief is not coerced by God and cannot be coerced by man; it occurs by choice, and the choice can be refused until it becomes fixed. The severity of the earlier verses is therefore not arbitrary. It describes the outcome of a freedom that has been used in one direction for a long time. The verse therefore holds the chapter's hardest statements and its clearest statement about freedom in a single pair of sentences, and it does so without reconciling them artificially.

**What the Verse Does Not Permit**

There is a consequence in the question that should be stated plainly, because it has been drawn throughout the tradition. If compulsion does not produce belief, then no ruler, no parent and no community can produce belief in another person by force, and any attempt to do so produces something other than belief. The verse therefore limits religious authority as well as religious effort. The messenger's task is delivery; everything beyond delivery is outside his power and outside his mandate. The chapter has been describing a messenger who could not guide, could not hasten and could not bring the punishment; this verse adds that he could not compel, and it says so in the form of a question that answers itself. The limit is therefore not a criticism of the messenger but a description of the task he was given, and the task stands complete the moment the delivery has been made and nothing further can be required of him.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:100

> It is not for any soul to believe except by Allah’s leave, and He will bring His wrath upon those who are unmindful

**Expanded Commentary**

**Belief Requires Permission**

The verse states a condition on belief, and the condition is divine. *Wa-mā kāna li-nafsin an tuʾmina illā bi-idhni llāh* — it is not for any soul to believe except by God's leave. The wording is absolute: no soul, without exception, and the negation covers the capacity itself rather than its exercise. The verse therefore establishes that belief is not a purely human achievement. Nothing in the statement denies the choice described in the previous verse; it adds a condition that the choice operates within. The two verses together give the complete picture: the choice is real, and its fruition is not self-produced.

There is an apparent tension between this verse and the one before it, and the tradition resolves it by holding both. The choice belongs to the servant, and the capacity that makes the choice fruitful belongs to God. The two statements are not contradictory if permission is understood as enablement rather than as coercion. A person is not forced to believe; he is made able to. The refusal remains entirely his own, and the possibility of acceptance is not. The verse therefore describes the asymmetry that runs through the whole chapter: the failure belongs to the one who refuses, and the capacity belongs to the One who grants it.

**A Statement From the Tradition**

The early Sufi figure Dhū'l-Nūn al-Miṣrī, who died in the middle of the third Islamic century, put the same point in a compressed formula preserved in al-Qushayrī's treatise. Its sense is that his knowledge of his Lord reached him from his Lord, and that without that giving there would have been no knowing. The formulation keeps both halves of the verse intact: the act of knowing belongs to the servant, and its possibility is a gift. The statement is not a denial of effort. It denies only that effort by itself produces the result, which is the same denial the verse makes in the form of a condition.

The second clause names a consequence. *Wa-yajʿalu r-rijsa ʿalā lladhīna lā yaʿqilūn* — He places filth upon those who do not use their reason. The word *rijs* denotes impurity and defilement, and the commentators identify it here either with divine anger or with the penalty that follows it. Both identifications are recorded, and they are compatible: the impurity is the state, and what it brings is its expression. The verse therefore describes a defilement that is placed rather than merely incurred, which continues the theme of the previous clause. What is granted to one group is placed upon another, and in both cases the agent is the same.

**“Those Who Do Not Use Their Reason”**

The group is defined by the absence of reasoning, and the verb is *yaʿqilūn* — to bind, to grasp, to understand. The Qur'an uses the term consistently for the faculty that connects evidence to conclusion. The verse therefore identifies the cause of the defilement not as ignorance in the sense of lacking information but as a failure to use the faculty that has been given. That is the same diagnosis the chapter has made throughout: the people who follow assumption rather than knowledge, who are heedless of the signs, who do not know. The verse states the consequence of that failure in the strongest term it has available.

The sequence is instructive. The previous verse established that belief cannot be compelled, by God or by man. This verse establishes that belief requires permission and that its absence has a consequence. Read together, the two verses describe a situation in which the messenger has no power over the outcome and the outcome is nonetheless grave. That is the position the chapter has been describing since its opening, and it is the position the Prophet occupies. The verse therefore does not soften the previous one; it completes it. The mission is limited, the stakes are not.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Answer to Despair**

There is a further use for the verse, and it applies to anyone carrying a message that is not received. The failure of others to believe is not evidence that the message is false, and it is not within the messenger's power to correct. What is within his power is the delivery, and the verse leaves him with exactly that. The defilement described in the second clause belongs to those who do not reason, and it is placed by the One who grants permission to those who do. The verse therefore assigns each party its portion and releases the messenger from a responsibility he never held, which is the same release the previous verse gave in the form of a question.

**The Verse Ends the Chapter's Argument**

The doctrinal section of this chapter closes here, and the two verses that form it are the summary of everything before. The message has been given with its evidence, the evidence has been shown to be sufficient, the response has been shown to be free, and the outcome has been shown to belong to God. Nothing in the verse leaves the reader with a task he cannot perform or a burden he has been given by mistake. The remainder of the chapter turns to instruction — what the Prophet is to say, what he is to follow, and how long he is to wait — and that instruction is coherent only because the doctrinal ground has been settled. The verse therefore does the last of the arguing, and it does it in two clauses. Two are enough, because the argument behind them has already occupied a hundred verses and nothing in the summary needs to be restated.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:101

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Consider all that is in the heavens and the earth!” Yet neither signs nor warners are of any benefit to those who refuse to believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Instruction to Look at Everything**

The verse opens with a command whose scope is total. Say: look at what is in the heavens and the earth. No particular object is named — not the sun, not the rain, not the alternation of night and day, all of which the chapter has already discussed. The instruction is to observe the whole of the created order, and the absence of specification is the point. Any object will do, because the argument does not depend on a selected exhibit. The command is addressed to a group that has been asking for signs, and it responds by pointing at everything that already is one. There is no need to produce anything new, and the verse makes that explicit by refusing to name a single thing.

The second clause identifies two means by which people are reached, and it names them as a pair. Signs — the evidence in the world. Warners — the people who draw attention to it. The pairing is significant because the chapter has been using both throughout. The creation, the provision, the senses and the alternation of night and day are the signs; the messengers narrated in the middle of the chapter are the warners. The verse therefore gathers the chapter's two methods into one statement and then states what happens when both are refused. Nothing is left out of the account. The reader is told that the full equipment has been supplied, and that its sufficiency is not the issue.

**The Limit Stated in the Same Verse**

What follows is a negation, and it is unqualified. Neither signs nor warners benefit a people who do not believe. The verse does not say the benefit is small, delayed or uncertain. It says there is none. The statement is severe, and it is placed immediately after the command to look, which produces a striking juxtaposition: look at everything, and know that looking will not help those who have already decided. The verse therefore states both the availability of the evidence and the futility of adding to it, in two clauses, without attempting to reconcile them. The reconciliation lies in the distinction between those who have not yet decided and those who have.

**Why the Limit Is Stated Here**

The placement is deliberate. The chapter has spent a hundred verses supplying signs and recording warnings, and this verse states the condition under which both fail. The statement is therefore a summary of the chapter's findings rather than a new claim. The mechanism was described earlier, where the sealing of hearts was explained as the result of habituated refusal; the verse here names the outcome of that process. Nothing in the wording suggests that the equipment is deficient. The deficiency is in the reception, and the verse locates it precisely — in a people who do not believe, which is a description of a settled state rather than of a momentary hesitation.

**The Verse Is Not Addressed to the Refusers**

There is a feature in the construction that determines who the verse is for. It begins with a command to the Prophet and continues with a statement about a third party. The people described as not benefiting are spoken about, not spoken to. The verse therefore addresses the messenger and, through him, everyone who has not yet closed the question. For that reader the verse is not a discouragement but an instruction: look, and look at everything. The limit applies to a group the reader is not yet in, and the verse leaves the boundary undefined so that the reader cannot place himself inside it by name.

The command is short, and the tradition's gloss on it is demanding. To look is not to glance. The Qur'an describes the failure it is guarding against: “How many signs in the heavens and the earth do they pass by with indifference!” (12:105). The image is of people walking past evidence daily without registering it. The same concern appears where reflection is commanded explicitly: “Have they ever reflected on the wonders of the heavens and the earth, and everything Allah has created, and that perhaps their end is near? So what message after this ˹Quran˺ would they believe in?” (7:185). The verse therefore asks for attention rather than information, and attention is the one thing the audience controls.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Final Word on Evidence**

The chapter's whole method has been evidential, and this verse states where that method stops. It does not stop because the evidence ran out. It stops because evidence addresses a mind that is still open, and there is a point past which openness is gone. The verse therefore makes a distinction the chapter has been working toward from the beginning: the sufficiency of what has been given is one question, and the condition of the one receiving it is another. Both are settled here, and they are settled separately. The evidence is complete, and it is available to anyone willing to look at the heavens and the earth. The refusal is a state, and no addition to the first alters the second. That is why the verse can issue the command to look and state the futility of looking in the same breath without contradicting itself. The command is for those who have not decided. The negation describes those who have. Nothing in the verse allows a reader to move himself into the second group by claiming it, because the second group is defined by a refusal he would have to be making in order to belong to it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:102

> Are they waiting for ˹anything˺ except the same torments that befell those before them? Say, “Keep waiting then! I too am waiting with you.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Leaves Only One Option**

The verse opens with a question that eliminates alternatives. Are they waiting for anything except the like of the days of those who passed away before them? The phrase "the days of those before them" is a Qur'anic idiom for the events that overtook earlier communities — the punishments that fell on Noah's people, on the messengers' opponents, on Pharaoh's court. The question asserts that nothing else remains on the list. Every other possibility has been exhausted by the preceding argument: the signs have been given, the warners have come, the demand for a date has been answered. What is left is the outcome those communities received, and the verse asks whether that is what is being waited for.

The command that follows is short and unexpected. *Fa-ntaẓirū* — wait, then. The Prophet is not told to argue further, to produce another sign, or to warn again. He is told to let the waiting continue. The command accepts the audience's position and declines to contest it, which is a stronger response than refutation. A person who is arguing is still trying to persuade; a person who says "wait" has stopped. The verse therefore marks the end of the argumentative section of the chapter. Everything after this point is declaration, not dispute, and the command to wait is the line between the two.

**“I Too Am Waiting With You”**

The second half of the reply is the verse's most striking feature. The Prophet joins the waiting. He does not stand outside it as one who knows the outcome and watches others approach it; he places himself inside the same interval. The statement is therefore not a threat delivered from safety. It is an announcement that the messenger and his audience are in the same situation with respect to the term — all of them are waiting, and none of them controls the arrival. The sharing of the wait removes the asymmetry that a threat would create, and it makes the statement a fact rather than a warning.

The formulation is not unique, and the parallel is close enough to be instructive. “Are they awaiting the coming of the angels, or your Lord ˹Himself˺, or some of your Lord's ˹major˺ signs? On the Day your Lord's signs arrive, belief will not benefit those who did not believe earlier or those who did no good through their faith. Say, ‘Keep waiting! We too are waiting’” (6:158). The two passages share the command and the shared waiting, and the longer version supplies what the shorter one leaves implicit: the arrival of the signs closes the period in which belief has value. The verse here therefore carries that meaning with it, and it does so without stating it, because the chapter has already stated it in the account of Pharaoh.

**What the Waiting Means for Each Party**

The shared waiting conceals a difference, and the verse leaves the reader to supply it. Both parties are waiting, and what they are waiting for is not the same thing. For the audience, the wait ends in the event they have been dismissing. For the messenger, it ends in the fulfilment of the promise the next verse states. The interval is identical for both parties and the outcomes at its end are opposite. The verse therefore does not need to spell out the difference; the command to wait, issued to both, is sufficient to establish the common condition, and the following verse supplies the divergence.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Answer to Delay**

The audience has been asking when. The chapter has answered that the term is fixed and cannot be moved, that the Prophet does not know its date, and that nothing escapes the witness. This verse gives the final answer: keep waiting. The reply is not evasion, and it is not a refusal to engage. It is the only answer consistent with everything the chapter has already established about the term and its fixing. The date is not available, the arrival is certain, and the interval is the only period in which anything can be done about it. The verse therefore closes the demand by granting it — the wait will continue, and it will end — and it places the messenger inside the wait so that the grant is not a dismissal.

**What the Waiting Reveals About the Waiters**

There is an inference in the verse that is worth drawing out. A person who waits is a person who has not dismissed the thing he waits for. Dismissal ends the relationship; waiting continues it. The audience's demand to be shown when the punishment will arrive is therefore not pure contempt — contempt would not ask. It is a refusal held alongside a question, and the verse answers the question by granting the wait rather than the date. That distinction matters for how the chapter understands its opponents. They are not described as incapable of understanding. They are described as people who have declined to act on what they understand, and who are now waiting for the outcome they declined to avoid. The command to wait is therefore not a dismissal of them. It is an acknowledgement of the position they have already taken, stated back to them in their own terms. A demand answered by a refusal would leave the demanders arguing; a demand answered by its own fulfilment leaves them with nothing at all to say, because the thing they asked for is now in progress and they are standing inside it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:103

> Then We saved Our messengers and those who believed. For it is Our duty to save the believers

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Present Tense Describing the Past**

There is a grammatical feature in this verse that carries meaning. The verb "We save" is in the present form, and the events it describes are past — the deliverance of Noah, of the messengers after him, of Moses and those with him. The tradition notes the point explicitly: the present tense refers back to the days of those who passed away before, and it means "then We saved." The effect of the tense is to make the past action available as a pattern. A past event narrated in the past tense belongs to history; the same event stated in the present tense belongs to a rule. The verse therefore does not merely report what happened. It states what happens, and the difference between those two is the whole content of the verse.

The objects of the saving are named in a pair, and the pairing is consistent across every account in the chapter. Noah was saved and those with him in the Ark. Moses was brought across the sea and the Children of Israel with him. The verse generalizes the pattern, and the generalization includes the followers rather than only the messenger. That inclusion matters, because the messengers could have been described as protected in their office alone. They are not. The verse states that those who believed were saved alongside them, which makes the promise extend to the community rather than stopping at its leader.

**“It Is a Duty Upon Us”**

The second sentence is the verse's strongest statement, and its wording is unusual. *Ḥaqqan ʿalaynā* — a duty upon Us, an obligation binding on Us. The Qur'an rarely describes God as bound by anything, and where it does, the binding is self-imposed. The phrase therefore states the promise in the most binding form the language allows. It is not a hope, an intention or a likelihood. It is described as something owed, and the one who owes it is the One who has already delivered on it repeatedly in the accounts just narrated. The verse therefore grounds the future promise in the past record, and it states the obligation in terms that leave no room for revision.

**The Promise Applied to the Present Community**

The tradition reads the verse as addressing the immediate situation: God will deliver the Prophet and his followers from the persecution of the Makkans, as He delivered earlier messengers and their followers from their persecutors. The application is not an extension of the verse but its intended reference. The same assurance is given elsewhere in explicit terms: “We certainly help Our messengers and the believers, ˹both˺ in this worldly life and on the Day the witnesses will stand forth ˹for testimony˺” (40:51). The two passages cover the same ground, and together they place the deliverance in both worlds. The verse here states it in the shortest form, as an obligation already incurred.

The placement is exact. The previous verse ended with the command to wait and the statement that the messenger waits too. The reader is left inside an interval with no stated outcome. This verse supplies the outcome, and it supplies it for both parties — the messengers and the believers are saved. The waiting is therefore not open-ended in its meaning, even though it is open in its duration. The verse converts the shared interval from a suspense into a known arrangement: the wait ends, and what ends it is a deliverance that God has described as owed. Nothing at all about the timing is given here, and nothing needs to be, because the obligation itself is what the verse is asserting and the date was never the point.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Guarantee**

The chapter has described destruction repeatedly — the drowning, the sealing of hearts, the preserved corpse, the communities warned and then overtaken. This verse states the other half of the record, and it states it as a rule rather than as a series of events. The messengers are saved and those who believe with them. The verse therefore supplies the balance that the narrative on its own could not supply, because narrative reports individual cases and this verse states the pattern behind them. The reader who has followed the chapter's accounts is told that the outcome for the believer is not an exception found in a few stories. It is described as a duty, and the description is what allows the promise to be relied on.

**The Verse and the Reader's Own Case**

The verse states a pattern, and patterns apply to whoever falls inside them. The messengers and those who believed were saved; the statement is given as an obligation, not as a record of exceptions. A reader therefore does not need to identify with a particular prophet or a particular century in order to be covered by it. The condition is belief, and the condition is stated in the verse itself. That is what makes the verse usable rather than merely historical. The chapter has narrated cases in which the deliverance came late, in which the numbers were small, and in which the believers were afraid throughout. None of those features excluded them. The verse therefore describes a rule that operated under the least favourable circumstances the chapter records, which is the strongest form in which a rule of that kind could be stated. A promise illustrated only by easy cases would be weak evidence; a promise illustrated by a drowning, a terror state and a small remnant of frightened youths is evidence of an entirely different order.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:104

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O humanity! If you are in doubt of my faith, then ˹know that˺ I do not worship those ˹idols˺ you worship instead of Allah. But I worship Allah, Who has the power to cause your death. And I have been commanded, ‘Be one of the believers,’

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Doubt Named and Then Answered**

The verse begins by identifying a possible state in its audience. If you are in doubt about my religion. The conditional is not dismissive; it takes the doubt seriously enough to answer it. What follows is not an argument but a declaration, and the declaration is the answer. The verse therefore treats religious doubt as something addressed by clarity of position rather than by accumulation of proof. A person uncertain about what a messenger believes is given the belief stated in full, in the messenger's own voice, without qualification. That is the method the verse uses, and it is the method the rest of the passage continues through the verses that follow.

The declaration has two parts, and the order is fixed. First: I do not worship those you worship apart from God. Second: I worship God. The negation comes first, and the reason is that the audience's assumption must be removed before the positive statement can be heard. A person who assumes that the messenger is proposing an additional deity alongside the existing ones will mishear any affirmation. The verse therefore clears the ground and then states the position. The structure is the same one used in the shortest sūrah of the Qur'an, where the declaration of a way of worship is separated from the worship of others, and the separation is what makes the statement unambiguous.

**“Who Will Take Your Souls”**

The affirmation carries a qualifier, and the qualifier is the verse's sharpest feature. I worship God — the One who will take your souls. The clause is not a general description of divinity. It is a specific statement about the audience's own end, and it is placed inside the declaration of worship so that the two cannot be separated. The tradition explains the purpose: the God the Prophet worships is the One whom people meet after death and who will inform them of what they used to do, whereas the gods of the audience have no role beyond this life. The qualifier therefore converts a theological statement into a personal one. The audience is told which of the two objects of worship will be present at their death.

**“I Have Been Commanded to Be Among the Believers”**

The final clause is a report of a command, and its wording is modest. The Prophet does not say he is the first of the believers, the greatest, or their leader. He says he has been commanded to be among them. The same formulation appears elsewhere: “And I am commanded to be the first of those who submit ˹to His Will˺” (39:12), and in a context where the command is paired with a prohibition: “Say, ‘I have been commanded to be the first to submit and not be one of the polytheists’” (6:14). The verse here uses the more inclusive form, and the inclusiveness is significant. The messenger places himself inside the community of believers rather than above it, which is the same self-description Noah gave in the account narrated earlier in this chapter.

The verse opens with *yā ayyuhā l-nās* — O humanity. The address is not to the Makkan opponents specifically, although they are the immediate occasion. It is to everyone, and the widening is deliberate. The declaration that follows is not a local position taken in a local dispute; it is a statement of worship addressed to the whole of the species. The chapter has used the same address where the revelation was described as an exhortation, a cure, a guidance and a mercy. The two uses are connected. A message given to humanity is answered by a declaration made to humanity, and the verse supplies that answer in the plainest terms available.

**A Statement of Position, Not an Argument**

The verse contains no evidence, no citation and no appeal to authority. It states what the messenger worships, what he does not worship, and what he has been commanded to be. The absence of argument is the point. The chapter has already made its case across a hundred verses; what remains is the position, and the position is stated rather than defended. A reader who has followed the argument receives the declaration as its conclusion. A reader who has not receives it as a claim that can be examined. In both cases the verse does the same work: it makes the messenger's position fully visible, so that nothing about it has to be inferred.

**The Declaration Against Social Pressure**

There is a social dimension in the verse that is easy to overlook. Worship in that society was not a private matter; it was the public grammar of belonging, and the gods of the audience were tied to lineage, status and the city's identity. To declare that one does not worship them was not a theological opinion but a withdrawal from a shared arrangement. The verse therefore records a costly statement, and it records it as something the messenger says aloud to the whole of humanity. The declaration is not made quietly, and it is not qualified for the sake of the hearers. That is what gives the following verses their weight. The commands quoted there are given to a man who has already stated his position publicly and has nothing further to protect by softening it. The declaration is therefore the ground on which the creed that follows can be received as instruction rather than as negotiation.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:105

> and, ‘Be steadfast in faith in all uprightness, and do not be one of the polytheists,’

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Continues Inside a Quotation**

The verse opens with a conjunction and continues the report begun in the previous one. The Prophet has said that he has been commanded; this verse supplies the next item in the list of commands. The nested quotation is preserved, and the nesting matters. What the reader receives is not a narration about a command but the command itself, quoted in the form in which it was given. The effect is to place the reader inside the address rather than outside it. He is not being told what the Prophet was told; he is being told the thing. That is why the verse can be so short and still function as instruction rather than as report.

**“Set Your Face”**

The idiom in the command is physical. To set the face toward something is to orient the whole person toward it — the direction of the body determines the direction of the attention, and the Qur'an uses the image where total commitment is required. The phrase occurs in several places with the same force, and it always denotes an undivided orientation rather than a preference. A person whose face is set toward something is not simultaneously facing elsewhere, and the idiom therefore excludes partial adherence. The verse's command is not to believe in a general sense but to orient, which is a claim about the arrangement of a life rather than about the contents of a mind.

The command specifies the manner of the orientation: as a *ḥanīf*. The term has a history in the Qur'an's vocabulary. It is used of Abraham, and it denotes one who turns away from every false object of worship toward the one true object — an inclination that is also a rejection. The word is paired throughout the Book with the negation of association, and the pairing is what gives it content. To be a *ḥanīf* is not to hold a mild or tolerant position; it is to have turned, and the turning has a direction away from something as well as toward something. The verse therefore supplies both the orientation and its exclusivity in a single word.

**Uprightness as Sincerity**

The tradition glosses the command as complete sincerity in serving God and turning away from what is other than Him. The gloss identifies two movements, and both are required. Sincerity directed toward God without the turning away is incomplete, because the thing turned away from continues to hold a share of the attention. The turning away without the sincerity is merely rejection. The verse's single word covers both, and the gloss makes explicit what the word implies. Read this way, the command is about the undivided character of worship rather than about its amount, and that is why it can be given to a prophet who is already worshipping.

The command is followed by a prohibition, and the prohibition names the alternative. Do not be among those who associate. The form is notable: the prohibition is not against an act but against membership in a group. The verse therefore treats association as a company a person keeps rather than only as something a person does. The distinction is real. A single act of association could be repented and left behind; belonging to a people defined by it is a standing condition. The verse forbids the condition, and it does so in a command addressed to the one furthest from it, which is how the chapter has consistently framed its instructions to the Prophet.

**Why Steadfastness Comes First in the List**

The commands quoted across these verses are ordered, and the order is meaningful. The first is orientation and steadfastness; the second is a prohibition against invoking what can neither benefit nor harm; the third is a statement about who removes harm and who gives good. The sequence moves from the general disposition to the specific act to the theological ground of both. Steadfastness comes first because the other two depend on it. A person who is not steadfast will invoke other things when the harm arrives, and will not trust that the good is in one hand. The verse therefore supplies the foundation on which the rest of the creed rests, and it supplies it as a command rather than as a description.

**The Command Reaches Beyond the Prophet**

The instruction is quoted, and quotations travel. A command reported inside a messenger's declaration is preserved precisely so that it can be applied by people who were not present when it was given. The verse therefore functions as instruction to the reader, and its content is general enough to allow that: set your face, be upright, do not belong to those who associate. Nothing in the wording depends on the Prophet's particular situation. What depends on it is the authority of the command, and that authority is established by the fact that it was given to him first. The reader receives an instruction whose provenance is stated and whose application is not limited, which is the form the chapter uses for everything it wants the community to carry forward. The content of the command also makes it durable. Orientation, sincerity and the refusal of association do not depend on a particular law, a particular place or a particular political arrangement. They can be performed by anyone, anywhere, under any regime, which is why the verse can be addressed to a prophet in one century and read by a believer in another without adjustment. The command is short because it has to survive translation into circumstances its first recipient could not have imagined.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:106

> and ‘Do not invoke, instead of Allah, what can neither benefit nor harm you—for if you do, then you will certainly be one of the wrongdoers,’

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Prohibition Against Invocation**

The verse forbids a specific act, and the act is invocation. *Lā tadʿu* — do not call upon. The verb covers supplication, appeal and the addressing of a request, and it is the primary act of worship in the Qur'an's vocabulary. The prohibition therefore goes to the centre of religious practice rather than to its edges. It is not a restriction on a ritual detail; it is a statement about whom a person addresses when he needs something. The verse places that question at the head of the creed it is building, and it answers it by exclusion before the next verse answers it by affirmation.

The objects of the prohibition are defined by a test, and the test is functional. Whatever can neither benefit you nor harm you is not to be invoked. The formulation appears repeatedly in the Qur'an, and it is always used as a decisive criterion. “Say, ˹O Prophet˺, ‘How can you worship besides Allah those who can neither harm nor benefit you? And Allah ˹alone˺ is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing’” (5:76). And in the longer form: “Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, ‘Should we invoke, other than Allah, those ˹idols˺ which cannot benefit or harm us, and turn back to disbelief after Allah has guided us?’” (6:71). The verse here uses the same test in the shortest available form, as part of a command rather than as a question.

**The Test of Benefit and Harm**

The criterion deserves a word of its own, because it is empirical rather than doctrinal. A person is not asked to accept a theology before applying the test. He is asked to observe what the object of his invocation actually does. Can it give him something he needs? Can it prevent something he fears? If the answer to both is no, the invocation has no object. The verse therefore appeals to the audience's own experience rather than to a revealed proposition, and the appeal is available to anyone at any time. The chapter has used the same method throughout — asking who provides, who guides, who gives life — and this verse reduces the whole line of questioning to a single test.

**“You Will Be Among the Wrongdoers”**

The consequence is stated absolutely, and its form matches the prohibition. Do not invoke — for if you do, you will certainly be among the wrongdoers. The certainty is marked by the particle, and the category is the chapter's standard one. Wrongdoing in this vocabulary is not a general moral failure; it is the placing of something where it does not belong. The verse therefore identifies invocation of a powerless object as a specific error with a specific name, and it states the naming as an unavoidable result rather than as a possible judgment. The reader is told what the act makes him, not merely what it risks.

The command is formally addressed to the Prophet, inside a quotation of what he was commanded. The device is the one the chapter has used repeatedly, and its effect is the same. A prohibition given to the messenger cannot be treated by his community as a concession for the weak. The reader receives the instruction at its most demanding, addressed to the person least likely to violate it, and therefore applicable to everyone else without exception. The verse therefore reaches the whole audience through the form of its address, and it does so without breaking the structure of the quotation that contains it.

**The Verse Completes the First Half of the Creed**

Read with the verse before it, this one completes a pair. The first commanded orientation and forbade belonging to the people who associate; this one forbids the act that defines that belonging. The two together state the negative half of the creed: where to face, and what not to call upon. What follows supplies the positive half — who removes harm and who gives good. The verse therefore occupies a precise position in the sequence, and its brevity is proportionate to its function. It states a prohibition and its consequence, and it leaves the ground of both to the verse that follows.

**The Test Is Functional, Not Doctrinal**

There is a methodological point in the criterion that deserves to be stated. The verse does not ask the reader to accept a proposition about the nature of the gods before applying the test. It asks him to observe what they do. Can they give? Can they prevent? Those are questions about outcomes, and they can be answered by anyone without theological training. The verse therefore places the argument on ground the audience already occupies. A person who has invoked something repeatedly has a record of what happened, and the record is the evidence. The verse therefore does not require the audience to accept anything new. It requires them to notice something old — the long history of requests made and outcomes observed — and to draw from it the conclusion they have so far avoided. That is a lower demand than belief, and it is why the prohibition is stated without argument. A person who has already performed the test a hundred times does not need to be persuaded of its result; he needs to be told what the result means. That is why the prohibition is stated so confidently and so briefly. It does not need to be defended at any length, because the test it rests on is one the audience has already performed many times over without ever drawing the conclusion.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:107

> and ‘If Allah touches you with harm, none can undo it except Him. And if He intends good for you, none can withhold His bounty. He grants it to whoever He wills of His servants. And He is the All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.’”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Harm That None Can Remove**

The verse states the ground on which the preceding prohibition rests, and it begins with harm. If God touches you with harm, none can remove it except Him. The statement is exhaustive in its negation. No intermediary, no intercessor, no object of invocation can undo what has been sent. The verse therefore completes the test given in the previous verse from the other side: the things that cannot benefit or harm are excluded not only because they are powerless in themselves but because the power to remove harm lies in exactly one place. The reader who has understood the test now understands why it is decisive.

The second clause mirrors the first, and the mirroring is exact. If God intends good for you, none can hold back His bounty. The symmetry is the verse's central claim. Both directions are closed to anyone but God — the harmful cannot be removed by another, and the good cannot be blocked by another. The consequence is that the whole of a person's situation lies in one hand, and no arrangement of creatures can alter it. The verse therefore removes the last justification for invoking anything else. If other powers could add or subtract, invocation of them would have an object. The verse denies both possibilities in a single pair of clauses.

**“He Grants It to Whomever He Wills”**

The third clause concerns the distribution of the good. He causes it to fall upon whomever He wills among His servants. The statement is about selection, and it is placed after the statement about power rather than before it. The order matters: the verse first establishes that no one can withhold the bounty, and then states that its arrival is determined by God's will. The same point is made elsewhere in a similar form, where provision is described as given to whom God wills without reckoning. The verse therefore does not present the distribution as arbitrary; it presents it as belonging to the same hand that controls the removal of harm, which is the only hand the preceding clauses have established.

The verse ends with forgiveness and mercy, and the placement of those two names is significant. Everything before them has been about power — harm none can remove, good none can withhold, bounty given to whom He wills. A verse ending there would leave the reader with a description of unqualified power. The two names convert the description. The One who holds both directions is described as forgiving and merciful, which tells the reader what the power is exercised by. The verse therefore does not soften its claim about sovereignty; it identifies the character of the sovereign, and it places that identification last so that it is what remains.

**The Verse as the Ground of Trust**

The creed quoted across these verses began with a command to be steadfast and upright. This verse supplies the reason steadfastness is possible. A person who believes that harm can be removed by someone other than God will turn to that someone when the harm arrives, and his steadfastness will fail at exactly the point where it is tested. A person who believes that the good can be blocked by a rival will seek to appease the rival. The verse removes both temptations by closing both directions, and the closure is what makes the earlier command practicable. The creed is therefore built in the order a person actually needs it: the orientation, the prohibition, and then the fact that makes both sustainable.

**The Creed Ends With Mercy**

The closing quotation mark falls at the end of this verse, which means the creed the Prophet was commanded to declare ends on forgiveness and mercy. That is a deliberate arrangement. The declaration began with a negation — I do not worship what you worship — and passed through orientation, prohibition and sovereignty. It ends with the two names that describe what the sovereign does with those who fail. The reader is therefore left not with the severity of the exclusivity but with the character of the One it excludes everything for. The verse therefore closes the quoted section on the note the chapter has been building toward, and it does so in three words.

**The Verse Removes the Last Reason to Look Elsewhere**

Read as the conclusion of the creed, this verse closes every door the previous commands left open. The command to be upright could be obeyed imperfectly if a person believed that other powers held a share of influence — he would then reserve part of his attention for them. The prohibition against invoking them could be broken under pressure if a person believed they might help. The verse removes both possibilities by stating that harm cannot be undone by anyone else and good cannot be blocked by anyone else. What remains is a single address, and the creed is complete. The two names at the end then describe the One to whom that single address is made, so that the exclusivity is not left as a bare demand but as a description of a relationship whose character has been named. A reader who takes the verse seriously no longer has a reason to divide his attention, and no reason to fear that dividing it would have helped. Both temptations are answered by the same pair of clauses, and the answer given to each of them is complete.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:108

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O humanity! The truth has surely come to you from your Lord. So whoever chooses to be guided, it is only for their own good. And whoever chooses to stray, it is only to their own loss. And I am not a keeper over you.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Truth Has Arrived**

The verse opens with a completed fact. The truth has come to you from your Lord. The verb is past and the particle confirms it: the arrival is not promised, approaching or conditional. It has occurred. The verse therefore begins from a settled position, and everything that follows depends on that settlement. A reader who disputes the arrival disputes the verse's premise; a reader who accepts it is left with the consequences stated in the next two clauses. The verse does not argue for the arrival here, because the chapter has spent a hundred verses doing so. It states it as the ground on which the closing offer is made.

**Guidance Benefits the One Guided**

The first consequence is stated with an emphasis that is easy to miss. Whoever is guided is guided only for his own soul. The restriction — only — is the point. The benefit does not accrue to the messenger, to the community as an institution, or to God. It belongs to the person guided. The same formulation appears elsewhere with the same restriction: “So whoever chooses to be guided, it is for their own good. And whoever chooses to stray, it is only to their own loss. You are not a keeper over them” (39:41). The verse therefore removes any suggestion that acceptance of the message is a favour conferred on its bearer. It is a gain retained entirely by the one who gains it.

The second consequence mirrors the first, and the mirror is exact. Whoever goes astray goes astray only to its detriment. The loss does not fall on the messenger, on the message, or on anyone else. The symmetry between the two clauses is the verse's central claim: both outcomes remain with the person who produces them. Nothing is transferred in either direction. The verse therefore states a closed economy of consequences, and it states it in the plainest terms available. A reader cannot improve his position by shifting the cost of his choice onto anyone, and he cannot reduce his gain by sharing it.

**“I Am Not a Keeper Over You”**

The final clause defines the messenger's role by exclusion. *Wa-mā anā ʿalaykum bi-wakīl* — I am not a guardian, trustee or keeper over you. The same statement appears elsewhere in the Qur'an, and each occurrence marks a limit on the mission. The Prophet is not a warder over his people; he is a bearer of good news and a warner. The verse therefore states what the messenger cannot do, and the statement is a release rather than a complaint. He is not responsible for the outcome of his audience's choice, and the verse says so in the first person, in his own voice, at the end of a chapter that has described his inability to guide from its opening verses.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Closing Offer**

The address is to humanity, and the offer is complete. The truth has come; the consequences are distributed; the messenger's role is defined. Nothing further is asked of the audience in this verse — no action, no declaration, no ritual. The offer is simply left with them. That is the chapter's final posture toward its audience, and it is the same posture it has held throughout: the message is given, the outcome is stated, and the choice remains where it began. The verse therefore closes the argumentative and declarative sections of the chapter without adding a demand, which is why the last verse can be addressed to the Prophet alone.

The tradition draws the consequence directly. Now that the truth has come, there is no excuse for rejecting it. The reasoning is that the arrival of the message ends the period in which ignorance was available as a defence. The verse therefore does something the chapter has been doing since its middle verses, where the universality of the messengers was established so that no community could plead that it had never been addressed. The closing verse applies the same principle to the individual reader. He has received the message; the consequences have been stated; the messenger has declared that he is not responsible for the outcome. What remains is entirely the reader's, and the verse leaves it there.

**What the Verse Leaves Behind**

The verse ends with a denial of responsibility, and the denial is the last thing the audience hears. That arrangement is deliberate. A messenger who claimed responsibility for his people's guidance would give them leverage: they could hold him accountable for an outcome he does not control, and he would be tempted to adjust the message in order to produce it. The verse removes that possibility from both sides. The messenger is released from a burden he never carried, and the audience is left without anyone to blame. What remains is a truth that has arrived, a set of consequences that stay with the person who chooses, and an interval in which the choosing happens. The verse therefore closes the chapter's address to humanity by returning everything it offered to the hands of the people it was offered to. There is no intermediary left in the transaction, no one to appeal to and no one to blame. The truth has arrived, the consequences have been assigned, and the interval in which the choice is made is the only portion of time the reader certainly possesses.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:109

> And follow what is revealed to you, and be patient until Allah passes His judgment. For He is the Best of Judges

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Commands Close the Chapter**

The final verse contains two imperatives and one statement, and the arrangement is the chapter's conclusion in miniature. Follow what has been revealed to you. Be patient until God judges. He is the best of judges. The first command concerns action, the second concerns duration, and the statement supplies the ground for both. Nothing in the verse is addressed to the audience. The closing words of the chapter are spoken to the messenger alone, which is a significant way to end. A hundred and eight verses have been directed outward — to humanity, to the opponents, to the readers. The last one turns inward, and it leaves the messenger with a task rather than with an argument.

**“Follow What Is Revealed to You”**

The command is general, and its generality is the point. No specific injunction is named, no particular ruling is cited. The instruction is to follow the revelation as a whole, and the same command appears elsewhere in the same terms: “˹O Prophet!˺ Follow what is revealed to you from your Lord—there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—and turn away from the polytheists” (6:106). And again: “Follow what is revealed to you from your Lord. Surely Allah is All-Aware of what you ˹all˺ do” (33:2). The verse here gives the command in its shortest form, without the accompanying prohibition, because the prohibitions have already been stated in the creed quoted in the preceding verses.

The second command specifies its own limit. Be patient — until God renders His judgment. The patience is therefore not open-ended in principle, even though its duration is unknown. The verse states that there is an endpoint and that it belongs to God, and both facts are what make the command bearable. The same instruction is given elsewhere with its difficulty acknowledged: “Be patient ˹O Prophet˺, for your patience is only with Allah's help. Do not grieve over those ˹who disbelieve˺, nor be distressed by their schemes” (16:127). The verse here supplies the reason patience is possible: the judgment will come, and it will not be rendered by the parties involved.

**“He Is the Best of Judges”**

The final clause names the quality of the coming judgment, and the superlative is the verse's last word on the subject. The chapter has argued at length that the judgment will be just — that no soul is wronged, that the settlement matches the account, that God is never unjust to His creation. This clause states the same claim in its shortest form and places it at the end. The effect is to leave the reader with the character of the judge rather than with the details of the case. Everything the chapter has described — the drowning, the preserved corpse, the sealing of hearts, the deliverance of the believers — is referred to a judgment whose quality has been stated in three words.

The tradition reads the command to be patient as containing a promise: God would help the Prophet overcome his enemies and grant him victory. The reading is supported by the structure. A command to wait for a judgment, followed by a statement that the judge is the best of judges, implies an outcome rather than merely an ending. The same assurance is given elsewhere in explicit form: “Indeed, messengers before you were rejected but patiently endured rejection and persecution until Our help came to them. And Allah's promise ˹to help˺ is never broken” (6:34). The verse therefore does more than instruct; it indicates what the waiting is waiting for.

**A Note on the Claim of Abrogation**

Some commentators held that this verse was later abrogated by the verse of the sword. The claim deserves a response rather than a mere report. What the verse commands is following the revelation and patience until God's judgment; neither element is a legal ruling that a later ruling could replace. Patience is not the absence of action, and the command to follow what is revealed is not suspended by any subsequent command — it is fulfilled by it. The stronger reading is therefore that the verse remains in force, and that its patience describes a disposition rather than a prohibition. The claim of abrogation is recorded in the tradition and it is a real position, but it rests on treating a command about endurance as though it were a ruling about conduct in war.

**The Chapter Ends Where It Began**

The sūrah opened with letters, a wise Book, and the statement that God created and then established Himself on the Throne. It closes with a command to follow what was revealed and to wait for the best of judges. The arc is complete. The opening established the source and the sovereignty; the closing states what the recipient is to do with both. Between them the chapter argued from creation, provision, the senses and the alternation of night and day; narrated Noah, the messengers after him, Moses and Pharaoh; described the friends of God and the fabricators; and stated the conditions of belief and its refusal. The final verse gathers all of it into two commands and one name, and it leaves the messenger — and the reader — inside the interval, following and waiting. Nothing in the chapter is left unresolved, and nothing is left for anyone else to finish. The revelation has been given, the outcome has been described, and the only task remaining is the one stated in the last two lines of the sūrah. Following and waiting are the only two things left to do, and both lie within the reach of anyone who has read this far.
